# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 23

## Page 11664

Volume Twenty-Three
The Book of Jurisprudence
Part Three:
From Prostration for Forgetfulness to the Prayer of Those with Valid Excuses

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

Chapter on Prostration for Forgetfulness

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him abundant peace.

Section on Prostration for Forgetfulness

Several important matters arise here, including questions concerning doubt, whether these prostrations should be performed before or after the concluding salutation, and whether they are obligatory. We therefore say—and there is neither might nor power except through Allah:

Regarding doubt, there are authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which are all consistent with one another, praise be to Allah. People have differed only because some of them have not understood his intent. In the two Sahīh collections, it is narrated from Abū

---

## Page 11665

Hurayrah that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When one of you stands to pray, Satan comes to him and confuses him until he no longer knows how many units of prayer he has performed. If any of you experiences this, let him perform two prostrations while seated.”

The two Sahīh collections also narrate from him that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the call to prayer is made, Satan retreats, passing wind so that he cannot hear the call. When the call ends, he returns. When the call is repeated, he retreats; when the repeated call ends, he returns, distracting the worshipper by saying, ‘Remember this; remember that,’ mentioning things he had not been thinking about, until the man no longer knows how many units of prayer he has performed. If any of you does not know how many he has performed, let him perform two prostrations while seated.”

In one wording narrated by al-Bukhārī:

“If any of you does not know how many units of prayer he has performed—three or four—let him perform two prostrations while seated.”

Another wording reads:

“He should perform the two prostrations for forgetfulness.”

This authentic hadith commands the two prostrations for forgetfulness when a person does not know how many units of prayer he has performed. This implies that the prostrations are obligatory, as the majority hold. It also calls them the two prostrations for forgetfulness, indicating that they are prescribed only for forgetfulness, as the majority hold.

His words, “Let him perform two prostrations while seated,” are unrestricted: he did not specify whether they should be performed before or after the concluding salutation. He did, however, command that they be performed before the person gets up. In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī narrated that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If any of you has doubts during his prayer and does not know how many units he has performed, three or four

---

## Page 11666

, let him set aside his doubt and proceed on the basis of what he knows for certain, then perform two prostrations before the concluding salutation. If he has prayed five units, these two prostrations will make his prayer even; if he has prayed exactly four, they will serve to humiliate Satan.”

This hadith instructs anyone who doubts and does not know to set aside his doubt. It also commands two prostrations before the concluding salutation.

People have differed in their understanding of his words, “If any of you has doubts.” Some have understood this to mean that anyone who lacks certainty is in doubt, even if he considers one of the two possibilities more likely. Thus, they regarded someone who considered one possibility more likely as being in doubt, even when those praying behind him agreed with him. They instructed him to disregard what he doubted and proceed on the basis of what he knew for certain. They said that the default assumption is that whatever is in doubt has not occurred. They therefore gave unconditional precedence to the presumption that the previous state continues, even when supporting evidence and indications pointed to the contrary, and gave no weight whatsoever to exercising judgment to determine what was most likely correct.

Some of them interpreted the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the other hadith, “Let him exercise his judgment,” as meaning that he should proceed on the basis of certainty.

Another group said that, if he is leading the prayer, the doubt intended is one in which the two possibilities are equally likely; if he is praying alone, it means what the first group described.

A third group said: Rather, doubt here means that the two possibilities are equally, or nearly equally, likely. When one outweighs the other, however, he should act on the more likely possibility. This is what is meant by exercising judgment. Three positions are reported from Imam Ahmad, corresponding to these three views.

The first is the view of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and the position preferred by many of Ahmad’s followers.

---

## Page 11667

The second is the view of al-Khiraqī and Abū Muhammad, who said that it is the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

The third is the view of many earlier and later scholars. It is reported from ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd and others, and is the position of Abū Hanīfah and his followers when forgetfulness recurs.

Ahmad said, as reported by al-Athram: “There is a distinction between exercising judgment and proceeding on the basis of certainty. The hadith of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf states: ‘If he does not know whether he has prayed three units or two, he should count them as two.’”

He said: “This person has acted on what is certain and proceeded on that basis. By contrast, someone who exercises his judgment has prayed three units, but a doubt enters his heart that he has prayed only two, although my stronger impression is that he has prayed three. Some misgiving has entered his heart, so he exercises his judgment to determine which possibility is most likely correct, and performs the prostrations after the concluding salutation.” He said: “Thus, there is a distinction between the two.”

I say: The hadith of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf mentioned by Ahmad is comparable to the hadith of Abū Saʿīd. It is recorded in the Sunan collections, and al-Tirmidhī and others have graded both hadiths authentic.

ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If any of you has doubts during his prayer and does not know whether he has prayed too many units or too few, then, if he doubts whether he has prayed one or two, let him count them as one. If he does not know whether he has prayed two or three, let him count them as two. If he does not know whether he has prayed three or four, let him count them as three, so that the doubt concerns only an additional unit. Then let him perform two prostrations while seated, before giving the concluding salutation, and then give the salutation.”

---

## Page 11668

Among the most authentic hadiths on this subject is Ibn Mas'ūd’s hadith concerning the exercise of judgment, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections. The hadith of Abū Sa'īd was recorded by Muslim alone, but the hadith of 'Abd al-Rahmān ibn 'Awf corroborates it. Together, the two are comparable to Ibn Mas'ūd’s hadith, recorded in the two Sahīh collections through Ibrāhīm, from 'Alqamah, from 'Abd Allāh ibn Mas'ūd, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed—Ibrāhīm said: ‘He prayed either more or fewer units.’ When he gave the concluding salutation, he was asked: ‘O Messenger of Allah, has something changed concerning the prayer?’ He asked: ‘What do you mean?’ They said: ‘You prayed such-and-such a number of units.’ He then folded his legs, faced the direction of prayer, performed two prostrations, and gave the concluding salutation. He then turned to face us and said: ‘Had anything changed concerning the prayer, I would have informed you. But I am only human: I forget just as you forget. So when I forget, remind me. If any of you has doubts during his prayer, let him exercise his judgment to determine what is correct, complete his prayer on that basis, and then perform two prostrations.’”

In one of al-Bukhārī’s chains of transmission, the wording is:

“He was asked: ‘O Messenger of Allah, has the prayer been shortened, or did you forget?’ He asked: ‘What do you mean?’ They said: ‘You prayed such-and-such a number of units.’ He then led them in two prostrations and said: ‘These two prostrations are for someone who does not know whether he has prayed too many units or too few. He should exercise his judgment to determine what is correct, complete his prayer on that basis, and then perform two prostrations.’”

Another version recorded by al-Bukhārī reads:

“Let him complete his prayer on that basis, then give the concluding salutation, and then perform two prostrations.”

A version recorded by Muslim reads:

“Let him consider which possibility is more likely to be correct.”

Another version he records reads:

“Let him exercise his judgment to determine what he believes to be correct.”

Another version reads:

“Let him exercise his judgment to determine which possibility is closest to being correct.”

---

## Page 11669

And in the two Sahīh collections, Ibrāhīm narrates from ‘Alqamah, from ‘Abd Allāh, who said:

“We prayed with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he prayed either more or fewer units. Ibrāhīm said: ‘By Allah, that uncertainty comes only from me.’ We said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, has something changed concerning the prayer?’ He replied: ‘No.’ We then told him what he had done, and he said: ‘If someone prays more or fewer units, let him perform two prostrations.’” The narrator said: “He then performed two prostrations.”

Some proponents of this view have interpreted exercising judgment to mean disregarding what is doubtful and proceeding on the basis of what is certain. This interpretation is weak for several reasons.

One is that the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, the Musnad, and other collections contain the wording:

“If, during your prayer, you are unsure whether you have prayed three, four, or more than four units, recite the tashahhud and then prostrate from a sitting position.”

Another is that the various wordings explicitly instruct the worshipper to exercise his judgment to determine what he believes to be correct, whether that is the greater or the smaller number. Had he been instructed simply to disregard what is doubtful in every case, there would be no exercise of judgment to determine what is correct.

Another is that Ibn Mas‘ūd, who narrated the hadith, explained it in this way. Successive generations of the scholars of Kufa, such as Ibrāhīm and his followers, learned this from him. Abū Hanīfah and his companions, in turn, learned it from Ibrāhīm.

Another is that here he instructed that the two prostrations be performed after the concluding salutation, whereas in the hadith of Abū

---

## Page 11670

Sa'īd he instructed that the two prostrations be performed before the concluding salutation.

Another reason is that he said there:

“If he has prayed five units, the two prostrations will make his prayer even in number; and if he has prayed the full four, they will humiliate Satan.”

This makes clear that he proceeds on the basis of certainty while remaining unsure whether he has prayed more or fewer units—whether he has prayed four or five. It also makes clear the benefit of the two prostrations in either of these two mutually exclusive cases.

In the hadith of Ibn Mas'ūd, he said:

“Let him exercise his judgment to determine what is correct, complete his prayer on that basis, and then perform two prostrations.”

Another wording states:

“Let him complete his prayer on that basis, then give the concluding salutation, and then perform two prostrations.”

He thus regarded what the worshipper did after exercising his judgment as completing his prayer, and described him here as completing his prayer without being in doubt about it.

However, the word “doubt” sometimes refers to anything short of certainty, even when there are indications and supporting evidence for it. Indeed, concerning his statement:

“We have more right to doubt than Abraham,”

it has been said that he described a state short of the reassurance of heart that Abraham sought as “doubt,” even though Abraham was certain and had no doubt that undermined his certainty. Thus, when his Lord asked him, “Do you not believe?” he replied, “Yes, but so that my heart may be reassured.” And Allah Most High said:

“Thus We showed Abraham the dominion of the heavens and the earth, so that he might be among those of sure faith.”

If even such a state has been called “doubt” in his statement, “We have more right to doubt than Abraham,” then what of someone who has no certainty? Thus, whoever acts on the stronger of two proofs

---

## Page 11671

has acted on knowledge, not on conjecture or doubt, even if he is not certain that there is no evidence stronger than that on which he has acted. The independent reasoning of scholars falls into this category. When a judge rules on the testimony of two upright witnesses, he rules on knowledge, not conjecture and ignorance. The same applies when he rules on a person’s admission, which is that person’s testimony against himself. Nevertheless, the underlying reality may differ from what appears outwardly, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said in an authentic hadith:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may be more eloquent in presenting their case than others. I judge only according to what I hear. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only assigning him a portion of the Fire.”

Now, you know that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not mean this kind of doubt when he said:

“If any of you is in doubt.”

Indeed, most people do not have absolute certainty, admitting no possibility of doubt, about the number of units in every prayer they have offered. Rather, they hold a belief about the number of units that they consider most likely to be correct. This is not doubt. The statement of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him,

“If any of you is in doubt,”

applies only to someone who has neither a belief he considers more likely to be correct nor a predominant conviction. If such a person exercises his judgment, considers the matter and reflects, it may become clear to him that one of the two possibilities is more likely, so that he is no longer in doubt. This is the person described in the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd: he was in doubt before exercising his judgment, but afterward he was no longer in doubt.

The same applies in other situations requiring the exercise of judgment. For example, someone may be unsure of the direction of prayer, then exercise his judgment until he considers one direction more likely to be correct; he is then no longer in doubt. The same applies to a scholar exercising independent judgment, someone who remembers what he had forgotten, and other such cases.

---

## Page 11672

The Prophet’s words in the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd,

“If any of you is in doubt,”

address someone whose doubt persists because he cannot exercise his judgment, having no indication or evidence that makes either possibility more likely, or because he has exercised his judgment and reflected but still finds neither possibility more likely.

Anyone who says that there is no evidence here to distinguish between the two possibilities is mistaken. If he is leading the prayer, he may find evidence in the agreement of those praying behind him. He may also rely on information given by someone else, even if that person is not praying with him, and thereby come to regard one possibility as more likely.

He may remember what he recited during the prayer, recalling that he recited two surahs in two units, and thus know that he has prayed two units, not one. He may remember having recited the first tashahhud (testimony of faith), and thus know that he has prayed two units, not one, and three, not two. He may remember having recited al-Fātihah alone in one unit and then in another, and thus know that he has prayed four, not three. He may remember having prayed two units after the first tashahhud, and thus know that he has prayed four, not three, and two, not one. He may remember having recited the first tashahhud and that his doubt concerns a unit after it, and thus know that he has prayed three, not two.

Another possibility is that something occurs during one of the units—whether supplication and humble devotion, coughing or the like, or something else—by which he recognizes that particular unit and knows that he prayed one, two or three units before it. His doubt is thereby dispelled.

Such cases are too varied to be exhaustively listed. People are constantly uncertain whether things happened or not. Then they remember and find indications that those things did happen, thereby dispelling

---

## Page 11673

their doubt. Thus, when a person exercises his judgment to determine what is most likely correct, he dispels his doubt. In this respect, there is no difference between someone leading the prayer and someone praying alone. If, after exercising his judgment, he concludes that he has prayed four units, then praying a fifth would mean that, in his own estimation, he has prayed five units, which he was not commanded to do. This differs from the case in which both possibilities are equally likely: uncertainty about having prayed too many or too few units is then unavoidable, and uncertainty about having prayed too many is preferable. Any extra unit prayed in a state of doubt is like one added inadvertently, and that does not invalidate his prayer. By contrast, if he is uncertain whether he has prayed too few, he is uncertain whether he has performed what he was commanded to do, so his obligation remains undischarged.

Furthermore, the possible positions on this matter are as follows:

One may say that a person must always disregard his doubt without exercising his judgment, or interpret exercising judgment as disregarding doubt. This plainly contradicts the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd. Alternatively, one may apply one hadith to someone leading the prayer and the other to someone praying alone. Yet both hadiths clearly address those performing prayer: the Prophet did not address prayer leaders in one and those praying alone in the other. Nor does the wording of either hadith indicate such a distinction. To claim that this is what the Messenger intended, without anything in his words indicating it, is to attribute concealment and obfuscation to him, whereas he is above such things. Moreover, when both possibilities are equally likely, the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd applies to everyone

---

## Page 11674

by consensus. It is therefore impermissible to exclude prayer leaders from its scope. The hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd covers the same cases as the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd. Thus, only the third possibility remains:

Both address the person who is in doubt. One commands him to exercise his judgment, if he can, thereby dispelling his doubt; the other tells him what to do if his doubt persists.

This is like telling a judge, “Rule on the basis of evidence,” or “Rule on the basis of witnesses,” and similar instructions. This applies when such evidence is available. If it is unavailable, he falls back on the presumption of continuity—namely, the presumption of freedom from liability.

Likewise, a person who doubts during prayer acts on whatever makes the correct course clear to him. If that is not possible, he falls back on the presumption of continuity. And Allah knows best.

Furthermore, acting on one’s considered judgment puts an end to Satan’s whisperings or reduces them. Without exercising his judgment, however, a person remains subject to Satan’s attempts to make him doubt whether he has performed what he has actually performed.

They have also said that if, after the closing salutation, a person doubts whether he omitted an obligatory act, he should disregard that doubt. This is only because he apparently gave the salutation after completing the prayer. Thus, what is apparently the case takes precedence over the presumption of continuity. This principle underlies most matters of religious law. The same applies to counting circuits around the Kaaba, passages between Safā and Marwah, stones thrown at the stoning sites, and similar acts.

What further clarifies this is that relying solely on the presumption that a prior state of nonexistence continues is the weakest of all proofs; even the slightest evidence outweighs it, as with the presumption of freedom from liability

---

## Page 11675

when denying that something is obligatory or prohibited. This, by universal agreement, is the weakest form of evidence, and all agree that it may be resorted to only after a thorough investigation into whether any evidence in the revealed law establishes an obligation or prohibition.

Some do not permit reliance on it to deny a ruling, but only to rebut an opponent and challenge his claim. Such a person says: “I neither affirm nor deny the obligation. Rather, I demand evidence from whoever affirms it, or challenge his claim, or rebut his attempt to establish an obligation without evidence.” This is the position taken by some followers of Abū Hanīfah. As for the literalists, it is their principal basis of argument, but only after investigating the evidence of the revealed law.

It is impermissible to assert that things are absent or nonexistent merely on the basis of this presumption of continuity, without adducing evidence that entails their nonexistence. Whoever does so is speaking falsely and without knowledge, for many things exist in the world of which a person knows nothing. His lack of knowledge is not knowledge of nonexistence. Nor does the mere presumption that events have not occurred yield knowledge that any particular event has not occurred, unless there is evidence establishing its nonoccurrence.

The presumption of continuity is, however, used to tip the balance when evidence conflicts. Any evidence establishing that something has occurred outweighs the mere presumption that it has not. This is the correct conclusion that the person praying is instructed to seek through considered judgment: any evidence indicating that he has prayed four units outweighs the presumption that he has not performed the prayer. This is the essence of the issue.

---

## Page 11676

Section

As for the “second question”—when the prostrations should be performed, before or after the salām (the salutation ending the prayer)—there are several well-known views.

One view is that they should always be performed before the salām; another, that they should always be performed after it; and a third distinguishes between additions and omissions. According to this third view, there is disagreement concerning cases of doubt.

Another view is that the basic rule is to prostrate before the salām, except in those cases where the Sunnah prescribes prostrating after it. In those cases, one prostrates afterward on the authority of the text, while all remaining cases follow the basic rule. This is the well-known position reported from Ahmad. The first view is that of al-Shāfi'ī; the second, that of Abū Hanīfah; and the third, that of Mālik and Ahmad, although differing reports have been transmitted from Ahmad. Thus, concerning someone who prays five units, two positions are reported from him as to whether the prostrations should be performed before or after the salām. A position has also been attributed to him that they should always be performed before the salām, but we have found no statement of his to that effect. It has likewise been attributed to him that they should always be performed after the salām, but this is entirely mistaken.

Al-Qādī and others say: “Imam Ahmad’s statements have never differed on the point that some of these prostrations

---

## Page 11677

are performed before the salām and others after it.”

Al-Qādī Abū Ya'lā said:
“Ahmad’s position does not differ concerning these two cases: the prostrations are performed after the salām when someone gives the salām with one or more units of prayer still remaining, and when he has doubts and exercises his judgment to determine what is most likely correct.”

Ahmad said, as reported by al-Athram:
“My position is this: in every case of forgetfulness in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have prostrated after the salām, one should prostrate after the salām. In all other cases, one should prostrate before the salām. This is sounder in principle, because these prostrations belong to the prayer, so one should perform them before giving the salām.”

He then said: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated after the salām in three cases, and before the salām in the others.”

I said: “Explain the three cases in which the prostrations come after the salām.”

He replied:
“‘He gave the salām after two units, then prostrated after the salām.’ This is the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn. ‘He gave the salām after three units, then prostrated after the salām.’ This is the hadith of 'Imrān ibn Husayn. And in the hadith of Ibn Mas'ūd concerning exercising judgment, he prostrated after the salām.”

Abū Muhammad said:
“Al-Qādī said: ‘Ahmad’s position does not differ concerning these two cases: the prostrations for them are performed after the salām.’ He added: ‘His position differed concerning someone who inadvertently prays five units: should he prostrate before or after the salām? Two positions are reported from him. In all cases other than these three, the prostrations are performed before the salām, with only one position reported from him. This was also the view of Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd, Abū Khaythamah, and Ibn al-Mundhir.’”

He added: Abū al-Khattāb also reported two further positions:

---

## Page 11678

One was that all such prostrations are performed before the salām; this is the position of al-Shāfi'ī.

The second was that prostrations for an omission are performed before the salām, on the basis of the hadith of Ibn Buhaynah, while those for an addition are performed after the salām, on the basis of the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn and the hadith of Ibn Mas'ūd concerning the occasion when the Prophet prayed five units. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Thawr.

Abū Hanīfah, his companions, and a group of scholars held that all such prostrations are performed after the salām.

I say: Ahmad holds that when someone discards his doubt and proceeds on the basis of certainty, he prostrates before the salām, as established in the authentic hadith. Thus, according to the position in which Ahmad agrees with Mālik, prostrations for an omission or doubt come before the salām, while those for an addition come after it. It has also been related from Mālik that one prostrates after the salām, because an addition is possible in this case, but not an omission.

The case of an addition concerning which Ahmad gave differing rulings is that of someone who prays five units. An authentic report establishes that the prostrations are performed after the salām. In that instance, however, he had forgotten. In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Mas'ūd said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led us in five units of prayer. When he turned away, the people began murmuring among themselves. He asked, ‘What is the matter with you?’ They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, has the prayer been increased?’ He replied, ‘No.’ They said, ‘But you prayed five units.’ He then turned back, performed two prostrations, and gave the salām. Then he said, ‘I am only human; I forget just as you forget.’”

In another version, he said: “I am only a human being like you. I remember just as you remember, and I forget just as you forget. So if any of you forgets, let him prostrate

---

## Page 11679

twice while seated.” Then the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, turned and performed two prostrations.

Al-Bukhārī reports from Ibn Mas'ūd:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed five units for the noon prayer, then performed two prostrations after giving the salām.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Mas'ūd reports:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the two prostrations for forgetfulness after giving the salām and speaking.”

This is the case concerning which Ahmad’s rulings differed: should one prostrate after the salām, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did, or before it if one remembers before giving the salām? The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only prostrated after the salām because he did not remember until he had given it and they reminded him.

According to one of the two reports of Ahmad’s position, prostration after the salām is not restricted to the particular case addressed by the text, as the majority—including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and others—have maintained. Likewise, prostration before the salām is not restricted to the particular case addressed by the text, as the majority—Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and others—have maintained. Rather, the correct view is that some prostrations are performed before the salām and others after it, as established by the authentic hadiths.

Anyone who holds that all such prostrations are performed before the salām, citing al-Zuhrī’s report that “the later of the two practices was prostration before the salām,” has claimed abrogation. This is a weak claim. For, concerning prostration after the salām in the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn, Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, and the majority

---

## Page 11680

maintain that it has not been abrogated. Only those who cite al-Zuhrī’s statement that Dhū al-Yadayn died before Badr and that this incident occurred early on claim that it was abrogated. Al-Zuhrī’s claim of abrogation rests on this premise, which is weak: Abū Hurayrah prayed behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, during the incident involving Dhū al-Yadayn, yet he only embraced Islam in the year of Khaybar.

Those who cite al-Zuhrī’s statement here have rejected his claim of abrogation there, while those who maintain that the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn was abrogated prescribe prostration after the salām. Thus, each group has claimed, without evidence, that the hadith was abrogated insofar as it contradicts its own position. Yet the hadith remains in force both in establishing that the prayer is not invalidated and in prescribing prostration after the salām. Neither ruling is opposed by any report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that would abrogate it.

Furthermore, abrogation can occur only through something that contradicts the abrogated ruling. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated after the salām, and no Muslim has transmitted that he forbade this. The claim of abrogation is therefore invalid.

If it is said that he subsequently prostrated before the salām, then, if this occurred in a different situation—as in the hadith of Ibn Buhaynah, when he stood up after two units of prayer, or in the hadith concerning doubt—there is no contradiction. However, the person making this assumption supposed that prostrating before the salām in one situation abrogated prostrating after it in another. This is his mistake. It has not been transmitted that, in one and the same situation, he sometimes prostrated before

---

## Page 11681

the salām and at other times after it. Even if this had been reported, it would indicate that both are permissible. Thus, the claim of abrogation in this matter is invalid.

How could his command to prostrate after the salām in a particular situation, and his own practice of doing so, be invalidated by something that does not contradict them?

As for those who maintain that all such prostrations should be performed after the salām and cite the hadith of Thawbān recorded in the Sunan collections:

“For every lapse of forgetfulness, there are two prostrations after the salām,”

this report is weak, because Ibn ‘Ayyāsh narrates it from narrators of the Hijaz, and his narrations from them are weak by agreement of the hadith scholars. They also cite the hadith of Ibn Ja‘far:

“Whoever is in doubt about his prayer should perform two prostrations after giving the salām.”

However, its chain includes Ibn Abī Laylā. Al-Athram said, “Neither report is established as authentic.” Moreover, the latter may be like the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“If he is in doubt, he should determine what is most likely correct,”

and may be an abridged version of that report. Such a report cannot stand against the authentic hadith of Abū Sa‘īd concerning doubt:

“He commanded two prostrations before the salām,”

nor against the hadith of Ibn Buhaynah recorded in the two Sahīh collections, which is a foundational report for the rulings on forgetfulness in prayer: he omitted the first tashahhud and prostrated before the salām. These authentic hadiths demonstrate the weakness of every position that generalizes by placing all such prostrations before the salām or all of them after it. What remains is to distinguish between the cases.

It may therefore be said: the Lawgiver is wise and does not distinguish between two things unless there is a difference between them. He would not prescribe some prostrations after the salām and others before it unless there were a difference between the two.

---

## Page 11682

The position of those who say, “Analogical reasoning requires all such prostrations to be performed before the salām, but textual evidence overrides that reasoning in certain cases, while the remaining cases continue to follow it,” requires two things: first, evidence establishing that all such prostrations should be performed before the salām; and second, an explanation of the distinctive factor in the exceptional cases that warrants distinguishing them from the others. Otherwise, if the reason requiring prostration before the salām applies to every case, the Lawgiver could not prescribe prostration after the salām in some of them. If He has distinguished between them for a reason, that reason must be specific to the exceptional cases. If the difference between the cases excluded and those retained is unknown, distinguishing between them has no evidential basis.

If he says:

“I know that the reason requiring prostration before the salām applies generally. However, when the text made its exceptions, I knew that a countervailing consideration existed in those cases.”

He is told in reply:

In a case not addressed by a text, there may be a reason requiring prostration before the salām, or there may be a reason requiring it after the salām. You do not know that the reason requiring prostration after the salām in those particular cases is absent from all others. Moreover, since some prostrations are performed after the salām, the complete set of grounds requiring prostration before the salām cannot apply universally. You therefore no longer have a general rationale on which to base a categorical assertion that prostration in an unresolved case must precede the salām, or that the reason requiring it after the salām is confined to the case addressed by the text. Thus, denying a distinction is an assertion made without evidence warranting the distinction. It amounts to allowing exceptions to the operation of the legal cause without showing that a condition is absent or an impediment is present. This is pure juristic preference

---

## Page 11683

in which no distinction has been established between the case treated by juristic preference and other cases. Thus, the strongest position is to distinguish between additions and omissions, and between doubt resolved by judging what is most likely and doubt resolved by proceeding on the basis of certainty.

This is one of the positions reported from Ahmad. Mālik’s position is close to it, but not identical. Besides giving effect to all the texts, this position draws a rationally intelligible distinction.

When there is an omission, such as leaving out the first tashahhud, the prayer needs to be made whole. The prostrations that make it whole are performed before the salām so that the prayer is completed through them, since the salām releases one from the prayer. When there is an addition, such as an extra rak‘ah, two additions should not be combined within the prayer. Rather, the prostrations are performed after the salām, for they serve to humiliate Satan and are like an independent prayer through which one remedies the deficiency in one’s prayer. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, treated the two prostrations as equivalent to a rak‘ah.

Likewise, when someone doubts and acts on what he judges most likely, he has completed his prayer, and the two prostrations serve only to humiliate Satan; they therefore follow the salām. Mālik, however, does not endorse acting on what one judges most likely, nor prostrating after the salām in this case.

Similarly, if someone pronounces the salām while part of his prayer remains, then completes it, he has thereby completed his prayer, while the earlier salām constitutes an addition. In this case, the prostrations follow the salām because they serve to humiliate Satan.

---

## Page 11684

But when someone doubts and cannot determine which possibility is more likely, he will have prayed either four or five rak‘ahs. If he has prayed five, the two prostrations make his prayer even-numbered, as though he had prayed six rather than five. This can only be achieved before the salām, whereas Mālik says that in this case he should prostrate after the salām. Thus, the position we have defended gives effect to all the hadiths without setting any aside, while applying sound analogy to cases not addressed by an explicit text and assimilating them to comparable cases that are.

This is further clarified by the fact that when an inadvertent error involves the salām, the prostrations are performed after the salām. Accordingly, one may argue as follows:

If someone inadvertently adds something other than the salām that belongs to the acts of prayer—such as a rak‘ah, a bowing, or a prostration—this is an addition that would invalidate his prayer if made deliberately, just as with the salām. It is therefore more appropriate to treat it by analogy with the salām than with omitting the first tashahhud or doubting and proceeding on the basis of certainty.

As for the argument that the prostrations belong to the prayer and should therefore be performed before the salām, the response is: if this were correct, all such prostrations would have to precede the salām. Since it is established that some follow the salām, we know that these prostrations are not inherently part of the prayer that must be performed before the salām.

This is countered by the argument that the prostrations are not among the obligations entailed by entering the inviolable state of prayer, since entering that state obligates only a prayer free of defects. These are claims unsupported by evidence. Rather, one should say: entering the inviolable state of prayer makes obligatory

---

## Page 11685

the prostrations by which the prayer is made whole.

It may also be said that some prostrations rectify the prayer when performed after the salām, so that the prayer does not contain two additions. Moreover, once the prayer is complete, they humiliate Satan and counter his intention with its very opposite. He sought to impair the servant’s prayer through the addition he introduced into it, so the servant is commanded to humiliate him by performing two additional prostrations after the salām. This increases his worship of Allah, his prostration before Allah, and his drawing near to Allah—the very thing Satan sought to diminish for him. Satan sought to reduce his good deeds, but Allah commanded him to complete his prayer and humiliate Satan.

Allah has pardoned whatever a person inadvertently adds to the prayer, whether a salām, an extra rak‘ah, or anything else, so he incurs no sin thereby. Nevertheless, his drawing near to Allah may be deficient because of the shortcomings caused by his forgetfulness. Allah therefore commanded him to make this complete through two prostrations in addition to the prayer. And Allah knows best.

Section

As for the obligation to perform these prostrations, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded them merely on account of doubt in the previously cited hadith of Abū Hurayrah, saying:

“When any one of you stands to pray, Satan comes to him and confuses him about his prayer until he no longer knows how much he has prayed. If any one of you experiences this, let him perform two prostrations while seated.”

He also commanded them when a person sets doubt aside

---

## Page 11686

saying in the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd:

“Let him set doubt aside and proceed on the basis of what he is certain of, then perform two prostrations before saying the salām. If he has prayed five rak‘ahs, these two prostrations will make his prayer even; and if he has prayed the full four, they will humiliate Satan.”

Likewise, in the hadith of ‘Abd al-Rahmān:

“Then let him perform two prostrations while seated, before saying the salām, and then say the salām.”

He also commanded this in Ibn Mas‘ūd’s hadith concerning the exercise of one’s best judgment, saying:

“Let him determine what is most likely correct, complete his prayer accordingly, and then perform two prostrations.”

Another wording states:

“These two prostrations are for someone who does not know whether he has added to his prayer or omitted something from it. Let him determine what is most likely correct, complete his prayer accordingly, and then perform two prostrations.”

In the other hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd, reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“We said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, has something new been introduced into the prayer?’ He replied, ‘No.’ We then told him what he had done, and he said, ‘If a person adds to the prayer or omits something from it, let him perform two prostrations.’” The narrator said: “Then he performed two prostrations.”

Thus, he commanded the two prostrations whenever a person adds something or omits something. What he meant was adding something he had prohibited or omitting something he had commanded. This establishes the obligation to prostrate for any commanded act that a person inadvertently omits, provided that the inadvertent omission does not require him to perform the omitted act itself, and whenever he inadvertently adds something prohibited.

Accordingly, whenever a person inadvertently omits any commanded act in the prayer, he must either perform it when he remembers it or perform the prostrations of forgetfulness. One of the two is indispensable.

---

## Page 11687

Thus, if a person forgets the prayer itself, he must perform it when he remembers it; there is no expiation for it other than that. The same applies if he forgets the purification required for it, as when the Prophet commanded the man who had left a small patch on his foot untouched by water to repeat his ablution and prayer. Likewise, if he forgets a rak‘ah, as in the hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn, he must perform what he forgot, either by adding it to what he has already prayed or by beginning the prayer anew.

These, then, are five authentic hadiths, in all of which he commands the person who forgets to perform the two prostrations of forgetfulness. When he inadvertently omitted the first tashahhud, he led the Muslims in performing these two prostrations before the salām. When he ended the prayer with the salām after two or three rak‘ahs, he prayed what remained and led the Muslims in performing the two prostrations after the prayer. When they reminded him that he had prayed five rak‘ahs, he performed the two prostrations after having said the salām and spoken.

This indicates that he consistently performed these two prostrations and emphasized their importance, and that he never omitted them when an instance of forgetfulness called for them. These are clear, evident proofs that they are obligatory. This is the position of the majority of scholars and the school of Mālik, Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah. Those who do not consider them obligatory have no evidence approaching this in strength.

Al-Shāfi‘ī did not consider them obligatory only because, in his view, there is no obligatory act within the prayer whose omission leaves the prayer valid, whether omitted deliberately or inadvertently. The majority of scholars—the three imams and others—hold that some obligatory acts of prayer may be omitted without invalidating it. However, Mālik, Ahmad and others say that deliberately omitting such an act invalidates the prayer and requires it to be repeated, whereas omitting it inadvertently requires the prostrations of forgetfulness.

Abū Hanīfah says:

If he omits it deliberately, he has acted wrongly

---

## Page 11688

and his prayer is deficient, but he is not required to repeat it. As for deliberate additions to the prayer, they all agree that some additions invalidate it when made deliberately but not when made inadvertently. When made deliberately, however, they invalidate the prayer, so there are no prostrations to perform; when made inadvertently, they say that the addition is excused, so the prostrations are not obligatory.

Some have cited as evidence the reported wording of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, in the hadith concerning doubt:

“The rak‘ah and the two prostrations would count as voluntary worship.”

This wording is not found in the Sahīh. The wording in the Sahīh is:

“Let him disregard his doubt and proceed on the basis of what he is certain of, then perform two prostrations before saying the salām. If he has prayed five rak‘ahs, they will make his prayer even-numbered; and if he has prayed a full four, they will humiliate Satan.”

He thus commanded the prostrations and explained their wisdom, whether the person had prayed five rak‘ahs or four. He said:

“If he has prayed five rak‘ahs, they will make his prayer even-numbered.”

This implies that offering voluntary prayer with an odd number of rak‘ahs is not permissible. Rather, Allah has commanded that the daytime prayers be made odd-numbered through Maghrib, and the night prayers through Witr. Here, when a person in doubt has unknowingly prayed five rak‘ahs, he made the two prostrations take the place of one rak‘ah, thereby making his prayer even-numbered.

He said:

“And if he has prayed a full four, adding nothing to the prayer, they will humiliate Satan.”

Thus, this wording—“The rak‘ah and the two prostrations would count as voluntary worship for him”—cannot be used as evidence unless it is established that it was spoken by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

---

## Page 11689

How, then, could it be cited when his wording in the Sahīh entails that both are obligatory—the rak‘ah and the two prostrations?

The scholars agree that the rak‘ah is obligatory. Wherever it is held that someone in doubt must disregard his doubt and proceed on the basis of what he is certain of, the rak‘ah about which he is uncertain is obligatory. Since it is obligatory by the explicit text and scholarly agreement, and the reported wording concerns both it and the prostrations—which are likewise commanded—it follows that the wording cited does not negate the obligation to perform the two prostrations, any more than it negates the obligation to perform the rak‘ah.

If the Messenger did indeed say these words, their meaning is that the worshipper is commanded to do this when in doubt. If his prayer was actually complete, with nothing missing, this would constitute an addition to his deeds for which he would receive a reward, as with voluntary worship. This is the case with anyone who takes precautions by performing an act whose obligatory status he doubts: if it is obligatory, he has fulfilled it; otherwise, it counts as voluntary worship for him. Thus, he described it as voluntary in reality only on the supposition that the four rak‘ahs had already been completed. Yet the worshipper’s very doubt introduced a deficiency into his prayer, so he was commanded to perform the two prostrations even if he had prayed four rak‘ahs, to humiliate Satan.

This is like their instructing someone who has doubts concerning something other than the obligatory act to do what establishes that he has discharged his obligation: in reality, only one act is obligatory, and the additional act is voluntary. Likewise, someone who cannot distinguish his sister through breastfeeding from an unrelated woman is instructed to avoid both, although in reality only one is forbidden to him. Thus, the act in doubt is called obligatory in the sense that he is required

---

## Page 11690

to perform it, while it is called voluntary on a particular supposition: he is rewarded and recompensed for it; like voluntary worship, it is not wasted effort. In itself, it was not obligatory upon him, but became obligatory because of his doubt. Moreover, according to one of the two reports from Ahmad, he requires the prayer to be repeated with the local imam, yet calls it voluntary, in accordance with the Prophet’s command, peace and blessings be upon him. Likewise, in the hadith of Abū Dharr, he said:

“Perform the prayer at its proper time, then make your prayer with them voluntary, and do not say, ‘I have already prayed.’”

It is voluntary in the sense that it is additional to the five basic obligatory prayers, even if it becomes obligatory for another reason, as happens with an act made obligatory by a vow.

Many of the early generations used the term nāfilah (“additional worship”) for worship that increases one’s good deeds, which applies to someone who has no sins. Hence, concerning His words:

“And during part of the night, pray with it as additional worship for you,”

they said that this additional worship was exclusive to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, because Allah had forgiven him, whereas others have sins which their prayers serve to expiate. Although this view calls for discussion, this is not the place for it. The point is that the term nāfilah has been used broadly: it may refer to something commanded, while its use may be denied for voluntary worship.

It has thus become clear that the prostrations of forgetfulness are obligatory. Their cause is either an omission or an addition, as he said in the two Sahīhs:

“If he adds something or omits something, let him perform two prostrations.”

An omission is illustrated by the hadith of Ibn Buhaynah: when he omitted the first tashahhud, he prostrated. As for an addition,

---

## Page 11691

it is illustrated by his prostrating when he prayed five units of prayer. He also instructed the person who is unsure whether he has added or omitted something to prostrate. These, then, are the causes mentioned in the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him: an addition, an omission, or doubt. It has already been established that, in cases of omission and doubt, one prostrates before the closing salutation, and in cases of addition, after it.

Section

If these prostrations are obligatory and someone omits them, deliberately or forgetfully, whether they are due before or after the closing salutation, there are several views within the school of Ahmad and among others.

One view is that deliberately omitting those due before the closing salutation invalidates the prayer, whereas omitting them out of forgetfulness does not, just as with the first tashahhud and other obligatory acts. Omitting those due after the closing salutation does not invalidate the prayer under any circumstances, since they serve to remedy a deficiency after the closing salutation, and their omission therefore does not invalidate it. This is the view preferred by many of Ahmad’s followers.

Another view is that omitting those due before the closing salutation invalidates the prayer in either case. If someone omits them out of forgetfulness and remembers shortly afterwards, he performs the prostrations; if a long interval has elapsed, he repeats the prayer. This is transmitted as one report from Ahmad and is the view of Mālik, Abū Thawr, and others. This view is sounder than the preceding one. For if something is obligatory within the prayer and a person fails to perform it out of forgetfulness, he remains bound to fulfil it, even though he incurs no sin. The same applies to the prayer itself: if he forgets it, he performs it

---

## Page 11692

when he remembers it. The same applies to any obligatory acts of the prayer that he forgets: he must perform them when he remembers, either as a supplement to the prayer or by beginning the prayer anew. One is not discharged from the obligation of the prayer, or of its obligatory components, except by performing them.

As for obligatory acts that are said to be waived through forgetfulness, such as the first tashahhud, no one has said that they are waived without a substitute. Rather, they are replaced by the prostrations of forgetfulness, unlike the essential elements, such as bowing and prostration, for which there is no substitute. As for saying that these acts are obligatory within the prayer yet may be waived without a substitute, we know of no one who has said this. If anyone has, it is a weak view contrary to the established principles.

These, then, are two views concerning the prostrations obligatory before the closing salutation when they are omitted out of forgetfulness. The disagreement concerning those obligatory after it is similar.

Many of those who regard them as obligatory incline to the view that omitting them does not invalidate the prayer. This is because they remedy a deficiency in an act of worship while remaining external to it, so their omission does not invalidate that act, just as with the compensatory acts for Hajj. However, a report from Ahmad indicates that the prayer is invalidated if one omits the prostrations prescribed after the closing salutation. Al-Athram also reported that Ahmad withheld judgment on this question. Regarding someone who forgot the prostrations of forgetfulness, Ahmad was reported to have said:

“If it concerns a minor instance of forgetfulness, I hope that nothing is required of him.”

I said, “What if it concerns something in which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, himself forgot?”

He said, “Ah,” but gave no answer.

He said, “I subsequently heard that he considered it recommended to repeat it.”

In such “questions on which he withheld judgment,” his followers derive two legal positions.

---

## Page 11693

In sum, one view is that he must repeat the prayer if he deliberately omits these prostrations; another is that he must repeat it whether he omits them deliberately or forgetfully. The correct view is that he must either perform these prostrations or repeat the prayer. There is disagreement over how long afterward he may still perform them.

One view is that he may perform them so long as he remains in the mosque, provided the interval is not prolonged. Another is that he may perform them even after a prolonged interval, so long as he remains in the mosque. A third is that he may perform them even after leaving and going farther away. The point is that he must either perform them or repeat the prayer, because they are obligatory: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded them to complete the prayer. A person’s obligation is therefore not discharged without them. If he commands them after the prayer’s closing salutation, and one is told, “Either perform them or you must repeat the prayer,” there is nothing untenable in this.

To say that the prayer is invalid means that it has not discharged one’s obligation. In this respect, there is no difference between prostrations prescribed before the closing salutation and those prescribed afterward. Allah Most High has permitted ending the prayer with the salutation only on condition that one performs the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If one does not perform them, He has not permitted one to leave the prayer. One has thus ended the prayer with a salutation that was not commanded, thereby invalidating it.

Similarly, we say of someone who converts his Hajj into an ‘umrah to be followed by Hajj: he is permitted to leave ihrām (ritual consecration) only if he intends to do so and perform Hajj that same year. If, however, he intends simply to leave ihrām, he is not entitled to do that. He remains in ihrām, and his departure from it is not valid. Yet ihrām is not ended by a person’s repudiating it, committing any of the acts prohibited during it, or vitiating it. Rather, he remains in it even when it has been vitiated. Prayer is different: it is invalidated by doing something incompatible with it or prohibited during it.

---

## Page 11694

Their analogy between prayer and Hajj is invalid. If someone deliberately omits any of the obligations of Hajj that can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering, his Hajj is not invalidated. Rather, he must make compensation, which remains an obligation upon him and is never waived. Prayer, however, is invalidated if he omits one of its obligations. If it is said that this omission is compensated for by prostration, this entails that the prostration remains an obligation upon him, just as compensation for Hajj remains obligatory upon him. As for waiving both the obligation and its substitute, this has no basis in Islamic law. An analogy with Hajj would therefore entail saying that these prostrations after the closing salutation remain an obligation upon him until he performs them. This position is not untenable, unlike their assertion that the obligation lapses in favour of a substitute.

However, there is no disagreement that compensation for Hajj—the sacrificial offering—may be performed independently. As for these prostrations, there is disagreement over whether they may be performed independently after a prolonged interval.

We have said that one must either perform them or repeat the prayer. Thus, the view that they may be performed even after a prolonged interval, like a forgotten prayer, is well-founded and strong. A somewhat weaker view is that even someone who deliberately omits them may perform them later, although he sins by delaying them. This is comparable to someone who deliberately delays a forgotten prayer after remembering it: he should perform it and seek Allah’s forgiveness for delaying it. The same applies to a prayer deliberately allowed to lapse, according to those who hold that it can be made up: he should perform it and seek Allah’s forgiveness for delaying it.

Likewise, he should perform the two prostrations wherever he remembers them and seek Allah’s forgiveness for the delay. This, too, is a well-founded position, since Islamic law sets no precise limit based on the length of the interval or other such considerations. Similarly, there is no evidence in Islamic law for distinguishing between the mosque and elsewhere. Likewise,

---

## Page 11695

there is no evidence in Islamic law for distinguishing between before and after the loss of ritual purity. Rather, he must perform the two prostrations as best he can. And Allah knows best.

Section

Regarding the prostrations prescribed before or after the closing salutation: is this timing obligatory or recommended? There are two views on this within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere.

Many followers of the four imams hold that the disagreement concerns only what is recommended, and that performing the prostrations before or after the closing salutation is permissible in all cases.

The second view is that the prostrations prescribed before the closing salutation must be performed before it, while those prescribed after it must only be performed afterwards. This is what the statements of Ahmad and other imams indicate, and it is the correct view.

In the hadith concerning setting doubt aside, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Let him perform two prostrations before giving the closing salutation.”

Another version reads:

“Before giving the closing salutation; then let him give the salutation.”

In the hadith concerning exercising one’s best judgment, he said:

“Let him determine what is most likely to be correct and proceed on that basis, then perform two prostrations.”

In a version recorded by al-Bukhārī:

“Let him complete his prayer on that basis, then give the closing salutation, then perform two prostrations.”

In the latter, he commanded the closing salutation followed by the prostrations; in the former, he commanded the prostrations before the closing salutation. Both are commands from him, and a command entails obligation.

---

## Page 11696

When he spoke in terms encompassing both cases, he said:

“If he adds or omits something, let him perform two prostrations.”

He also said:

“If any of you does not know how much he has prayed, let him perform two prostrations while seated.”

Thus, when he mentioned omissions, additions and doubt in general terms, he commanded two prostrations without stipulating that they be performed before the closing salutation. When he commanded the worshipper to exercise his best judgment, he commanded the two prostrations after the closing salutation. These, then, are his commands, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning these matters; do not depart from them.

“It is not for a believing man or a believing woman, when Allah and His Messenger have decided a matter, to have any choice in their affair.”

However, someone who always performs the prostrations before the closing salutation, or always performs them afterwards, acting on an interpretation, incurs no blame. If the Sunnah subsequently becomes clear to him, he should henceforth act according to what has become clear to him, without having to repeat his prayers.

Likewise, anyone who omits an obligation because he did not know it was obligatory must fulfil it once he learns of its obligation, but need not repeat what he has already performed, according to the more correct of the two views within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. The same applies to someone who does something prohibited in prayer without knowing that it is prohibited, then learns of the prohibition—for example, someone who used to pray in camel resting places, or who did not perform an obligatory ablution because he did not know it was obligatory, such as ablution after eating camel meat.

This differs from the case of someone who forgets: a person who knows of the obligation but forgets must pray when he remembers, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sleeps through a prayer or forgets it, let him pray it when he remembers it.”

As for someone who did not know of the obligation, once he learns of it, he must perform the prayer whose time is then due and the prayers thereafter, but need not repeat his earlier prayers. As is established in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to the Bedouin

---

## Page 11697

who performed his prayer incorrectly:

“Go back and pray, for you have not prayed.” He replied, “By Him who sent you with the truth, I do not know how to pray any differently. Teach me what will suffice for my prayer.” So the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught him.

He commanded him to repeat the prayer then due, but did not command him to repeat his earlier prayers, despite his saying, “I do not know how to pray any differently.”

Likewise, he did not command ‘Umar and ‘Ammār to make up their prayers: when ‘Umar entered a state of major ritual impurity, he did not pray, while ‘Ammār rolled on the ground as an animal does. Nor did he command Abū Dharr to make up the prayers he had omitted while in a state of major ritual impurity. He did not command the woman suffering from irregular bleeding to make up what she had missed, despite her saying, “I suffer such severe irregular bleeding that it has prevented me from fasting and praying.” Nor did he command those who ate during Ramadan until they could distinguish white cords from black ones to repeat their fasts.

When prayer was first prescribed, each prayer consisted of two units. Then, after the Prophet emigrated, the prayer of those not travelling was increased to four obligatory units. Many Muslims in Mecca, Abyssinia and the desert regions did not learn of this until some time later and continued to pray two units. Yet he did not command them to repeat the prayers they had performed. Likewise, he did not command those who had prayed towards the former direction of prayer to repeat the prayers they had offered facing it before learning of the ruling that superseded it.

It is thus clear that there is no difference between a command introducing a new obligation and one abrogating an earlier ruling. The obligation to perform the two additional units was newly instituted, whereas the obligation to face the Kaaba abrogated an earlier ruling. Likewise, the testimony of faith in prayer and other acts became obligatory only at a later stage, and many

---

## Page 11698

Muslims did not learn of the obligation until some time later. Another example of an abrogated ruling is that a number of leading Companions did not perform ritual bathing after intercourse without ejaculation. Rather, they held that ritual bathing was required only after ejaculation, until its abrogation was established for them. For some, its abrogation was never established. They would therefore pray without the purification required by Islamic law, being unaware of its obligation; one of them would pray while in a state of major ritual impurity.

Section

If someone forgets the prostrations of forgetfulness until he has done something incompatible with prayer, such as speaking:

It is established in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of Ibn Mas‘ūd, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “prostrated after the salām and after speaking.” The account in the two Sahīh collections explains this:

“He led them in the noon prayer, praying five units. When he turned away, the people began murmuring among themselves. He asked, ‘What is the matter with you?’ They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, has anything been added to the prayer?’ He replied, ‘No.’ They said, ‘But you prayed five units.’ He then turned back, performed two prostrations, and concluded with the salām.”

This is the view of the majority of scholars, and it is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others. It is reported from Abū Hanīfa that if someone speaks after the salām, the obligation to perform the prostrations of forgetfulness lapses, because speech is incompatible with prayer, just like the occurrence of ritual impurity. It is reported from al-Hasan and Muhammad that if he turns his face away from

---

## Page 11699

the qiblah, he neither resumes the prayer nor performs the prostrations. The correct view is that of the majority, as the Sunnah explicitly establishes. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated after finishing the prayer, turning away and facing them, and after they had spoken, he had questioned them, and they had answered him. The hadith of Dhū al-Yadayn is even more explicit on this point:

“He prayed two units, then went over to a timber placed crosswise in the mosque and leaned against it. Dhū al-Yadayn then asked him, ‘Has the prayer been shortened, or have you forgotten?’ He answered him, then questioned the Companions, who confirmed what Dhū al-Yadayn had said. He returned to his place, prayed the remaining two units, then performed the two prostrations of forgetfulness after the salām.”

Those who were quick to leave had already gone out, saying, “The prayer has been shortened! The prayer has been shortened!”

The hadith of ‘Imrān, reported in the two Sahīh collections, states:

“He concluded the afternoon prayer with the salām after three units, then entered his home. Al-Khirbāq approached him and told him what he had done. He came out, dragging his cloak, until he reached the people and asked, ‘Is what this man says true?’ They replied, ‘Yes.’”

This incident was either distinct from the first, or it was the same incident, but one of the two narrators was unsure whether he had concluded the prayer after two units or three. One mentioned his going over to the timber placed crosswise in the mosque, while the other mentioned his entering his home. After all this speech and action, and his departure from the mosque, there is no doubt that he instructed those who had left early as to what they were to do.

Thus, either they—or some of them—returned to the mosque and completed the prayer with him, after having left the mosque and said, “The prayer has been shortened! The prayer has been shortened!”

---

## Page 11700

or they completed it on their own once they learned the Sunnah. In either case, they completed it after considerable movement and after leaving the mosque.

As for claiming that they were instructed to restart the prayer, no one has transmitted this. Had he instructed them to do so, it would have been transmitted. Nor were they at fault for what they had done.

In one form of the prayer of fear, he leads one group in a unit of prayer while the other faces the enemy. The first group then goes to join their companions’ ranks while still in prayer, moving about and turning their backs to the qiblah. The other group then comes, and he leads them in one unit, after which they go to join their companions’ ranks. Each group then prays another unit on its own. This involves considerable walking and turning away from the qiblah while they are still in prayer.

For either group, there may be a delay between the first and second units as they walk to their companions’ ranks, their companions come to stand behind the imam, pray behind him, and then return to the other group’s ranks. Only after all this do they pray the second unit. They remain standing in prayer throughout this movement and waiting, but do not perform that unit until all of this is over. It is thus clear that uninterrupted succession between the units of prayer is not obligatory when facing the enemy. It is even less necessary for the two prostrations to follow the prayer without interruption.

This differs from uninterrupted succession between the constituent parts of a single unit. This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad. Hence, if someone forgets an essential element, such as bowing, and remembers while still in the first unit—for example, after performing both prostrations—he performs the bowing and what follows it

---

## Page 11701

and what he performed before bowing is disregarded, since the interval is brief. This is the view of these scholars. If, however, he has begun the second unit—either its recitation, according to them, or its bowing, according to [these scholars. If he has begun the second unit—either its recitation, according to them, or its bowing, according to] (*) Mālik—then, according to al-Shāfiʿī, everything he performed after the omitted bowing is disregarded until he bows in the second unit. That bowing takes the place of the bowing of the first unit, even if the interval has been long. Thus, he combines parts of two units to make one unit. Ahmad preferred this to the position of the Kufan scholars, and it has been reported as one position transmitted from him.

The well-known position transmitted from him and from Mālik, however, is that they do not combine the two units in this way. Rather, the unit whose essential element was forgotten is disregarded, and this unit takes their place. According to their principle, therefore, a lack of uninterrupted succession invalidates the unit: its bowing and prostration must not be separated by an intervening act extraneous to it. For the smallest unit of prayer is a single unit. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever catches one unit of the prayer has caught it.”

A unit only constitutes a unit when its parts follow one another without interruption. If someone bows, then performs acts extraneous to the prayer, and then prostrates, this does not constitute a unit composed of bowing and prostration. Rather, it is an isolated bowing and an isolated prostration, and this is not prayer. Prostration follows bowing; prayer therefore requires bowing followed by prostration, and prostration followed by bowing. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere. These people, however, have the excuse of fear, while those others have the excuse of forgetfulness and lack of knowledge.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 191):
> The text in square brackets was inadvertently repeated.

---

## Page 11702

There is also disagreement about performing the prostrations of forgetfulness and resuming the prayer after a long interval.

One view is that, if the interval is long, one neither performs the prostrations nor resumes the prayer. Those who hold this view define a long interval on no basis beyond their own assertion. This is the position of many followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, including al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā and others. They say that the interval may be short even if one has left, and long even if one has remained seated.

Another view is that one may perform the prostrations as long as one remains in the mosque; once one leaves, one may no longer do so. This is the position mentioned by al-Khiraqī and others, and it is explicitly reported from Ahmad. It is also the position of al-Hakam and Ibn Shubruma. This sets a limit based on place rather than time, specifically the place of worship.

A further view is that either factor precludes performing the prostrations: a long interval or leaving the mosque. Another position transmitted from Ahmad, however, is that one performs the prostrations even after leaving the mosque and going far away. This is also one position held by al-Shāfiʿī, and it is the stronger view. Setting a limit based on place or time has no foundation in the revealed law, especially when the time has not been precisely defined. People’s customary practice provides no known boundary between a long and a short interval to which one might refer, nor is there any scriptural evidence establishing such a boundary. With regard to performing the prostrations and resuming the prayer, the scriptural evidence makes no distinction between a long and a short interval, or between leaving the mosque and remaining in it. Indeed, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had entered his home

---

## Page 11703

and those in a hurry had left, as noted earlier. Even if no scriptural ruling had been transmitted concerning this, it is known that this salām did not prevent completing the rest of the prayer by building upon what had already been performed. Likewise, the two prostrations of forgetfulness may be performed whenever one remembers them. If one omits them deliberately, there are two possible views:

Either one must still perform them, while bearing the sin of delaying them, just as one performs the compensatory acts required for deficiencies in Hajj. They remain an outstanding obligation until performed. Uninterrupted continuity is therefore not a condition for them, unlike the units of prayer, for which it is required when one is able. Thus, if someone deliberately ends the prayer with the salām before completing it, his prayer is invalid by universal agreement. This is because the prayer itself is a single act of worship with its own entry into and release from ritual restrictions. By contrast, the two prostrations after the salām are performed after release from the prayer’s restrictions, just as tawāf al-ifādah (the principal circumambulation of Hajj) is performed after the initial release from the pilgrim’s ritual restrictions.

Or uninterrupted continuity is a condition for these prostrations when one is able, and this requirement is waived only for an excuse, such as forgetfulness or inability, just as with continuity between the units of prayer. According to this view, whenever one delays them without an excuse, one’s prayer becomes invalid. For it has only been prescribed that they be separated from the prayer by the salām itself, and the command is to perform them immediately after the salām. Thus, whenever one knowingly and deliberately speaks, stands up, or does anything else that interrupts continuity, without an excuse, one’s prayer becomes invalid, just as it does if one omits the two prostrations before the salām.

---

## Page 11704

Section

As for the takbīr (saying “Allah is the Greatest”) during the prostrations of forgetfulness, the two Sahīh collections record in the hadith of Ibn Buhaynah:

“When he had completed his prayer, he performed two prostrations from his seated position before saying the salām, pronouncing the takbīr at each prostration. The people performed them with him to make up for the sitting he had forgotten.”

This concerns prostration before the salām. As for prostration after it, the hadith concerning Dhū al-Yadayn, narrated by Abū Hurayrah in the two Sahīh collections, states:

“He then prayed two units and said the salām. Then he pronounced the takbīr and prostrated, pronounced the takbīr and rose, pronounced the takbīr and prostrated, then pronounced the takbīr and rose.”

Pronouncing the takbīr is the position of the general body of scholars. However, they differed over the tashahhud (the seated testimony of faith) and the salām, holding three views:

It is reported from Anas, al-Hasan and ʿAtāʾ that these two prostrations involve neither a tashahhud nor a salām. Those who held this view likened them to the prostration of recitation: since they constitute a separate act of prostration, they involve neither a tashahhud nor a salām, just like the prostration of recitation. No one has transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said the salām in the prostration of recitation. Ahmad and others likewise stated this.

Ahmad said: “As for the salām, I do not know what its basis is.” The majority of the early generations held that there is no salām in it. Those who affirmed a salām in it did so by analogy, but this is a weak analogy, because it

---

## Page 11705

treats it as a prayer. Weaker still is the position of those who affirm a tashahhud in it by analogy.

The second view is that these two prostrations involve a tashahhud: one recites the tashahhud and says the salām when performing them after the salām. This is reported from Ibn Abbās, al-Nakha'ī, al-Hakam, Hammād, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā'ī, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad and the advocates of juristic opinion.
