The third view is that they involve a salām without a tashahhud. This is the view of Ibn Sīrīn. Ibn al-Mundhir said: “The salām following them is established through several chains of transmission, whereas the evidence establishing the tashahhud is open to question.” It is reported from Atā' that one may, if one wishes, recite the tashahhud and say the salām, or refrain from doing so.

Abū Muhammad said:

“It is possible that the tashahhud is not obligatory, because the apparent meaning of the first two hadiths is that he said the salām without a tashahhud, and they are more authentic than this report. Furthermore, these are separate prostrations, so no tashahhud is required for them, just as with the prostration of recitation.”

I say:

As for the salām following these two prostrations, it is established in the authentic hadiths of Ibn Mas'ūd and Imrān. As mentioned earlier, the two Sahīh collections record that Ibn Mas'ūd said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed—Ibrāhīm said, ‘He added to or omitted from the prayer.’ When he had said the salām, he was asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, has something changed concerning the prayer?’ He asked, ‘What is that?’ They replied, ‘You prayed such and such.’ He then folded his legs, faced the qiblah, performed two prostrations and said the salām. Then he turned to face us.”

The hadith continues.

---

## Page 11706

The two Sahīh collections also record that Imrān ibn Husayn said:

“He prayed one rak‘ah, then said the salām, then performed two prostrations, then said the salām.”

Muhammad ibn Sīrīn likewise mentioned this when narrating the hadith of Abū Hurayrah. He said: “I was informed that Imrān ibn Husayn said: ‘Then he said the salām.’” Ibn Sīrīn would narrate only from trustworthy narrators.

The difference between these two prostrations and the prostration of recitation is that these two constitute a prayer. They are two prostrations that have been made to stand in place of one rak‘ah and to compensate for a deficiency in the prayer. They therefore have a prescribed means of leaving the state of prayer, just as they have a prescribed means of entering it. This is what constitutes prayer, as he said:

“The key to prayer is purification; one enters its sacred state through the takbīr and leaves it through the salām.”

As for the prostration of recitation, it is an act of humble submission to Allah. Ibn Umar and others would perform it without ablution. It is reported that Uthmān ibn Affān said concerning a menstruating woman who hears a verse of prostration: “She should bow her head.” Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib held the same view. He added: “One should say: ‘O Allah, to You I have prostrated.’”

Al-Sha‘bī said: “Whoever hears a verse of prostration while without ablution should prostrate in whatever direction he is facing.” The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated, and the Muslims and polytheists, jinn and humans, prostrated with him. Thus, both disbelievers and Muslims performed it. Pharaoh’s sorcerers also prostrated. On this basis, it does not fall within the definition of prayer.

The two prostrations, however, resemble the funeral prayer: although it consists solely of standing, it is nevertheless a prayer with a prescribed entry into and exit from its sacred state. For this reason, the Companions would purify themselves for it.

---

## Page 11707

and Ibn Abbās permitted dry ablution for it if one feared missing it. This is also the view of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. Like the two prostrations for forgetfulness, it requires facing the Kaaba and forming rows, as in prayer. The follower follows the imam, neither saying the takbīr nor giving the salām before him, just as in prayer.

The prostration of recitation is different: according to many scholars, the listener may prostrate even if the reciter does not. The reported hadith,

“You are our imam; had you prostrated, we would have prostrated,”

is one of the reports Atā attributes directly to the Prophet without a connected chain, and is among the weakest such reports, as Ahmad and others stated. Those who hold that the listener should prostrate only when the reciter does so do not consider him to be following the reciter in every respect. The listeners are not required to prostrate together in a row, as they do behind the imam for forgetfulness. Nor must the leader be his imam as in prayer, and the follower may rise before his imam. It is therefore clear that he is not following him in a prayer, even if he may be said to follow him outside prayer, just as someone saying “Āmīn” follows the person making supplication, or a listener follows a reciter.

Section

As for reciting the tashahhud in the two prostrations for forgetfulness, those who affirm it rely on the report narrated from Imrān ibn Husayn:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led them in prayer

---

## Page 11708

and made an error through forgetfulness, so he prostrated twice, then recited the tashahhud, then gave the salām.” Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī narrated it, and al-Tirmidhī said: “A good, singular hadith.”

I say:

Its being singular implies that its narrator has no corroborating transmitter; rather, he alone narrated it. This weakens the hadith in a matter such as this. It is established that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated after the salām on more than one occasion: in the hadith of Ibn Masʿūd, when he prayed five units, and in the reports of Abū Hurayrah—the account of Dhū al-Yadayn—and Imrān ibn Husayn concerning his giving the salām, whether these concerned two incidents or one. It is also established that he said:

“When one of you is in doubt about his prayer, let him seek what is correct and complete his prayer on that basis, then give the salām, then prostrate twice.”

In the authentic hadith of Abū Hurayrah, he said:

“When one of you experiences that, let him prostrate twice.”

None of his statements contains a command to recite the tashahhud after the prostrations, nor do the authentic hadiths accepted by the scholars state that he recited it after the prostrations. Moreover, this tashahhud after the two prostrations is a lengthy act, taking as long as the two prostrations or longer. Something like this would be remembered and accurately recorded, and there would be ample interest and reason to transmit it. Had he recited the tashahhud, those who mentioned his prostrating would have mentioned it. Indeed, there would have been greater reason to mention it than to mention the salām and the takbīr when lowering and rising. These are brief utterances, whereas the tashahhud is a lengthy act. How, then, could they transmit the former but not the latter?

According to those who hold that it should be recited, this tashahhud is like the final tashahhud, since it is followed by the salām.

---

## Page 11709

so it is recommended to accompany it with blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and supplication, just as when one prays the two units of Fajr or a single unit of Witr and recites the tashahhud. Moreover, the authentic version of Imrān’s hadith contains no mention of the tashahhud. That a single narrator alone transmits such an addition, despite the ample interest and reasons for transmitting it, weakens its standing. Furthermore, this sole transmitter must be assessed: if he alone transmitted a hadith, would that establish it as something prescribed for Muslims?

Furthermore, the tashahhud was prescribed only in a complete prayer comprising bowing and prostration. It was not prescribed in the funeral prayer, even though al-Fātihah is recited in it. The two prostrations for forgetfulness involve no recitation. Thus, if the tashahhud is not prescribed in a prayer that includes recitation but no bowing or prostration, the same applies to a prayer containing neither standing, recitation, nor bowing.

It may also be said that this is more appropriate and beneficial. The tashahhud is not prescribed after the two prostrations of the prayer itself; rather, it is recited after two units, not after every pair of prostrations. Thus, if no tashahhud is recited after the two prostrations of the prayer itself, even though they complete a full unit, there is all the more reason not to recite it after the two prostrations for forgetfulness. This is because the two prostrations for forgetfulness generally stand in place of one unit. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If he has prayed five units, the two prostrations make his prayer even; but if he has prayed the complete number, they will be a humiliation for Satan.”

He thus treated them as one unit, not two. Moreover, this is a unit connected to the rest of the prayer, unlike the single unit of Witr that stands on its own. For this reason, continuity with the prayer is obligatory: the two prostrations must be performed immediately after the salām

---

## Page 11710

without deliberately delaying them. It is therefore as though he had performed them before the salām. Since he does not repeat the tashahhud after performing them before the salām, neither should he repeat it after performing them after the salām.

Moreover, the purpose is to conclude his prayer with prostration, not with the tashahhud. This is demonstrated by the fact that prostration before the salām was prescribed after the tashahhud, not before it. Thus, it is clear that it was made the conclusion of the prayer, with nothing remaining afterward except to leave it. Furthermore, repeating the tashahhud and supplication would mean repeating them after only a short interval. This is therefore not prescribed, just as repeating them is not prescribed when one prostrates before the salām.

Moreover, if a tashahhud followed these two prostrations, what was prescribed would not be merely two prostrations. Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded only two prostrations, with nothing added, and called them the two that humiliate Satan. Adding a tashahhud after the prostrations is therefore like adding recitation before them or adding an opening takbīr. It is well known that they have no opening takbīr of their own; rather, one says the takbīr as one lowers oneself, not while remaining seated. This shows that the prayer’s opening takbīr also covers them, making them part of the prayer, just as when they are performed before the salām. They therefore have no tashahhud of their own.

One does, however, conclude them with the salām, because the first salām no longer counts and did not serve to conclude them: the salām is pronounced only upon leaving the prayer. Some of the Companions and Successors maintained that there is no salām after these two prostrations, just as they have no opening takbīr of their own. But the correct view is to distinguish between the two, as established in the authentic Sunnah. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11711

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man led a congregation in a four-rak‘ah prayer, forgot the tashahhud, and stood up. Some of them alerted him by saying “Glory be to Allah,” but he did not sit back down. He completed his prayer, prostrated, and pronounced the salām. Some said, “He should have been made to sit back down,” while others said, “Had he sat back down, his prayer would have been invalid.” Which group is correct?

He replied:

The imam who missed the first tashahhud and stood up, then did not return to sitting when alerted, but prostrated for forgetfulness before the salām, acted correctly. This is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whoever says that he should have sat back down is mistaken; rather, what he did was better.

As for the claim that returning to sitting would have invalidated his prayer, the scholars hold two views:

The first is that returning would have invalidated his prayer. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad according to one reported opinion.

The second is that if he returned before beginning the recitation, his prayer would not have been invalidated. This is the well-known opinion reported from Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11712

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

An imam stood up for a fifth rak‘ah. The congregation alerted him by saying “Glory be to Allah,” but he paid no heed to what they said, thinking he had made no mistake. Should they stand up with him or not?

He replied:

If they stand up with him out of ignorance, their prayer is not invalidated. But if they do so knowingly, they should not follow him. Rather, they should wait until he pronounces the salām with them, or pronounce the salām before him. Waiting is better. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11713

Chapter on Voluntary Prayer

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

Which is better: learning the Quran or seeking knowledge?

He replied:

Knowledge that is individually obligatory—such as knowing what Allah has commanded and what He has forbidden—takes precedence over memorizing portions of the Quran that one is not obliged to memorize. Acquiring the former is obligatory, whereas pursuing the latter is recommended, and what is obligatory takes precedence over what is recommended.

As for memorizing the Quran, it takes precedence over much of what people call knowledge, which is either false or of little benefit. It should also come first in the education of someone wishing to study religious knowledge, both its fundamentals and its branches. The prescribed course for such a person in these times is to begin by memorizing the Quran, for it is the foundation of the religious sciences. This is contrary to the practice of many adherents of religious innovation, non-Arabs and others, who occupy themselves with superfluous branches of learning, such as speculative theology or disputation

---

## Page 11714

and scholarly disagreements, or uncommon legal questions, or following others’ opinions where there is no need to do so, or rare hadith reports that are neither reliably established nor beneficial, and many mathematical studies unsupported by proof, while neglecting to memorize the Quran, which is more important than all of these. A question of this kind therefore requires a detailed distinction. The purpose of the Quran is to understand its meanings and act upon it. If this is not the aim of the person who memorizes it, he is not among the people of knowledge and religious devotion. Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

He was asked:

Which is better and brings greater reward: repeatedly reciting the Quran or reviewing jurisprudence?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The speech of created beings cannot compare with Allah’s speech, for the superiority of the Quran over all other speech is like Allah’s superiority over His creation.

As for what is better for a particular person, this depends on his needs and what benefits him. If he knows the Quran by heart but needs to learn something else, learning what he needs is better than repeating a recitation that he has no need to repeat. The same applies if he has memorized enough of the Quran and needs knowledge of another kind.

---

## Page 11715

Likewise, if he has memorized the Quran or part of it but does not understand its meanings, learning to understand those meanings is better than reciting what he does not understand. As for someone who reads jurisprudence as an act of worship, reciting the Quran as an act of worship is better for him. Reflecting on the meanings of the Quran is also better than reflecting on words he has no need to ponder. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

For someone who has memorized the Quran and is in no danger of forgetting it, which is better: reciting the Quran, or glorifying Allah and engaging in other acts of remembrance and seeking forgiveness throughout his time? This is asked in light of his knowledge of the reports concerning “enduring righteous deeds,” declaring that “none has the right to be worshipped but Allah,” saying “There is no power nor strength except through Allah,” “the master supplication for forgiveness,” and “Glory and praise be to Allah; glory be to Allah, the Magnificent.”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. The answer to this question and others like it rests on two principles.

The first principle is that reciting the Quran is, as a category, better than other forms of remembrance, just as remembrance is, as a category, better than supplication. Thus, in a hadith in Sahīh Muslim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best speech after the Quran consists of four phrases, which are themselves from the Quran: ‘Glory be to Allah,’ ‘Praise be to Allah,’ ‘None has the right to be worshipped but Allah,’ and ‘Allah is Most Great.’”

---

## Page 11716

Al-Tirmidhī also relates from Abū Sa‘īd that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever is kept by reciting the Quran from remembering Me and asking Me, I shall grant him the best of what I give those who ask.”

Likewise, a hadith in the Sunan collections relates that a man asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I am unable to learn any of the Quran, so teach me something that will suffice for my prayer.” He replied: “Say: ‘Glory be to Allah; none has the right to be worshipped but Allah; Allah is Most Great.’”

For this reason, recitation in prayer is obligatory: according to the leading scholars, it is replaced with remembrance only when one is unable to recite. A substitute is inferior to what it replaces.

Furthermore, recitation requires freedom from major ritual impurity, whereas remembrance and supplication do not. An act prescribed only in a more complete state is superior. Thus, because prayer requires freedom from both major and minor ritual impurity, it is superior to recitation alone. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Remain steadfast, though you will never do so perfectly, and know that the best of your deeds is prayer.”

For this reason, scholars have explicitly stated that prayer is the best voluntary act of bodily worship. Moreover, anything on which the Quran is written may be touched only by someone in a state of ritual purity.

Scholarly consensus has been reported that recitation is superior. However, a group of spiritual teachers have given preference to remembrance. Some have claimed that it is preferable for the accomplished practitioner who strives diligently, as Abū Hāmid mentions in his books. Others have said that it is preferable for the beginner on the spiritual path, and this is closer to what is correct.

---

## Page 11717

This is clarified under the second principle, namely:

An otherwise less excellent deed may be accompanied by circumstances that make it superior to the more excellent one. This is of two kinds: the first concerns what is prescribed for everyone; the second varies according to people’s circumstances.

The first kind includes association with a particular time, place, or act that makes the deed preferable. Examples include the periods after the dawn and afternoon prayers, and other times when prayer is prohibited. Recitation, remembrance, and supplication are superior at such times. The same applies to places where prayer is prohibited, such as bathhouses, camel resting places, and graveyards: remembrance and supplication are superior there. Likewise, for someone in a state of major ritual impurity, remembrance is superior; for someone in a state of minor ritual impurity, recitation and remembrance are superior. Thus, when the more excellent deed is disliked because it entails harm, the less excellent deed becomes superior in those circumstances; indeed, it is what is prescribed.

The same applies while bowing and prostrating. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating. When bowing, magnify the Lord; when prostrating, strive earnestly in supplication, for then you are likely to be answered.”

The scholars agree that recitation while bowing or prostrating is disliked. They differ, however, over whether this invalidates the prayer, holding two opinions, both represented within the school of Imām Ahmad. This is out of honour and reverence for the Quran, so that it is not recited

---

## Page 11718

in a state of submission and lowliness, just as its recitation during a funeral procession is disliked, and most scholars dislike reciting it in a bathhouse. The period after the tashahhud (testimony of faith in prayer) is a time for supplication, prescribed through the Prophet’s practice and command, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Supplication at that point is superior; indeed, it is what is prescribed, rather than recitation or remembrance. Likewise, during circumambulation, at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, and when stoning the pillars, what is prescribed is remembrance and supplication. Scholars have differed over whether recitation during circumambulation is disliked, holding two well-known opinions.

(The second kind is when a servant is unable to perform the more excellent deed: either he cannot perform it at all, like someone who has not memorized the Quran and cannot memorize it—as in the case of the Bedouin who questioned the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or he cannot perform it fully, although he can perform the less excellent deed fully.

This explains why some have said that remembrance is superior to reciting the Quran: a person making such a statement may be describing his own condition. Most spiritual wayfarers—indeed, even those among them who possess spiritual insight—speak only of what they have tasted and experienced, rather than stating something applicable to everyone. For experiential knowledge concerns specific particulars, whereas knowledge encompasses what is general and universal.

Through remembrance, such a person may experience a collected heart, strengthened faith, the dispelling of intrusive whisperings, and increased tranquillity, light, and guidance that he does not experience when reciting the Quran. Indeed, when he recites the Quran, he may not understand it, or may fail to engage his heart and understanding, and he is beset by intrusive whisperings

---

## Page 11719

and distracting thoughts. Similarly, some people find their hearts more focused when reciting, understanding, and reflecting upon the Quran than when praying; indeed, in prayer they experience the opposite. Not everything that is superior is prescribed for everyone; rather, each person is prescribed to do what is best for him. For some, charity is better than fasting, while for others the reverse is true, even though charity is superior as a category of deeds. For some, Hajj is better than jihad—as with women and those unable to undertake jihad—even though jihad is superior as a category of deeds. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Hajj is the jihad of every weak person.”

There are many similar examples. Once these two principles are understood, the answers to these questions become clear.

With this understood, we say: the prescribed remembrances for particular times, such as responding to the caller to prayer, are better than reciting the Quran at those times. Likewise, the words that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed for the morning, the evening, and going to bed take precedence over other practices.

As for someone who rises during the night, recitation is better for him if he is able to do it; otherwise, he should do what he can. Prayer is superior to both recitation and remembrance. Hence, when Allah abrogated the obligation of standing in prayer at night, He directed them instead to recitation, saying:

“Your Lord knows that you stand in prayer for a little less than two-thirds of the night, or half of it, or a third of it, as do a group of those with you. Allah determines the night and the day. He knows that you will not be able to keep account of it, so He has turned to you in mercy. Therefore, recite as much of the Quran as is easy for you”—and the rest of the verse.

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11720

He was asked:

“Who is better: a reciter of the Quran who does not act upon it, or a devout worshipper?”

He replied:

If the worshipper worships without knowledge, he may be worse than a sinful scholar, or the sinful scholar may be worse than him. If, however, he worships Allah with knowledge, fulfilling his obligations and avoiding what is forbidden, then he is better than the sinful scholar—unless that scholar’s good deeds outweigh his evil deeds to such an extent that the good remaining to his credit exceeds the good deeds of that worshipper. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Which is better: listening to the Quran or offering voluntary prayers? Is recitation disliked when prayers other than the obligatory prayers are being performed, or not?”

He replied:

Someone reciting the Quran while others are offering voluntary prayers must not recite so loudly that he distracts them. “For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came out to his Companions

---

## Page 11721

while they were praying before dawn, and said: ‘O people, each of you is conversing privately with his Lord, so do not raise your voices over one another in recitation.’” Recitation within voluntary prayer is generally better; however, reciting the Quran and listening to it may be better for some people. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Which is better when one rises at night: prayer or recitation?”

He replied:

Prayer is better than recitation outside prayer. The leading scholars have expressly stated this. The Prophet also said:

“Remain steadfast, though you will never achieve perfect steadfastness. Know that the best of your deeds is prayer, and none but a believer consistently maintains his ablution.”

However, if someone finds greater enthusiasm, reflection, and understanding when reciting outside prayer than within it, then whatever benefits him more is better for him.

He was asked about a man who wished to earn reward:

“Is it better for him to recite the Quran, or to remember Allah and glorify Him?”

---

## Page 11722

He replied:

Reciting the Quran is generally better than remembering Allah, and remembering Allah is generally better than supplication. Yet an otherwise less excellent deed may be better in certain circumstances. Prayer, likewise, is better than all of these. Nevertheless, recitation, remembrance, and supplication are better than prayer at times when prayer is prohibited, such as the five prohibited times and during the sermon. Glorifying Allah while bowing and prostrating is better than recitation, and the final tashahhud (testimony of faith) is better than remembrance.

Some people may benefit more from a less excellent deed, depending on their circumstances: their hearts may be more focused on it, they may feel more at ease with it, and they may have greater strength for it. This is the case, for example, with someone who sometimes experiences these things when remembering Allah but not when reciting the Quran. Thus, a deed that he performs fully is better for him than one he performs deficiently, even though the latter kind of deed is [better] (1).

A person may also be unable to perform the better deed, in which case what he is able to do is better for him. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Misreadings).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the al-Shāmila Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 11723

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“What does our master say about someone who recites aloud while people are offering Sunnah prayers or the prayer of greeting the mosque, disturbing them with his loud recitation? Is his reciting aloud disliked or not?”

He replied:

No one may recite aloud, whether in prayer or outside it, if others are praying in the mosque and his loud recitation disturbs them. Indeed:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came out to the people while they were praying in Ramadan and reciting aloud. He said, ‘O people, each of you is conversing privately with his Lord, so do not raise your voices over one another in recitation.’”

He also replied—may Allah Most High, Most High, have mercy on him:

No one may recite aloud in a manner that disturbs others, such as those who are praying.

---

## Page 11724

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“What about standing in honor of a copy of the Quran and kissing it? Is opening it to seek an omen also disliked?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. We know of nothing transmitted from the early generations concerning standing in honor of a copy of the Quran and kissing it. Imam Ahmad was asked about kissing a copy of the Quran and said:

“I have heard nothing concerning this.”

However, it is reported that Ikrimah ibn Abī Jahl would open a copy of the Quran, place his face upon it, and say:

“The words of my Lord! The words of my Lord!”

Yet although the early generations were not accustomed to standing in its honor, neither were they accustomed to standing for one another, except in such cases as someone returning after an absence. Thus Anas said:

“No one was dearer to them than the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet when they saw him, they would not stand, knowing that he disliked it.”

It is best for people to follow the way of the early generations in everything, so they should not

---

## Page 11725

stand except on occasions when they used to stand. However, if people are accustomed to standing for one another, it may be argued that, were they to refrain from standing in honor of a copy of the Quran while maintaining this custom, their conduct would be neither good nor praiseworthy. Rather, they would be more deserving of censure, since they stand for one another but not for a copy of the Quran, which is more deserving of this honor. The respect and reverence due to it are not due to anything else. Indeed, none but someone in a state of ritual purity may touch the Quran, whereas people touch one another while in a state of ritual impurity. This is especially so because standing for it honors Allah’s sacred things and symbols in a way that standing for anything else does not. Those leading jurists who have mentioned people standing in honor of a copy of the Quran have done so approvingly, not disapprovingly.

As for opening a copy of the Quran to seek an omen, nothing concerning this has been transmitted from the early generations, and later scholars differed over it. Al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā mentioned this disagreement: he reported that Ibn Battah practiced it, while others disliked it. This is not the kind of good omen that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, loved, for he loved good omens and disliked superstitious omens.

The good omen he loved is when someone undertakes something, or resolves to undertake it, placing his trust in Allah, and then hears a pleasing word that cheers him—for example, hearing someone call out, “O Najīh (successful one)! O Muflih (prosperous one)! O Sa‘īd (fortunate one)! O Mansūr (victorious one)!” or something similar, as when “on his journey of migration he met

---

## Page 11726

a man and asked him, ‘What is your name?’ He replied, ‘Buraydah.’ He said, ‘O Abū Bakr, our situation has become easier.’”

As for superstitious omens, this is when someone has undertaken something, trusting in Allah, or intends to undertake it, then hears something displeasing, such as “It will not be completed” or “It will not succeed,” and takes it as an evil omen and abandons his undertaking. This is forbidden. As reported in the Sahīh, Mu‘āwiyah ibn al-Hakam al-Sulamī said:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, some among us take things as evil omens.’ He replied, ‘That is something one of you feels within himself, but it must not deter you.’”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus forbade a person from allowing superstitious omens to deter him from what he intended. Both in his liking for good omens and in his dislike of superstitious omens, he followed only the path of seeking Allah’s guidance, trusting in Him, and taking the means He had prescribed. He did not make a good omen a command or an incentive to act, nor an evil omen a prohibition against acting. It is the people of pre-Islamic ignorance, who sought decisions through divining arrows, who took such things as commands and prohibitions. Allah prohibited seeking decisions through divining arrows in two verses of His Book.

Whenever they wished to undertake something, they would cast lots for it using objects such as arrow shafts, pebbles, or the like. They marked one with a sign for good and another with a sign for evil, leaving a third unmarked. If one came out, they proceeded; if the other came out, they refrained; and if the unmarked one came out, they cast the lots again.

Practices falling under this heading include divination using pebbles, barley, boards, pieces of wood, or paper inscribed with letters of the abjad alphabet or lines of

---

## Page 11727

poetry, or similar means of seeking guidance about what a person should do or refrain from doing. Such practices are forbidden because they fall under seeking decisions through divining arrows. Rather, one is encouraged to seek the Creator’s guidance, consult His creatures, and rely on scriptural evidence that clarifies what Allah loves and is pleased with, and what He dislikes and forbids.

These practices are sometimes intended to determine whether an action a person undertakes is good or evil, and sometimes to ascertain what is beneficial in matters past and future. Neither use is sanctioned by Islamic law. Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best.

---

## Page 11728

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said (1):

Section

People have disagreed over which is better: more frequent bowing and prostration, or prolonged standing in prayer.

Three views have been reported from Ahmad on this matter. The first is that more frequent bowing and prostration is better; this is the view adopted by a group of his followers. The second is that the two are equally meritorious. The third is that prolonged standing is better; this is also reported from al-Shāfi‘ī.

We say: This question has two forms. The first is to prolong the standing while keeping the bowing and prostration brief. The question then is:

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The original reads: “This is among the things he wrote in the citadel.”

---

## Page 11729

Which is better: this, or more frequent bowing and prostration with briefer standing? In the latter case, the standing, bowing, and prostration are kept proportionate, all being brief.

The second case is to prolong the standing and likewise prolong the bowing and prostration. The question then is: which is better—this, or more frequent standing, bowing, and prostration? In both alternatives, the standing, bowing, and prostration are kept proportionate. But which is better: prolonging the prayer’s standing, bowing, and prostration, or performing them more frequently while keeping them brief? Regarding this case, Abū Muhammad and others mentioned three reported views. The wording of others indicates that the disagreement also concerns the first case.

The correct view regarding the first case is that keeping the prayer brief, with more frequent bowing and prostration and briefer standing, is better than prolonging the standing alone while keeping the bowing and prostration brief. Those who preferred prolonged standing cited the authentic hadith:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked, ‘Which prayer is best?’ He replied, ‘Prolonged devotion.’”

They assumed that “prolonged devotion” meant prolonged standing, even when accompanied by brief bowing and prostration. But that is not so.

For devotion means sustained worship and obedience. Someone who prolongs prostration may be described as devoutly obedient. Allah Most High says:

“What of one who is devoutly obedient during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, fearing the Hereafter and hoping for his Lord’s mercy?”

Thus, He describes him as devoutly obedient while prostrating, just as he is devoutly obedient while standing, and mentions prostration before standing.

---

## Page 11730

And in another verse, He says:

“And those who spend the night before their Lord, prostrating and standing.”

He did not say “in devotion.” Thus, He referred to standing using the word for standing, not the word for devotion.

Allah Most High also says:

“And stand before Allah in devout obedience.”

A person standing may or may not be devoutly obedient, and the same applies to someone prostrating. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore explained that the best prayer is that marked by prolonged devotion. This includes devotion both while prostrating and while standing. This hadith supports the second case: prolonging the standing, bowing, and prostration in prayer is preferable to increasing their number, because prolonged devotion is achieved by prolonging them, not by increasing their number. As for preferring prolonged standing with brief bowing and prostration over more frequent bowing and prostration, that is mistaken.

Prostration, as a form of worship, is superior to standing in several respects:

First: Prostration is itself an act of worship and is only proper when performed as worship of Allah alone. Standing, however, is only an act of worship when accompanied by that intention, for a person stands while attending to worldly affairs and is not forbidden to do so.

Second: Obligatory prayer must include prostration. Likewise, every prayer that includes bowing must also include prostration; prostration is never dispensed with in such a prayer, under any circumstances. It is therefore the mainstay of prayer. Standing, however, may always be omitted in voluntary prayer, and in prayer on a mount while travelling. Standing is also waived in obligatory prayer for someone who is ill, and likewise for a follower whose imam prays sitting, as

---

## Page 11731

reported in the authentic hadiths.

Whether this is held to apply to the entire Muslim community or exclusively to the Messenger, standing has been waived for the follower in certain circumstances. Prostration, however, is not waived for anyone praying either standing or sitting. If a sick person is unable to make its gesture, he performs as much of it as he can—namely, gesturing with his head—which constitutes prostration for someone in his condition. If he cannot gesture with his head, there are two views, both reported from Ahmad.

The first is that he gestures with his eyes. Those who hold this view regard his eye gestures as his bowing and prostration, so they do not waive them.

The second is that the obligation to pray is waived in this condition, and prayer performed in this manner is invalid. This is the view of Abū Hanīfa, and it is better supported by the evidence. Gesturing with the eyes is not one of the acts of prayer, nor does it distinguish bowing from prostration or standing from sitting. Rather, it is a kind of purposeless movement that Allah Most High has not prescribed.

Gesturing with the head, by contrast, involves lowering it, which fulfils part of what the person praying has been commanded to do. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith whose authenticity is agreed upon:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

Of prostration, he can perform nothing but

---

## Page 11732

this gesture. Moving the eyes, however, is not prostration in any sense. The proponents of both views thus agree that prayer must include prostration: one holds that gesturing with the eyes constitutes prostration, while the other holds that it does not, and therefore the person does not pray. If prayer were valid without prostration despite the ability to perform it, one could pronounce the opening takbīr, recite, say the tashahhud, and conclude with the salutation of peace, thereby performing the verbal components without the physical acts. Yet I know of no one who has held that prayer is valid through words alone; rather, prostration is indispensable. Standing and recitation, by contrast, are waived when one is unable to perform them, by agreement of the imams. This establishes that prostration is the greatest of prayer’s pillars, whether verbal or physical.

The third argument:

Standing becomes an act of worship only through recitation or the remembrance and supplication performed during it, as when standing in the funeral prayer. Merely standing has never been prescribed as an act of worship when remembrance is possible. Prostration, by contrast, is prescribed as an act of worship in its own right. Even outside prayer, prostration has been prescribed for Quran recitation, gratitude, and other purposes.

As for someone praying behind an imam who does not recite, he listens to his imam’s recitation, and his listening is an act of worship. If he does not hear it, there is disagreement over whether recitation is obligatory for him, though it is better for him to recite. Those who say that recitation is not required of him, or that it is not recommended for him, hold that the imam’s recitation counts as his own, since he follows the imam.

---

## Page 11733

If it is asked, “What if someone who cannot recite the Quran is unable to recite words of remembrance either?” the answer is: This situation is rare or impossible, for no one is unable to remember Allah. He must proclaim Allah’s greatness and offer whatever praise and declarations of His oneness he can. According to the view that these words should be repeated, there are two views as to whether they should equal al-Fātihah in length, in light of the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“When you stand to pray, if you know any Quran, recite it. Otherwise, praise Allah, proclaim His greatness and declare His oneness, then bow.”

Narrated by Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī.

Ahmad said that if someone stands for the second unit of prayer after forgetting some of the pillars of the first, then remembers before beginning the recitation, he should continue, and this unit takes the place of the former one. For the purpose of standing is recitation.

Hence they said: An act that constitutes worship in its own right, such as bowing or prostration, does not require a verbal pillar; whereas an act that does not constitute worship in its own right, such as standing or sitting, requires a verbal pillar. Since prostration constitutes worship in its own right, it is thus known to be superior to standing.

The fourth argument:

It may be said that standing is distinguished by Quran recitation, since recitation during bowing and prostration has been prohibited, and reciting the Quran is superior to glorifying Allah. In this respect, standing has an advantage. This is the argument of those who regard the two as equal. They say that prostration is superior in itself, while the remembrance recited during standing is superior; thus each is superior in one respect, or the two are equal.

However, it may be answered that Quran recitation is waived in certain circumstances, and both recitation and standing are waived for someone who joins the prayer late, as in the hadith of Abū Bakrah. And in the Sunan collections:

---

## Page 11734

“Whoever catches a unit of prayer has caught the prostration.”

This is the view of the overwhelming majority of scholars; dissent on this matter is exceptional. Moreover, the prayer of someone unable to recite the Quran is valid without recitation, by scholarly consensus. As related in the Sunan collections:

“A man said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I am unable to learn any of the Quran, so teach me something that will suffice in its place.’

“He replied, ‘Say: Glory be to Allah; all praise belongs to Allah; none has the right to be worshipped but Allah; Allah is the Greatest; and there is no might or power except through Allah.’

“The man said, ‘This is for Allah, but what is there for me?’

“He replied, ‘Say: O Allah, forgive me, have mercy on me, provide for me, and guide me.’”

Furthermore, if someone forgets to recite during prayer, one view holds that his prayer is valid; this has been reported from al-Shāfiʿī. Another view holds that if he forgets the recitation in the first unit, he should recite in the second enough for both units; this has been reported from Ahmad. Prostration, however, is never waived. Thus, prostration is known to be superior to recitation, just as it is superior to standing. A latecomer to the prayer builds upon the recitation of the imam who appointed him as his replacement, just as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) built upon Abū Bakr’s recitation.

The fifth argument:

It is established in an authentic report that:

“The Fire consumes every part of the son of Adam except the place of prostration.”

It therefore consumes the foot, even though it is the place of standing.

---

## Page 11735

The sixth argument:

Allah Most High says:

“On the Day when the Shin is uncovered and they are called to prostrate, but cannot do so.”

“Their eyes will be downcast, humiliation overwhelming them, although they used to be called to prostrate while they were sound.”

It is established in authentic hadiths: “When He manifests Himself to them on the Day of Resurrection, the believers will prostrate before Him, while anyone who used to prostrate in this world for show will have his back become like a solid slab.” Thus, they are commanded to prostrate on the plains of the Resurrection, rather than perform any other part of the prayer. This shows that prostration is superior to the other parts.

The seventh argument:

It is established in authentic hadiths that when people ask the Messenger to intercede on the Day of Resurrection, he says:

“I will go, and when I see my Lord, I will fall down in prostration before Him and praise my Lord with words of praise that He will inspire me with, which I cannot now express.”

Thus, when he sees Him, he prostrates and praises Him. Then he is told:

“O Muhammad, raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be granted what you ask; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.”

This shows that prostration is superior to the other parts.

The eighth argument:

Allah Most High says:

“No! Do not obey him, but prostrate and draw near.”

It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The nearest a servant comes to his Lord is when he is prostrating.”

This explicitly states that a person is nearer to Allah while prostrating than in any other posture, clearly demonstrating the superiority of prostration over the other postures.

Muslim narrated this hadith in his Sahīh on the authority of Abū Hurayrah:

---

## Page 11736

that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The nearest a servant comes to his Lord is when he is prostrating, so supplicate frequently.”

The ninth argument:

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh that Maʿdān ibn Abī Talhah said:

“I met Thawbān, the client of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), and said, ‘Tell me of a deed by which Allah will admit me to Paradise’—or he said, ‘of the deeds most beloved to Allah.’ He remained silent. Then I asked him a second time, and he said, ‘I asked the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) about that, and he said: “Prostrate frequently to Allah, for every prostration you make to Allah is a means by which Allah raises you one degree and removes one sin from you.”’”

Maʿdān said: “Then I met Abū al-Dardāʾ and asked him, and he told me the same as Thawbān had told me.”

If he asked him about the deeds most beloved to Allah, this explicitly establishes that prostration is more beloved to Allah than the other acts. If he asked him about what would cause Allah to admit him to Paradise, he directed him to prostration rather than standing, showing that it is more conducive to attaining the intended goal.

Those who consider frequent prostration superior to prolonged prostration cite this hadith as evidence, because he said:

“For every prostration you make to Allah is a means by which Allah raises you one degree and removes one sin from you.”

Yet it does not substantiate that view, for although every prostration earns that reward, prostrations are of different kinds. If one prostration is superior to another, the elevation in rank it brings is greater, as is the remission of sins it brings, just as a prostration in which one has greater humility and presence of heart is superior

---

## Page 11737

to others, so too a prolonged prostration in which one devotes oneself to one’s Lord is superior to a brief one.

The tenth argument:

Muslim also narrated that Rabīʿah ibn Kaʿb said:

“I used to spend the night with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), bringing him water for his ablution and whatever he needed. He said to me, ‘Ask.’ I said, ‘I ask to be your companion in Paradise.’ He said, ‘Would you ask for anything else?’ I said, ‘That is what I want.’ He said, ‘Then help me to achieve that for you by prostrating frequently.’”

He had asked for a lofty rank, yet all the Prophet requested of him was frequent prostration. This provides stronger evidence that frequent prostration is superior. However, one may reply that someone who prostrates frequently may perform many lengthy prostrations or many brief ones, and the former is superior.

Moreover, there will necessarily be frequent prostration in either case. If someone prays eleven lengthy rakʿahs, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) used to do, while another worshipper prays twenty rakʿahs in the same amount of time, the latter has performed more prostrations. Yet the former’s prostrations are superior and more complete, whereas the latter’s are more numerous. No one is entitled to say that prostrations which are more numerous but brief are superior to those which are also numerous, yet more complete and prolonged, as in the prayer of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).

---

## Page 11738

The eleventh argument:

The places where one prostrates are called masājid (places of prostration). As Allah Most High says:

“And the mosques are for Allah, so do not invoke anyone along with Allah.”

He also says:

“And who is more unjust than one who prevents Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques?”

And:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain the mosques of Allah.”

And:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of prayer.’”

They are not called maqāmāt (places of standing) unless prostration has been performed in them. This shows that the greatest act of prayer is prostration, for He designated the places of prostration by reference to the act performed there.

The twelfth argument:

Allah Most High says:

“Only those believe in Our verses who, when reminded of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise, and are not arrogant.”

Although this includes the prostration of recitation, it applies even more strongly to prostration in prayer, for a person’s need for this prostration is greater in every circumstance. He has thus made falling down in prostration something without which faith cannot be attained, and has singled it out for mention. This is one of its distinguishing merits. Likewise, He says of His prophets:

“When the verses of the Most Merciful are recited to them, they fall down in prostration and weeping.”

He also says in that verse:

“Their sides forsake their beds as they call upon their Lord in fear and hope.”

Supplication during prostration is better than supplication in other postures, as established in authentic hadiths, such as his saying in the hadith of Abū Hurayrah:

“The closest a servant comes to his Lord is while prostrating, so supplicate abundantly.”

And likewise what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from

---

## Page 11739

Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, drew back the curtain while the people stood in rows behind Abū Bakr. He said, ‘O people, nothing remains of the glad tidings of prophethood except a good dream that a Muslim has or that someone has about him. Indeed, I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating. When bowing, therefore, magnify the Lord; and when prostrating, strive earnestly in supplication, for then your supplications are likely to be answered.’”

Several hadiths establish that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, supplicated while prostrating. More than one hadith makes clear that this occurred during his night prayer. Thus, although His words,

“Their sides forsake their beds as they call upon their Lord in fear and hope,”

include supplication in every posture of prayer, prostration has a particular merit over the other postures, just as the end of the prayer has a particular merit over its other parts. Hence, it is reported in the Sunan collections:

“Supplication is best in the latter part of the night and at the end of the obligatory prayers.”

These arguments, along with others, show that prostration, as a category of worship, is superior to standing and recitation. If it were possible to make prostration longer than standing, that would be better. However, this would cause great hardship, so prostration has been made shorter than standing. Nevertheless, the Sunnah is to prolong it when one prolongs the standing, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do in his prayer. It is reported:

“He would keep the standing and sitting brief, and prolong the bowing and prostration.”

When he prolonged the standing in the eclipse prayer, he also prolonged the bowing and prostration. Likewise, the authentic hadith of Hudhayfah relates:

“When he recited Sūrat al-Baqarah and Sūrat al-Nisā’

---

## Page 11740

and Sūrat Āl ‘Imrān,” he said, “he bowed for about as long as he had stood, and prostrated for about as long as he had bowed.”

In the authentic hadith of al-Barā’, he said:

“His standing, bowing, standing upright again, prostration, sitting between the two prostrations, and sitting between the salutation and departure were all nearly equal in length.”

Another version states:

“Except for the standing and sitting.”

It is also authentically reported from ‘Ā’ishah:

“He would remain in a single prostration for as long as it takes a person to recite fifty verses.”

These hadiths show that prolonging the prayer—its standing, bowing, and prostration—is better than increasing the number of these acts while keeping them brief. This is the third view concerning the second case. Those who regard the two as equal say that the hadiths on this matter conflict, but that is not so. His statement,

“The best prayer is prolonged devotion,”

includes prolonging both standing and prostration. The same applies to what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from ‘Ammār, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A man’s lengthy prayer and brief sermon are a sign of his understanding of religion. So prolong the prayer and keep the sermon brief.”

He also said:

“Whoever leads the people in prayer should keep it brief; when he prays alone, he may prolong it as much as he wishes.”

We have already explained that the hadiths establishing the superiority of prostration do not contradict this.

It is well known that the best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Furthermore, when he performed the eclipse prayer, he could have prayed ten or twenty rak‘ahs, increasing the number of times he stood and prostrated. Yet he did not do so; rather, he prayed

---

## Page 11741

two rak‘ahs, prolonging the standing, bowing, and prostration in both, and including two standings and two bowings in each rak‘ah. Accordingly, more frequent bowing and prostration are better than prolonged standing without prolonged bowing and prostration. But prolonging the standing, bowing, and prostration is better than prolonging the standing alone, and better than performing more bowings, prostrations, and standings within the same amount of time.

The discussion concerns a fixed period of time—a third, half, or sixth of the night, or an hour—and which of these approaches is better. In the two Sahīhs, Umm Hāni’ said, concerning the eight rak‘ahs he prayed on the day of the conquest:

“I never saw him offer a briefer prayer than this, yet he performed the bowing and prostration fully.”

In a version narrated by Muslim:

“Then he stood and prayed eight rak‘ahs. I do not know whether his standing, bowing, or prostration was longest; they were all nearly equal.”

This shows that he prolonged the bowing and prostration almost as much as the standing, and that her statement, “I never saw him offer a briefer prayer than this,” was an account of what she herself had witnessed. Umm Hāni’ was not in his company in every circumstance. Perhaps she meant to rule out a greater number of rak‘ahs, for he never prayed eight rak‘ahs together more briefly than these. His night prayer was longer than that, and during the day he never prayed eight consecutive rak‘ahs. Rather, he would offer the obligatory prayer, praying two rak‘ahs after Zuhr and four or two before it. Or perhaps he shortened this prayer because time was limited, for he offered it during the day while occupied with the affairs of the conquest of Mecca

---

## Page 11742

just as he would shorten the obligatory prayer when travelling, even reciting the two surahs of refuge in Fajr. It is also reported that he recited al-Zalzalah in both rak‘ahs of Fajr. Such shortening was due to particular circumstances.

Those who favour more numerous rak‘ahs over longer ones have cited the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd, who said:

“I know the Mufassal surahs that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to recite, two surahs in each rak‘ah.”

They take this to indicate that he did not prolong the standing. Yet this provides no proof for their position. First, he combined two Mufassal surahs. Moreover, he would recite a surah slowly and distinctly, so that its recitation took longer than that of a longer surah.

Furthermore, Hudhayfah reported that:

“He recited al-Baqarah, al-Nisā’ and Āl ‘Imrān while standing in a single rak‘ah.”

Ibn Mas‘ūd also mentioned that he prolonged the prayer until, as he put it, “I contemplated doing something wrong: sitting down and leaving him.” Clearly, this would not have happened with just two surahs. Thus, it is clear that he recited two surahs on some occasions. There is no doubt that he made some rak‘ahs longer than others, as ‘Ā’ishah and others reported. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11743

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Allah has mentioned night prayer in several verses, sometimes praising it and sometimes commanding it—initially as an obligation, which He then replaced with a recommendation. This applies if night prayer does not include the ‘Ishā’ prayer, but rather means praying after sleep. For Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib and others said, “Whoever prays ‘Ishā’ in congregation has obtained his share of prayer on Laylat al-Qadr.” He thus counted this as night prayer.

It is reported from ‘Ubaydah al-Salmānī that night prayer remains obligatory and that its obligation has not been abrogated, even if one prays only for as long as it takes to milk a sheep. If this is intended to include the witr prayer, then it is the view of many scholars.

The evidence for this is the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd: when the Prophet said, “Pray witr, O people of the Quran,” a Bedouin asked, “What is the Messenger of Allah saying?” He replied, “This is not addressed to you or your companions.”

Thus, he addressed the people of the Quran with a command concerning night prayer that he did not address to others.

---

## Page 11744

In keeping with this, His words, “So recite whatever is easy of it,” have been interpreted as reciting it at night so as not to forget it. And the Prophet said:

“I examined the sins of my community and found among them that a man is granted a verse by Allah, but sleeps instead of reciting it until he forgets it.”

In the Sahīh, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever prays ‘Ishā’ in congregation, it is as though he has spent half the night in prayer; and whoever prays Fajr in congregation, it is as though he has spent the entire night in prayer.”

This means Fajr together with ‘Ishā’. This indicates that these two prayers are not themselves part of night prayer, but that whoever performs them is like someone who has spent the night in prayer.

Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, the righteous will be amid gardens and springs, receiving what their Lord has given them. Indeed, before that they were doers of good. They used to sleep but little of the night, and before dawn they would seek forgiveness.”

And He says:

“Those who are patient, truthful, devoutly obedient, charitable, and who seek forgiveness before dawn.”

According to the soundest interpretation, this means that they used to sleep little. Thus, qalīlan (“little”) is in the accusative, governed by yahja‘ūn (“they sleep”), while mā serves for emphasis. This is like His words:

“Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little do they believe.”

His words, “They used to sleep but little of the night,” are explained in Sūrat al-Muzzammil by His words:

“Stand in prayer at night, except for a little—half of it, or a little less, or a little more—and recite the Quran with measured recitation.”

The portion excepted from the command is the “little” mentioned in those surahs. It is little in relation to the combined span of night and day. For if they sleep for a third, a half, or two-thirds of the night, this is little compared with the portion of night and day during which they do not sleep, whether or not they sleep during the day.

---

## Page 11745

It has also been said that not a single night passed without their praying during it. On this interpretation, what is meant is sleeping through the entire night. This interpretation is weak, however, because sleeping through the night is forbidden, since the ‘Ishā’ prayer is obligatory. Allah Most High says:

“Only those believe in Our signs who, when reminded of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise, without arrogance. Their sides forsake their beds as they call upon their Lord in fear and hope, and they spend from what We have provided them. No soul knows what joy has been kept hidden for them as a reward for what they used to do.”

In the hadith of Mu‘ādh, he said, “O Messenger of Allah, tell me of a deed that will admit me to Paradise and keep me far from the Fire.”

He replied, “You have asked about a momentous matter, yet it is easy for whomever Allah makes it easy: worship Allah without associating anything with Him, establish prayer, give zakat, fast Ramadan, and perform pilgrimage to the House.”

Then he said, “Shall I direct you to the gates of goodness? Fasting is a shield, charity extinguishes sin as water extinguishes fire, and so does a man’s prayer in the depths of the night.” Then he recited, “Their sides forsake their beds as they call upon their Lord in fear and hope, and they spend from what We have provided them,” continuing until he reached “do.”

Then he said, “Shall I not tell you of the head of the matter, its pillar, and its pinnacle? The head of the matter is Islam, its pillar is prayer, and its pinnacle is jihad in the cause of Allah.”

Then he said, “Shall I tell you what holds all of that together?”

I said, “Yes.” He took hold of his tongue and said, “Restrain this.”

I said, “O Messenger of Allah, will we really be held accountable for what we say?”

He replied, “May your mother be bereaved of you, O Mu‘ādh! What casts people into the Fire on their faces—or he said, on their noses—except the harvests of their tongues?”

---

## Page 11746

And Allah Most High says:

“Is one who is devoutly obedient during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, fearing the Hereafter and hoping for his Lord’s mercy, [like one who is not]? Say, ‘Are those who know equal to those who do not know?’ Only people of understanding take heed.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Among the People of the Book is an upright community who recite Allah’s verses during the hours of the night, prostrating themselves.”

After saying:

“Establish prayer from the sun’s decline until the darkness of the night, and the recitation at dawn. Indeed, the recitation at dawn is witnessed,”

Allah Most High says:

“And during part of the night, rise to pray with it as an additional act of worship for you; perhaps your Lord will raise you to a station of praise.”

And He says in Sūrat al-Muzzammil:

“Stand in prayer at night, except for a little,”

through to His words:

“Indeed, rising at night makes a deeper impression and is more conducive to sound recitation.”

When the obligation was abrogated, the recommendation remained. Ahmad and others said: “Nāshi’ah (rising at night) occurs only after sleep; one says nasha’a when someone rises.”

Allah Most High says:

“The servants of the Most Merciful are those who walk humbly upon the earth, and when the ignorant address them, they respond with words of peace.”

“And those who spend the night before their Lord, prostrating and standing in prayer.”

And His words, Most High:

“Indeed, it is We who have sent down the Quran to you, progressively.”

“So be patient with your Lord’s decree, and do not obey any sinner or ungrateful disbeliever among them.”

“And remember the name of your Lord morning and evening.”

“And during part of the night, prostrate yourself before Him, and glorify Him through the long night.”

This includes the ‘Ishā’ prayer, witr, and night prayer, because He says:

“And glorify Him through the long night.”

And His words, Most High:

“We certainly know that your heart is distressed by what they say.”

“So glorify

---

## Page 11747

your Lord with praise, and be among those who prostrate.”

This is unrestricted; He did not confine it to any particular time. All praise belongs to Allah alone. May Allah send blessings and abundant peace upon Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

He was asked:

“Is it permissible for a man who has not performed witr after ‘Ishā’ to omit it?”

He replied:

All praise belongs to Allah. Witr is a strongly emphasized sunnah by the consensus of the Muslims. Anyone who persistently abandons it has his testimony rejected.

The scholars differed over whether it is obligatory. Abū Hanīfah and a group of Ahmad’s followers considered it obligatory, whereas the majority—including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad—did not, because:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to perform witr on his mount.”

An obligatory prayer is not performed on a mount. Nevertheless, by the consensus of the Muslims, witr is a strongly emphasized sunnah that no one should abandon. It is more emphatically prescribed than the sunnah prayers associated with Dhuhr, Maghrib, and ‘Ishā’, and it is superior to all voluntary daytime prayers, such as the Duhā prayer. Indeed, the best prayer after the obligatory prayers is night prayer. The most emphatically prescribed of these prayers are witr and the two rak‘ahs before Fajr. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11748

He was asked:

“If a man is travelling and shortening his prayers, must he pray witr or not? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.”

He replied:

Yes, he should perform witr while travelling, for:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to perform witr both while travelling and at home.”

He would pray on his mount, whichever direction it took him, and perform witr on it, but he would not perform the obligatory prayer on it.

He was asked:

“What should someone do if he sleeps through the witr prayer?”

He replied:

He should pray it between dawn and the Fajr prayer, as ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, ‘Ā’ishah, and others did.

Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan that Abū Sa‘īd said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sleeps through witr or forgets it, let him pray it when morning comes or when he remembers.”

---

## Page 11749

The reports from Ahmad differ as to whether one should also make up the even-numbered prayer accompanying witr. The correct view is that one should make it up as well.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sleeps through a prayer or forgets it should pray it when he remembers it, for that is its appointed time.”

This encompasses obligatory prayers, night prayers, witr, and the regular Sunnah prayers.

‘Ā’ishah said:

“When sleep or illness prevented the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from praying at night, he would pray twelve rak‘ahs during the day.”

Narrated by Muslim.

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sleeps through his regular portion of nighttime recitation, or any part of it, then recites it between the Fajr and Zuhr prayers, will have it recorded for him as though he had recited it at night.”

Narrated by Muslim. The same applies to the regular Sunnah prayers. It is authentically reported of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When he and his Companions slept through the Fajr prayer while travelling, he prayed the two-rak‘ah Sunnah prayer of Fajr, then prayed Fajr after sunrise.”

“When he missed the Sunnah prayer that follows Zuhr, he prayed it after ‘Asr.”

‘Ā’ishah said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not pray four rak‘ahs before Zuhr, he would pray them afterwards.”

Narrated by al-Tirmidhī.

Abū Hurayrah narrated that he said:

“Whoever has not prayed the two rak‘ahs of Fajr should pray them after sunrise.”

Narrated by al-Tirmidhī and graded authentic by Ibn Khuzaymah.

---

## Page 11750

There is another view on this matter: witr is not to be made up. This is one report from Ahmad, based on the report that the Prophet said:

“When dawn breaks, the time for night prayer and witr has passed.”

Those who hold this view say that witr is intended to be the final act of worship at night, just as Maghrib is the odd-numbered prayer concluding the day’s worship. This is why, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, missed his night prayers, he would pray twelve rak‘ahs during the day. Had witr been included among them, they would have been thirteen rak‘ahs.

The correct view is that witr is to be made up before the Fajr prayer. Once that prayer has been performed, making up witr no longer serves the purpose for which it was prescribed. Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām was asked about a Shāfi‘ī imam who leads a congregation of Hanafīs and Shāfi‘īs, with the Hanafīs praying separately when it comes to witr.

He replied:

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Night prayer is offered two rak‘ahs at a time. If you fear that dawn is approaching, pray one rak‘ah to make what you have prayed odd-numbered.”

It is also established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would pray witr as a single rak‘ah separate from those preceding it, and that he would pray witr as five or seven rak‘ahs, giving the salām only at the end.

---

## Page 11751

The vast majority of scholars hold that all these forms are permissible, including praying witr as three rak‘ahs with a single salām, as established in the Sunnah. However, these hadiths did not reach all the jurists. Some therefore disliked praying witr as three joined rak‘ahs resembling the Maghrib prayer, as reported from Mālik and some Shāfi‘ī and Hanbalī scholars. Others disliked praying witr in any other form, as reported from Abū Hanīfah. Still others disliked praying witr as five, seven, or nine joined rak‘ahs, as stated by some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, and by Mālik. The correct view is that when the imam follows any practice established in the Sunnah and offers witr in any of the forms mentioned, those praying behind him should follow him in this. Allah knows best.

He was also asked about praying two rak‘ahs after witr.

He replied:

As for praying two rak‘ahs after witr, Muslim narrated in his Sahīh concerning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He would pray two rak‘ahs after witr while seated.”

This was also narrated from Umm Salamah through some authentic chains of transmission:

“He would do this when he prayed witr as nine rak‘ahs.”

For he used to pray witr

---

## Page 11752

as eleven rak‘ahs, then he would pray witr as nine and pray two rak‘ahs after witr while seated. Most jurists had not heard this hadith, which is why they objected to this practice. Ahmad and others, however, had heard it and knew it to be authentic. Ahmad permitted offering these two rak‘ahs while seated, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had done. Whoever does so should not be censured. By consensus, however, this prayer is not obligatory, and whoever omits it is not to be blamed. Nor should it be called “zahhāfah.” No one may oblige people to perform it or object to those who do.

What should be condemned, however, is the practice of some people who perform two prostrations on their own after witr. This is done by some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad who are associated with religious learning and devotion. They base their practice on the report:

“He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would offer two prostrations after witr.”

This was narrated by Abū Mūsā al-Madīnī and others. They mistakenly thought that it meant two prostrations on their own. Rather, it means that he would pray two rak‘ahs, as is explicitly stated in the authentic hadiths. The word sajdah (prostration) can refer to a rak‘ah, as in Ibn ‘Umar’s statement:

“I learned from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the practice of offering two prostrations before Zuhr.”

And so the hadith continues. What is meant here is two rak‘ahs, as explicitly explained in the authentic versions of the report.

Likewise, his saying:

“Whoever catches one prostration of Fajr before the sun rises has caught Fajr.”

By this he meant one rak‘ah, as is explicitly explained in the well-known narration.

---

## Page 11753

Some have thought that what is meant is a prostration on its own, but this is mistaken. No scholar has held that catching the prayer depends on catching a single prostration on its own.

Rather, scholars hold three opinions concerning what is required to catch the Friday prayer and the congregational prayer. The soundest is that one has not caught either unless one catches a rak‘ah; merely joining with the opening takbīr does not count as catching the congregational prayer. It is widely reported from the Companions that whoever catches less than one rak‘ah of the Friday prayer must pray four rak‘ahs.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever catches one rak‘ah of the prayer has caught the prayer.”

Accordingly, if a traveller catches one rak‘ah behind a resident imam, should he pray the full prayer or shorten it? There are two opinions on this.

The point here is that the word sajdah (prostration) means a rak‘ah, for prayer may be referred to by its constituent parts: standing, sitting, bowing, prostration, glorification of Allah, and recitation of the Quran.

Even more objectionable is the practice of some people who perform a single prostration on its own after the salutation ending the prayer. This is an innovation, and no imam is reported to have recommended it. Acts of worship are founded on revealed law and adherence to it, not on whims and innovation. Islam rests on two foundations: that we worship Allah alone, and that we worship Him as He has prescribed through His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—not according to whims and innovations.

---

## Page 11754

Section

As for the prayer called “al-zahhāfah” and their claim, “Whoever does not observe it regularly is not among Ahl al-Sunnah”—by which they mean the two rak‘ahs prayed while seated after witr—the Muslims unanimously agree that this prayer is not obligatory. Even if someone omits it throughout his life and never performs it even once, he does not thereby become one of the people of innovation or deserve blame or punishment. Nor is he to be shunned or branded with any derogatory label whatsoever.

Indeed, even if a person omits practices more firmly established than this, such as prolonging the night prayer as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do, or praying eleven rak‘ahs as he used to do, and similar practices, he does not thereby depart from the Sunnah, become an innovator, or deserve blame. The Muslims nevertheless agree that praying eleven lengthy rak‘ahs at night, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do, is better than omitting that and praying two rak‘ahs while seated after witr.

For it is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim from ‘Ā’ishah that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray eleven rak‘ahs at night while seated. Later, he began praying nine, sitting after the eighth and ninth, and would not give the salutation until after

---

## Page 11755

the ninth. He would then pray two rak‘ahs while seated. Later, he began praying witr with seven or five rak‘ahs. When he prayed witr with five, he would sit only after the fifth, then pray two rak‘ahs while seated.”

When he prayed witr with seven rak‘ahs, one report states that he would sit only after the seventh; another states that he would sit after the sixth and seventh, then pray two rak‘ahs after witr while seated.

This authentic hadith is evidence that he did not observe these two rak‘ahs regularly. How, then, can it be said that whoever does not observe them regularly is not among Ahl al-Sunnah?

The scholars differ as to whether this prayer is prescribed at all. Many scholars have said that it is not prescribed under any circumstances, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Make witr your last prayer at night.”

Some of these scholars interpreted the two rak‘ahs that he reportedly prayed after witr as the two rak‘ahs before Fajr. However, the hadiths are authentic and explicit that he used to pray two rak‘ahs while seated after witr, apart from the two rak‘ahs before Fajr.
