One wording reports that he used to perform two prostrations after witr. Some shaykhs assumed that this meant two prostrations on their own, so they would perform two standalone prostrations after witr. This is an innovation that no Muslim scholar has considered recommended, nor did any of the early generations practise it. They were misled by the expression “two prostrations,” which here means two rak‘ahs, as in the statement of Ibn ‘Umar:

“I learned from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, two ‘prostrations’ before Zuhr and two after it, two after Maghrib, and two after ‘Ishā’

---

## Page 11756

and two before Fajr”—meaning two rak‘ahs.

Perhaps some might say: These two rak‘ahs that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray seated after witr stand in the same relation to the witr of the night as the two rak‘ahs after Maghrib stand to the witr of the day. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Maghrib is the witr of the day, so make your night prayer odd-numbered.”

Ahmad narrated this in al-Musnad.

Although Maghrib is the witr of the day, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray two rak‘ahs after it. This did not cause Maghrib to cease being witr, because those two rak‘ahs complement the obligatory prayer and make up for any lapses or deficiencies in it. As transmitted in reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he said:

“A servant may finish his prayer with only half of it recorded for him, or a third, or a quarter, or a fifth”—and he went on to say, “or only a tenth.”

Thus, the Sunnah prayers were prescribed to make up for deficiencies in the obligatory prayers. Since the two rak‘ahs after Maghrib make up for deficiencies in the obligatory prayer, they do not cause it to cease being witr, just as performing the two prostrations of forgetfulness would not do so. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made up for deficiencies in the witr of the night by praying two rak‘ahs after it. This is why he would supplement it when he prayed witr with nine, seven, or five rak‘ahs: its number of rak‘ahs fell short of eleven. Here, the shortfall in number is an outward deficiency. If he also prayed these two rak‘ahs when he prayed witr with eleven, they would then have served to make up for a deficiency in the quality of

---

## Page 11757

prayer, even if he performed them seated; for the witr of the night is below the witr of the day in rank, and so is lesser in its manner of performance. This occupies an intermediate rank between the two prostrations of forgetfulness and two full rak‘ahs. There are thus three degrees of making up deficiencies. Forgetfulness is made up for by two prostrations; but that deficiency is an outward shortfall in the amount of prayer performed, so making it up is obligatory and directly connected to the prayer. The two separate rak‘ahs, however, make up for deficiencies in its inward substance. In this way, one’s prayer becomes complete, as stated in the Sunan collections:

“The first of a servant’s deeds for which he will be brought to account is prayer. If he has completed it, [all is well]; otherwise, it will be said: ‘See whether he has any voluntary prayers.’”

The rest of his deeds will then be dealt with in the same way. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offer the qunūt supplication in the ‘Ishā’ prayer or in the Fajr prayer? And what practice were the Companions following when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed away?”

He replied:

As for qunūt in the Fajr prayer, it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would offer it in times of calamity. On one occasion, he offered qunūt for a month, supplicating against a group of disbelievers who had killed some of his Companions, and then discontinued it. On another occasion, he offered qunūt, supplicating for some of his Companions who were being held captive by people preventing them from emigrating to join him.

---

## Page 11758

And his Rightly Guided Caliphs after him likewise offered qunūt of this kind. He neither observed it continually nor abandoned it altogether. Scholars hold three views on the matter:

One view is that observing it continually is sunnah.

Another is that qunūt has been abrogated and that all of it is an innovation.

The third view—which is correct—is that it is sunnah when the need arises, as practised by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Rightly Guided Caliphs.

As for qunūt in witr, it is permissible but not obligatory. Some of his Companions did not offer it; some offered it during the latter half of Ramadan; and some offered it throughout the year. Among the scholars, some recommend the first practice, such as Mālik; some recommend the second, such as al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, according to one reported view; and some recommend the third, such as Abū Hanīfah and Imam Ahmad, according to another reported view. All these practices are permissible, and whoever follows any of them is not to be blamed. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11759

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

As for qunūt, people take two extreme positions and a middle course. Some hold that qunūt should be offered only before bowing, while others hold that it should be offered only after it. The jurists among the scholars of hadith, such as Ahmad and others, permit both, since both are established in the authentic Sunnah. Nevertheless, they prefer qunūt after bowing because it is more frequently reported and more consistent with analogy. The hearing of supplication accords with the worshipper’s words, “Allah hears whoever praises Him,” for it is prescribed to praise Allah before supplicating to Him. The opening surah of the Quran is structured in this way: it begins with praise and ends with supplication.

Likewise, people hold three views regarding the prescription of qunūt in the Fajr prayer, although they agree that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt in Fajr. Some say that it has been abrogated, for he offered it and then discontinued it, as reported in the authentic hadiths.

---

## Page 11760

Anyone who says, “What he discontinued was supplicating against those disbelievers,” has either not encountered the wording of the hadith or encountered it without examining it carefully. For the two Sahīh collections report that ‘Āsim al-Ahwal said:

“I asked Anas ibn Mālik about qunūt: ‘Was it before bowing or after it?’ He replied, ‘Before bowing.’ I said, ‘But so-and-so told me that you said it was after bowing.’ He replied, ‘He spoke falsely. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only offered qunūt before bowing. I believe he sent a group known as the Quran reciters, numbering about seventy men, to some polytheists other than those people. There was a treaty between them and the Messenger of Allah, and he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt for a month, supplicating against them.’”

Likewise, the hadith narrated by Ahmad and al-Hākim from al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, from Anas, states:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, continued to offer qunūt until he departed this world.”

Its wording is clarified in the version stating that “he continued to offer qunūt before bowing.” Here, qunūt means prolonged standing, not supplication. This is how it is explained in the transmitted wording. Further clarification comes from the report in the two Sahīh collections that Muhammad ibn Sīrīn said:

“I asked Anas, ‘Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offer qunūt in the Fajr prayer?’ He replied, ‘Yes, briefly, after bowing.’”

He thus stated that his qunūt was brief and occurred after bowing. Since the word qunūt means sustained obedience, any prolongation of standing, bowing, or prostration is called qunūt. As Allah Most High says:

“Or one who is devoutly obedient during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing.”

For this reason, when Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, was asked about regular qunūt, he said, “We have neither heard of it nor seen it.”

This is one view. Others say that qunūt is, rather, a regular Sunnah, since it is established that the Prophet

---

## Page 11761

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt, and it is reported of him:

“He continued to offer qunūt until he departed this world.”

This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s view. Some who held this view recommended it in all the prayers, since it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt in them. Reports through several chains establish this concerning Maghrib, ‘Ishā’, and Zuhr. However, no one narrated that he regularly offered qunūt using a particular supplication. They therefore recommended using the supplication for qunūt in Witr which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī:

“O Allah, guide me among those You have guided,”

and so on to its end.

Other scholars, including hadith-oriented jurists such as Ahmad, took a middle course, saying:

It is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt in response to calamities inflicted by the enemy, such as the killing or detention of his Companions and similar events. He offered qunūt seeking Allah’s help, just as he sought rain during drought. His seeking help when needed was like his seeking provision when needed, for people’s affairs depend upon victory and provision. As Allah Most High says:

“Who has fed them against hunger and made them secure against fear.”

And as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Are you granted victory and provision except through the weak among you—through their supplications, prayers, and seeking forgiveness?”

And as he said in describing the abdāl:

“Through them you are provided for, and through them you are granted victory.”

Likewise, Allah mentions these two things in Sūrat al-Mulk and makes clear that both are in His hand, glory be to Him, saying:

“Or who is there to serve as an army for you, helping you against the Most Merciful? The disbelievers are only deluded.”

“Or who is there to provide for you if He withholds His provision?”

Then he discontinued qunūt, and a report explicitly explains that he discontinued it because the reason for it no longer existed.

---

## Page 11762

Likewise, ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, would offer qunūt when news of the Muslim armies was slow to reach him. ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, also offered qunūt when fighting opponents from among the Khawārij and others.

They said:

Discontinuing a practice does not constitute abrogation, for what abrogates must be incompatible with what it abrogates. If the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did something to meet a need and then discontinued it when that need ceased, this would not constitute abrogation. Indeed, even if he had discontinued it altogether, that would indicate that both performing it and omitting it were permissible, not that performing it was prohibited.

They said:

We know unequivocally that he did not offer qunūt as a regular practice, for there would have been ample motivation and reason to transmit such a practice. No Companion ever reported that, in his qunūt at Fajr or similar prayers, he supplicated except on behalf of some people or against others. Nor did any of them ever report that he regularly offered qunūt after bowing, or regularly supplicated in qunūt before bowing. More than one Companion rejected qunūt as a regular practice. Once this is understood, it becomes certain that no such regular practice existed, just as it is known that “Come to the best of deeds” was not part of the regular call to prayer. Rather, some Companions used it on particular occasions to urge people to pray.

This, then, is the most balanced view: qunūt is prescribed and has not been abrogated, but it is prescribed in response to a need that arises, not as a regular Sunnah practice. This is another principle concerning obligatory and recommended acts, comparable to the principle discussed earlier regarding what is waived on account of an excuse. For every regularly prescribed obligatory or recommended act

---

## Page 11763

is waived on account of a temporary excuse, so that it is no longer obligatory or recommended, just as many obligatory and recommended acts are waived because of travel, illness, or fear. Likewise, temporary circumstances may make something obligatory or recommended when it is not ordinarily so. Thus, both the prescription and the waiving of acts of worship may be regular or occasion-dependent. This applies equally to establishing an obligation or recommendation and to waiving it. Confusion arises when something occasion-dependent is treated as regular, or when something regular is regarded as unchanging under all circumstances. Whoever understands the distinction between regularly prescribed practices and those prescribed for particular occasions will find many of these difficulties resolved.

He was asked:

Is regularly offering qunūt in the Fajr prayer a Sunnah? And what of those who say, “It is among those elements of prayer whose omission is remedied by prostration, and only a deficiency requires a remedy”?

As for the hadith,

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, continued to offer qunūt until he departed this world,”

is this an authentic hadith? Does it refer to this particular qunūt? What are the scholars’ views on this, and what evidence does each of them offer?

If someone offers qunūt because of a calamity, must he use a particular wording, or may he supplicate as he wishes?

---

## Page 11764

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. It is established in the authentic hadith collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“offered qunūt for a month, supplicating against Ri‘l, Dhakwān, and ‘Usayyah,”

then discontinued it. This was after they had killed the Quran reciters among the Companions. It is also established that some time later, after the Treaty of al-Hudaybiyyah and the conquest of Khaybar, he offered qunūt, supplicating for his oppressed Companions in Mecca. In his qunūt, he would say:

“O Allah, deliver al-Walīd ibn al-Walīd, ‘Ayyāsh ibn Abī Rabī‘ah, Salamah ibn Hishām, and the oppressed believers. O Allah, deal severely with Mudar, and afflict them with years like those of Joseph.”

“He would offer qunūt, supplicating for the believers and cursing the disbelievers, and his qunūt was in the Fajr prayer.”

It is established in the authentic hadith collections

“that he offered qunūt in the Maghrib, ‘Ishā’, and Zuhr prayers,”

and in the Sunan collections,

“that he offered qunūt in the ‘Asr prayer”

as well.

Muslims have differed over qunūt, holding three views:

The first is that it has been abrogated and is therefore not prescribed under any circumstances. This rests on the reasoning that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt and then discontinued it, and that discontinuing an act abrogates its practice. Likewise, his later remaining seated for funerals was considered to abrogate his earlier practice of standing. This is the view of a group of scholars from Iraq, including Abū Hanīfah and others.

---

## Page 11765

The second is that qunūt is always prescribed, that observing it regularly is Sunnah, and that it is to be offered in the Fajr prayer.

Some who hold this view say that the Sunnah is to offer it silently before bowing, after the Quran recitation, and to use only the supplications beginning:

“O Allah, we seek Your help,”

to its end, and:

“O Allah, You alone we worship,”

to its end. This is Mālik’s view.

Others say that the Sunnah is to offer it aloud after bowing. They recommend using the qunūt supplication that al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“O Allah, guide me among those You have guided,”

to its end, although they may allow qunūt both before and after bowing.

Those who hold this view may cite the words of Allah Most High:

“Guard the prayers, especially the middle prayer, and stand before Allah in devout obedience.”

They say that the middle prayer is Fajr and that qunūt is to be offered in it.

Both premises are weak. As for the first, authentic reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establish:

“The middle prayer is ‘Asr.”

No one familiar with the transmitted hadiths doubts this. Hence, hadith scholars and others have agreed on it, although the Companions and scholars expressed various views on the matter, speaking according to their own scholarly judgment.

---

## Page 11766

As for the second premise, qunūt means persevering in obedience, which applies both to standing and to prostrating. As Allah Most High says:

“Or one who is devout during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, fearing the Hereafter.”

Even if it meant remaining standing—as has been suggested concerning His words:

“O Mary, be devoutly obedient to your Lord, prostrate and bow”—

it would not be permissible to interpret this as prolonging the standing specifically for supplication rather than for any other purpose. Allah has commanded people to stand before Him in devout obedience, and a command entails obligation. Yet, by consensus, the disputed practice of standing for supplication is not obligatory. Moreover, someone standing while reciting the Quran is also devoutly obedient to Allah.

It is also established in an authentic hadith that:

“When this verse was revealed, they were commanded to remain silent and forbidden to speak.”

Thus, silence is part of fulfilling the devout obedience commanded here. It is well known that this is obligatory throughout the standing portion of prayer.

Furthermore, His words:

“And stand before Allah in devout obedience”

are not restricted to the middle prayer, whether that is Fajr or ‘Asr. Rather, they are conjoined with His words:

“Guard the prayers, especially the middle prayer.”

Thus, the command to observe devout obedience accompanies the command to guard the prayers. Since guarding them encompasses all the prayers, standing likewise encompasses them all.

They also cite the report narrated by Imam Ahmad in his Musnad and al-Hākim in his Sahīh, through Abū Ja‘far al-Rāzī, from al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, from Anas:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, continued to offer qunūt until he departed this world.”

They say that the words in the other hadith, “Then he ceased doing so,” mean that he ceased supplicating against those

---

## Page 11767

tribes, but did not cease qunūt itself. This alone does not establish a regular sunnah practice in prayer. Al-Hākim’s grading of a report as authentic carries less weight than al-Tirmidhī’s grading of one as good. He frequently grades fabricated reports as authentic, for he is known for his leniency in this regard. Moreover, this very hadith does not specify whether qunūt was before or after bowing. Yet he said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt after bowing for only one month.”

This is an authentic, explicit report from Anas that he offered qunūt after bowing for only one month, so that interpretation is invalid. Qunūt before bowing may mean prolonging the standing before bowing, whether or not any additional supplication is made. In that case, the wording would not indicate qunūt in the sense of supplication.

Some have held that offering qunūt regularly in all five prayers is recommended, arguing that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered it in them, without distinguishing between regular and occasional qunūt. This is an anomalous view.

The third view is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt because of a circumstance that befell him, then ceased when that circumstance no longer applied. Thus, qunūt is prescribed in times of calamity. This is the view held by the jurists among the hadith scholars, and it is what is transmitted from the Rightly Guided Caliphs, may Allah be pleased with them. For when ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, fought the Christians, he recited against them the qunūt supplication

---

## Page 11768

that is well known:

“O Allah, punish the disbelievers among the People of the Book,” and so on.

This is the supplication that some people have made a sunnah practice in the qunūt of Ramadan. Yet this qunūt is not a regular sunnah, either in Ramadan or at any other time. Rather, ‘Umar offered qunūt because of the calamity that had befallen the Muslims, and his supplication in it suited that calamity. Likewise, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, first offered qunūt against the clans of Banū Sulaym who had killed the Quran reciters, he supplicated against them in terms suited to his purpose. Later, when he offered qunūt for the oppressed among his Companions, he made a supplication suited to that purpose.

The Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Rightly Guided Caliphs thus establishes two points:

First: the supplication of qunūt is prescribed when circumstances call for it; it is not a permanent sunnah practice in prayer.

Second: the supplication offered in it is not a fixed formula. Rather, on each occasion one offers a supplication appropriate to it, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did on the first and second occasions mentioned. So too did ‘Umar and ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with them both. When the latter fought his opponents during the civil strife, he offered qunūt with a supplication suited to his purpose.

What makes this clear is that, had the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regularly offered qunūt with a fixed supplication, the Muslims would have transmitted this from their Prophet. Such a matter would have given them every reason and incentive to transmit it. They are, after all, the very people who transmitted the supplications he offered in qunūt without maintaining them as a regular sunnah, such as his supplication against those who killed his Companions and his supplication for the oppressed among

---

## Page 11769

his Companions. They also transmitted the qunūt of ‘Umar and ‘Alī against those with whom they were at war.

How, then, could the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, have regularly offered qunūt in the dawn prayer or any other prayer, using a fixed supplication, without this being transmitted from him in any report, whether authentic or weak? Indeed, the Prophet’s Companions, who knew his Sunnah best and were most eager to follow it, such as Ibn ‘Umar and others, rejected this practice. Ibn ‘Umar even said:

“We neither saw nor heard of it.”

In another version:

“Tell me about this standing of yours, offering supplication: we neither saw nor heard of it.”

Would any Muslim say that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regularly offered qunūt when Ibn ‘Umar says, “We neither saw nor heard of it”? Other Companions besides Ibn ‘Umar likewise counted this among newly introduced practices.

Anyone who carefully considers the hadiths on this subject will know with absolute certainty that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not regularly offer qunūt in any of the prayers, just as he knows with certainty that he did not regularly offer qunūt in the noon, night, and sunset prayers. Those who regard qunūt in these prayers as a regular sunnah practice rely on the same kind of evidence as those who regard it as a regular sunnah practice in the dawn prayer.

There is no doubt that authentic reports establish that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, offered qunūt in these prayers. However, the Companions explained the supplication he offered, the reason for his qunūt, and that he discontinued it once its purpose had been achieved. They reported this concerning

---

## Page 11770

qunūt in the dawn prayer and in the night prayer as well.

This is further clarified by the following: those who consider it a Sunnah practice in prayer to offer qunūt regularly using the supplication of al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī or the two surahs of Ubayy have no evidence beyond a supplication offered on a particular occasion. When qunūt is prescribed in prayer, it is prescribed for the imam, those praying behind him, and someone praying alone. More clearly still, if someone were to make the qunūt of al-Hasan or the two surahs of Ubayy a regular Sunnah practice in the sunset and night prayers, his position would resemble that of someone who makes this a regular Sunnah practice in the dawn prayer. For the latter have no evidence concerning the dawn prayer except qunūt offered on a particular occasion, with a supplication suited to that occasion. No Muslim has transmitted any other supplication for its qunūt, just as none has been transmitted for the sunset and night prayers.

Some scholars fell into this misunderstanding concerning the dawn prayer only because qunūt was offered in it more often, and because it is longer, with qunūt corresponding to the prayer in this respect. Reports reached them that he had offered it continually, so they thought that the Sunnah was to observe it regularly. Then, finding no Sunnah report specifying his supplication, they prescribed these supplications transmitted for the witr prayer, although they do not regard this as a regular Sunnah practice in witr.

This disagreement over qunūt has many parallels in Islamic law. Often, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would do something for a particular reason, and some people would make it a Sunnah practice without distinguishing between a regular Sunnah and an occasional one. Others, seeing that he did not do it most of the time, regard it as an innovation. If reports of his doing it on certain occasions have reached them, they treat this as a special case or as something subsequently abrogated. An example is offering voluntary prayers in congregation. It is established in the Sahīh that “he prayed at night, with Ibn

---

## Page 11771

‘Abbās on one occasion and Hudhayfa ibn al-Yamān on another.” Others also prayed behind him. Likewise, “he led ‘Itbān ibn Mālik in voluntary prayer in congregation in his house,” and “he led Anas ibn Mālik, his mother, and the orphan in prayer in his home.”

Some people use this as a precedent for newly introduced practices such as the “al-Alfiyya prayer” on the night of mid-Sha‘bān, the Raghā’ib prayer, and similar prayers which they regularly perform in congregation. Others disapprove of voluntary prayer in congregation because they consider congregational worship to have been prescribed only for the five daily prayers, just as the call to prayer was prescribed only for those five prayers.

Clearly, the correct position is what the Sunnah teaches: offering voluntary prayer in congregation is not disliked, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, himself did so. Nor, however, should it be made a regular Sunnah practice—for example, by appointing a regular imam for the mosque to lead people in prayer between the sunset and night prayers, or in the middle of the night, just as he leads them in the five daily prayers. Similarly, one may not institute a call to prayer for the two Eid prayers or other prayers like the call for the five daily prayers. This is why the Companions objected to those rulers of their time who did so.

Somewhat similar is the scholars’ disagreement over the number of units of night prayer in Ramadan. It is established that Ubayy ibn Ka‘b used to lead the people in twenty units of Ramadan night prayer, followed by three units of witr. Many scholars therefore held this to be the Sunnah, because he established this practice among the Emigrants and the Helpers, and no one objected. Others recommended thirty-nine units, on the grounds that this was the longstanding practice of the people of Medina.

---

## Page 11772

Another group said: It is established in an authentic report from ‘Ā’isha that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would not pray more than thirteen units, whether in Ramadan or at other times.”

Some became confused over this issue because they thought that the authentic hadith contradicted the established practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs and the Muslims. The correct view is that all these practices are good, as Imam Ahmad, may Allah be pleased with him, explicitly stated. No fixed number of units is prescribed for the night prayer in Ramadan, since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe a particular number. Accordingly, the number of units may be increased or decreased depending on how long or short the standing is.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to stand for a long time in prayer at night. Indeed, it is established in an authentic report from Hudhayfa that “he would recite al-Baqara, al-Nisā’, and Āl ‘Imrān in a single unit of prayer, so that prolonged standing made a greater number of units unnecessary.”

When Ubayy ibn Ka‘b led the people as a single congregation, he could not keep them standing for so long, so he increased the number of units to compensate for the shorter standing. They made the number twice that prayed by the Prophet, who used to pray eleven or thirteen units at night. Later, the people of Medina became less able to sustain prolonged standing, so they increased the number of units until it reached thirty-nine.

---

## Page 11773

A related example is that when Allah Most High prescribed the five daily prayers in Mecca, He prescribed each as two units. They remained so during travel, while the prayers offered when not travelling were increased, as established in an authentic report from ‘Ā’isha, may Allah be pleased with her, who said:

“When he migrated to Medina, the prayers offered when not travelling were increased. The Maghrib prayer was made three units because it is the day’s odd-numbered prayer. The Fajr prayer, however, remained two units because of its prolonged recitation, which made a greater number of units unnecessary.”

The scholars have differed over which is better: prolonging the standing or increasing the number of bowings and prostrations, or whether the two are equal. These are three views, all of which are reported from Ahmad. An authentic report establishes that the Prophet was asked:

“Which prayer is best?” He replied, “That with prolonged standing.”

It is also established that he said:

“You will never make a single prostration to Allah without Allah raising you one degree thereby and removing one sin from you.”

He said to Rabī‘a ibn Ka‘b:

“Help me to help you by prostrating frequently.”

It is well known that prostration in itself is better than standing, but the remembrance recited while standing—namely, Quran recitation—is better. The precise conclusion is that it is best for the prayer to be balanced: if one prolongs the standing, one should also prolong the bowing and prostration, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do in his night prayers, as reported by Hudhayfa and others. Likewise,

---

## Page 11774

his obligatory prayers, eclipse prayer and other prayers were balanced. If one weighs the merits of prolonging the standing, bowing and prostration while performing fewer units of prayer against shortening them while performing more units, the two approaches are close in merit. One may be better in a particular situation. Thus, when he offered the forenoon prayer on the day of the conquest of Mecca, he prayed eight brief units rather than limiting himself to two long ones. The Companions likewise increased the number of units in the Ramadan night prayers when prolonged standing became burdensome for those praying behind them.

What we have explained makes clear that qunūt is offered in times of calamity, and that its supplication has no single fixed wording. Nor should one supplicate with whatever happens to occur to him. Rather, one should offer supplications sanctioned by the Sharia that suit the reason for the qunūt. Just as, when praying for rain, one supplicates in a manner suited to that purpose, so too, when praying for victory, one supplicates in a manner suited to that purpose. The same applies when supplicating outside the prayer for that reason: one would offer supplications appropriate to the purpose. This is what is established by the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the practice of his rightly guided caliphs.

Those who say that qunūt is among the subsidiary elements of prayer whose omission is remedied by prostrations for forgetfulness base this on the view that it is a Sunnah practice recommended for regular observance, like the first tashahhud and similar acts. It has become clear, however, that this is not the case: it is not a regular Sunnah practice, and no prostrations for forgetfulness are made on its account. Nevertheless, someone who holds that view on the basis of an interpretive judgment is accorded allowance for that interpretation, as in other matters open to scholarly judgment. For this reason, someone praying behind an imam should follow him in matters where scholarly judgment is permissible.

---

## Page 11775

so if the imam offers qunūt, he should join him in it, and if the imam omits it, he should not offer it. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The imam has only been appointed to be followed.”

He also said:

“Do not go against your imams.”

It is also established in the Sahīh collection that he said:

“They lead you in prayer. If they do so correctly, both you and they will be rewarded; if they err, you will be rewarded, while they will bear the blame.”

Do you not see that if the imam recited a surah in addition to al-Fātihah in the last two units of prayer and made them longer than the first two, following him in that would be obligatory? Going ahead of the imam, however, is not permissible. Thus, if he offers qunūt, those praying behind him may not go ahead of him; they must follow him. This is why, although ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd objected to ʿUthmān’s praying four units at Minā, he nevertheless prayed four units behind him. When asked about this, he replied, “Discord is evil.” Likewise, when a man asked Anas ibn Mālik about the time for throwing the pebbles, he informed him, then said, “Do as your imam does.” And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“It is not lawful for a man leading people in prayer to single himself out in supplication to their exclusion. If he does so, he has betrayed them.”

Is it recommended, then, for the imam to include those praying behind him whenever he supplicates to Allah, Mighty and Majestic? And is it authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to supplicate exclusively for himself during his prayer, without including those praying behind him?

---

## Page 11776

How, then, can these two be reconciled?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that Abū Hurayrah said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “What do you say during your silence between the opening takbīr and the recitation?” He replied:

“I say: ‘O Allah, distance me from my sins as You have distanced the east from the west. O Allah, cleanse me of my sins as a white garment is cleansed of dirt. O Allah, wash away my sins with water, snow, and hail.’”

This authentic hadith clearly shows that he supplicated specifically for himself while leading the prayer. Likewise, the hadith of ʿAlī concerning the opening supplication, which begins, “I have turned my face towards Him who created the heavens and the earth,” includes:

“So forgive me, for none forgives sins but You. Guide me to the best character, for none guides to it but You. Turn bad character away from me, for none turns it away from me but You.”

It is also established in the Sahīh collection that, after raising his head from bowing and saying:

“None can withhold what You give, and none can give what You withhold,”

he would say:

“O Allah, purify me of my sins with water, snow, and hail. O Allah, cleanse me of sins as a white garment is cleansed of dirt.”

All these transmitted reports concerning his supplications after the tashahhud (testimony of faith), whether describing his own practice or conveying his instructions, give only singular wording. For example, he said:

“O Allah, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of Hell, from the punishment of the grave, from the trials of life and death, and from the trial of the False Messiah.”

Likewise, his supplication between

---

## Page 11777

the two prostrations is reported in the Sunan collections in narrations from Hudhayfah and Ibn ‘Abbās. In both instances, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was leading the prayer: in one, he led Hudhayfah, and in the other, Ibn ‘Abbās. Hudhayfah’s narration gives the wording:

“My Lord, forgive me. My Lord, forgive me.”

Ibn ‘Abbās’s narration includes:

“Forgive me, have mercy on me, guide me, grant me well-being, and provide for me,”

and similar wording. These hadiths in the Sahīh and Sunan collections show that the imam supplicates in the singular at these points in the prayer. The scholars likewise agree on this wherever they consider such supplications to be prescribed.

Once this is understood, it becomes clear that the aforementioned hadith, if authentic, refers to supplications to which the follower says “Amen,” such as the qunūt supplication. For when the follower says “Amen,” he too is supplicating. Allah Most High said to Moses and Aaron:

“The prayer of you both has been answered.”

Yet one of them was supplicating while the other was saying “Amen.”

When the follower says “Amen” to the imam’s supplication, the imam should therefore supplicate in the plural, as in the supplication in al-Fātihah:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

The follower only says “Amen” because he believes that the imam is supplicating for them both. If the imam does not do so, he has betrayed the follower’s trust.

As for those points at which each person supplicates for himself, such as the opening supplication, the supplication after the tashahhud, and the like, just as the follower supplicates for himself, so too does the imam. Likewise, the follower glorifies Allah while bowing and prostrating when the imam glorifies Him during

---

## Page 11778

bowing and prostrating, just as he recites the tashahhud when the imam does and says “Allah is the Greatest” when the imam does. If the follower fails to do so, he is the one at fault.

Even if this hadith were authentic and explicitly contradicted the widely reported, mass-transmitted hadiths and the practice of the Muslim community and its imams, it would not be accorded weight. How much less so when it is not authentic, although some have graded it good. Even if it supported that interpretation, its wording would be general, whereas those other hadiths are specific, and the specific takes precedence over the general. Moreover, its wording,

“and singles himself out for a supplication to their exclusion,”

refers to cases in which they have no share in any supplication. This cannot be the case when they say “Amen.” When they say “Amen” to his supplication whenever he supplicates, they obtain through their own action what he obtains. This is why the qunūt supplication uses the plural:

“O Allah, we seek Your help and Your guidance,”

and so forth. In such cases, he should use the plural form and follow the sunnah as prescribed. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is performing the tarāwīh prayer after Maghrib a sunnah practice or an innovation? It has been mentioned that Imam al-Shāfiʿī performed it after Maghrib and completed it after ʿIshāʾ.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The sunnah is to perform tarāwīh after ʿIshāʾ, as the early generations and the imams agreed. The aforementioned report attributed to

---

## Page 11779

al-Shāfiʿī—may Allah be pleased with him—is false. The imams prayed it only after ʿIshāʾ during the time of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his Rightly Guided Caliphs. This has also been the practice of the imams of the Muslims; none is known to have deliberately performed it before ʿIshāʾ. This prayer is called the Ramadan night prayer, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has made fasting Ramadan obligatory upon you, and I have established its night prayer as a sunnah for you. Whoever fasts it and performs its night prayer will have his previous sins forgiven.”

Night prayer, whether in Ramadan or at other times, is performed only after ʿIshāʾ. The Sunan collections explicitly state:

“When he led them in the Ramadan night prayer, he prayed after ʿIshāʾ.”

The night prayer of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was his witr prayer. In Ramadan and at other times, he would pray eleven or thirteen rakʿahs at night, but he made them lengthy. Since this was difficult for people, Ubayy ibn Kaʿb led them in twenty rakʿahs during the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, shortening the standing in them and praying witr afterwards. Increasing the number thus compensated for the length of the standing. Some of the early generations prayed forty rakʿahs with shorter periods of standing, followed by three rakʿahs of witr. Others prayed thirty-six rakʿahs, followed by witr. Their established practice was to perform their night prayer after ʿIshāʾ.

However, the Rāfidah dislike the tarāwīh prayer. If they perform it before ʿIshāʾ, it is not the tarāwīh prayer, just as, when they perform ablution, they wash

---

## Page 11780

their feet at the beginning of ablution and wipe over them at its end. Whoever performs it before ʿIshāʾ has followed the path of innovators who oppose the Sunnah. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Is the practice of imams in our time of reciting Sūrat al-Anʿām in a single rakʿah during Ramadan, on the night preceding Friday, an innovation or not?”

He answered:

Yes, it is an innovation. It has not been reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or from any of the Companions, the Successors, or other imams that they deliberately singled this out. Those who do so rely solely on what is reported from Mujāhid and others, that Sūrat al-Anʿām was revealed all at once, escorted by seventy thousand angels: “So recite it all at once, because it was revealed all at once.” This is a weak argument, and reciting it all at once involves several objectionable features.

Among them is that whoever does this makes the second rakʿah of the prayer excessively longer than the first, whereas the Sunnah is to make the first longer than the second, as is authentically reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Another is making the latter part of the night prayer longer than its beginning. This is contrary to the Sunnah, for he used to make the earlier rakʿahs he prayed longer than the later ones. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11781

He was asked:

“Some people pray two rakʿahs in congregation after tarāwīh, then complete a total of one hundred rakʿahs at the end of the night. They call this salāt al-qadr (the Prayer of Decree). Some imams have declined to perform it. Who is correct: those who perform it or those who refrain? Do any of the imams consider it recommended, or is it disliked? Should it be performed and encouraged, or abandoned and forbidden?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Rather, those who decline to perform it and refrain from it are correct. None of the imams of the Muslims has recommended this prayer. Indeed, it is a disliked innovation by agreement of the imams. Neither the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—nor any of the Companions or Successors performed this prayer. None of the imams of the Muslims recommends it. It should be abandoned and forbidden.

As for reciting the Quran during tarāwīh, this is recommended by agreement of the imams of the Muslims. Indeed, one of the foremost purposes of tarāwīh is the recitation of the Quran, so that Muslims may hear the words of Allah. The Quran was revealed in the month of Ramadan, and during it, “Gabriel used to review with the Prophet

---

## Page 11782

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the Quran. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was the most generous of people, and he was at his most generous in Ramadan, when Gabriel would meet him and review the Quran with him.”

He was asked about the sunnah prayer associated with ʿAsr:

“Has any hadith concerning it been reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace? Which of the differing views concerning it is correct?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. What is authentically reported from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is the hadith of Ibn ʿUmar:

“I learned from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the following ten rakʿahs: two before Zuhr and two after it, two after Maghrib, two after ʿIshāʾ, and two before Fajr.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever offers twelve voluntary rakʿahs in a day and a night, Allah will build him a house in Paradise.”

The Sunan collections explain these as:

“Four before Zuhr and two after it, two after Maghrib, two after ʿIshāʾ, and two before Fajr.”

It is also authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer. Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer. Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.” Then, on the third occasion, he added: “For whoever wishes,” lest people adopt it as a sunnah. This hadith thus indicates that one may pray

---

## Page 11783

before ʿAsr, before Maghrib, and before ʿIshāʾ. It has also been authentically reported that:

“The companions of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to offer two rakʿahs between the adhān and iqāmah of Maghrib. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would see them without forbidding them, but he did not do so himself.”

Such prayers are good, but are not a sunnah, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—disliked their being adopted as a sunnah. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not pray before ʿAsr, before Maghrib, or before ʿIshāʾ. These prayers should therefore not be adopted as a sunnah, although offering them at those times is not disliked. This differs from what he both practised and encouraged, which is more strongly emphasized.

It has been reported that:

“He used to offer four rakʿahs before ʿAsr.”

But this report is weak. It has also been reported that:

“He used to offer two rakʿahs.”

What is meant here is the two rakʿahs before Zuhr. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Does ʿAsr have a regular sunnah prayer or not? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. What is established in the Sahīh concerning the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is that:

“He used to offer ten or twelve rakʿahs alongside the obligatory prayers: two or four before Zuhr and two after it, two after Maghrib, two after ʿIshāʾ, and two before Fajr.”

It is likewise established in the Sahīh that

---

## Page 11784

the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever offers twelve rak‘ahs of voluntary prayer, apart from the obligatory prayers, in a day and night, Allah will build for him a house in Paradise.”

The Sunan collections specify four before Zuhr, two after it, two after Maghrib, two after ‘Ishā’, and two before Fajr. The Sahīh contains only these three reports on this matter: those of Ibn ‘Umar, ‘Ā’ishah, and Umm Habībah.

As for prayer before ‘Asr, no report stating that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to pray before ‘Asr is free of weakness—indeed, error. One example is a report attributed to ‘Alī stating that he used to offer some sixteen rak‘ahs, including some before ‘Asr. This report has been criticized, for those who took care to transmit accounts of his voluntary prayers, such as ‘Ā’ishah and Ibn ‘Umar, explained which prayers he used to offer.

Likewise, he did not offer prayers before Maghrib or before ‘Ishā’. His companions, however, used to pray before Maghrib, between the adhān and iqāmah, while he watched them without objecting. It is also authentically reported in the Sahīh that he said:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer; between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.” Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes.”

This was because he disliked people adopting it as a sunnah. This shows that praying before ‘Asr, Maghrib, and ‘Ishā’ is good, but is not a sunnah. Thus, if someone wishes to pray before ‘Asr, just as he prays before Maghrib and ‘Ishā’, on this basis, that is good. But to believe that it is a regular sunnah prayer which the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to offer, as he prayed before and after Zuhr and after Maghrib, is mistaken.

The prayers offered alongside the obligatory prayers fall into three ranks. The first comprises the sunnah prayer before Fajr and Witr. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded these two, but did not command the others. Both are sunnah by agreement of the imams. The Prophet

---

## Page 11785

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to offer both when travelling and when resident. Mālik did not recognize any regular sunnah prayer apart from these two.

The second rank comprises the prayers he used to offer alongside the obligatory prayers when resident: ten rak‘ahs and thirteen rak‘ahs. Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad recognized regular sunnah prayers with a specified number of rak‘ahs alongside the obligatory prayers, unlike Mālik.

The third rank comprises voluntary prayers that may be offered at these times without being treated as regular sunnah prayers, since the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—neither offered them consistently nor specified a number of rak‘ahs for them. Prayer before ‘Asr, Maghrib, and ‘Ishā’ falls into this category. The Duhā prayer is somewhat similar. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Is the sunnah prayer before ‘Asr recommended?”

He answered:

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not use to offer any prayer before ‘Asr. Rather, he used to pray before Zuhr—either two or four rak‘ahs—and after it. He would also pray two rak‘ahs after Maghrib, two after ‘Ishā’, and two before Fajr. As for before ‘Asr, Maghrib, and ‘Ishā’, he did not use to pray then. However, it is authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.” Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes.”

This was because he disliked people adopting it as a sunnah. Thus, whoever wishes to offer voluntary prayer before ‘Asr does well, but should not adopt it as a regular sunnah. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11786

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“May missed regular sunnah prayers be made up?”

He answered:

If a regular sunnah prayer, such as the sunnah prayer associated with Zuhr, is missed, may it be made up after ‘Asr? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad:

The first is that it is not to be made up. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

The second is that it is to be made up. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s view, and it is the stronger view. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“What about someone who does not consistently observe the regular sunnah prayers?”

He answered:

If someone persists in abandoning them, this indicates a lack of religious commitment, and his testimony is rejected according to the schools of Ahmad, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others.

---

## Page 11787

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Are there sunnah prayers associated with a traveller’s obligatory prayers? Allah has reduced the four-rak‘ah prayers to two rak‘ahs out of mercy towards His servants, so what evidence supports prescribing sunnah prayers? ‘Umar objected to someone offering voluntary prayer after the obligatory prayer. Do any schools, such as that of Abū Hanīfah, regard sunnah prayers as strongly recommended during travel? Has this been reported from Abū Hanīfah or not?”

He answered:

The voluntary prayers that the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—is authentically reported to have offered while travelling include the two rak‘ahs before Fajr. Indeed, when he and his Companions overslept on their return from Khaybar, they made up these two rak‘ahs along with the obligatory prayer. The same applies to the night prayer and Witr.

For it is authentically reported:

“He used to pray on his mount, whichever direction it took him, and would offer Witr on it, but he would not offer the obligatory prayers on it.”

As for prayer before and after Zuhr, it has not been reported that he offered it while travelling, and he did not offer any additional prayer alongside Zuhr. Likewise, at Minā he would pray two rak‘ahs at a time, and no one reported that he offered any additional prayer alongside them.

---

## Page 11788

And Ibn ‘Umar was the most knowledgeable of people concerning the sunnah and the most diligent in following it. As for the scholars, they differed over whether this was recommended. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“What about praying after the adhān for Maghrib and before the obligatory prayer?”

He answered:

As the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—had instructed him, Bilāl would leave an interval between his adhān (call to prayer) and his iqāmah (call to commence prayer), allowing enough time for two rak‘ahs. Some Companions would pray two rak‘ahs between the two calls, and the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—would see them and approve of their doing so. He said:

“Between every two calls there is a prayer; between every two calls there is a prayer; between every two calls there is a prayer.” Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes,” lest it be taken as an established sunnah.

If the caller leaves an interval of that length between the two calls, this prayer is good. But if he follows the adhān immediately with the iqāmah, then the sunnah is to occupy oneself with responding to the caller, for the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, say what he says.”

No one should forgo responding to the caller in order to pray these two rak‘ahs, for “the sunnah for anyone who hears the caller is to repeat what he says, then send blessings upon

---

## Page 11789

the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—and say, ‘O Allah, Lord of this perfect call,’” and so on, then offer supplication afterward.

He was asked:

“A woman regularly offers prayers at night, but at times she is unable to stand. She was told that a prayer offered sitting earns half the reward of a prayer offered standing. Is this correct?”

He answered:

Yes. It is authentically reported that the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said:

“A prayer offered sitting earns half the reward of a prayer offered standing.”

However, if a person habitually prays standing and sits only because he is unable to stand, Allah grants him the reward of praying standing, for the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, the deeds he used to perform while healthy and at home are recorded for him.”

Even if illness prevented him from offering the prayer altogether, Allah would record its full reward for him because of his intention and his doing whatever he could. How much more so, then, if he is unable to perform only some of its actions!

---

## Page 11790

He was asked:

“What is the meaning of the Prophet’s words—peace and blessings be upon him—‘Do not turn your homes into graves’?”

He answered:

The wording of the hadith is:

“Offer some of your prayers in your homes.”

If you do not remember Allah in them, you are like the dead, and your homes are like graves. For it is authentically reported that the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said:

“The likeness of one who remembers his Lord and one who does not is that of the living and the dead.”

Another wording states:

“The likeness of a house in which Allah is remembered and one in which He is not remembered is that of the living and the dead.”

He was asked:

“What about the prayer of mid-Sha'bān?”

He answered:

If a person prays on the night of mid-Sha'bān alone or in a private congregation, as groups among the early generations used to do, that is better. As for gathering in mosques for a prayer with a fixed number of units—such as regularly gathering to offer one hundred units of prayer with a thousand recitations of “Say: He is Allah, One”—this is an innovation that none of the imams regarded as recommended. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11791

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

As for the Raghā’ib prayer, it has no basis; rather, it is an innovation. It is therefore not recommended, whether performed in congregation or individually. It is established in Sahīh Muslim that:

“The Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—forbade singling out the night preceding Friday for night prayer, or Friday itself for fasting.”

The report cited concerning this prayer is a fabrication, by scholarly consensus. None of the early generations or the imams ever mentioned it.

As for the night of mid-Sha‘bān, hadiths and reports have been narrated concerning its merit, and it is related that some of the early generations used to pray on that night. Thus, someone who prays alone on that night has a precedent among the early generations and grounds for doing so. Such a practice should not be condemned.

As for praying on that night in congregation, this falls under a general principle concerning gatherings for acts of obedience and worship. These are of two kinds. The first is a regularly prescribed practice, whether obligatory or recommended, such as the five daily prayers, the Friday prayer, the two Eid prayers, the eclipse prayer, the prayer for rain, and Tarāwīh. These are regularly prescribed practices that should be observed consistently.

The second consists of gatherings that are not regularly prescribed, such as gathering for voluntary prayer—including night prayer—or for reciting the Quran, remembering Allah, or supplication. There is nothing wrong with this, provided it is not made a regular custom. For the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—

---

## Page 11792

occasionally offered voluntary prayer in congregation, but did not do so regularly except in the cases already mentioned. When his Companions gathered, they would ask one of them to recite while the others listened. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb would say to Abū Mūsā, “Remind us of our Lord,” and he would recite while they listened. It has also been reported that:

“The Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—came out to the people of the sheltered platform while one of them was reciting, and sat with them.”

The well-known hadith has also been narrated concerning the angels who travel about seeking gatherings of remembrance. Thus, if some people were to gather on certain nights for voluntary prayer, without making this a regular custom resembling an established sunnah, it would not be disapproved. But making it a recurring custom tied to particular times is disapproved, because this alters Islamic law and makes what is not prescribed resemble what is prescribed. If that were permissible, it would likewise be permissible to institute another prayer at midmorning or between the noon and afternoon prayers, Tarāwīh in Sha‘bān, a call to prayer for the two Eid prayers, or a pilgrimage to the Rock in Jerusalem. This would alter and change Allah’s religion. The same applies to the night of the Prophet’s birth and other such occasions.

Disapproved innovations are practices that are not recommended in Islamic law; they consist in instituting what Allah has not authorized. Whoever makes something part of religion and a means of drawing near to Allah without its being prescribed by Allah is a misguided innovator. This is what the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—meant when he said:

“Every innovation is misguidance.”

Innovation is thus the opposite of prescribed religion. Prescribed religion comprises whatever Allah and His Messenger have commanded, whether as an obligation or as a recommendation, even if it was not practised during his lifetime. Examples include gathering behind a single imam for Tarāwīh, compiling the Quran into a single volume, fighting and killing the apostates and the Khawārij, and similar matters. Whatever Allah and His Messenger have not prescribed is innovation and misguidance, such as singling out

---

## Page 11793

a place or time for a gathering devoted to worship, just as the Lawgiver has singled out the prayer times, Fridays, and the Eids; and just as He has accorded Mecca its special distinction, and distinguished the three mosques and all other mosques by the prayers and various acts of worship He has prescribed in each, as appropriate.

This explanation makes clear how the scriptural texts and scholarly consensuses that constitute the evidence of Islamic law are reconciled. What is meant by innovation is the opposite of prescribed religion: whatever has not been prescribed as part of the religion. Whenever a scriptural text or scholarly consensus establishes that an act is something Allah and His Messenger love, that act thereby ceases to qualify as an innovation. I have explained this at length in a substantial treatise on one of the major principles.

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

“The Raghā’ib prayer” is an innovation by agreement of the leading scholars of the religion. Neither the Messenger of Allah—peace and blessings be upon him—nor any of his caliphs instituted it, nor did any of the leading scholars of the religion recommend it, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfa, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā‘ī, al-Layth, and others. The hadith narrated concerning it is a fabrication, by consensus of the scholars versed in hadith.

The same applies to the prayer mentioned for the first Friday night of Rajab, the prayer on the night of the Ascension, the Alfiyya prayer on the night of mid-Sha‘bān, and the prayers on Sunday, Monday, and other days of the week. Although some authors have mentioned these in works of spiritual edification, there is no disagreement among scholars versed in hadith that all the reports concerning them are fabricated. None of the leading scholars of the religion recommended these prayers. And in Sahīh Muslim

---

## Page 11794

it is narrated from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said:

“Do not single out Friday night for prayer, nor Friday for fasting.”

The hadiths cited concerning fasting on Friday and on the nights of the two Eids are lies attributed to the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Is the Raghā’ib prayer recommended or not?”

He replied:

Neither the Messenger of Allah—peace and blessings be upon him—nor any of his Companions, the Successors, or the leading scholars of the Muslims performed this prayer. Neither the Messenger of Allah—peace and blessings be upon him—nor any of the early generations or the leading scholars encouraged it, nor did they mention any virtue peculiar to this night. The hadith narrated from the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—concerning it is a fabricated lie, by agreement of those versed in this matter.

For this reason, the scholars who have carefully examined the matter have stated that this prayer is discouraged, not recommended. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11795

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section: Prostration in the Quran

Quranic passages concerning prostration are of two kinds: accounts of those who prostrate, praising them; or commands to prostrate, censuring those who fail to do so.

The first kind includes the prostration passage in al-Aʿrāf:

“Indeed, those who are with your Lord are not too proud to worship Him; they glorify Him and prostrate to Him.”

He mentions this after commanding people to listen to the Quran and remember Him. In al-Raʿd:

“To Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and on earth, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows, morning and evening.”

And in al-Nahl:

“Have they not considered all that Allah has created, whose shadows turn to the right and the left, prostrating to Allah in humble submission?”

“To Allah prostrate all living creatures in the heavens and on earth, as do the angels, who are not too proud.”

---

## Page 11796

“They fear their Lord above them and do what they are commanded.”

And in Surah Subhān:

“Indeed, those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited to them, fall upon their faces in prostration.”

“And they say, ‘Glory be to our Lord! Surely the promise of our Lord is bound to be fulfilled.’”

“And they fall upon their faces, weeping, and it increases them in humility.”

This is an account of prostrating alongside those who heard the Quran and prostrated. Likewise, in Maryam:

“Those were the ones whom Allah blessed among the prophets, from the descendants of Adam, and of those We carried with Noah, and from the descendants of Abraham and Israel, and from those We guided and chose. When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down in prostration, weeping.”

These prophets prostrated when the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, and those who were given knowledge before the Quran prostrate when the Quran is recited to them. The apparent sense here is prostration without further qualification, like the prostration of the magicians, and as in His words:

“Enter the gate prostrating, and say, ‘Forgive us.’”

Even if what is meant here is bowing, prostration is submission and humility before Him; hence the term is used to express submission. As the poet said:

You see the hills there prostrate before the hooves.

A number of lexicographers said, “Prostration is humility and submission,” and cited the following verse:

---

## Page 11797

His nose bowed low, never raised;
His gaze downcast, his ears deaf.

Sahl ibn Abd Allāh was asked, “Does the heart prostrate?” He replied, “Yes, in a prostration from which it never raises its head.”

In Surah al-Hajj, the first prostration passage is a statement:

“Do you not see that to Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on earth, as do the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the animals, and many people? But many others have deserved punishment. Whoever Allah humiliates, none can honor. Indeed, Allah does whatever He wills.”

The second is a command coupled with bowing, which is why it became a matter of disagreement.

The prostration passage in al-Furqān:

“When they are told, ‘Prostrate to the Most Merciful,’ they say, ‘And what is the Most Merciful? Should we prostrate to whatever you command us?’ And it increases them in aversion.”

This is a statement accompanied by censure of those who are commanded to prostrate but do not; it is not praise. Likewise, the prostration passage in al-Naml:

“I found her and her people prostrating to the sun instead of Allah. Satan has made their deeds appealing to them and barred them from the way, so they are not guided.”

“So that they do not prostrate to Allah, who brings forth what is hidden in the heavens and the earth and knows what you conceal and what you reveal.”

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, Lord of the Mighty Throne.”

This is a statement implying censure of those who prostrate to other than Allah and do not prostrate to Allah. According to the reading alā yā usjudū (“Lo! Prostrate!”), however, it is a command.

In “Alif Lām Mīm Tanzīl al-Sajdah”:

“Only those believe in Our verses who, when reminded

---

## Page 11798

of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise, and are not arrogant.”

This is among the most emphatic ways of issuing a command and restricting faith to those who comply, for He denies faith to anyone who is reminded of his Lord’s verses but does not prostrate upon being reminded of them.

In “Sād,” there is an account of David’s prostration, which He calls bowing. In “Hā Mīm Tanzīl,” there is an explicit command:

“Among His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon. Do not prostrate to the sun or to the moon, but prostrate to Allah, who created them, if it is Him that you worship.”

“But if they are arrogant, those who are with your Lord glorify Him night and day, and never tire.”

In “al-Najm,” there is an explicit command:

“So prostrate to Allah and worship Him.”

In “al-Inshiqāq,” there is an explicit command to prostrate upon hearing the Quran:

“What is the matter with them that they do not believe, and when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate?”

In “Read in the name of your Lord who created,” there is an unqualified command:

“Prostrate and draw near.”

Thus, the first six passages, up to and including the first in al-Hajj, contain statements and praise. The remaining nine, beginning with the second in al-Hajj, contain commands and censure of those who do not prostrate, except for “Sād.”

We therefore say: Scholars have disagreed over whether the prostration of recitation is obligatory. Some say it is obligatory; others say it is not. Still others say it is obligatory when a prostration verse is recited during prayer; this is one position reported from Ahmad. What appears correct to me is that it is obligatory.

The verses containing praise do not, by themselves, establish an obligation. As for the verses containing commands and censure, it might be argued that those expressed without qualification refer to prayer, such as the second passage in al-Hajj and those in al-Furqān and Iqra. This interpretation is weak. How, then, can it be maintained when some of these passages link prostration to recitation, as in His words:

---

## Page 11799

“Only those believe in Our verses who, when reminded of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise, and are not arrogant.”

This denies faith in the verses to anyone who does not fall down in prostration when reminded of them. Anyone who hears them has been reminded of them. Likewise, in Surah al-Inshiqāq:

“What is the matter with them that they do not believe, and when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate?”

This censures those who do not prostrate when the Quran is recited to them, just as His words:

“What is the matter with them that they turn away from the reminder?”

“Why do you not believe in Allah when the Messenger calls you to believe in your Lord?”

“What is the matter with these people that they can hardly understand any discourse?”

Likewise, in Surah al-Najm, His words:

“Do you then marvel at this discourse, and laugh rather than weep, while you amuse yourselves heedlessly? So prostrate to Allah and worship Him.”

This is an emphatic command following mention of the discourse—that is, the Quran—which indicates that hearing it is the occasion for the command to prostrate. However, just as the prostration commanded upon hearing the Quran is not confined to prostration in prayer, neither is it confined to the prostration of recitation. Anyone who assumes either has erred. Rather, it encompasses both, as the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained. The Sunnah interprets and clarifies the Quran and guides us to its meaning.

Prostration upon hearing a prostration verse is an independent prostration performed upon hearing that verse, whether it is recited together with other passages of the Quran or on its own. It is not a prostration performed upon the recitation of any Quranic passage whatsoever. It is thus prostration in response to a passage belonging to the Quran and, specifically, to the command to prostrate; the command therefore encompasses it. It also encompasses prostration in response to the Quran more generally, and does so even more emphatically. For He, glorified and exalted be He,

---

## Page 11800

said:

“Only those believe in Our verses who, when reminded of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise, and are not arrogant.”

These words imply that none believes in His verses except one who, when reminded of them, falls down in prostration and glorifies his Lord with praise, without arrogance.

It is well known that His words “Our verses” do not mean only the verses of prostration, but the entire Quran. Thus, whenever a person is reminded of any of the Quran’s verses, he must fall down in prostration. This is the case with someone praying: he is reminded of Allah’s verses through the imam’s recitation, while the imam is reminded through his own recitation. They are therefore not believers unless they fall down in prostration. This is their prostration in prayer, performed in an established sequence: they move first into bowing, then into prostration. Prostration is performed twice, as the Messenger explained, so that it comprises two descents: a descent from standing, which is the first prostration, and a descent from sitting, which is the second.

This is among the evidence for the obligation to sit between the two prostrations and remain still while sitting, as established by the Sunnah. For falling down in prostration can only be from sitting or standing. If the separation between the two prostrations is as thin as a sword’s edge, or the posture is nearer to sitting, this does not constitute falling down in prostration. However, whoever permitted this supposed that prostration is achieved simply by placing the head on the ground, however that is done. That is not so. Rather, it is enjoined as He says:

“When reminded of them, they fall down in prostration.”

He did not say, “They prostrate.” Thus, falling down in prostration is enjoined

---

## Page 11801

as He mentions in this verse. Falling down chin-first is itself an intended act of worship, just as placing the forehead on the ground is an intended act of worship. This is indicated by His words, Most High:

“Indeed, those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited to them, fall down upon their chins in prostration,

and say, ‘Glory be to our Lord! Surely the promise of our Lord will be fulfilled.’

And they fall down upon their chins, weeping, and it increases them in humility.”

He thus commends and praises these people for falling down “upon their chins”—that is, onto their chins—in prostration; and, secondly, for falling down “upon their chins”—that is, onto them—while weeping. This makes clear that falling down chin-first is itself an intended act of worship that Allah loves. Falling down does not mean pressing the chin against the ground as the forehead is pressed against it. Rather, falling down chin-first marks the beginning of bowing, while prostration is the culmination of that descent. A person prostrates upon his forehead, not his chin, but he descends chin-first. The chin is the lower extremity of the face, its lowest part and the closest to the ground. Thus, when someone descends chin-first, his face and head descend in submission to Allah. From that moment, prostration has begun. Just as placing the forehead on the ground is the final stage of prostration, descending chin-first is its first stage. A full descent begins from standing or sitting.

It has been reported from Ibn ‘Abbās that “They fall down upon their chins” means “upon their faces.”

Al-Zajjāj said:

“A person who falls down from standing falls upon his face. The chin is where the two jawbones meet, and it is the cartilaginous part of the face. When he begins to fall, the part of his face closest to the ground is his chin.”

---

## Page 11802

Ibn al-Anbārī also said:

“The first part of someone falling down to meet the ground, before he lowers his forehead, is his chin. Hence He says, ‘upon their chins.’ The meaning may also be that they fall upon their faces, with the chin standing for the face, just as a part may stand for the whole, or a species for its genus.”

I say:

Someone who falls down chin-first does not prostrate upon his chin, for the chin is not one of the parts of the body upon which one prostrates. Rather, these are seven, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have been commanded to prostrate upon seven parts of the body: the forehead”—and he pointed with his hand to his nose—“the two hands, the two knees, and the two feet.”

Were someone to prostrate upon his chin, his forehead would be raised. Bringing both into contact with the ground is impossible or difficult, because the nose lies between them and protrudes, preventing both from touching the ground at the same time. Thus, the person prostrating descends chin-first and prostrates upon his forehead. This is the descent into prostration.

Then He says:

“And they fall down upon their chins, weeping.”

This is a descent in weeping, which may or may not be accompanied by prostration.

The first case is exemplified by His words:

“When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down in prostration and weeping.”

Here there is falling down, prostration, and weeping.

The second case is exemplified by His words:

“And they fall down upon their chins, weeping.”

A person may weep out of fear of Allah, humbling himself by falling down, even without descending as far as prostration.

---

## Page 11803

And this too is an act of worship, for it involves falling down before Allah and weeping for Him. Both are acts of worship offered to Allah. Indeed, weeping for Allah—as when someone weeps out of fear of Allah—is among the finest acts of worship. It has been reported:

“Two eyes will not be touched by the Fire: an eye that spends the night standing guard in Allah’s cause, and an eye that sheds a tear the size of a fly’s head out of fear of Allah.”

In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Seven will be shaded by Allah in His shade on the Day when there is no shade but His: a just ruler; a young man who grew up worshipping Allah; two men who love one another for Allah’s sake, meeting and parting on that basis; a man who remembers Allah in solitude and whose eyes overflow with tears; a man whose heart remains attached to the mosque from the time he leaves it until he returns; a man who gives charity so secretly that his left hand does not know what his right hand spends; and a man whom a woman of high standing and beauty invites to herself, but who says, ‘I fear Allah, Lord of all worlds.’”
