The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned these seven because each perfected the act of worship he undertook. An author has written a work describing them, entitled al-Lum‘a fī Awsāf al-Sab‘a (A Glimpse of the Qualities of the Seven).

The just ruler has fulfilled all the duties of rulership. The young man who grew up worshipping Allah has fulfilled all the duties of worshipping Allah. The man whose heart is attached to the mosques has fully maintained their worship through the five daily prayers, in accordance with His words:

“The mosques of Allah are maintained only by those who believe in Allah.”

The chaste man has perfected his fear of Allah; the giver of charity has perfected his giving for Allah’s sake; and the one who weeps has perfected his sincerity.

---

## Page 11804

As for His words concerning David, peace be upon him:

“He fell down bowing and turned in repentance.”

There is no doubt that he prostrated. The Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslims establish that he prostrated to Allah. Allah, glorified be He, praised him for falling down bowing. This falling down is the beginning of prostration. He thus mentioned the beginning of David’s action—his falling down bowing—to make clear that this is itself an intended act of worship, even though he fell down in order to prostrate. Likewise, He praised the prophets because:

“When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down in prostration and weeping.”

And He praised “those who were given knowledge before it,” saying that:

“When it is recited to them, they fall down on their chins in prostration,”

and:

“They fall down on their chins, weeping.”

This is because falling down is the beginning of the submission that is incompatible with arrogance. The arrogant person dislikes falling down and prefers always to remain erect and elevated whenever falling down entails abasement, humility, and reverent submission. This is why arrogant people, Arabs and non-Arabs alike, disdain it. If one of them dropped something, he would not pick it up, lest he have to lower himself and bend down.

Falling down means lowering the face and head, the highest and noblest part of a person. A person was created upright and erect; when he lowers his head, especially in prostration, this expresses the utmost abasement. For this reason, prostration is fitting only for Allah. Whoever prostrates to anyone other than Him is a polytheist, and whoever does not prostrate to Him is too arrogant to worship Him. Both are disbelievers, among the people of the Fire.

Allah Most High said:

“Your Lord has said, ‘Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who are too arrogant to worship Me will enter Hell in humiliation.’”

And He said:

---

## Page 11805

Most High:

“Among His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon. Do not prostrate to the sun or to the moon, but prostrate to Allah, who created them, if it is Him alone that you worship.”

And He said in the account of Bilqīs:

“I found her and her people prostrating to the sun instead of Allah. Satan has made their deeds seem attractive to them and has barred them from the way, so they are not guided, and do not prostrate to Allah, who brings forth what is hidden in the heavens and the earth and knows what you conceal and what you reveal. Allah—there is no deity except Him, Lord of the Mighty Throne.”

The sun is the greatest thing seen in the visible realm, and the most far-reaching in its benefits and effects. Thus, the prohibition against prostrating to it applies all the more to lesser things, such as stars, trees, and the like.

His words:

“But prostrate to Allah, who created them,”

indicate that prostration is for the Creator, not for a created being, however great its standing; rather, it is for the One who created it. This applies to anyone who intends to worship Him alone, as He said:

“If it is Him alone that you worship.”

It is not fitting for such a person to prostrate to these created things. Allah Most High said:

“But if they are arrogant, those who are with your Lord glorify Him night and day, and never grow weary.”

For He, glorified be He, knew that among the children of Adam there are those who are too arrogant to prostrate to Him. He therefore said that those greater than these people are not too arrogant to worship their Lord. Rather, they glorify Him night and day without weariness or boredom, unlike human beings.

Here He described them as glorifying Him, while He described them as both glorifying Him and prostrating in His words:

“Indeed, those who are with your Lord are not too arrogant to worship Him. They glorify Him, and to Him they prostrate.”

---

## Page 11806

and they stand before Him in rows, as they themselves say:

“Indeed, we are those who stand in rows, and indeed, we are those who glorify Him.”

It is reported in the Sahīh that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Will you not form rows as the angels form rows before their Lord?”

They asked, “How do the angels form rows before their Lord?”

He replied, “They fill the foremost rows in order and stand close together within each row.”

Section

His verses, glorified be He, require two things. The first is to understand them and reflect upon them, so as to know what they contain. The second is to worship Him and humble oneself before Him upon hearing them. Reciting them and listening to them require both.

If someone hears them without understanding them, he is blameworthy; and if he understands them without acting upon their contents, he is blameworthy. Rather, everyone who hears them must understand them and act upon them, just as everyone must listen to them. Whoever turns away from listening to them is a disbeliever. Whoever does not understand what he is commanded to do in them is a disbeliever. Whoever knows what he is commanded to do but neither acknowledges its obligation nor carries it out is a disbeliever. He, glorified be He, censures the disbelievers for both.

This is exemplified by His words:

“What is the matter with them that they turn away from the Reminder, like startled wild donkeys fleeing from a lion?”

And His words:

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘Do not listen to this Quran, and drown it out with noise, so that you may prevail.’”

And His words:

“A Book

---

## Page 11807

whose verses are explained in detail—an Arabic Quran for people who know, bringing good news and warning. Yet most of them turn away, so they do not listen.”

There are many similar passages. Concerning those who neither understand His verses nor reflect upon them, He says:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear. But even if He had made them hear, they would have turned away in aversion.”

Thus He censures them for not understanding, and because, even if they understood, they would not act upon what they knew. The Most High says:

“Do not be like those who say, ‘We hear,’ but do not listen. Indeed, the worst of living creatures in Allah’s sight are the deaf and dumb who do not reason. Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear.”

And He says:

“And those who, when reminded of their Lord’s verses, do not fall upon them deaf and blind.”

Ibn Qutayba said: “They did not disregard them, as though they were deaf and had not heard them, [or blind and] had not seen them.”

Another scholar of Arabic said: “They did not remain in their former state, as though they had neither heard nor seen, even though they had not actually fallen down. The Arabs say, ‘I insulted so-and-so, and he stood weeping, sat lamenting, turned to apologize, and went on boasting,’ even though he neither stood nor sat.”

I say: There is wisdom in His use, glorified be He, of the expression ‘falling down’ rather than another expression. For if they fell down while deaf and blind, that would not be praiseworthy, but blameworthy. How much more so if they were deaf and blind without falling down!

Two things, then, are essential: one must fall down in prostration, and one must hear and see the light and guidance contained in His verses

---

## Page 11808

and clarification. Similarly, when prayer was prescribed, recitation while standing was prescribed within it, followed by bowing and prostration.

The first words Allah revealed of the Quran were:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”

He thus opened the surah with the command to recite and concluded it with the command to prostrate, saying:

“Prostrate and draw near.”

His words, Most High,

“Only those believe in Our verses who, when reminded of them, fall down in prostration and glorify their Lord with praise,”

indicate that being reminded of them—as through their recitation in prayer—makes prostration and glorification obligatory. Whoever does not fall down in prostration and glorify his Lord with praise when reminded of them is not a believer. This includes verses that do not prescribe prostration, which constitute the great majority of the Quran’s verses. The Quran contains more than six thousand verses, whereas the verses of prostration number only a little over ten.

His words, “when reminded of them,” encompass all the verses. Thus, being reminded of these verses—all of them—makes glorification and prostration obligatory. This is among the evidence cited for the obligation of glorification and prostration. The general body of the Sharia’s evidence from the Quran and Sunnah likewise indicates this: glorifying Allah in some form is obligatory. Whoever does not glorify Allah during prostration has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger. If he uses any prescribed form of glorification, that suffices.

The jurists have three views on this issue. One view holds that no remembrance is obligatory at all.

---

## Page 11809

And another view holds that glorification is obligatory and that one must specifically say, “Glory be to my Lord, the Most High”; nothing else suffices. A third view holds that glorification in some form is obligatory, although this particular form is superior to others, because it was commanded that it be used in prostration. Other forms have also been authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

His statement, “Use it in your prostration,” calls for a discussion that does not belong here. For it may be said that whoever glorifies his Lord, by whichever name he glorifies Him, has glorified the name of his Lord, the Most High. Likewise, by whichever name he invokes Him, he has invoked his Lord, to whom belong the most beautiful names. As He says:

“Say: Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful. By whichever name you call upon Him, to Him belong the most beautiful names.”

And He says:

“To Allah belong the most beautiful names, so call upon Him by them.”

If He may be invoked by all His most beautiful names, and whoever invokes Him by any name has invoked the One to whom belong the most beautiful names, then He is likewise glorified by all His most beautiful names, and whoever glorifies Him by any name has glorified the One to whom belong the most beautiful names. Some names, however, may be more excellent than others. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the command to prostrate follows upon the recitation of the Quran in its entirety, as in this verse and in His words, Most High:

“What is the matter with them that they do not believe?”

“And when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate?”

This encompasses the entire Quran: anyone to whom the Quran is recited is commanded to prostrate. The person praying has had the Quran recited to him, and that is grounds for the command to prostrate. Thus he listens to the Quran and prostrates. The imam and the person praying alone hear their own recitation, thereby reciting the Quran to themselves. And it may

---

## Page 11810

be said: “They do not pray.” But His words, “They fall down in prostration,” explicitly refer to prostration in its familiar sense, because it is coupled with the expression “fall down.”

As for this verse, its interpretation is disputed. Abū al-Faraj said:

“Concerning ‘And when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate,’ there are two views. The first is that they do not pray. This was stated by 'Atā' ibn al-Sā'ib. The second is that they neither submit to it nor humble themselves before it. This was stated by Ibn Jarīr and preferred by Judge Abū Ya'lā.

“He said: ‘Some have cited this verse as evidence that the prostration of recitation is obligatory, but it does not establish that. Rather, it means that they do not humble themselves. Do you not see that He connected prostration with the entire Quran, whereas prostration is prescribed only at particular passages within it?’”

I say: The first view is the one mentioned by many Quran commentators, who mention no other, such as al-Tha'labī and al-Baghawī. They also related it from Muqātil and al-Kalbī. It is the view transmitted from the Quran commentators among the early generations, and it is held by the general body of scholars.

As for the second view, I know of no one who has transmitted it from any of the early generations. Those who expressed it did so only because they considered that not everyone who hears something of the Quran is obliged to prostrate. They therefore sought to interpret the verse as referring to something obligatory in every circumstance. Thus they said: “They submit and humble themselves.” For this is enjoined

---

## Page 11811

upon everyone to whom the Quran is recited. The word “prostration” is used to mean submission and humility in general, as has been explained at length elsewhere. But they may be answered as follows:

Submission is commanded, and a person’s submission and humility are not complete without prostration in its familiar sense. This is, in general, obligatory upon everyone, and it is what is meant whenever prostration is ascribed to the children of Adam in the Quran. It is the human being’s submission to the Lord, and the Lord accepts nothing less than this submission from people, for it is the utmost expression of a servant’s submission. Every creature has a form of submission suited to it, and that is its prostration.

As for claiming that human prostration means only a submission that does not involve placing the face on the ground, such a usage is unknown. Rather, it should be said: They are commanded to prostrate when the Quran is recited to them. Even if full prostration does not immediately follow their hearing the Quran, this necessarily takes place between two prayers. When they undertake the prayer, they perform the prostration required of them. When the Quran was recited to them, they had already attained a measure of submission and humility by believing it to be obligatory and resolving to comply. Thus, when they believe that prayer is obligatory and resolve to comply, this is the beginning of the prostration they are commanded to perform; then, when they pray, they complete it.

Similarly, He said concerning the polytheists:

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakat, then let them go their way.”

Thus, when they repent and commit themselves to prayer, fighting against them is to cease. This is the beginning of their establishing it; then, when they perform it, they have completed its establishment. But if they commit themselves to it

---

## Page 11812

in words but do not perform it, then they are to be fought.

Further evidence for this is the report established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated upon reciting this surah during prayer. Both collections relate that Abū Rāfiʿ said:

“I performed the ʿIshā prayer with Abū Hurayrah. He recited ‘When the sky splits open’ and prostrated. I asked, ‘What is this?’ He replied, ‘I prostrated upon reciting it behind Abū al-Qāsim, and I shall continue to prostrate upon reciting it until I meet him.’”

The scholars agree that this hadith is authentic. As for his prostration during this surah, it was reported by Muslim, but not by al-Bukhārī. Prostrating upon its recitation is the position of the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others. It is also the position of Ibn Wahb and others among Mālik’s followers.

How, then, can it be claimed that the word “prostration” in this surah means only submission and humility in general, and that its wording does not indicate prostration in its familiar sense? Were this correct, that specific act of prostration would not be prescribed when the surah is recited, especially during prayer. This also makes clear the answer to those who counter the use of this verse as evidence that prostration upon recitation is obligatory by saying that what is meant is submission.

If it is said, “If prostration is interpreted as prayer, as most scholars have said, then prostration upon recitation is not obligatory,” the answer is: Prayer is intended in connection with Quran recitation in general, as explained above. This verse obliges anyone to whom the Quran is recited to prostrate.

---

## Page 11813

so if it is recited to him outside prayer, he must prostrate soon afterward, when the time for prayer comes. For there is no hour in which the Quran is recited to him that is not a time for an obligatory prayer which he must perform, since less than half a day separates him from the time of an obligatory prayer. If he does not pray, he is among those who do not prostrate when the Quran is recited to them. If the Quran is recited to him during prayer, he must perform one prostration in which he goes down from standing and another in which he goes down from sitting, both following bowing, as the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained.

As for prostration upon reciting this verse, it is the specific prostration known as prostration upon recitation. This is a prostration to be performed promptly upon hearing this verse, for it commands him to prostrate when the Quran is recited to him. Responding with the utmost promptness therefore entails performing the prostration of recitation upon hearing it, then prostrating when other verses are recited, as explained above.

This verse commands prostration when either it or another verse is recited to him. It is this verse that commands prostration upon the recitation of the Quran, unlike the other verses upon whose recitation no prostration is performed. It thus contains a particular exhortation to prostrate, besides its general status as part of the Quran. A prostration is therefore prescribed specifically upon its recitation, and one also prostrates in prayer when it is recited, just as one prostrates when other verses are recited.

This is how the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained it, for he prostrated upon its recitation during prayer. When his action implements a command or explains an unelaborated text, it carries the same ruling as that command or text. This therefore indicates

---

## Page 11814

that the prostration he performed when reciting this surah is obligatory, especially since he was in prayer. Prayer is obligatory, as is completing it, so its course may only be interrupted by an act better than completing it. It follows that performing the prostration of recitation during prayer is better than completing the prayer without it. If someone deliberately added to the prayer an act of the same kind as its prescribed acts, his prayer would be invalid. Here, however, the prostration of recitation is prescribed within it.

Two positions are reported from Ahmad concerning whether this prostration is obligatory during prayer. The stronger view is that it is obligatory, as we explained earlier, for several reasons. Among them is that the imams themselves are commanded to pray as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed, and this is how he prayed. And Allah knows best.

His words,

“do not prostrate,”

rather than “do not pray,” indicate that prostration is intended for its own sake and that this includes prostration both within and outside prayer. It also encompasses submission and humility, as illustrated. The Quran therefore requires prostration in the full scope of the term, encompassing all its forms: there is no form of prostration that the Quran does not require. Whoever does not prostrate at all when it is recited to him is a disbeliever. However, not every form of prostration is obligatory at every time; rather, this is governed by what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained. Nevertheless, the verse indicates that prostration is to be repeated whenever the Quran is recited to him.

---

## Page 11815

and this is obligatory when the Quran is recited to him, both within and outside prayer, as explained above. And Allah knows best.

As for the unqualified command to prostrate, it undoubtedly encompasses the five daily prayers, which are obligatory by consensus. It also encompasses the prostration of recitation, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed prostration at these passages. What is recited must therefore be the reason for the prostration; otherwise, the prostration would be unrelated to it. What these passages contain is precisely a command, indicating that this prostration is among the forms of prostration commanded. Otherwise, how could the very prostration associated with the command fall outside its scope? This is like the angels’ prostration to Adam when they were commanded to prostrate. Thus, the authentic hadith states:

“When the son of Adam recites a verse of prostration, Satan withdraws, weeping and saying: ‘Woe to him! The son of Adam was commanded to prostrate, and he prostrated, so Paradise is his. I was commanded to prostrate, but I refused, so the Fire is mine.’”

Narrated by Muslim. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned this to encourage this prostration. It therefore indicates that this prostration is commanded, just as prostration to Adam was: both involve a command, and prostration has been prescribed in response to it. Whoever prostrates follows the example of the angels, while whoever refuses follows the example of Iblīs. Indeed, this is prostration to Allah, so it is greater than prostration to Adam.

This hadith alone suffices to establish that it is obligatory, as do the verses containing the qualified command and those containing the unqualified command.

---

## Page 11816

Moreover, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited Sūrat al-Najm, he prostrated, and the Muslims and polytheists, jinn and humans, prostrated with him, as established in the Sahīh in a report from Ibn ‘Abbās. The Sahīh also contains a report from Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“They all prostrated except one of the polytheists, who took a handful of pebbles and said, ‘This is enough for me.’” He added, “I later saw him slain as a disbeliever.”

This indicates that they had been commanded to perform this prostration and that whoever omitted it was blameworthy. It was not the prostration performed in prayer; rather, it was an act of submission to Allah. Among them were disbelievers and people who had not performed ablution. Rather, this was a prostration of submission upon the recitation of His words.

Likewise, Allah praised those who prostrate upon hearing His words, saying:

“When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down prostrating and weeping.”

And He said:

“Indeed, those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited to them, fall upon their chins in prostration. And they say, ‘Glory be to our Lord! Surely the promise of our Lord has been fulfilled.’ And they fall upon their chins, weeping, and it increases them in humility.”

Even if this is said to encompass prostration within prayer, since they bow and prostrate upon hearing the Quran, it undoubtedly encompasses the prostration of recitation all the more. In prayer, prostration is only one part of the prayer, whereas here He mentions a prostration on the chins by itself. It can therefore no longer be interpreted as bowing, since bowing is not performed upon the chins.

His expression li-l-adhqān (“to the chins”) means “upon the chins,” just as He says, “and laid him down on his forehead,” meaning “upon his forehead.” His expression “upon the chins” indicates the completeness of their prostration

---

## Page 11817

and that they prostrated on their noses as well as their foreheads, until their chins touched the ground, unlike those who prostrate only on their foreheads. Even someone prostrating on his nose may not bring his chin into contact with the ground unless he lowers himself further.

Those who do not consider it obligatory cite the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prostrate when Zayd recited Sūrat al-Najm to him. They also cite the following account concerning Umar:

“When he recited Sūrat al-Nahl from the pulpit and reached the verse of prostration, he descended and prostrated, and the people prostrated. The following Friday, he recited it again until he reached the verse of prostration. He said, ‘O people, we come to passages of prostration: whoever prostrates has done right, and whoever does not prostrate incurs no sin.’ In another wording: On the second Friday, they craned their necks in anticipation, so he said, ‘We come to the verse of prostration, but it has not been made obligatory upon us. You have, however, shown your eagerness.’ He then descended and prostrated.”

The response is that the incident involving Zayd was a particular case. Perhaps the Prophet did not prostrate because Zayd did not prostrate, just as Ibn Mas‘ūd said, “You are our leader: if you prostrate, we will prostrate.”

Uthmān said, “Prostration is required only of someone who sits down to listen to the recitation and listens attentively.” This indicates that it is obligatory for someone listening attentively, but not for someone who merely hears it. Likewise, the report from Ibn Mas‘ūd indicates that it is not obligatory if the reciter does not prostrate.

It may also be said, according to those who hold that prostration during this surah is prescribed, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had an excuse. Some therefore say, “It is possible that he was not in a state of

---

## Page 11818

ritual purity.” However, the view that prostration is permissible without ritual purity may be stronger.

It has also been said that prostration in al-Najm alone was abrogated, unlike in Iqraʾ and al-Inshiqāq, for an authentic report establishes “that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prostrated during both, and Abū Hurayrah prostrated with him.” Abū Hurayrah embraced Islam after Khaybar. This refutes the claim that he did not prostrate during any of the Mufassal surahs after the Hijrah. As for al-Najm, however, Zayd’s hadith explicitly states that he did not prostrate during it.

Those who hold this view say that the abrogation therefore applies specifically to this surah, not to others. Since Satan had made his interjection, when some supposed that the Prophet had agreed with them, the Prophet abandoned prostration during it altogether to close off this avenue. In prayer, it comes at the end of the standing portion, and the prostration of the prayer suffices in its place. This view is therefore more likely to be correct than the alternatives. And Allah knows best.

As for the report concerning Umar, even if it were explicit, it would amount only to his own statement and the assent of those present, who were not all the Muslims. The statement of Uthmān and others indicates that it is obligatory.

Furthermore, it may be said that Umar perhaps meant that prostration had not been prescribed for us in this particular situation: when the imam recites the passage from the pulpit. This is clarified by the fact that prostration in this situation

---

## Page 11819

is not like prostration in general, because it requires the imam to interrupt the sermon and make many movements. The Sunnah is to deliver the sermon without interruption. When these two considerations conflict, prostration is no longer obligatory, because the reciter is engaged in a superior act of worship: delivering the sermon to the people. If he does prostrate, however, that is permissible.

For this reason, Mālik and others say that this prostration is not recommended. He said: “It is not our practice for the imam to prostrate when he recites from the pulpit.” Likewise, he did not consider prostration recommended in prayer, whether the recitation is silent or audible.

Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, Abū Hanīfah, and others say that it is not recommended in prayers with silent recitation. Yet Abū Hanīfah considers prostration obligatory, and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, considers it obligatory within prayer. Nevertheless, they did not recommend it in this situation; rather, they regarded maintaining the continuity of the prayer as preferable. Similarly, Umar may have meant that it was not prescribed in a situation such as this, just as some also say that it is not recommended in this situation.

This is comparable to supplication at Arafat: since the Sunnah is to continue it without interruption, it was not interrupted for the afternoon prayer; rather, that prayer was performed beforehand. Likewise, the preacher’s purpose on Friday is to address the people, enjoin them to do good and forbid them from wrongdoing, and then lead the prayer immediately afterwards. They are therefore not required to turn away from this purpose, especially since prostration will follow it. This indicates that prostration of recitation may be set aside for something superior to it. Do you not

---

## Page 11820

see the case of someone reciting privately on Friday? It may be said that it is not recommended for him to prostrate apart from the congregation, just as someone praying behind an imam is not prescribed to prostrate for his own forgetfulness, because following the imam takes precedence over prostration. Even if he is far away and we hold that it is recommended for him to recite, his case is comparable to that of someone for whom reciting behind his imam is recommended. Were he to recite a verse of prostration, he would not prostrate independently of the imam. I know of no disagreement concerning this.

Here, maintaining conformity with the imam in outward actions is better than prostrating for recitation or forgetfulness; indeed, he is forbidden to do so independently. On Friday, the people prostrated only because Umar prostrated. Had he not prostrated, they would not have prostrated then. Thus, if Umar’s report may mean that prostration was not made obligatory upon us in this situation, it no longer constitutes evidence, even if it were traced back to the Prophet.

Moreover, prostration upon reciting the Quran is one of Islam’s manifest rites. When the Quran is recited in the congregational mosque, all the people prostrate to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Omitting this detracts from that rite. For this reason, we consider the stronger view to be that the Eid prayer is an individual obligation, as held by Abū Hanīfah and others. This is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s views and one of the two positions in Ahmad’s school.

The view that it is not obligatory is exceedingly implausible, for it is among Islam’s greatest rites. People assemble for it in greater numbers than for the Friday prayer, and proclaiming Allah’s greatness is prescribed during it. The view that it is a collective obligation lacks a clear criterion, for if it were attended in

---

## Page 11821

a large city by forty men, its purpose would not be fulfilled. That purpose is achieved only when all the Muslims attend, as with the Friday prayer.

As for the Eid sacrifice, the stronger view is that it too is obligatory, for it is among Islam’s greatest rites. It is the sacrificial rite observed throughout all towns and cities, and sacrifice is coupled with prayer in His words:

“Indeed, my prayer, my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

The Most High also says:

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice.”

Thus, He commanded sacrifice just as He commanded prayer. The Most High also says:

“For every community We have appointed a sacrificial rite, so that they may mention Allah’s name over the livestock He has provided for them. Your God is One God, so submit to Him, and give good news to those who humbly submit.”

And He says:

“We have made the sacrificial camels among Allah’s sacred rites for you; in them there is good for you. So mention Allah’s name over them as they stand in rows. Then, when they have fallen onto their sides, eat from them and feed the needy, whether they ask or not. Thus We have subjected them to you, so that you may be grateful.”

“Neither their meat nor their blood reaches Allah, but your piety reaches Him. Thus He has subjected them to you, so that you may glorify Allah for having guided you. And give good news to those who do good.”

This sacrifice is part of the religion of Abraham, whose religion we have been commanded to follow, and it commemorates the story of the son offered in sacrifice. How, then, could it be permissible for all Muslims to abandon it, with not a single one of them performing it? For all Muslims to abandon this would be more serious than abandoning Hajj in some years. They have stated that holding Hajj every year is a collective obligation because it is among Islam’s manifest rites. The sacrifices offered on Eid al-Adhā are likewise. Indeed, these sacrifices are offered in every town

---

## Page 11822

alongside the prayer. This makes the worship and remembrance of Allah, and the slaughter and sacrifice offered to Him, manifest in a way that Hajj does not, just as the remembrance of Allah is made manifest by proclaiming His greatness during the festivals. Hadiths have also commanded this sacrifice. Its obligation has been derived as a position within Ahmad’s school; it is also the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two positions in Mālik’s school—or the apparent position of his school.

Those who deny its obligation have no explicit text supporting them. Their principal evidence is the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever intends to offer a sacrifice, when the ten days begin, must not remove any of his hair or nails.”

They say: “An obligation is not made conditional upon a person’s intention.”

This statement requires clarification. An obligation is not left to a person’s choice, such that he is told, “Do it if you wish.” Rather, an obligation may be expressed conditionally to clarify a particular ruling, as in His words:

“When you rise for prayer, wash…”

They have understood the implicit meaning here to be, “When you intend to rise.” They have likewise understood the other command to mean, “When you intend to recite, seek refuge.” Yet purification is obligatory, and recitation in prayer is obligatory. He has also said:

“It is only a reminder to all worlds,”

“for whoever among you wills to take the straight path.”

Yet willing to take the straight path is obligatory.

Furthermore, not everyone is obliged to offer a sacrifice. It is obligatory only for someone who has the means, and it is such a person who intends to offer it. Similarly, he said:

“Whoever intends to perform Hajj should hasten to do so, for an animal may stray and a need may arise.”

Yet Hajj is obligatory for whoever is able to undertake it. Thus, his words, “Whoever intends to offer a sacrifice,” are like his words, “Whoever intends to perform Hajj

---

## Page 11823

should hasten to do so.” Its obligation is then conditional upon having the means to offer it beyond one’s basic needs, as with the obligatory charity at the end of Ramadan. A single sheep may be sacrificed on behalf of a household—the head of the household, his wives and children, and those living with them—as the Companions used to do.

As for reports that some Companions did not offer a sacrifice but bought meat instead, this may have been a matter of disagreement, just as they disagreed over whether ʿumrah was obligatory. It may also be that someone who did not offer a sacrifice lacked sufficient means that year and intended thereby to rebuke those who ostentatiously offered sacrifices for other than Allah. Alternatively, he may have refrained from offering it that year in order to rebuke them, thus omitting an obligation for an overriding benefit. Similarly, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“I considered ordering that the prayer be established, then going with men carrying bundles of firewood to people who do not attend the prayer and burning their houses down upon them, were it not for the women and children in those houses.”

He would thus have forgone the obligatory Friday and congregational prayers in order to punish those who stayed away. This falls under jihad, for which the time available may be short, so it takes precedence over Friday and congregational prayers. If an official in authority, such as a public inspector or another official, were to miss Friday prayer on some occasions to identify and punish those who do not attend it, that would be permissible. This would constitute a valid excuse for missing Friday prayer, since punishing those people is a duty specifically incumbent upon him that can only be carried out in this way. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) made it clear that, were it not for the women and children

---

## Page 11824

he would have burned down the houses upon their occupants. But those houses contained women and children, for whom neither Friday prayer nor congregational prayer is obligatory, so punishing them is impermissible. Likewise, a pregnant woman is not stoned until she gives birth, because killing the unborn child is impermissible, as in the hadith of the woman of Ghāmid.

Section

No formal opening or closing is prescribed for prostration upon Quran recitation.

This is the established Sunnah of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), the practice of the majority of the early generations, and the position explicitly transmitted from the renowned imams. Accordingly, this prostration is not a prayer, so the conditions required for prayer do not apply to it. Rather, it may be performed without ritual purity, as Ibn ʿUmar used to do. Nevertheless, performing it while fulfilling the conditions required for prayer is preferable, and one should not neglect those conditions without an excuse. Prostrating without ritual purity is better than omitting the prostration.

However, it may be argued that prostration is not obligatory in this state, just as it is not obligatory for someone who hears the recitation, or for someone whose reciter does not prostrate, although the majority of scholars permit prostration in the latter case. Similarly, there is unanimous agreement that someone following an imam in prayer must prostrate in accordance with his imam, even among those who say that prostration is not obligatory otherwise. Some have interpreted Zayd’s hadith as indicating that

---

## Page 11825

the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) was not in a state of ritual purity. Similarly, Friday prayer is not obligatory for the sick, the traveller or the slave, although they may perform it. This is especially pertinent since most scholars permit this prostration only with ritual purity. The stronger view, however, is that it may be performed without ritual purity, on account of the hadith. What is reported from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) concerning it is a single takbīr (“Allah is Most Great”), since one is not moving from one act of worship to another.

Al-Bukhārī accordingly entitled a chapter:

(Chapter: Muslims Prostrating Alongside Polytheists, Although a Polytheist Is Impure and Is Without Ablution)

He said: “Ibn ʿUmar used to prostrate without ablution.” He also mentioned “the Prophet’s prostration upon reciting Sūrat al-Najm: when he prostrated, the Muslims and the polytheists prostrated with him.”

This hadith appears in the two Sahīh collections through two routes: the hadith of Ibn Masʿūd and that of Ibn ʿAbbās. They did this in following the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) when he recited His words:

“So prostrate to Allah and worship Him.”

It is well known that ritual purity is not a condition for worship in general; rather, it is required for prayer. Likewise, among the various forms of prostration, ritual purity is required for some—namely, prostration offered to Allah, such as prostration in prayer and the two prostrations for forgetfulness—unlike prostration upon Quran recitation, prostration of gratitude, and prostration on witnessing divine signs.

Further evidence for this is that Allah described the sorcerers’ prostration when they believed in Moses in a manner expressing approval of that prostration. Undoubtedly, they had neither performed ablution nor known what ablution was. It is thus clear that prostration to Allah on its own is among the things loved by

---

## Page 11826

Allah and approved by Him, even if the person performing it has not performed ablution. The religious law of those before us is binding upon us unless our law provides otherwise. This is a prostration of faith. A comparable instance is that of

“those who embraced Islam and sought protection by prostrating. Khālid did not accept this from them and killed them. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) then sent ʿAlī, who paid half the blood money for them. He did not disapprove of their prostration.”

They had not yet embraced Islam or learned about ablution; rather, they prostrated to Allah as an act of entering Islam, just as the sorcerers had prostrated.

Further evidence for this is that Allah commanded the Children of Israel to enter the gate prostrating and to say, “Forgive our sins.” It is well known that He did not command them to perform ablution, nor was ablution prescribed for them; rather, it is exclusive to the community of Muhammad. This applies whether what is meant is prostration on the ground or bowing.

If bowing is meant, then it is an independent act of worship involving humble submission to Allah and is of the same nature as prostration. Our religious law, however, prescribes prostration as an independent act. As for bowing on its own, there is disagreement about it: some scholars have permitted it in place of prostration upon Quran recitation.

Moreover, Allah has described the prophets as prostrating independently of prayer, as in His words:

“Those were the ones upon whom Allah bestowed favour among the prophets, from the descendants of Adam and of those We carried with Noah, and from the descendants of Abraham and Israel, and from those We guided and chose. When the verses of the Most Merciful were recited to them, they fell down prostrate and weeping.”

They had not been commanded to perform ablution, for ablution is exclusive to the community of Muhammad, as stated in the authentic hadiths: “They

---

## Page 11827

will be raised on the Day of Resurrection with radiant faces, hands and feet from the traces of ablution, and the Messenger will recognize them by this distinguishing mark.” This indicates that no others share this distinction with them.

The report narrated by Ibn Mājah and others—that he performed ablution, washing each part once, twice and three times, and said:

“This is my ablution and the ablution of the prophets before me”—

is weak according to hadith scholars. Such a report cannot be used as evidence. Nor do the People of the Book possess any account of a prophet performing ablution as Muslims do. Bathing to remove major ritual impurity, however, was prescribed for them, but they had no dry ablution when water was unavailable. Among the distinctions granted to this community are dry ablution for both major and minor ritual impurity, and ablution with water.

If it is said, “Those prophets prostrated without ablution only because they were permitted to pray without ablution,” the reply is:

Allah has not related to us in the Quran that any of them prayed without ablution. We follow only those aspects of the prophets’ religious laws that Allah has related to us and that our Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) has told us about. He related these things to us so that we might learn from them. He said:

“Those are the ones whom Allah has guided, so follow their guidance.”

Likewise, He mentioned that those who were given knowledge before it:

“When it is recited to them, they fall down on their faces in prostration,”

“And say, ‘Glory be to our Lord! Surely the promise of our Lord is bound to be fulfilled.’”

“And they fall down on their faces, weeping, and it increases them in humility.”

---

## Page 11828

And Allah Most High has made ritual purification obligatory for prayer, as He commanded in the Quran and as is established from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“Allah does not accept the prayer of any of you who has invalidated his ablution until he performs ablution again.”

This was recorded in both Sahīh collections. Another authentic report relates that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Allah does not accept prayer without purification, nor charity from misappropriated wealth.”

The Muslims have unanimously agreed that ritual purification is obligatory for prayer. What remains to be discussed is what constitutes “prayer.” Those who require ritual purification for a prostration performed on its own have differed among themselves. They have held that it should conclude with the salutation of peace. Some have said that one should pronounce “Allah is Greatest” twice: once to commence it and once for the prostration. Others have said that one should recite the tashahhud (testimony of faith) during it. Yet they have no report from either the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) or any of the Companions supporting any of these views. Rather, these are opinions they advanced because they considered it a prayer.

One of them said: “Prayer must consist of at least two units; anything less does not constitute prayer, apart from the single unit of witr.” He cited the report in the Sunan collections from Ibn ʿUmar that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Prayer by night and by day is offered two units at a time.”

This was the view of Ibn Hazm. He did not stipulate ritual purification for anything short of that, whether the funeral prayer or anything else. This view, too, is weak, for the hadith is weak. The wording found in the authentic collections and transmitted by reliable narrators is:

“Night prayer is offered two units at a time.”

As for the words “and by day,” this is an addition transmitted only by al-Bāriqī, and it was judged weak by Ahmad

---

## Page 11829

and others. The Messenger is the authority on what constitutes prayer. The Sunan collections contain the hadith of Alī, who reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The key to prayer is purification; one enters its sacred state by declaring ‘Allah is Greatest’ and leaves it by the salutation of peace.”

This is also reliably preserved as a statement of Ibn Masūd himself. It thus clarifies that “prayer” is that whose key is purification, whose sacred state begins with declaring “Allah is Greatest” and ends with the salutation of peace. This encompasses everything that begins and ends in this manner, including prayer involving bowing and prostration, whether offered two units at a time, as a single unit, as three consecutive units, or as more than that.

It also encompasses the funeral prayer, since its sacred state begins with declaring “Allah is Greatest” and ends with the salutation of peace. The Companions were instructed to observe ritual purification when they distinguished between it and the prostration of recitation. This is what al-Bukhārī mentioned in his Sahīh. In the chapter entitled “The prescribed practice in the funeral prayer,” he said:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘Whoever offers the funeral prayer,’ and said, ‘Offer the funeral prayer for your companion,’ and said, ‘Offer the funeral prayer for al-Najāshī.’ He called it a prayer, although it contains neither bowing nor prostration. One does not converse during it, and it includes declaring ‘Allah is Greatest’ and the salutation of peace. Ibn Umar would offer it only in a state of ritual purity. He would not offer it at sunrise or sunset, and he would raise his hands.

“Allah Most High said:

“‘And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave.’

“It also involves rows and an imam.”

All these features that he mentioned are absent from the prostrations of recitation and gratitude

---

## Page 11830

and prostration in response to signs. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not call this prayer, nor was forming rows and having an imam stand ahead of the congregation prescribed for it, as is prescribed for the funeral prayer, the two prostrations for forgetfulness performed after the salutation of peace, and all other prayers. Nor did the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prescribe a salutation of peace for it. No such practice has been reported from him through either an authentic or a weak chain of transmission; rather, it is an innovation. Nor did he prescribe an opening declaration of “Allah is Greatest” for it. It has only been reported that he declared “Allah is Greatest” during it, either when rising or when lowering himself. This hadith is in the Sunan collections.

Ibn Abbās permitted dry ablution for the funeral prayer when water was unavailable. This is the view of many scholars, and it is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. This indicates that Ibn Abbās considered ritual purification a condition for it. Likewise, these features are absent from circumambulation: it has no salutation of peace, speaking during it is permissible, and it involves neither rows nor an imam. Allah has mentioned those who circumambulate alongside those who pray, both in His Book and in the Sunnah of His Messenger. It has not been reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded ritual purification for circumambulation. However, he and the Companions would circumambulate in a state of ritual purity, and they would offer the two units of prayer after circumambulation. Prayer is offered only in a state of ritual purity. The prohibition reported concerns only circumambulation by a menstruating woman, for he said:

“A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites except circumambulation of the House.”

Some have said that this is on account of the mosque; others, on account of circumambulation; and others, on account of both. Allah Most High said to Abraham (peace be upon him):

“And purify My House for those who circumambulate it.”

This therefore entails purifying it of menstrual blood and other impurities.

---

## Page 11831

Moreover, Abraham and the prophets after him used to circumambulate without ablution, just as they prayed without ablution. Their law is our law except where it has been abrogated. We have been commanded to perform ablution for prayer, but ablution has not been made obligatory for us for anything else. Likewise, the earth has been made a place of prayer and a means of purification for us: wherever a Muslim is when prayer falls due, he has his place of prayer and his means of purification. Even if he is in a state of major ritual impurity, he may perform dry ablution and pray.

Those before us did not have this concession. Rather, they were prohibited from praying in a state of major ritual impurity until they bathed, just as someone in that state is prohibited from remaining in the mosque and reciting the Quran. Someone in a state of minor ritual impurity, however, may remain in the mosque, whether observing a devotional retreat or not, and may recite the Quran.

What is reported from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) concerning this prostration is a single declaration of “Allah is Greatest,” for he was not moving from one act of worship to another.

---

## Page 11832

Shaykh al-Islām (may Allah have mercy on him) was also asked:

A man is reciting the Noble Book in a gathering. Upon reciting a verse of prostration, he stands up and then prostrates. Is standing up before prostrating better than prostrating from a seated position, or not? Does his doing so constitute showing off and hypocrisy?

He replied:

Indeed, performing the prostration of recitation from a standing position is better than doing so from a seated position. This has been stated by scholars among the followers of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, and others, and has also been reported from 'Ā'ishah. The same applies to the prostration of gratitude, as Abū Dāwūd reported in his Sunan that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prostrated in gratitude from a standing position.

This is also clear upon reflection, for praying standing is better than praying sitting. It is authentically established concerning the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Sometimes he would pray sitting, but when he was about to bow, he would bow and prostrate from a standing position. At other times, he would bow and prostrate from a seated position.”

The latter may have been due to an excuse or to demonstrate its permissibility. However, his deliberate effort, despite praying sitting, to rise so that he could bow and prostrate from a standing position shows that this is better. It is more complete and expresses greater humility, since it involves lowering his head and the limbs with which he prostrates to Allah from a standing position.

---

## Page 11833

Whoever maintains a prescribed routine of worship, such as the forenoon prayer, night prayer, or another practice, should perform it wherever he is. He should not abandon his prescribed routine merely because he is among people, if Allah knows in his heart that he performs it privately for His sake, while striving to keep it free from showing off and whatever undermines sincerity.

For this reason, al-Fudayl ibn 'Iyād said:

“Abandoning a deed for people's sake is showing off, and performing it for people's sake is associating partners with Allah.”

Performing it where he earns the livelihood that helps him worship Allah is better for him than performing it somewhere that disrupts his livelihood and consequently preoccupies his heart. The more a prayer allows the heart to be fully attentive and free from distracting whispers, the more complete it is.

If someone forbids a prescribed act merely on the claim that it constitutes showing off, his prohibition is to be rejected on several grounds:

First: Prescribed acts are not to be forbidden for fear of showing off. Rather, people are to be instructed to perform them and to be sincere in doing so. When we see someone performing them, we let him continue, even if we are certain that he is showing off. Allah says of the hypocrites:

“Indeed, the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He deceives them. When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off before people and remembering Allah only a little.”

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and the Muslims allowed these people to continue their outward religious observances, even though they were showing off, and did not forbid their outward practice, because the harm in abandoning the outward display of

---

## Page 11834

prescribed acts is greater than the harm in displaying them for show, just as the harm in abandoning the outward profession of faith and performance of the prayers is greater than the harm in displaying them for show. Moreover, condemnation applies only to the corruption involved in doing so to be seen by people.

Second: Condemnation applies only to what Islamic law condemns. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“I have not been commanded to probe people’s hearts or split open their bellies.”

‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said: “Whoever shows us good, we love him and extend our loyalty to him on that account, even if his inner state is otherwise. Whoever shows us evil, we hate him on that account, even if he claims that his inner state is sound.”

Third: Allowing such objections would lead people given to polytheism and corruption to condemn people of goodness and religion. Whenever they saw someone openly performing a prescribed act established by the Sunnah, they would say, “He is showing off.” Those who are truthful and sincere would then stop openly performing prescribed acts for fear of their disparagement and blame. Good would thus fall into disuse, while those who associate partners with Allah would retain their power, openly displaying evil with no one to condemn them. This is among the gravest harms.

Fourth: Such conduct is one of the hallmarks of the hypocrites: disparaging those who openly perform prescribed deeds. Allah Most High says:

“Those who find fault with the believers who give charity freely, and with those who can find nothing to give except what their meagre means allow, and ridicule them

---

## Page 11835

—Allah ridicules them, and they will have a painful punishment.”

When the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) urged people to give in the year of the expedition to Tabūk, one of the Companions brought a purse so heavy that he could barely carry it. They said, “He is showing off.” Another brought a sā‘ (a measure of grain), and they said, “Allah certainly had no need of so-and-so’s sā‘.” They disparaged both, so Allah revealed this verse. It became a warning to those who disparage believers who obey Allah and His Messenger. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

If a man hears a passage of the Quran containing a verse calling for prostration and prostrates without ablution, does he sin, become a disbeliever, or have his wife divorced from him?

He answered:

He does not become a disbeliever, nor is his wife thereby divorced, but according to most scholars he sins. However, some scholars of Abū Hanīfa’s school have stated that anyone who performs without ablution a prayer for which ritual purity is unanimously held to be a prerequisite—such as the five daily prayers—thereby becomes a disbeliever. If he becomes a disbeliever, he is an apostate, and according to Abū Hanīfa, an apostate’s marriage is dissolved. Yet this ruling of disbelief has not been transmitted from Abū Hanīfa himself or from his two principal students, but only from his followers. The majority of scholars hold that he is subject to discretionary punishment and does not become a disbeliever unless he deems this permissible and mocks the prayer.

As for the prostration of recitation, some scholars have held that it is permissible without

---

## Page 11836

ritual purity. There is consensus that a person does not become a disbeliever by doing something whose permissibility scholars dispute. The majority of scholars hold that an apostate’s marriage is not dissolved unless his wife’s waiting period ends without his having returned to Islam. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Should the supplication for istikhāra (seeking Allah’s guidance) be recited during the prayer or after the closing salutation?

He answered:

It is permissible to supplicate before or after the closing salutation, whether in the istikhāra prayer or other prayers. Supplicating before the closing salutation is better, for most of the Prophet’s supplications (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) were made before the closing salutation. Moreover, before the closing salutation, the worshipper has not yet concluded the prayer, so this is better. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 11837

And Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah (may Allah have mercy on him) said:

Section

The times when prayer is prohibited, and the disagreement concerning prayers performed for specific reasons and other prayers. There is considerable confusion on this subject.

We say:

It is established by revealed texts and scholarly consensus that the prohibition does not encompass all prayers. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Whoever completes one rak‘ah before the sun rises has caught the prayer in time.” Another version reads, “Let him add another to it”; another, “Let him complete his prayer”; and another uses “a prostration.”

All these versions are authentic. Likewise, he said:

“Whoever completes one rak‘ah of ‘Asr before the sun sets has caught the prayer in time.” Another version reads, “Let him complete his prayer”; another, “Let him add another to it”; and another uses “a prostration.”

This includes his command to perform the second rak‘ah of Fajr as the sun rises. It also establishes that if someone performs one rak‘ah of ‘Asr as the sun sets, it is valid

---

## Page 11838

and he is commanded to add another to that rak‘ah. This latter ruling is the position of the four imams and other scholars.

As for the former ruling, it is the view of the majority of scholars. It is reported from ‘Alī and several other Companions and Successors, and was the position of the Companions collectively. It is established that Abū Bakr al-Siddīq recited Sūrat al-Baqarah in the Fajr prayer. When he concluded the prayer, he was told, “The sun almost rose.” He replied, “Had it risen, it would not have found us heedless.”

These words, addressed by al-Siddīq to the Companions, explain that sunrise would not have harmed their prayer. It would not have found them heedless, but rather remembering Allah and obeying His words:

“Remember your Lord within yourself, humbly and fearfully, without raising your voice, in the mornings and evenings, and do not be among the heedless.”

This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Ishāq, Abū Thawr and Ibn al-Mundhir.

These scholars hold that a person may make up a prayer missed through sleep or forgetfulness during the prohibited times. Abū Hanīfah and those who agree with him, however, hold that his prayer becomes invalid because it has become a missed prayer, and, in their view, missed prayers cannot be made up during the prohibited times. This differs from that day’s ‘Asr prayer, which is being performed within its prescribed time. They cited as evidence the postponement of the prayer on the day when he and his Companions slept through it until the rising of

---

## Page 11839

the sun. The majority offered several responses.

First: the delay was due to the place, because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“This is a valley in which Satan was present with us.”

Second: this indicates permissibility, not obligation.

Third: at most, this applies to someone beginning to make up a missed prayer. Someone who has prayed one rak‘ah before sunrise, however, has caught the prayer within its time, as he said, “He has caught it.” The second rak‘ah is performed as a continuation, just as a latecomer to the congregational prayer does when he catches one rak‘ah.

They said: There is greater excuse for this than for delaying ‘Asr until sunset, since sunset can be observed and one can pray before it. As for sunrise, before the sun rises, one does not know when it will rise. Thus, when he prays, he is praying within the prescribed time. For this reason, someone who delays the prayer but finishes it before sunrise incurs no sin. It is established in the hadiths concerning prayer times that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “concluded the prayer with the salutation on the second day while someone was saying, ‘The sun has risen, or almost risen.’”

He also said in an authentic hadith:

“The time for Fajr lasts until the sun rises.”

And he said:

“The time for ‘Asr lasts until the sun turns yellow.”

In another wording:

“So long as it has not drawn near to setting.”

Thus, whoever performs the entire Fajr prayer before sunrise incurs no sin, whereas whoever prays ‘Asr at sunset without an excuse is sinful. As stated in the authentic hadith: “That is the hypocrite’s prayer! That is the hypocrite’s prayer! He watches the sun until

---

## Page 11840

it is between Satan’s two horns, then he rises and pecks out four rak‘ahs, remembering Allah only a little.” Yet the Messenger considered him to have caught the prayer within its time. This is the time allowed in cases of necessity, as with someone who wakes from sleep, a menstruating woman whose period ends, an unbeliever who embraces Islam, or someone who regains sanity or consciousness. Anyone who could have prayed earlier, however, sins by delaying it until then and is among those who pray but are heedless of their prayers. Nevertheless, performing it at that time is better than letting it lapse, for letting it lapse is a major sin.

In the two Sahih collections, he is reported to have said:

“Whoever misses the prayer—the ‘Asr prayer—it is as though he has lost his family and his wealth.”

As for someone who prays before sunrise, he incurs no sin. If this applies to someone who has prayed one rak‘ah after sunset, then someone who has prayed one rak‘ah before sunrise, or has prayed it before any part of the sun has risen, is…

As for their statement, “That person performs the second rak‘ah after sunset, at a time when prayer is permitted, unlike the former, who performs the second at a prohibited time,” the reply is:

The discussion concerns both matters. Why do you permit him to pray ‘Asr at a prohibited time, even though the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him,

---

## Page 11841

limited the time for ‘Asr to before the sun sets or approaches sunset, while you do not permit Fajr to be performed at a prohibited time?

Second: Although the person praying ‘Asr performs the second rak‘ah outside a prohibited time, the person praying Fajr performs the first outside a prohibited time. Moreover, the latter has the advantage of having performed the first rak‘ah within its time, without censure or prohibition, unlike the person praying ‘Asr, who performs the first under censure and prohibition.

In any case, the hadith and their agreement establish that he did not prohibit every prayer. Rather, that day’s ‘Asr prayer is performed at a prohibited time, as established by the explicit text and their agreement. Likewise, the second rak‘ah of Fajr is performed then, as established by the explicit text and affirmed by the majority.

If it is said, “He is censured for praying ‘Asr at a prohibited time. How, then, can they say, ‘He did not prohibit it before censuring it’?”—the censure concerns only delaying it until this time. Once he has sinned by delaying it, he is instructed to perform it at this time rather than let it lapse, for letting it lapse is a greater sin and is impermissible under any circumstances. Performing it while bearing some sin is better than letting it lapse and thereby incurring an even greater sin.

The Lawgiver always gives precedence to the greater of two benefits by forgoing the lesser, and averts the greater of two evils by accepting the lesser. This is like a traveler who has water that he needs for purification: he is instructed to purify himself with it. If he pours it away, he sins and is instructed to perform dry ablution, and his prayer would be

---

## Page 11842

better performed with dry ablution than allowed to lapse. However, there are two views on whether he must repeat it, both found within the school of Ahmad and in other schools. Someone who lets the prescribed time lapse cannot repeat the prayer, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

In any case, the explicit text, together with their agreement, establishes that the prohibition does not encompass every prayer. The majority have supported making up missed prayers at a prohibited time by citing his statement in the authentic hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“Whoever sleeps through a prayer or forgets it, let him offer it when he remembers it. There is no expiation for it other than that.”

The hadith of Abū Qatādah, also recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, states—in Muslim’s wording:

“There is no negligence in sleep; negligence lies only in wakefulness, when someone does not offer a prayer until the time for the next prayer arrives. Whoever does that, let him offer it when he awakens, and when the next day comes, let him offer it at its proper time.”

He thus commanded him to pray when he awakens and when he remembers, and this encompasses every time. This general wording takes precedence over the general wording of the prohibition, because it has been established that the latter does not encompass obligatory prayer, whether performed within its time or made up afterward: it does not encompass that day’s ‘Asr prayer, nor does it encompass the second rak‘ah of Fajr.

Furthermore, when he awakens or remembers, that is the time for that prayer. Performing it then is therefore performing it within its time, just as one performs that day’s ‘Asr within its time. Moreover, the former has an excuse, whereas the latter does not. It may, however, be objected:

If this person who has missed the prayer were to delay it until the prohibited time ended, he would not thereby miss it a second time. This differs from ‘Asr, which would lapse if he did not offer it then; the same applies to the second rak‘ah of Fajr.

---

## Page 11843

The reply is:

This implies that the prayer may be delayed for an overriding benefit, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delayed it and said:

“This is a valley in which Satan was present with us.”

Another example is delaying it until someone else has purified himself so that they can pray together in congregation, just as they prayed Fajr behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, after sleeping through it. This differs from Fajr and ‘Asr whose prescribed times have not yet elapsed: under no circumstances may they be allowed to lapse. What we have explained establishes that the prohibition does not encompass every prayer even at sunrise and sunset; this applies all the more to the other times.

Section

Jubayr ibn Mut‘im reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O descendants of ‘Abd Manāf, do not prevent anyone from circumambulating this House and praying at whatever hour he wishes, by night or day.”

This was recorded by the compilers of the Sunan. Al-Tirmidhī said, “It is an authentic hadith.” The imams al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Thawr and others cited it as evidence and acted upon it, permitting circumambulation and prayer after Fajr and ‘Asr, as was also reported from Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn al-Zubayr and other Companions and Successors. As for the three times, two positions are reported from Ahmad. Other scholars, such as Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and others, do not permit the two rak‘ahs of circumambulation during

---

## Page 11844

the times when prayer is prohibited. The evidence supports the former view for several reasons:

First: His words,

“Do not prevent anyone from circumambulating this House and praying at whatever hour he wishes, by night or day,”

expressly encompass all times. How, then, can it be said that the five prohibited times are not included?

Second: No case has been excluded from this general statement by either a scriptural text or scholarly consensus, whereas the hadith prohibiting prayer is subject to exceptions established by both scriptural texts and consensus. A general statement whose scope remains intact takes precedence over one whose scope has been restricted.

Third: People have continued to circumambulate the House and pray beside it ever since Abraham, the close friend of Allah, built it. Before the Hijrah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions would circumambulate it and pray beside it. Likewise, after the conquest of Mecca, Muslims frequently circumambulated it and prayed beside it. Had the two rak‘ahs of circumambulation been prohibited during the five times, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have issued a general prohibition, since Muslims needed to know this, and that prohibition would have been transmitted. Yet no Muslim has reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited this, even though circumambulation is more frequent and easier at the two ends of the day.

Fourth: Prohibiting this would deprive people of the benefits of circumambulation and prayer.

---

## Page 11845

Fifth: The prohibition was imposed solely to block a means to wrongdoing, and an act prohibited on this basis may be performed when an overriding benefit warrants it. Prayer itself is among the finest deeds and greatest acts of worship, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Remain steadfast, though you will never do so perfectly, and know that the best of your deeds is prayer.”

There is therefore nothing harmful in prayer itself that calls for its prohibition. At sunrise and sunset, however, Satan positions himself alongside the sun, and the disbelievers prostrate to it at those times. Someone praying then resembles them in the general act of prayer—namely, prostration. Although Muslims neither worship what they worship nor share their intention, they resemble them outwardly. Prayer was therefore prohibited at these two times to block the means to wrongdoing, so that resemblance to the disbelievers would cease and Muslims would not resemble them in their association of partners with Allah.

Similarly, he prohibited being alone with a non-mahram woman, travelling with her, and looking at her because of the wrongdoing to which these things may lead. He also prohibited a woman from travelling unless accompanied by her husband or a mahram—a male relative whom she is permanently forbidden to marry. Likewise, he prohibited insulting the gods of the polytheists, lest they insult Allah without knowledge. He also prohibited eating foul things because of the effects of consuming them as nourishment, which give rise to forbidden deeds. There are other examples of this kind.

Yet what is prohibited to block a means to wrongdoing becomes permissible when there is an overriding benefit. Thus, it is permissible to look at a woman one intends to marry, and to accompany a woman on a journey when there is fear for her safety—for example, when she is leaving dār al-harb (the abode of war), as in the journey of Umm Kulthūm, or in ‘Ā’ishah’s journey, after she had fallen behind, with Safwān ibn

---

## Page 11846

al-Mu‘attal. Such conduct was prohibited only because it leads to wrongdoing; if it brings about an overriding benefit, it does not lead to wrongdoing.

This applies to unrestricted voluntary prayer: it may lead to wrongdoing, and people have no need to perform it during the prohibited times, since there is ample time when prayer is permissible. Indeed, prohibiting it at certain times brings other benefits, including allowing people some respite from the exertion of worship, just as sleep and other forms of rest refresh them.

For this reason, Mu‘ādh said:

“I seek reward for my sleep just as I seek reward for my standing in prayer.”

Another benefit is that withholding prayer for a time awakens people’s longing for it and makes it dearer to them, so that they approach it with greater energy and eagerness. When an act of worship is assigned particular times, people feel more enthusiasm for it than they do for something performed constantly.

Another benefit is that people grow weary, bored, and restless with something done constantly. Prohibiting it at certain times removes that weariness. There are other kinds of benefits in prohibiting unrestricted voluntary prayer as well. Thus, this prohibition wards off harms and secures benefits without causing any benefit to be lost.

As for prayers performed for a specific reason, prohibiting some of them would cause their benefit to be lost, depriving people of opportunities for worship, obedience, earning reward and recompense, and attaining great benefit in their religion—opportunities that cannot be recovered. Examples include repeating a prayer with the neighborhood imam, the prayer upon entering a mosque, the prostration of recitation, the eclipse prayer, and similar acts.

---

## Page 11847

In other cases, prohibiting them diminishes their benefit, as with the two units of prayer following circumambulation, especially for visitors who wish to make the most of circumambulation during those days. Both they and the city’s residents circumambulate at the beginning and end of the day.

The sixth argument:

Prayers performed for a specific reason are prompted by that reason, not performed on account of the time itself. This is unlike unrestricted voluntary prayer, which has no specific reason. Thus, the harm underlying the prohibition arises only from prayer without a specific reason, not from prayer performed for a reason. Hence, in the hadith narrated by Ibn ‘Umar, the Prophet said:

“Do not deliberately time your prayers to coincide with sunrise or sunset.”

The arguments we have mentioned also support making up missed prayers during the prohibited times.

Section: Repeating a prayer

If the congregational prayer begins while a person is in the mosque, he may repeat the prayer during a prohibited time, according to the majority, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Thawr, and others.

Abū Hanīfah and others, however, included this among the prohibited prayers. The majority supported their position with three hadiths:

---

## Page 11848

The first:

The hadith of Jābir ibn Yazīd ibn al-Aswad, who narrated that his father said:

“I accompanied the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, on his Hajj and prayed Fajr with him in the mosque of al-Khayf while I was a young man. When he finished his prayer, he noticed two men at the back of the congregation who had not prayed with him. He said, ‘Bring them to me.’ They were brought to him, trembling. He asked, ‘What prevented you from praying with us?’

“They replied, ‘O Messenger of Allah, we have already prayed at our lodgings.’

“He said, ‘Do not do that. If you have prayed at your lodgings and then come to a mosque where a congregation is praying, pray with them; it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.’”

This was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan, such as Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and others, as well as Ahmad and al-Athram.

The second:

The report narrated by Mālik in al-Muwatta’, from Zayd ibn Aslam, from Bishr ibn Mihjan, from his father:

“He was sitting with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, when the call to prayer was made. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, rose and prayed, then returned while Mihjan remained seated where he was. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, asked, ‘What prevented you from praying with the people? Are you not a Muslim?’

“He replied, ‘Indeed I am, O Messenger of Allah, but I have already prayed at home.’ The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, told him, ‘When you come, pray with the people, even if you have already prayed.’”
