This report supports the ruling through its general wording, whereas the first explicitly permits repeating the prayer after Fajr.

The third:

The report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Abū Dharr, who said: The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said to me, “How will you fare when you have rulers over you

---

## Page 11849

who delay the prayer beyond its appointed time, or neglect it until its time has passed?”

Abū Dharr said: “I asked, ‘What, then, do you command me to do?’ He replied, ‘Offer the prayer at its appointed time. Then, if you find them praying it, pray with them, for it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.’”

In another version narrated by Muslim:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, struck my thigh and said, ‘How will you fare if you live among people who delay the prayer beyond its appointed time?’ I asked, ‘What, then, do you command me to do?’ He replied, ‘Offer the prayer at its appointed time, then go about your business. If the congregation begins the prayer while you are in the mosque, pray with them.’”

In yet another version narrated by Muslim:

“Offer the prayer at its appointed time. Then, if you find the prayer being offered, pray, and do not say, ‘I have already prayed, so I will not pray.’”

These texts unquestionably include the Zuhr and Asr prayers, since these were the two prayers that the rulers used to delay. This was unlike Fajr, which they did not offer after sunrise. Likewise, they did not delay Maghrib, but they would sometimes delay Asr until the sun began to set.

Thus, he commanded him to offer the prayer at its appointed time and then, despite having already offered it, to pray it again with them as a voluntary prayer. This would be during a time when prayer is prohibited, since he had already prayed Asr, and because they might delay Asr until the sun turned yellow. This explicitly establishes repeating the prayer during a prohibited time.

---

## Page 11850

Section

Funeral prayer after Fajr and after Asr

Ibn al-Mundhir said: “The Muslims are unanimously agreed on offering the funeral prayer after Fajr and after Asr.”

Ahmad’s position did not vary concerning those three types: they may be performed during the prohibited times, because specific hadiths establish their permissibility at those times. He therefore exempted them from the prohibition. He also exempted the funeral prayer during the two periods on the grounds of Muslim consensus.

As for the other prayers associated with specific causes—such as the prayer of greeting the mosque, the prostration of recitation, and the eclipse prayer, as well as the two rak‘ahs following circumambulation during the three prohibited periods, and the funeral prayer during those three periods—his statements differed concerning them.

His well-known position is that they are prohibited. This was the view adopted by many of his followers, including al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī, and others. It is also the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah. Abū Hanīfah, however, permits prostration after Fajr and Asr; it is not obligatory in his view.

The second position reported from him permits all prayers associated with specific causes. This was the view adopted by Abū al-Khattāb, and it is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī. It is the stronger view on this issue for several reasons:

---

## Page 11851

among them is that the command to offer the prayer of greeting the mosque is established in the two Sahīh collections. Abū Qatādah reported that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“When any of you enters the mosque, let him pray two rak‘ahs before sitting down.”

He also reported:

“I entered the mosque while the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) was sitting among the people, and I sat down. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, ‘What prevented you from praying two rak‘ahs before sitting down?’ I replied, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I saw you sitting and the people sitting.’ He said, ‘When any of you enters the mosque, he must not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.’”

This contains both a command to pray two rak‘ahs before sitting down and a prohibition against sitting down before praying them. It applies generally at all times, and its general scope remains intact: no case has been excluded by a scriptural text or scholarly consensus. The hadith prohibiting prayer, however, is known not to apply universally. A general text whose scope remains intact takes precedence over a general text whose scope has been restricted. The latter is known not to apply universally, unlike the former, for the grounds for its general applicability remain in force, and no exception to it is known.

The second reason:

Both compilers related in their Sahīh collections that Jābir said:

“A man came while the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was delivering a sermon to the people. He asked, ‘Have you prayed, so-and-so?’ He replied, ‘No.’ He said, ‘Stand up and pray.’” Another narration reads: “Pray two rak‘ahs.”

In Muslim’s version, Jābir said that he then said:

“When any of you comes on Friday while the imam is delivering the sermon, let him pray two rak‘ahs and keep them brief.”

Ahmad followed this hadith, with no differing position reported from him, as did the other jurists of hadith, such as al-Shāfi‘ī, Ishāq, and Abū Thawr

---

## Page 11852

and Ibn al-Mundhir, as was also reported from several of the early generations, such as al-Hasan, Makhūl, and others. Many scholars were unaware of this hadith and therefore prohibited prayer during the sermon, since it is a time when prayer is prohibited. This was reported from Shurayh, al-Nakha‘ī, and Ibn Sīrīn, and it is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Layth, Mālik, and al-Thawrī. It is also the logical implication of the position of those who prohibit the prayer of greeting the mosque during the prohibited times, for the prohibition against prayer while the preacher is on the pulpit is even more emphatic. Indeed, the worshipper is forbidden to do anything that distracts him from listening; even telling his companion, “Listen attentively,” constitutes idle speech. Thus, if he is commanded to offer the prayer of greeting the mosque during the sermon, there is all the more reason for this command to apply at other times.

Some scholars of our school have argued that when someone enters the mosque outside the times when prayer is prohibited, it is recommended for him to pray, citing the Prophet’s words:

“When any of you enters the mosque while the imam is delivering the sermon, he must not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.”

They also said that prayer must cease once the imam sits on the pulpit: no one may pray except someone entering the mosque, who offers the prayer of greeting the mosque and keeps it brief. This is a clear contradiction. Rather, if the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded the prayer of greeting the mosque in this situation—when prayer and anything else that distracts from listening are prohibited—then there is all the more reason to permit it during the other prohibited times. This is further demonstrated by the fact that, in this situation, no funeral prayer is offered, nor is circumambulation performed

---

## Page 11853

around the House, nor are the two rak‘ahs of circumambulation offered while the imam is delivering the sermon. This shows that the prohibition here is more emphatic and restrictive than the prohibition after Fajr and ‘Asr. Thus, if he commanded the prayer of greeting the mosque here, there is all the more reason for that command to apply there. This is clear and evident. There is neither might nor power except through Allah.

The third argument:

It may be said that certain prayers have been established as exceptions to the prohibition, such as the current ‘Asr prayer, the two rak‘ahs of Fajr, a missed prayer, the two rak‘ahs of circumambulation, and a prayer repeated in the mosque. It is therefore established that prayers offered during the prohibited times fall into two categories: those that are prohibited and those that are prescribed and not prohibited. There must therefore be a distinction between them, since the Lawgiver does not treat like cases differently, making one commanded and the other forbidden.

The distinction may be that the permitted prayer has a specific cause. Whoever offers such a prayer does so because of that cause, rather than simply offering an unrestricted voluntary prayer. Were he not to offer it, he would miss its benefit—just as someone entering the mosque would miss the reward of the prayer of greeting the mosque. Likewise, he would miss the benefit of the eclipse prayer, the prostration of recitation, and all other prayers tied to specific causes.

Alternatively, the distinction may be something else. If the former is true, the intended distinction between prayers tied to specific causes and other prayers is established.

If the latter is true, they should be told:

You do not know what the distinction is. You know only that he prohibited some prayers and permitted others, without knowing the distinction between them. You therefore have no right to pronounce on the remaining cases of

---

## Page 11854

dispute, whether by prohibiting or permitting them. For the distinction drawn by the Lawgiver in the cases addressed by the texts—permitting some and prohibiting others—may also extend to the disputed cases, either prohibiting or permitting them, yet you do not know which of the two categories they belong to. You may therefore prohibit only what you know he prohibited because it lacks the feature that makes it permissible, and permit only what you know he permitted because that feature applies to it. Declaring things lawful or unlawful without a distinguishing principle derived from the Lawgiver is not permissible.

If it is said:

“The hadiths prohibiting prayer are general, so we apply them generally except where evidence establishes an exception. Whatever we know to be excluded because a specific text addresses it, we exclude on that basis; otherwise, we retain their general application.”

The response is:

This would hold only if this general text, from which exceptions have been made, were not opposed by stronger general texts whose scope remains intact, and if the cases excluded from it were known to possess a distinctive feature warranting the distinction. If it were established that this was a general text from which certain cases were excluded on account of a feature absent from all other cases, the remaining cases would continue to fall within its general scope. But how can that apply when its scope is no longer unrestricted, when it is opposed by both specific hadiths and general hadiths whose scope remains intact, and when the cases excluded from it possess no distinctive feature warranting their exclusion alone? Rather, other cases share the feature warranting their exclusion, or have an even stronger claim to be excluded.

---

## Page 11855

And Muslims’ general need for the prayer of greeting the mosque is greater than their need for the two units of prayer following circumambulation, since circumambulation can be postponed, whereas the prayer of greeting the mosque cannot. Moreover, if a man enters the mosque at a time when prayer is prohibited and sits down without praying, he acts contrary to the Prophet’s command—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and forfeits this benefit, even if he is not guilty of sinful disobedience. If he remains standing or refrains from entering the mosque, that is a great harm.

Some people perform the Sunnah prayer before Fajr at home and then come to the mosque. Among those who regard the greeting prayer as disliked, some stand at the mosque door until the call to commence prayer is given, then enter and pray with the congregation. They thus deprive themselves of entering Allah’s house at that noble time and remembering Allah there. Others enter and sit down without praying, thereby acting contrary to the command.

This and similar considerations demonstrate conclusively that Muslims are commanded to perform the greeting prayer at all times. Muslims have always entered the mosque at both ends of the day. Had they been forbidden to perform the greeting prayer at those times, the Messenger’s prohibition would have been widely known. How could that be, when he commanded anyone entering the mosque while the preacher is on the pulpit not to sit down until he has prayed two units of prayer? Does his command to perform it at that time not indicate that it should also be performed at other times?

The fourth argument:

As we explained earlier, the prohibition was intended to block a path to polytheism. Prayers performed for a specific reason, however, have an overriding benefit, and the worshipper performs them because of that reason, rather than praying without a specific reason. This precludes such resemblance.

---

## Page 11856

The fifth argument:

It is established in an authentic report that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—made up the two units of prayer associated with Zuhr after Asr. This was making up a voluntary prayer at a prohibited time, although it could have been made up at another time. Voluntary prayers whose opportunity would be lost if they were not performed at the prohibited times have an even stronger claim to permissibility than making up a voluntary prayer at such a time when it could be performed otherwise. This applies especially to prayers that have been commanded, such as the prayer of greeting the mosque and the eclipse prayer.

A group of Ahmad’s followers, including Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, held that the regular Sunnah prayers may be made up after Asr, but not at the other prohibited times, such as the three specified periods. He stated that Ahmad’s position was that the Sunnah prayer before Fajr may be made up after the obligatory prayer, although Ahmad preferred that it be made up in the forenoon.

Imam Ahmad said: “If he performs the two units after Fajr, that suffices him. As for me, I prefer that.”

Regarding making up Witr after dawn, he mentioned that Ahmad’s explicitly stated position was that one should perform it.

Al-Athram said: “I heard Abū Abd Allāh being asked, ‘May a man perform Witr after dawn has broken?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

He said: “This has been related from Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn Umar, Ibn Abbās, Hudhayfah, Abū al-Dardā’, Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit, Fadālah ibn Ubayd, Ā’ishah, and Abd Allāh ibn Āmir ibn Rabī‘ah. It is also related from Alī ibn Abī Tālib. When it was mentioned to him that Abū Mūsā had said, ‘Whoever performs Witr after the muezzin has no valid Witr,’ they asked Alī. He said, ‘I know: he may perform Witr up until the prayer.’ He rejected

---

## Page 11857

that, and he mentioned no disagreement except from Abū Mūsā, although it should not be performed after dawn.

He said:

The authentic hadiths prohibiting prayer do not explicitly prohibit it before the Fajr prayer; on this point there is only the hadith of Abī. Ahmad cited as evidence the report of Abū Nadrah al-Ghifārī from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that he said:

“Allah has granted you an additional prayer, Witr, so perform it between Isha and the dawn prayer.”

This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and the majority.

Mālik said: “Whoever misses the night prayer may perform it after dawn, before performing the dawn prayer.”

He said: Ibn Abī Mūsā [al-Khiraqī] (*) also reported this in al-Irshād as Ahmad’s position, by analogy with Witr.

I say:

The position he chose does not contradict what al-Khiraqī and other early scholars of the school stated. Al-Khiraqī mentioned that four types of prayer are permissible at all the prohibited times: making up missed prayers, the two units following circumambulation, praying when the call to commence the prayer is given while one is in the mosque, and the funeral prayer. However, he mentioned the prohibition of the eclipse prayer and the prostration of recitation in their respective chapters. Thus, he did not prohibit making up Sunnah prayers at the prohibited times.

Shaykh Abū Muhammad and a group of Ahmad’s followers therefore adopted the view that the regular Sunnah prayers may be made up after Asr, but not at the other prohibited times, and that other prayers occasioned by specific causes should not be performed, such as the prayer of greeting the mosque, the eclipse prayer, and the prayer for seeking Allah’s guidance

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 191):
> “The name ‘al-Khiraqī’ was inserted by the copyist. Ibn Abī Mūsā is Muhammad ibn Ahmad al-Hāshimī (d. 428). See the source of the quotation, al-Mughnī, 2/531.”

---

## Page 11858

and the prayer of repentance, the Sunnah prayer after ablution, and the prostration of recitation—neither at this time nor at any other prohibited time. This is because authentic hadiths establish that the regular Sunnah prayers may be made up then. They said: The prohibition at this time is less stringent than at other times, since the Companions differed over it; therefore, the other prohibited times cannot be treated in the same way. Moreover, the regular Sunnah prayers have a special distinction.

This distinction is weak. The Prophet’s commands—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to perform the prayer of greeting the mosque, the eclipse prayer, and the prostration of recitation carry greater weight than making up a missed Sunnah prayer. If the latter is permissible, then the former is all the more permissible. There is no command from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to make up Sunnah prayers, nor even a command to perform the Sunnah prayer in question—the Sunnah prayer of Dhuhr. Rather, he performed it, observed it regularly, and made it up when he missed it. What he commanded his community to do—especially when he also practised it himself—is more emphatically prescribed than what he did without commanding them to do it.

Thus, if they may perform the latter during the prohibited times, then performing the former is all the more permissible. If the Sunnah prayer of Dhuhr may be made up after Asr, then making up the Sunnah prayer of Fajr after Fajr is all the more permissible, for that is its time. If the latter can be delayed until sunrise, the former can likewise be delayed until sunset. They used to pray between the call to the Maghrib prayer and the call to commence it, while he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—watched them and approved of their doing so.

He said:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.” Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes,” lest people adopt it as a regular Sunnah practice.”

---

## Page 11859

Section

The prohibition concerning Asr is tied to the performance of the Asr prayer. Once a person has performed it, he may not pray afterwards, even if others have not yet performed it. As long as he has not performed it, he may pray. This is established by the textual evidence and by agreement, for the prohibition is tied to the act of praying.

As for Fajr, there is a well-known disagreement, and two positions are reported from Ahmad.

One view is that the prohibition is tied to the break of dawn, after which no voluntary prayer may be offered apart from the two rakahs. This is the view of a group of the early generations and the position of Abū Hanīfah. Al-Nakha‘ī said: “They used to disapprove of voluntary prayer after dawn.”

The other view is that it is tied to the act of praying, as with Asr. This is the view of al-Hasan and al-Shāfi‘ī, for the prohibition is established only after the prayer, just as with Asr.

The hadiths prohibiting prayer treat the two prayers alike. Thus, in both Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Trustworthy men testified to me—the most trustworthy of them in my eyes being Umar—that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade prayer after Fajr until sunrise, and after Asr until sunset.”

---

## Page 11860

Likewise, both collections report from Abū Hurayrah, with the wording:

“And prayer after Fajr until sunrise, and after Asr until sunset.”

Both collections also report from Abū Sa‘īd that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no prayer after Fajr until the sun has risen high, and no prayer after Asr until the sun has set.”

Muslim’s wording is:

“There is no prayer after the Fajr prayer or after the Asr prayer.”

Sahīh Muslim also contains the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah, who said: I asked, “O Messenger of Allah, tell me about prayer.” He said:

“Perform the Fajr prayer, then refrain from prayer until the sun rises, for it rises between the two horns of a devil, and at that time the disbelievers prostrate to it. Then pray, for prayer is attended and witnessed, until the shadow of a spear is at its shortest. Then refrain from prayer, for at that time Hell is stoked. When the shadow begins to lengthen again, pray, for prayer is witnessed and attended, until you perform Asr. Then refrain from prayer until the sun sets, for it sets between the two horns of a devil, and at that time the disbelievers prostrate to it.”

The hadiths specifically concerning sunrise, sunset, and the sun’s reaching its zenith include the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the sun’s rim appears, delay prayer until it has fully emerged; and when the sun’s rim disappears, delay prayer until it has fully set.”

This is Muslim’s wording. Sahīh Muslim also reports from ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir, who said:

“There were three times at which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade us to pray or to bury our dead: when it begins to rise

---

## Page 11861

above the horizon until it has risen high; when the sun stands at its zenith until it begins to decline; and when the sun approaches sunset until it has set.”

The time of the sun’s zenith is not mentioned in most of the hadiths. Al-Bukhārī did not include the hadith concerning it, but Muslim transmitted it from ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir and ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah. Al-Sunābihī also related a corroborating report.

Ahmad relied on these three reports. When the concession allowing prayer at midday on Friday was mentioned to him, he said:

“This is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through three routes: the hadith of ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir, the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah, and the hadith of al-Sunābihī.”

Al-Khiraqī did not include this among the times when prayer is prohibited. Rather, he said:

“One may make up missed obligatory prayers, perform the prayer associated with circumambulation, and repeat the prayer if one is in the mosque when the call to commence prayer is given and has already prayed—all during any time when prayer is prohibited: after Fajr until sunrise, and after Asr until sunset.”

This implies that there are no prohibited times other than these two. It also implies that the prayers he permits may be performed during the prohibited times, in accordance with one of the two reported positions. It further implies that the prohibition is tied to the performance of the prayer, for he said, “After Fajr until sunrise,” and did not say “Fajr.” Had the prohibition applied from the onset of dawn, he would have excepted the two rak‘ahs; instead, he excepted both the obligatory and the voluntary prayer. These are also the Messenger’s own words: he prohibited prayer after the Fajr prayer until sunrise, just as he prohibited prayer after Asr until sunset.

---

## Page 11862

It is well known that, had he meant the time itself, he would have excepted the two sunnah rak‘ahs of Fajr and the obligatory prayer, just as an exception appears in his prohibition when he said:

“There is no prayer after dawn except two rak‘ahs.”

Since he did not mention this exception in those hadiths, we know that he meant the performance of the prayer, as authentic hadiths explicitly explain.

Moreover, the appointed time for a prescribed prayer—both its obligatory and sunnah components—cannot be a time when prayer is prohibited. The period after dawn is the time for Fajr, both its sunnah and obligatory prayers. How, then, can this be called a prohibited time? Can a time be designated as prohibited while prayer without any particular occasion is regularly prescribed in it, and one is instructed to seek out that time for prayer? This is a contradiction. Furthermore, this period has been designated as a time for prayer until sunrise, unlike Asr, whose appointed time ends when the sun turns yellow.

The prohibition is because the disbelievers prostrate to the sun, and this does not occur from the onset of dawn. Thus, the prohibition originally concerns sunrise and sunset, as in the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar. However, he prohibited prayer after the two prayers as a precaution against what might lead to the prohibited act: someone offering voluntary prayers after them might continue until praying at sunrise or sunset. The prohibition during these two periods is less stringent. For this reason, he regularly observed the two rak‘ahs after Asr until Allah took his soul.

As for the period before the Fajr prayer, there is no basis for prohibiting prayer then. Nevertheless, no prayer is prescribed as a sunnah at that time except Fajr—its sunnah and obligatory prayers. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would pray at night and perform Witr. Then, when dawn broke, he would offer the two rak‘ahs, followed by the obligatory prayer, and he would sometimes lie down

---

## Page 11863

to rest, either after Witr or after the two rak‘ahs of Fajr. Whenever sleep or illness prevented him from praying at night, he would offer twelve rak‘ahs during the day in place of his night prayer. He would not make them up before the Fajr prayer, because there was not enough time: these prayers were lengthy, and he would offer Fajr while it was still dark. An authentic hadith states:

“Whoever sleeps through his regular portion of recitation, then recites it between the Fajr and Zuhr prayers, will have it recorded for him as though he had recited it at night.”

Clearly, if he could recite some of it before the Fajr prayer, that would be better still. Nevertheless, if he recites it before noon, it is recorded for him as though he had recited it at night, because this period is regarded as an extension of the preceding night. Thus, before noon one says, “We did it this past night,” whereas after noon one says, “We did it last night.” This is the forenoon period, which serves as a substitute for the night vigil. Hence:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, slept through his night prayer, he would make it up in the forenoon, offering twelve rak‘ahs.”

This interpretation has also been reported from ‘Umar and other Companions concerning Allah’s words:

“And He is the One Who made the night and the day succeed one another, for whoever wishes to be mindful or grateful.”

Thus, after dawn, the only prayers he prescribed for the Muslims were the regular sunnah prayer and the obligatory Fajr prayer. Other prayers were not prescribed as sunnah, nor were they prohibited, provided they were not treated as an established sunnah. As stated in the authentic hadith:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer; between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.” Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes,” lest people treat it as an established sunnah.

---

## Page 11864

This establishes that prayer is permissible between every adhān and iqāmah, just as:

“The Companions would offer two rak‘ahs between the adhān and iqāmah of Maghrib, while the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, watched them and approved of their doing so.”

The same applies between the adhān and iqāmah of ‘Asr and ‘Ishā’, and likewise of Fajr and Zuhr. However, the two rak‘ahs between the adhān and iqāmah of Fajr are undoubtedly an established sunnah. Additional prayers may be offered, but should not be treated as an established sunnah: they should neither be observed regularly nor be enjoined upon all Muslims as an established sunnah is. An established sunnah applies to Muslims generally and is observed regularly, just as the two rak‘ahs before Fajr and their regular observance are recommended for them all.

Thus, it is correct to say, “There is no sunnah prayer after dawn except two rak‘ahs.” A general prohibition, however, is not correct. Someone may not have risen to pray during the night and may wish to pray at this time. The early generations recommended that such a person make up his Witr—and indeed his night prayer—at this time. In their view, this is better than delaying it until the forenoon.

Section

There are several views concerning prayer at midday on Fridays and other days.

One view prohibits it without exception; this is the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

Another permits it without exception, as implied by al-Khiraqī’s wording; this is also reported from Mālik.

A third distinguishes Friday from other days; this is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī. ‘Atā’ permitted it on Fridays in winter, but not in summer, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah:

“Then, after sunrise, pray, for prayer is witnessed and attended, until there is vertical alignment

---

## Page 11865

of the shadow with the spear. Then refrain from prayer, for Hell is stoked at that time. When the shadow returns, pray.” Thus, he explained the prohibition at that time by the fact that Hell is then stoked.

At sunrise and sunset, however, he explained it by the devil’s accompanying the sun, saying:

“Then refrain from prayer until the sun rises, for it rises between the two horns of a devil.”

Concerning sunset, he said:

“Then refrain from prayer until the sun sets, for it sets between the two horns of a devil.”

As for the devil accompanying the sun at its zenith, this is not mentioned in any hadith except that of al-Sunābihī, who said:

“The sun rises with the devil’s horn alongside it. When it rises higher, he accompanies it; then, when it reaches its zenith, he accompanies it. When it passes the zenith, he accompanies it; when it draws near to setting, he accompanies it; and when it sets, he accompanies it.”

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore forbade prayer at those times.

However, it has been said that al-Sunābihī’s status as a Companion has not been established, and that he therefore did not hear this from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is unlike the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah, which is authentic and which he heard directly from him. This is further supported by the fact that most hadiths mention only the prohibition at sunrise and sunset, or after the two prayers. This indicates that the prohibition at midday is of a different kind, with a reason distinct from that applying to those two times.

This is further clarified by the fact that the disbelievers prostrate to the sun at sunrise and at

---

## Page 11866

sunset, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, informed us. As for their prostrating to it before it passes its zenith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not mention this practice of theirs or give it as the reason for the prohibition.

Moreover, precisely determining this time is difficult. It is established in an authentic hadith that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the heat becomes intense, delay the prayer until it is cooler, for intense heat comes from the scorching heat of Hell.”

The scholars have unanimously agreed that this hadith is authentic and have accepted it. He thus stated that intense heat comes from the scorching heat of Hell. This accords with his words:

“For Hell is stoked at that time.”

He also instructed that prayer be delayed until it is cooler. This indicates that prayer is prohibited during intense heat because that heat comes from the scorching heat of Hell.

In summer, Hell is stoked at midday; hence the prohibition of prayer at midday in hot weather. The worshipper is instructed to delay the prayer beyond the sun’s passing its zenith until it becomes cooler. Yet once the sun passes its zenith, the shade returns and shadows lengthen after reaching their shortest extent. This is part of the prescribed practice of delaying prayer until it is cooler. Prayer is therefore permissible from the moment the sun passes its zenith, as stated in the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah:

“Then refrain from prayer, for Hell is stoked at that time. When the shadow returns, pray.”

This indicates that prayer is prescribed from the moment the shade begins to return: that is, when the shadow returns from the west towards the east and begins to lengthen again after diminishing.

For this reason, they said that the term *fay’* (returning shade) applies specifically to shade after the sun has passed its zenith, because it conveys the sense of

---

## Page 11867

returning.

The term *zill* (shade), however, encompasses both. Before sunrise, the shade extends, as Allah Most High says:

“Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shade? Had He willed, He could have made it stationary.”

Then, when the sun rises, it serves as a guide to the shade, distinguishing shade from sunlight. The sun dispels the shade, continuing to do so as it shortens until midday. Once the sun passes its zenith, the shade again extends eastward, where it began, after the sun had first dispelled it from the east and then from the west. It then returns towards the east and then the west, continuing to extend and lengthen until sunset, when the shade dispels all sunlight.

For this reason, he said in the hadith of ‘Amr ibn ‘Abasah:

“Then refrain from prayer, for Hell is stoked at that time. When the shade returns, pray.”

Accordingly, those who permit prayer on Friday at this time say that Hell is not stoked on Friday, as has been narrated. They also say that delaying the Friday prayer until it becomes cooler is not recommended. Rather, it may be performed immediately after the sun passes its zenith, as established by the authentic Sunnah and unanimous agreement. Delaying it until it becomes cooler would impose hardship on people.

According to Ahmad and others, the Friday prayer may be performed when the sun is at its zenith, as more than one Companion did. How, then, can this be a prohibited time when the Friday prayer is permissible then? Obligatory prayers performed within their appointed times are not prescribed during a prohibited time without an excuse, as we have stated concerning Fajr. This would be contradictory.

In short, the permissibility of prayer at the zenith on Friday follows more clearly from Ahmad’s principles than from those of others, for he permits the Friday prayer at the zenith and does not regard

---

## Page 11868

that as a prohibited time. Indeed, it has even been held within his school that the Friday prayer is permissible only at that time—the very time at which prayer is otherwise prohibited. Thus, the distinction between Friday and other days is clear. Delaying prayer until it becomes cooler, which is prescribed on other days, is not prescribed on Friday; rather, it is prohibited. The reason given for this delay is that intense heat comes from the scorching breath of Hell. Likewise, the reason given for the prohibition of prayer at midday is that Hell is then stoked. This is of the same nature as his statement:

“For intense heat comes from the scorching breath of Hell.”

If the former rationale applies only to days other than Friday, the latter does likewise. According to what this rationale entails, prayer at the zenith is not prohibited in winter or on Friday. This is supported by the report in the Sunan collections concerning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He forbade prayer at midday except on Friday.”

This report carries greater weight than the evidence they adduced to argue that the prohibition concerning Fajr is tied to the time itself. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whomever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whomever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 11869

Section: Prayers with Specific Causes May Be Offered at Times When Prayer Is Prohibited

We have previously written at length, in Alexandria and elsewhere, explaining that this is the sounder of the two scholarly views. It is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, adopted by Abū al-Khattāb.

We had previously withheld judgment because of certain evidence cited by those who prohibit these prayers. Upon examining that evidence closely, however, we found that the hadiths were either weak or did not support their conclusion. We explained that there are several proofs for this position.

Among them is that the hadiths commanding prayers with specific causes, such as his statement:

“When one of you enters the mosque, let him not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs,”

retain their general applicability without exception. By contrast, not a single hadith prohibiting prayer is wholly general in its applicability; rather, all are subject to exceptions. Priority must therefore be given to the general wording that admits no exceptions, for its authority as evidence is agreed upon by the early generations and the majority who affirm the applicability of general wording. This is not the case with general wording subject to exceptions, and the former is undoubtedly stronger than the latter.

Another proof is that it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded the performance of the prayer

---

## Page 11870

for greeting the mosque by someone who enters during the sermon, with no contrary view reported from him on this point, since an explicit text establishes it. The prohibition against prayer during the sermon is undoubtedly stricter; if this prayer may be offered then, it may all the more readily be offered at the times under discussion.

Another proof is the wording of Ibn ‘Umar’s hadith in the two Sahīh collections:

“Do not deliberately time your prayers to coincide with sunrise or sunset.”

Deliberately choosing a time means doing so intentionally and purposefully, and this applies only to unrestricted voluntary prayer. As for prayer with a specific cause, the worshipper has not deliberately chosen that time; rather, he offers it because of its cause, which has prompted him to perform it. This qualified, explanatory wording clarifies the other wordings and shows that the prohibition concerns only deliberately choosing those times. If it encompassed both kinds of prayer, singling out deliberate timing would serve no purpose, and the ruling would have been made contingent upon wording that has no bearing on it.

Another proof is that the permissibility of certain prayers with specific causes has already been established. Some are established by explicit texts, such as the second rak‘ah of Fajr, the two rak‘ahs following circumambulation, and repeating a prayer with the local imam. Others are established by both explicit texts and scholarly consensus, such as ‘Asr at sunset and the funeral prayer after ‘Asr. Upon examining the grounds for their permissibility, no sound underlying reason can be found other than their having a specific cause. The ruling must therefore be tied to that. Otherwise, what difference is there between repeating a prayer and the prayer for greeting the mosque, when the command to offer the latter is more authentically established? Likewise, he commanded the eclipse prayer in many authentic hadiths.

---

## Page 11871

Our point here is this: prayer at a prohibited time must either be purely harmful and never prescribed under any circumstances, like prostrating to the sun itself, or be prescribed in some circumstances but not others. The first possibility is false, since explicit texts and scholarly consensus establish that ‘Asr is to be offered at sunset, before the sun’s entire disk has disappeared.

His saying is established in the two Sahīh collections:

“Whoever catches one rak‘ah of ‘Asr before the sun sets has caught the prayer, and whoever catches one rak‘ah of Fajr before the sun rises has caught the prayer.”

The former ruling is agreed upon, while the latter is the position of the majority. Abū Hanīfah distinguishes between Fajr and ‘Asr, saying that if the sun rises, the prayer becomes invalid, because it then remains overdue while its performance is prohibited. In the case of ‘Asr, however, sunset ushers in a time when prayer is permissible, not a time when it is prohibited.

Ahmad and the majority have judged this distinction unsound. They say: the issue concerns ‘Asr during sunset, which is a prohibited time, just as the period after sunrise is a prohibited time. One is not permitted to delay ‘Asr until then, but one may have an excuse, as when a menstruating woman becomes free of menstruation or a sleeping person awakens. Even supposing that someone delayed it without an excuse, he is still commanded to offer it during the prohibited time, despite being able to pray it after sunset.

If it is said, “Offering it within its appointed time is obligatory,” the reply is: “Making up a missed prayer immediately is also obligatory,” because he said:

“Whoever sleeps through a prayer or forgets it must pray it when he remembers it; there is no expiation for it other than that.”

---

## Page 11872

Furthermore, if someone offers one rak‘ah of Fajr before sunrise, he has begun the prayer before the prohibited time. This is less objectionable than beginning it during the prohibited time, although the latter is permissible according to the majority. Once it is established that prayer during the most strictly prohibited times—sunrise and sunset—is not purely harmful and therefore never prescribed, but is prescribed in certain circumstances, it follows that offering some prayers at these times does not entail the harm underlying the prohibition. Were that harm present, no prayer would be permissible at those times.

That being so, the established rule of Islamic law is that a prayer—indeed, any act of worship—that would be missed if delayed must be performed within its time as best one can. This applies even if its performance entails omitting an obligation or doing something forbidden that would not be permissible if one could perform it properly within its time. Examples include praying without recitation; the prayer of someone who is unclothed, someone who is ill, a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, or someone with urinary incontinence; praying in a state of ritual impurity without either a full ritual bath or ablution; praying in a direction other than the qiblah; and similar prayers whose performance is not forbidden when one is able to perform them in the prescribed manner within the appointed time. Nevertheless, one must perform the prayer within its time despite these deficiencies, lest it be missed, even if one could perform it fully after its time has passed.

It is thus clear that observing the appointed time of prayer takes precedence over all its other obligations. The same applies to voluntary prayer: if someone can pray only while unclothed, facing a direction other than the qiblah, or suffering from urinary incontinence, he should pray just as

---

## Page 11873

he would offer the obligatory prayer; for if he were to perform it only under perfect conditions, he would be unable to perform it at all. Performing it with deficiencies is therefore better than abandoning it altogether.

Accordingly, if prayers with specific causes are not performed during the prohibited times, they will be missed and left unperformed, and their benefit will be lost. Unrestricted voluntary prayer is different, since there is ample time to perform it. Refraining from it during the prohibited times fulfils the purpose of the prohibition: preventing resemblance to the polytheists who prostrate to the sun at those times. As explained above, fulfilling this purpose does not require prohibiting every prayer. Rather, prohibiting some prayers achieves it, so prohibiting unrestricted voluntary prayer suffices.

Moreover, prohibiting prayer at those times falls under blocking the means to harm, lest resemblance to the polytheists lead to associating partners with Allah. Whatever is prohibited to block a means to harm, rather than because it is harmful in itself, becomes prescribed when it offers an outweighing benefit. A benefit should not be forfeited unless the harm outweighs it. Prayer offered to Allah at those times is not itself harmful; rather, it is a means that may lead to harm. If the benefit cannot be attained except through that means, it becomes prescribed, and it suffices to [prohibit] it where no such benefit is involved. This is the case with unrestricted voluntary prayer: prohibiting it neither causes harm nor forfeits a benefit, since it can be performed at other times.

This is a principle upheld by Ahmad and others: whatever falls under “blocking the means to harm” is prohibited only when it is not needed. When, however, it is needed to secure a benefit that cannot be attained except through it, it may nevertheless be prohibited. Hence, in contracts, a distinction is drawn between legal stratagems and blocking the means to harm: the person employing a stratagem

---

## Page 11874

intends a forbidden outcome, so this is prohibited. As for a means that may lead to something forbidden, the person employing it does not intend that outcome. He is prohibited from using it when he has no need for it, but not when he needs it. Mālik, however, takes blocking such means so far as to prohibit them even when they are needed.

All “prayers with specific causes” are missed if postponed until after the prohibited time. These include the prostration of recitation, the prayer upon entering a mosque, the eclipse prayer, and prayer immediately after purification, as mentioned in the hadith of Bilāl. The same applies to the prayer for guidance when the matter for which guidance is sought would be missed if the prayer were delayed.

Likewise, there is the prayer of repentance. When someone sins, he must repent immediately, and he is encouraged to offer two units of prayer and then repent, as mentioned in the hadith of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq. Other examples include making up the regular sunnah prayers, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, made up the two sunnah units of Zuhr after ‘Asr, and approved a man’s making up the two sunnah units of Fajr after the Fajr prayer. Although these prayers can be delayed, doing so forfeits the benefit of making them up promptly. Making up missed prayers immediately is prescribed: obligatory for obligatory prayers and recommended for recommended prayers.

Al-Shāfi'ī permits making up prayers during the prohibited times, even though he does not require that they be made up immediately, because they are prayers with specific causes. Yet these prayers are not lost with the passing of

---

## Page 11875

the prescribed time; what is lost, however, is the merit of performing them sooner and discharging one’s obligation. Similarly, a person without clothing or one who has performed dry ablution may pray at the beginning of the prescribed time, even if he could pray at its end with ablution and proper covering. In obligatory prayer, however, he needs to discharge his obligation; with the regular sunnah prayers, he needs to make up any deficiencies in his obligatory prayer, for these sunnah prayers complement it. He also needs to avoid prolonging the delay: he is commanded to perform these prayers at their appointed time, so performing them closer to that time comes nearer to fulfilling the command than performing them later. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

Thus, he should perform them as close to their appointed time as he can. Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī permits performing the regular sunnah prayers during the prohibited times, agreeing with Abū al-Khattāb. Abū al-Khattāb, however, extends this permission generally, as does al-Shāfi‘ī, and this is the correct view.

If it is asked, “But does someone who intends to fill all his time with prayer not miss out on unrestricted voluntary prayer?”

The answer is: this is not prescribed; rather, it is prohibited. No human being can pray continuously throughout the day and night. He must have time to rest and sleep. It is established in the two Sahīh collections: “One of a group of men said, ‘I will fast and never break my fast.’ Another said, ‘I will stand in prayer and never sleep.’ Another said, ‘I will not marry women.’ Another said, ‘I will not eat meat.’ The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

---

## Page 11876

‘But I fast and break my fast, stand in prayer and sleep, marry women and eat meat. Whoever turns away from my sunnah is not of me.’”

Indeed, it has been said that part of the wisdom behind prohibiting unrestricted voluntary prayer at certain times is to give people a respite during those periods, so that they return to prayer with renewed energy. They are drawn to what they have been prevented from doing and become eager to pray after resting. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11877

He was asked:

“Someone saw a man offering voluntary prayer at a prohibited time and told him, ‘The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade prayer at this time.’ The relevant hadith expressing disapproval of this was cited to him, but the man replied, ‘I will not listen to it; I shall pray however I wish.’ What should be done with him?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

As for voluntary prayer without a specific occasion, the imams agree that it is forbidden after the dawn prayer until sunrise and after the afternoon prayer until sunset. ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb used to strike those who prayed after the afternoon prayer. Whoever does this is therefore subject to discretionary punishment, following the precedent established by ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, since hadiths prohibiting this have been transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, through numerous independent chains.

As for prayers prompted by a specific occasion, such as the prayer of greeting the mosque and the eclipse prayer, these are subject to scholarly disagreement and interpretation. If he is offering a prayer concerning which there is legitimate scope for scholarly judgment, he is not to be punished.

As for his rejection of the hadiths without evidence, his insulting the person who forbade him, and his saying to that person:

---

## Page 11878

“I shall pray however I wish”—he is subject to discretionary punishment for this, since a man must pray as prescribed for him, not however he wishes. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“If a man enters the mosque at a prohibited time, may he offer the prayer of greeting the mosque?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

The scholars hold two views on this issue, both of which are reported from Ahmad. The first, which is the view of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, is that he should not offer it. The second, which is the view of al-Shāfi‘ī, is that he should offer it. This latter view is stronger, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When one of you enters the mosque, he should not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.”

This command applies to all times, and no case is known to have been excepted from it. As for his prohibition of prayer after dawn breaks and after sunset, several cases have been excepted from it. These include making up missed prayers, the two rak‘ahs following circumambulation of the Kaaba, repeating a prayer with the local imam, and other cases. A general text from which no exceptions have been made takes precedence over a general text from which exceptions have been made.

---

## Page 11879

Moreover, prayer during the sermon is prohibited just as it is at these two times, or even more emphatically so. Yet it is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When one of you enters the mosque while the preacher is on the pulpit, he should not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.”

Since he commanded the prayer of greeting the mosque at this time, when prayer is prohibited, the same applies all the more to the other prohibited time. Ahmad’s position on this did not vary, because it is established by the authentic Sunnah. This differs from the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, who held that it is prohibited in both circumstances, for this authentic Sunnah had not reached them. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Should the prayer of greeting the mosque be offered at the prohibited times or not?”

He answered:

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When one of you enters the mosque, he should not sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.”

If someone enters at a prohibited time, should he pray? The scholars hold two views, but the stronger view is that he should pray. Many exceptions have been made to the Prophet’s prohibition of prayer after Fajr and after ‘Asr. An exception has also been made to the comparable prohibition during the sermon, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When one of you enters the mosque while the imam is delivering the sermon, he should not

---

## Page 11880

sit down until he has prayed two rak‘ahs.” If he commanded the prayer of greeting the mosque during the sermon, then there is all the more reason to offer it at these times. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“If a man performs ablution before sunrise and before sunset, having already prayed Fajr, may he offer a prayer in gratitude for his ablution?”

He answered:

There is disagreement on this matter. The more appropriate view is that he should do so, on the strength of the hadith of Bilāl.

---

## Page 11881

Chapter: Congregational Prayer

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is congregational prayer an individual obligation, a collective obligation, or a sunnah? If it is an individual obligation and someone prays alone without an excuse, is his prayer valid or not? What are the scholars’ views on this matter, what evidence does each cite, and which view is strongest?”

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

The scholars agree that congregational prayer is among the most emphatically prescribed acts of worship, the noblest acts of obedience, and the greatest outward observances of Islam. They also agree upon its merit as authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“A man’s prayer in congregation exceeds his prayer alone by twenty-five degrees.”

This is how it is narrated in the hadiths of Abū Hurayrah and Abū Saʿīd: twenty-five degrees. The hadith of Ibn ʿUmar mentions twenty-seven. All three are recorded in the Sahīh. The two figures have been reconciled by explaining that the hadith mentioning twenty-five refers to the additional merit

---

## Page 11882

between prayer offered alone and prayer in congregation; this additional merit amounts to twenty-five degrees. The hadith mentioning twenty-seven counts his prayer alone, his prayer in congregation, and the difference in merit between them, making a total of twenty-seven.

Any devotee who imagines that praying alone—whether in seclusion or otherwise—is better is mistaken and astray. Even further astray is someone who recognizes congregational prayer only when led by the infallible imam, thus depriving the mosques of all the congregational worship commanded by Allah and His Messenger, while filling them with innovations and deviations forbidden by Allah and His Messenger. He thereby resembles someone who forbids worshipping the Most Merciful and commands worshipping idols.

For Allah, glorified is He, has prescribed prayer and other acts of worship in mosques. As He, Most High, says:

“Who is more unjust than one who prevents Allah’s name from being mentioned in His mosques and strives to bring about their ruin?”

And He, Most High, says:

“Do not have relations with them while you are observing a devotional retreat in the mosques.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of prayer.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain Allah’s mosques,”

through to His words:

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakāh, and fear none but Allah maintain Allah’s mosques. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

And He, Most High, says:

“In houses which Allah has permitted to be raised and His name to be remembered, He is glorified morning and evening by men whom neither commerce nor sale distracts from the remembrance of Allah”—to the end of the verse.

And He, Most High, says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

---

## Page 11883

And He, Most High, says:

“And mosques in which Allah’s name is mentioned abundantly.”

As for grave shrines and similar sites, the leading scholars of the Muslims agree that singling them out for prayer, supplication, or other acts of worship is no part of Islam. Whoever believes that prayer, supplication, and remembrance of Allah at such sites are better than in mosques has disbelieved. Indeed, the prohibition against using them for such purposes is established by mass-transmitted Prophetic reports.

Thus, it is established in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

He was warning against what they had done. ʿĀ’ishah said: “Were it not for that, he would have left his grave in the open, but he disliked its being made a place of prayer.”

The two Sahīh collections also report that a church in Abyssinia, its beauty, and the images within it were mentioned to him. He said:

“When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of prayer over his grave and make those images within it. They will be the worst of creation before Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

It is also established in Sahīh Muslim, in the hadith of Jundub, that five days before his death he said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Beware! Do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.”

The Musnad reports that he said:

“Among the worst of creation are those who are alive when the Hour overtakes them, and those who take graves as places of prayer.”

Mālik’s Muwattaʾ reports that he said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped. Allah’s wrath was intense against people who took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

The Sunan collections report that he said:

“Do not make

---

## Page 11884

my grave a place of festivity, and send blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.”

The point here is that the leading scholars of the Muslims agree that performing the five daily prayers in mosques is among the greatest acts of worship and the noblest means of drawing near to Allah. Whoever considers foregoing them better than performing them, preferring seclusion and solitude to the five daily prayers in congregation, or regards supplication and prayer at grave shrines as better than in mosques, has cast off the bonds of religion and followed a path other than that of the believers.

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows a path other than that of the believers, We will leave him to what he has chosen and burn him in Hell—what an evil destination!”

Beyond this, however, scholars have differed over whether congregational prayer is an individual obligation, a collective obligation, or an emphasized Sunnah. There are three views:

One view is that it is only an emphasized Sunnah. This is the position commonly attributed to the followers of Abū Hanīfah, most followers of Mālik, and many followers of al-Shāfiʿī. It is also mentioned as one reported position of Ahmad.

Another view is that it is a collective obligation. This is the preferred position in the school of al-Shāfiʿī, the view of some followers of Mālik, and one view within the school of Ahmad.

A third view is that it is an individual obligation. This is the position explicitly stated by Ahmad

---

## Page 11885

and other leading scholars of the early generations, jurists versed in hadith, and others. Those who hold this view differ over whether the prayer of someone who prays alone without an excuse is valid. They have two positions. The first is that it is invalid. This was the view of a group of Ahmad’s early followers, as reported from them by Judge Abū Ya‘lā in Sharh al-Madhhab, and of some of their later scholars, such as Ibn ‘Aqīl. It was also the view of a group of the early generations and was adopted by Ibn Hazm and others.

The second is that his prayer is valid, although he sins by abandoning congregational prayer. This is the position transmitted from Ahmad and held by most of his followers.

Those who deny that congregational prayer is obligatory cite the Prophet’s statement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that congregational prayer is superior to a man’s prayer alone. They argue: “If it were obligatory, the prayer of someone praying alone would be invalid, and there would be no comparison of merit.” They interpret the reports concerning those whom the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—intended to burn as referring either to those who abandoned Friday prayer or to the hypocrites who stayed away from congregational prayer while harbouring hypocrisy. They maintain that burning them would have been on account of their hypocrisy, not because they missed congregational prayer while praying in their homes.

Those who hold it to be obligatory cite the Quran, the Sunnah, and reports from the early generations.

As for the Quran, Allah Most High says:

“When you are among them and lead them in prayer, let a group of them stand with you”—to the end of the verse.

This verse contains two proofs:

---

## Page 11886

The first is that He commanded them to pray in congregation with him during the prayer in times of fear. This proves that congregational prayer is obligatory in times of fear and, by stronger reason, that it is obligatory in times of safety.

The second is that He prescribed the prayer in times of fear in congregation and permitted within it things that are otherwise impermissible without an excuse, such as turning one’s back to the qiblah and making extensive movements. These are impermissible without an excuse by scholarly consensus. The same applies, according to the majority, to separating from the imam before the concluding salutation, and likewise to falling behind in following the imam—as the rear row delays after bowing with the imam when the enemy is in front of them.

They argue: These actions invalidate the prayer if performed without an excuse. If congregational prayer were merely recommended rather than obligatory, this would entail committing a prohibited act that invalidates the prayer and abandoning the obligatory following of the imam for the sake of something recommended—even though they could have prayed individually, each performing a complete prayer. It is therefore established that congregational prayer is obligatory.

Furthermore, Allah Most High says:

“Establish prayer, give zakah, and bow with those who bow.”

This means either performing the act together—that is, praying in congregation—or what is intended by His words:

“And be with the truthful.”

If the second meaning were intended, there would be no difference between saying, “Pray with those who pray,” “Fast with those who fast,” and “Bow with those who bow.” Yet the context indicates that bowing is specifically singled out in this regard.

---

## Page 11887

If it is objected, “But the entire prayer is performed in congregation,” the answer is: Bowing was specifically mentioned because it is through bowing that one catches the prayer. Whoever catches the bowing thereby catches the prostration. He therefore commanded that by which one catches a unit of prayer, just as He said to Mary:

“Be devoutly obedient to your Lord, prostrate, and bow with those who bow.”

Had He said, “Be devoutly obedient with those who are devoutly obedient,” this would have indicated an obligation to join in time for the standing. Had He said, “Prostrate,” this would not have indicated an obligation to join in time for the bowing. By contrast, His words, “And bow with those who bow,” indicate a command to join in time for the bowing and what follows it, but not what precedes it. This is precisely the point.

(As for the Sunnah, there are the widely reported hadiths on this subject, such as the hadith of Abū Hurayrah, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have considered ordering that the prayer be commenced, then appointing a man to lead the people in prayer, and then going to people who do not attend the prayer and burning down their houses with them inside.”

Thus, he contemplated burning those who did not attend the prayer. In another wording, he said:

“The most burdensome prayers for the hypocrites are the ‘Ishā’ and Fajr prayers. If they knew what reward they contain, they would come to them even if they had to crawl. I have considered ordering that the prayer be commenced…”

The hadith continues. In the Musnad and elsewhere, it reads:

“Were it not for the women and children in the houses, I would order that the prayer be commenced…”

The hadith continues. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained that he had contemplated burning down the houses with those who did not attend the prayer inside them. He also explained that what prevented him from doing so was solely the presence of women and children, for they are not obliged to attend the prayer. Burning down the houses would have killed people whom it was not permissible to kill. This would have been comparable to carrying out the prescribed punishment on a pregnant woman.

---

## Page 11888

And He, glorified and exalted be He, has said:

“Were it not for believing men and believing women whom you did not know, whom you might have trampled underfoot, thereby unknowingly incurring guilt on their account—so that Allah might admit into His mercy whomever He wills—had they been separated, We would surely have inflicted a painful punishment upon those among them who disbelieved.”

As for those who interpret this as referring to failure to attend the Friday prayer, the context of the hadith shows the weakness of their view: he mentioned the ‘Ishā’ and Fajr prayers, then followed this by expressing his intention to burn those who did not attend the prayer.

As for those who interpret the punishment as being for hypocrisy rather than for failure to attend the prayer, their view is weak for several reasons:

First, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would excuse the hypocrites only with regard to inward matters; he would punish them for their outward failure to fulfil an obligation or their commission of a forbidden act. Had their conduct not involved neglecting an obligation, he would not have burned them.

(Second, he made the punishment consequent upon failure to attend the prayer. The ruling must therefore be linked to the reason he specified.

Third, the hadith of Ibn Umm Maktūm will be cited later, Allah willing. He asked the Prophet for permission to pray in his home, but he did not grant him permission. Ibn Umm Maktūm was a believer, one of the finest believers, whom the Quran praised, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to

---

## Page 11889

appoint him as his deputy in Medina, and he would give the call to prayer for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Fourth, this is also evidence that congregational prayer is obligatory. It is established in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere that ‘Abdullāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“Whoever wishes to meet Allah tomorrow as a Muslim should perform these five prayers where the call to them is made. Allah has prescribed for His Prophet the ways of guidance, and performing these five prayers in the mosques where the call to them is made is among the ways of guidance. If you were to pray in your homes as this man who stays away prays in his home, you would abandon the way of your Prophet; and if you abandoned the way of your Prophet, you would go astray. I remember a time when none of us would stay away from them except a hypocrite whose hypocrisy was known. Indeed, a man would be brought in, supported between two men, until he was made to stand in the row.”

‘Abdullāh ibn Mas‘ūd thus stated that no one would stay away from them except a hypocrite whose hypocrisy was known. This demonstrates that their obligatory nature was firmly established among the believers, and they could only have learned this from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Had they regarded congregational prayer as merely recommended, like night prayer, the voluntary prayers accompanying the obligatory prayers, the midmorning prayer, and the like, some of them would have performed it and others would not, while remaining believers. Thus, the Bedouin said to him:

“By Allah, I will neither add to that nor subtract from it.”

He replied, “He will prosper if he is truthful.”

It is well known that any duty from which none but a hypocrite would stay away was obligatory upon every individual, as was their departure for the expedition to Tabūk, for the Prophet

---

## Page 11890

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded all the Muslims to join it and permitted no one to stay behind except those who stated that they had an excuse, whom he excused on that account. Then, when he returned, Allah exposed the hypocrites’ secrets, stripped away their concealment, and made clear that they had stayed behind without an excuse. Those who stayed behind without an excuse while remaining believers were punished by being shunned—even by their wives—until Allah accepted their repentance.

(If it is said: “Then today you judge those who stay away from congregational prayer to be hypocrites, and permit houses to be burned down upon them if there are no children inside.”

The answer is:

There are acts that are obligatory, yet a person’s interpretation exempts him from the penalty. Today, many believers do not consider congregational prayer obligatory upon them, and so neglect it on the basis of an interpretation. In the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, no one had such an interpretation available to him, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had directly commanded them to attend.

Furthermore, it is established in the Sahīh and the Sunan collections:

“A blind man asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for permission to pray at home, and he granted it. When the man turned to leave, he called him back and asked, ‘Do you hear the call to prayer?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Then respond to it.’”

Thus, he commanded him to respond when he heard the call. For this reason, Ahmad held congregational prayer to be obligatory upon anyone who hears the call. According to another wording in the Sunan collections:

“Ibn Umm Maktūm said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, my home is far away, Medina has many harmful creatures, and I have a guide who is ill-suited to my needs. Can you grant me a concession to

---

## Page 11891

pray in my home?’ He asked, ‘Do you hear the call to prayer?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘I can grant you no concession.’” This explicitly establishes the obligation of congregational prayer, even though the man was a believer.

As for their argument based on a man’s prayer in congregation being superior to his prayer alone, there are two responses, turning on the validity of praying alone without an excuse. Those who consider his prayer valid say:

Congregational prayer is obligatory, but it is not a condition for the prayer’s validity. The same applies to the prayer’s timing: if someone delays the ‘Asr prayer until the sunlight turns yellow, he is sinful, yet his prayer remains valid. Indeed, the same applies even if he delays it until only enough time for one unit of prayer remains, as established in the Sahīh:

“Whoever catches one unit of the ‘Asr prayer has caught the ‘Asr prayer.”

They further argue that describing one act as superior does not establish that the inferior act is permissible. Allah Most High says:

“When the call is made for prayer on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of Allah and leave off trade. That is better for you.”

Thus, He describes hastening to the Friday prayer as better than trading, although hastening to it is obligatory and trading is forbidden. Allah Most High also says:

“Tell the believing men to lower their gaze and guard their chastity. That is purer for them.”

Those who hold that prayer performed alone is invalid unless there is an excuse cite the evidence establishing the obligation of congregational prayer. They argue that whatever is established as obligatory in prayer is a condition for its validity, like all its other obligatory elements.

---

## Page 11892

As for the prescribed time, it cannot be recovered once lost: when it has passed, the prayer can no longer be performed within it. The analogous case is missing the Friday prayer or a congregational prayer that cannot be attended later. If someone misses the obligatory Friday prayer, he is sinful and must pray Zuhr, since nothing else is possible. Likewise, someone who misses the congregational prayer he is obliged to attend, when no other congregation is available, prays alone. His prayer is valid in this case because he cannot pray in congregation, just as the Zuhr prayer is valid for someone who misses the Friday prayer. The obligation of congregational prayer is no greater than that of Friday prayer.

The issue under discussion concerns someone who prays alone at home without an excuse, after which the congregational prayer begins. According to them, he must attend the congregation, just as someone who prays Zuhr before the Friday prayer must still attend the Friday prayer. They cite as evidence the hadith of Abū Hurayrah in the Sunan collections, reporting that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever hears the call and then fails to respond without an excuse has no prayer.”

This is further supported by the saying:

“There is no prayer for one who lives near the mosque except in the mosque.”

This is well known as a saying of ‘Alī, ‘Ā’ishah, Abū Hurayrah and Ibn ‘Umar. Al-Dāraqutnī also narrated it as traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and some hadith masters lent support to that attribution.

They said: Nowhere in the speech of Allah or His Messenger is a negation known to be applied to an act prescribed by religion except because something obligatory within it has been omitted, as in his sayings:

“There is no prayer without the Opening Chapter of the Quran,”

and:

“There is no faith in one who is not trustworthy,”

and other such statements.

---

## Page 11893

These scholars responded to the hadith concerning the superiority of congregational prayer by saying that it applies to someone with a valid excuse, such as illness. It is comparable to the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The prayer of one who sits is worth half the prayer of one who stands, and the prayer of one who lies down is worth half the prayer of one who sits.”

His declaring a man’s prayer in congregation superior to his prayer alone is like his declaring prayer standing superior to prayer sitting. It is well known that standing is obligatory in obligatory prayers, but not in voluntary prayers, just as congregation is obligatory for obligatory prayers, but not for voluntary prayers.

To discuss this more fully: scholars differed over this hadith, holding two views as to whether the two reports concern someone with a valid excuse or someone without one.

One group said that they concern someone without a valid excuse. They argued that someone with a valid excuse receives his full reward, citing the report established in the two Sahīh collections from Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, the deeds he used to perform while healthy and resident are recorded for him.”

They said: If the sick person and the traveller have recorded for them what they used to do while healthy and resident, how can the prayer of someone with a valid excuse, performed sitting or alone, be inferior to his prayer seated in congregation?

These scholars understood the superiority of prayer standing to concern voluntary prayers, not obligatory prayers, because standing is obligatory in obligatory prayers. Whoever adopts this view must consequently allow a healthy person to perform voluntary prayers lying down, since it is established that he said:

“Whoever prays sitting receives half the reward of one who stands.”

Indeed,

---

## Page 11894

a group of later scholars of the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad followed this argument through consistently and permitted a man to perform voluntary prayers lying down without a valid excuse, on the strength of this hadith and because it cannot be understood as referring to a sick person, as explained above. Most scholars, however, rejected this and considered it an innovation and a novelty introduced into Islam.

They said:

No one in Islam is known ever to have prayed lying on his side while healthy. Had this been prescribed, Muslims would have done it during their Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or afterwards. The Prophet himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have done it at least once to demonstrate its permissibility. He used to perform voluntary prayers sitting and pray on his mount in whichever direction it was heading, and he would perform the witr prayer on it, though he did not perform obligatory prayers on it. Had this been permissible, he would have done it at least once, or his Companions would have done so.

Despite the strength of their argument, those among these scholars who rejected this practice but did not regard congregational prayer as obligatory contradicted themselves. They understood his saying:

“Prayer in congregation is twenty-five degrees superior to a man’s prayer alone”

to refer to someone without a valid excuse. They may therefore be asked: Why does the superiority mentioned here concern someone without a valid excuse, while the superiority mentioned there concerns someone with a valid excuse? Is this not a contradiction?
