As for those who regarded congregational prayer as obligatory and understood the stated superiority to concern someone with a valid excuse, they applied their argument consistently. In that case, the hadith provides no evidence for the validity of praying alone without a valid excuse.

---

## Page 11895

As for their opponent’s appeal to his saying:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, he is credited with the deeds he used to perform while healthy and not travelling.”

Their response is that this hadith indicates that he is credited with the same reward he used to receive when healthy and not travelling, because he intends to perform those deeds but is prevented by a valid excuse. This is a “principle of the Sharia”: whoever firmly resolves to perform a deed and does whatever he can of it is counted as one who performs it. Thus, someone who regularly performed a deed while healthy and not travelling, remains resolved to perform it, and does whatever he can during illness or travel is counted as one who performs it.

Likewise, the Sunan collections relate that someone who purifies himself at home, then goes to the mosque to catch the congregational prayer but finds that he has missed it, is credited with the reward of praying in congregation. It is also established in the Sahīh that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are men in Medina who have been with you on every journey you have made and in every valley you have crossed.” They asked, “Even though they are in Medina?” He replied, “Even though they are in Medina; a valid excuse has held them back.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Those believers who remain behind—other than those with disabilities—are not equal to those who strive in Allah’s cause with their wealth and their lives,” to the end of the verse.

This and similar evidence make clear that someone with a valid excuse is credited with the same reward as a healthy person if he intends to perform the deed and has done whatever he can. This does not mean that his deed itself is equivalent to that of a healthy person. The hadith does not state that a sick person’s prayer is, in itself, equal in reward to a healthy person’s prayer, nor that the prayer of someone praying alone with a valid excuse is, in itself, equivalent to a man’s prayer in congregation.

---

## Page 11896

but only that he is credited with the deeds he used to perform while healthy and not travelling, just as he is credited with the reward of congregational prayer when he misses it despite intending to attend.

Moreover, not everyone with a valid excuse is credited with the equivalent of a healthy person’s deeds. This is granted only when he intends to perform those deeds but is unable to do so. Thus, the hadith indicates that someone who habitually prayed in congregation and prayed standing, but then ceased doing so because of illness, is credited with what he used to do while healthy and not travelling. Likewise, someone who offers voluntary prayers on his mount while travelling, having previously offered them standing when not travelling, is credited with what he used to do when not travelling. However, someone who was not accustomed to praying in congregation or standing, and who then falls ill and prays alone or seated, is not credited with the equivalent of the prayer of a healthy person who is not travelling. Anyone who applies the hadith to someone without a valid excuse must consequently regard his prayer while seated as equivalent to prayer while standing, and his prayer alone as equivalent to prayer in congregation. This is a false position, supported by neither a scriptural text nor analogical reasoning, and no one has maintained it.

Furthermore, it may be said:

The Prophet’s declaring prayer in congregation superior to prayer alone, prayer while standing superior to prayer while seated, and prayer while seated superior to prayer while lying down—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—indicates only the superiority of one prayer over the other where both prayers are valid.

---

## Page 11897

As for whether the less meritorious prayer is valid wherever the other prayer is valid, the hadith neither affirms nor denies this. Nor was the hadith intended to explain when prayer is valid or invalid. Rather, the requirements to stand or sit, and when these requirements are waived, as well as the requirement to pray in congregation and when it is waived, must be established from other evidence.

Likewise, this hadith does not address whether a person with a valid excuse is credited with the full measure of his deeds; that must be established from other hadiths. The other scriptural texts have made clear that the full reward is granted to someone who used to perform the more meritorious deed while healthy and not travelling, not to everyone.

Other texts establish the obligation to stand in obligatory prayer, such as the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to ʿImrān ibn Husayn:

“Pray standing; if you cannot, then seated; and if you cannot, then lying on your side.”

He also demonstrated the permissibility of offering voluntary prayers seated: when he saw people praying seated, he approved of their doing so. He himself would pray seated, in addition to offering voluntary prayers on his mount while travelling.

Similarly, other texts establish the obligation to pray in congregation. Each hadith must therefore be given its due: there is no contradiction or incompatibility between them. Only someone who makes them bear meanings they do not indicate, and fails to give them their due through faulty examination and interpretation, imagines such contradiction and incompatibility. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11898

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about questions that frequently arise and involve trials, difficulty, and hardship when following the opinion of a particular imam. Among them was the question of congregational prayer: Is it obligatory or recommended? If we say it is obligatory, is prayer valid without it when one is able to attend?

He replied:

As for congregational prayer, some have said that it is recommended, others that it is a collective obligation, and others that it is an individual obligation. This last view is what the Quran and Sunnah indicate. Allah commanded it in circumstances of fear, so in circumstances of safety it is all the more binding and emphatic. Allah Most High also said:

“And bow with those who bow.”

This is a command to pray in congregation. It is also established in the Sahīh:

“Ibn Umm Maktūm asked the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to permit him to pray at home. He asked, ‘Do you hear the call to prayer?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Then respond to it.’ In another narration: ‘I find no concession for you.’”

Ibn Umm Maktūm was a righteous man, and concerning him Allah Most High revealed His words:

---

## Page 11899

“He frowned and turned away because the blind man came to him.”

He was one of the Emigrants, and none of the Emigrants stayed away from congregational prayer except a hypocrite. It thus becomes clear that a believer has no concession to abandon it.

It is also established in the authentic hadith collections that he said:

“I have indeed considered ordering the prayer to be commenced, then appointing a man to lead the people in prayer, then setting out with men carrying bundles of firewood to people who do not attend the prayer, and burning their houses down upon them.”

Another narration states:

“Were it not for the women and children in the houses…”

He thus explained that the only thing preventing him from burning those who stayed away from congregational prayer was the presence of women and children in the houses. Punishing them is impermissible, since congregational prayer is not obligatory upon them.

The claim that this concerned the Friday prayer, or that it was on account of their hypocrisy, is weak. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did not kill the hypocrites on account of their hypocrisy; rather, he would punish them only for an outwardly manifest sin. Had staying away from congregational prayer not been a sin deserving punishment, he would not have punished them. The hadith itself makes clear that it concerns staying away from the night and dawn prayers. The hadith of Ibn Umm Maktūm has already been mentioned, along with the fact that he was not granted permission to stay away from congregational prayer.

Furthermore, most of the obligatory elements of prayer are set aside for the sake of congregational prayer, both in the prayer of fear and in other circumstances. Were congregational prayer not obligatory, setting aside some obligations for its sake would not have been commanded, for one is not commanded to set aside obligations for something that is not obligatory.

---

## Page 11900

Section

If someone stays away from congregational prayer without an excuse, there are two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

The first is that his prayer is valid, based on the Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A man’s prayer in congregation surpasses his prayer alone by twenty-five degrees.”

The second is that his prayer is invalid, based on the report in the Sunan collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever hears the call and then fails to respond without an excuse has no prayer.”

And on his saying:

“One who lives near a mosque has no prayer except in the mosque.”

ʿAbd al-Haqq al-Ishbīlī regarded this report as strong. Moreover, if congregational prayer is obligatory, then whoever omits an obligatory element of prayer has not performed a valid prayer. The hadith concerning its superior merit is understood to refer to circumstances in which there is an excuse, as in his saying:

“The prayer of one who sits is worth half the prayer of one who stands, and the prayer of one who stands is worth half the prayer of

---

## Page 11901

one who sits.” This applies generally to both obligatory and voluntary prayers. A person may not perform an obligatory prayer sitting or lying down unless he has an excuse. Nor, according to the overwhelming majority of earlier and later scholars, may he offer voluntary prayers lying down, apart from one view within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. It is well known that offering voluntary prayers while lying down is an innovation that none of the early generations practised.

The Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“When a servant falls ill or travels, the deeds he used to perform while healthy and resident are recorded for him,”

indicates that this reward is recorded for him on account of his intention, even though he is not performing the deeds he habitually performed before his illness or journey. This means that someone who misses congregational prayer because of illness or travel, but habitually attended it, has the reward of congregational prayer recorded for him. If he did not habitually attend it, that reward is not recorded for him, although in either case the act itself earns him the reward of praying alone. The same applies to a sick person who prays sitting or lying down.

According to this view, if a man prays alone and can subsequently pray in congregation, he should do so. If he cannot pray in congregation, he should seek Allah’s forgiveness, like someone who misses the Friday prayer and prays Zuhr instead. If a man sets out intending to join the congregation but finds that they have already prayed, he receives the reward of someone who prayed in congregation, as established in the Sunnah of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

If he catches one rak‘ah with the imam, he has caught the congregational prayer; if he catches less

---

## Page 11902

than one rak‘ah, he receives the reward of congregational prayer on account of his intention. But is he considered to have caught the congregation, or is he regarded as someone who prayed alone? Scholars within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad hold two views on this.

The first is that he is regarded as someone who prayed in congregation, which is also the view of Abū Hanīfah.

The second is that he is regarded as someone who prayed alone, which is also Mālik’s view. This is the sounder position, because it is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever catches one rak‘ah of the prayer has caught the prayer.”

For this reason, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Mālik and the majority of scholars say that a person has not caught the Friday prayer unless he catches one rak‘ah of it. Abū Hanīfah and those who agree with him, however, say that he has caught it if he joins them during the tashahhud.

One practical consequence of this disagreement concerns a traveller praying behind a resident imam. If he catches one rak‘ah, he must perform the prayer in full. If he catches less than one rak‘ah, the two views mentioned above apply.

The correct view is that a person has caught neither the Friday prayer nor the congregational prayer unless he catches one rak‘ah. Anything less does not count for him; he performs it only to follow the imam. Even if it is after the salām, he is like someone praying alone, by agreement of the imams.

---

## Page 11903

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

As for congregational prayer, follow what the Book, the Sunnah and the statements of the Companions indicate: it is obligatory in the absence of a valid excuse, and its obligation is waived when there is such an excuse.

Likewise, give precedence to imams according to the criteria established by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah should lead the people in prayer. If they are equal in recitation, then the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah; if they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then the one who emigrated earliest.”

Thus, a distinction is made between knowledge of the Book and knowledge of the Sunnah, as the hadith indicates. Giving some imams precedence over others on these grounds applies only when they are equal in knowing how to perform the prayer as prescribed, in performing it according to the Sunnah, and in possessing the religious integrity needed to ensure that the follower’s prayer behind them is not deficient.

If two imams are equal in the completeness of their own prayers and of the prayers offered behind them, precedence is given to the one most proficient in recitation, then to the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. Otherwise, excellence in the prayer itself takes precedence over a quality possessed by its imam, and the knowledge and religious integrity required for the prayer take precedence over such qualities as are merely recommended.

---

## Page 11904

Others may hold that it is a strongly emphasized Sunnah, or that it is a communal obligation. They also differ over who should take precedence in leading the prayer.

He instructs them to arrange their rows as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded, observing five Sunnah practices: straightening the rows, standing closely together within them, bringing the rows close together, filling the first row before the next, and positioning the imam centrally. This includes forbidding what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade—praying alone behind the row—and ordering anyone who does so to repeat the prayer, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded in two reliably established hadiths. He ordered the person praying alone behind the row to repeat his prayer, just as he ordered the man who prayed improperly to repeat his prayer, and the man who performed his ablution improperly, leaving a fingernail-sized area of his foot untouched by water, to repeat it. These cases establish that ritual purity, standing in rows and performing the pillars of prayer are requirements for its validity.

Among those who did not follow the hadith concerning someone praying alone behind the row, such as Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī, were some whom it had not reached or who did not consider its authenticity established. Al-Shāfi‘ī regarded it as contradicted by the fact that the imam prays alone, by the fact that Mulayka, the grandmother of Anas, prayed behind them, and by the hadith of Abū Bakra, who bowed before reaching the row.

As for Ahmad, his principle concerning hadiths that appear to conflict in two similar but nonidentical cases is to apply each hadith to its proper circumstances and not reject either one

---

## Page 11905

on the strength of the other.

In a case like this, he says: When a woman is with other women, she prays among them. But when she is with men, she prays only behind them, even if she is alone, because she is forbidden to stand alongside men in their rows. Standing apart from the men is therefore more appropriate for her than joining their rows. Likewise, when she leads women in prayer, she stands among them because this affords her greater concealment, just as someone leading unclothed people in prayer stands among them, even though the Sunnah for a clothed man leading the prayer is to stand ahead of the row.

We say: The imam is not in the same position as those following him. His prescribed practice is to stand ahead, not alongside others in a row, whereas theirs is to form rows. The fact that the imam and the woman stand alone does, however, indicate that a man following the imam may stand alone when there is a need—namely, when he can find no place to pray except on his own. This follows by analogy from the position of Ahmad and others. Moreover, obligations in prayer and elsewhere are waived when valid excuses exist. Forming rows is only one of the obligations of prayer, so inability to do so waives it in congregational prayer, just as other obligations are waived, whether they concern the congregation or the prayer itself.

For this reason, maintaining congregational prayer in circumstances of fear, illness and the like—even when this involves turning away from the qiblah, considerable movement, ceasing to follow the imam, or a sick person omitting standing—is preferable to praying individually. Accordingly, some of Ahmad’s followers held that a person following the imam may stand ahead of him when necessary, as in crowded conditions and the like, although this is not permissible without such a need. This has also been reported among the descriptions of the prayer in times of fear. For this reason, he and other imams of the Sunnah waived certain requirements of congregational prayer, such as

---

## Page 11906

the imam’s uprightness, the lawfulness of the pledge of allegiance and similar requirements, when there is a need. Thus, they permitted—and indeed required—performing Friday prayers, the two Eid prayers, prayers in times of fear, pilgrimage rites and the like behind sinful imams and in unlawfully seized places, if refraining would lead to abandoning Friday and congregational prayers, discord within the Muslim community, or similar consequences. As stated in the hadith of Jābir:

“A wicked man must not lead a believer in prayer unless the latter is compelled by a ruler whose sword or whip he fears.”

For, at most, the imam’s uprightness is an obligatory requirement, which is waived when a valid excuse exists, just as many obligations are waived for valid excuses in congregational prayer in times of fear.

Whoever understands this principle—that even the obligations of prayer itself are waived for valid excuses, and likewise the requirements of congregational prayers and similar acts—has been guided to the middle course taught by the Sunnah. This lies between entirely neglecting some obligations of the Sharia, as some people may fall into doing, and insisting so excessively upon a particular obligation that, when unable to fulfil it, one abandons other, more binding obligations despite being able to fulfil them, as others may fall into doing. Fulfilling what one can, rather than what lies beyond one’s ability, is the middle course between these two extremes.

This principle also underlies questions of migration and resolve, which form the basis of “the issue of leadership,” where the action is not performed and capacity is insufficient. Accordingly, Ahmad, in an explicitly reported statement, and a group of his followers held that someone offering an obligatory prayer may, when needed, follow an imam offering a voluntary prayer, as in prayer in times of fear, or when the person offering the obligatory prayer is unable to recite, as in

---

## Page 11907

the hadith of ʿAmr ibn Salamah and Muʿādh, and similar cases. Yet, according to one of the two views reported from him, he does not permit this without a need. If, however, he permits it without restriction, there is no issue. Some of his followers do not permit it under any circumstances. Thus, there are three positions within his school and other schools. Unconditional prohibition is the well-known position reported from Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, while unconditional permission is the position of al-Shāfiʿī.

A similar issue is that of a follower ceasing to follow his imam before the closing salutation. Three views are reported from him on this:

The middle position permits this when needed, as the first group does in prayer in times of fear, and as did the man for whom Muʿādh prolonged the ʿIshā prayer, when its length became burdensome for him. The second prohibits it without exception, as does Abū Hanīfah. The third permits it without restriction, as does al-Shāfiʿī.

For this reason, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, he permitted a woman to lead men in prayer when needed—for example, if she can recite while they cannot, so that she leads them in the Tarāwīh prayers. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted Umm Waraqah to lead the members of her household in prayer and appointed a muezzin for her. She stands behind them, even though they are following her, because of the need. This is evidence for those who permit a follower to stand ahead of the imam when needed. This is so despite the saying reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “No woman shall lead

---

## Page 11908

a man,” and despite the fact that prohibiting women from leading men in prayer is the position of the majority of scholars.

On the basis of this principle, Ahmad applied the widely transmitted saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning the imam:

“When he prays sitting, all of you should pray sitting.”

The Prophet explained this by saying that it resembles the non-Arabs’ practice of standing for one another. Thus, the obligation to stand was waived for those following the imam because standing entails the harms to which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, referred: differing from the imam and imitating the non-Arabs in standing for him.

This was also the practice of Companions who led the prayer after him, such as Usayd ibn al-Hudayr: when they fell ill, they prayed sitting, and the people behind them also sat. Ahmad nevertheless disapproved of this when the imam was not the regularly appointed imam, since there was no need to diminish the prayer by following him. For the same reason, he also disapproved of it when the regularly appointed imam suffered from a chronic illness, since he would then be obliged to step down as imam.

He did not consider this ruling abrogated by the fact that, during his illness, the Prophet prayed sitting in the course of a prayer while those following him stood. There is no contradiction between what he commanded and what he did. Moreover, the Companions continued to act upon his command after his death, despite having witnessed his action. A distinction is therefore made between sitting from the beginning of the prayer and sitting partway through it, since both are permissible: his action in no way prohibits what he commanded.

These questions involve subtle discussions for which this is not the place. The sole purpose here is to draw attention to the principles of the Sharia that sound hearts recognize

---

## Page 11909

and which are indicated by the words of Allah Most High:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able,”

and by the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

The purpose is also to explain that, when two obligations cannot both be fulfilled, the more weighty takes precedence and the other is waived on grounds recognized by the Sharia, and to highlight certain guidelines underlying the reasoning of the scholars, may Allah be pleased with them.

He was asked:

About people who hear the call to prayer but do not respond. Some pray at home, while others are not seen praying: a number of people see them, yet never see them at prayer. Their conduct, concerning prayer and other matters, is pleasing neither to Allah nor to His Messenger. Is it permissible for someone who sees a person in this condition to turn away from him, or to greet him? Give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

Also, is it permissible for a man who serves as imam in a mosque where, on some days, only two or three men pray, and who leads the prayer there seeking Allah’s reward, [to pray elsewhere]? Likewise, if he receives payment for leading the prayer, but seeks to pray elsewhere only for the merit of congregational prayer, is that permissible? Give us a ruling; may Allah have mercy on you.

He answered:

Praying in the congregations established in mosques is among the manifest rites of Islam and its practices that lead to right guidance. As recorded in the Sahīh, Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“These five prayers, in the mosque where the prayer is established

---

## Page 11910

are among the practices that lead to right guidance. Allah has prescribed for your Prophet the practices that lead to right guidance. If you were to pray in your homes as this man who stays away prays in his home, you would abandon your Prophet’s sunnah; and if you abandoned your Prophet’s sunnah, you would go astray. No one stays away from them except a known hypocrite. Indeed, a man would be brought in, supported between other men, until he was placed in the row.”

In the Sahīh, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“I have indeed considered ordering that the prayer be commenced, then setting out with men carrying bundles of firewood to people who do not attend the prayer, and burning their houses down upon them.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah reported:

“A blind man came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have no guide to lead me to the mosque.’ He asked him for permission to pray at home, and he granted him permission. When the man turned to leave, he called him back and asked, ‘Do you hear the call to prayer?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Then respond.’”

In one narration in the Sunan, he asked, “Do you hear the call?” He replied, “Yes.” He said, “I can find no concession for you.”

In the Sunan, Ibn ‘Abbās reported:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Whoever hears the call and is not prevented from answering it by an excuse’—they asked, ‘What is an excuse?’ and he replied, ‘Fear or illness’—‘the prayer he offers will not be accepted.’”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd.

All Muslims agree that congregational prayer is strongly emphasized in the religion.

---

## Page 11911

And it is an individual obligation according to most of the early generations and the leading scholars of hadith, such as Ahmad, Ishāq and others, as well as some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and others. Other groups among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and others regard it as a communal obligation; this is the preferred view among al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers. A man who persists in abandoning congregational prayer is a man of ill conduct: he is to be rebuked and deterred from doing so. Indeed, he is to be punished for it, and his testimony is to be rejected, even if congregational prayer is considered a strongly emphasized sunnah.

As for someone known for sinful conduct who neglects prayer, he falls within Allah’s words:

“But they were succeeded by generations who neglected prayer and followed their desires; so they will meet with ruin.”

He must be punished for this in a manner that induces him to abandon forbidden acts and fulfil his obligations. If someone is the regular imam of a mosque, and congregational prayer there cannot be maintained without him, his praying there is better than his praying elsewhere, even if the congregation elsewhere is larger.

Anyone known to neglect obligations or commit forbidden acts openly deserves to be shunned and denied greetings as a disciplinary measure until he repents. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 11912

He was asked about a man whose example others follow in neglecting congregational prayer.

He replied:

Anyone who believes that praying at home is better than praying in congregation in the Muslims’ mosques is a misguided innovator, by the consensus of the Muslims. Congregational prayer is either an individual obligation or a communal obligation.

The evidence from the Quran and Sunnah indicates that it is an individual obligation. Even those who consider it a strongly emphasized sunnah rather than obligatory censure anyone who persistently neglects it. Indeed, according to them, anyone who persistently neglects sunnah practices of lesser importance than congregational prayer loses his standing as an upright person, and his testimony is not accepted. What, then, of someone who persistently neglects congregational prayer?

By the consensus of the Muslims, he is to be commanded to attend it and censured for neglecting it. A person who persists in neglecting the regular sunnah prayers, which are of lesser importance than congregational prayer, is not to be permitted to adjudicate, give testimony or issue fatwas. What, then, of congregational prayer, which is the greatest of Islam’s outward observances? And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11913

He was asked about a man who lives near the mosque but does not attend congregational prayer, citing his shop as an excuse.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. He is to be commanded to pray with the Muslims. If he does not pray, he is to be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death.

If he is clearly neglecting prayer, his claim, “I will pray when I have finished,” is not to be accepted. Rather, anyone whose dishonesty has become apparent is not to be taken at his word, and he is to be compelled to comply with what Allah and His Messenger have commanded.

He was asked about two men who disagreed over “praying alone.” One of them said: The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Prayer in congregation is twenty-five times better than prayer offered alone.”

The other said: “Whenever congregational prayer is held somewhere other than a mosque, it is equivalent to praying alone.”

He replied:

Congregational prayer is not equivalent to praying alone. Rather, congregational prayer is better, even when offered somewhere other than a mosque. However, the scholars differed as to whether a person who prays in congregation at home

---

## Page 11914

is thereby exempt from attending congregational prayer in the mosque, or must still attend it there. He should not absent himself from congregational prayer in the mosque except for a valid excuse, as indicated by the Prophetic traditions and transmitted reports. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man arrives near the end of a congregational prayer, and another congregation will pray afterward. Is it recommended that he join the first congregation at the end of their prayer, or should he wait for the next congregation?

He replied:

If he catches less than one rakʿah (unit of prayer), the answer depends on whether a person is considered to have caught the congregational prayer by catching less than one rakʿah, or whether he must catch a full rakʿah.

Abū Hanīfah’s position is that he is considered to have caught it. He applied this analogy consistently, even holding that a person who catches the sitting of the Friday prayer has caught that prayer and should therefore complete it as a Friday prayer.

Mālik’s position is that a person is not considered to have caught it unless he catches one rakʿah. He applied this principle consistently, even to someone who catches the end of the prescribed prayer time.

The situations in which this issue arises are of several kinds:

The first is the Friday prayer.

The second is attaining the merit of congregational prayer.

---

## Page 11915

And the third is a traveller catching part of a resident’s prayer.

The fourth is catching part of the prayer before its prescribed time ends, such as catching part of the dawn prayer before sunrise.

The fifth is catching the end of the prescribed time, as when a menstruating woman becomes pure, an insane person regains sanity, or an unbeliever embraces Islam at the end of that time.

The sixth is having that same amount of time available at the beginning of the prescribed time, according to those who hold that this establishes the obligation to pray. This sixth principle is itself disputed.

As for al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, they adopted Mālik’s position concerning the Friday prayer, because the Companions agreed on this. They said that whoever catches one rakʿah of the Friday prayer should add another to it, whereas whoever joins them during the final sitting for the testimony of faith should pray four rakʿahs.

As for the remaining issues, there is disagreement within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad: two opinions are attributed to al-Shāfiʿī, and two positions are transmitted from Ahmad. Many of their followers consider Abū Hanīfah’s position stronger.

The stronger view, however, is Mālik’s position, as al-Khiraqī stated concerning some of these cases. For it is authentically reported from Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever catches one rakʿah of the prayer has caught

---

## Page 11916

the prayer.” This is a general text encompassing every case of catching one rakʿah of the prayer, whether this concerns joining a congregation or praying within the prescribed time.

It is also reported in the two Sahīh collections that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever catches one rakʿah of the dawn prayer before sunrise has caught the dawn prayer, and whoever catches one rakʿah of the afternoon prayer before sunset has caught the afternoon prayer.”

This explicitly concerns completing one rakʿah within the prescribed time.

Some have challenged this by citing the wording found through certain routes of transmission:

“Whoever catches one prostration.”

They supposed that this includes someone who catches the first prostration. This is incorrect, for “prostration” here means a rakʿah, as in the hadith of Ibn ʿUmar:

“I learned from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to perform two prostrations before the noon prayer, two after it, and two after the sunset prayer…”

In the well-known wording, it says “two rakʿahs.” Likewise, it is reported:

“He used to pray two prostrations after witr.”

These were two rakʿahs, as explicitly explained in the authentic hadith. Anyone who performs just two prostrations after witr on the basis of this report is mistaken, by agreement of the imams. Moreover, according to them, the ruling does not depend on catching one of the two prostrations. It is thus clear that they accept the hadith.

Accordingly, if someone catches less than one rakʿah and another congregation follows, then praying a complete prayer with that congregation is preferable, since he will thereby have prayed in congregation, unlike in the first case. But if he catches one rakʿah—or less than one rakʿah, on the view that this counts as having caught the congregational prayer—then a conflict arises between his joining

---

## Page 11917

this congregation and his joining the second from its beginning, for joining a congregation from its beginning is preferable, as indicated in what has been reported about joining it in full. If the two congregations are otherwise equal, the second is preferable. If, however, the first is distinguished by its fuller merit, its greater numbers, the excellence of its imam, or its being the regularly established congregation, then it is preferable in that respect, whereas the second is preferable because one joins it in full. Sometimes one consideration may outweigh the other, and sometimes the reverse.

If, however, the second congregation is more complete in its performance of the prayer, or superior in its imam or congregation, then it has an additional advantage. An issue of this kind was not known among the early generations except when someone could catch the prayer at another mosque, since there were not two regular imams leading prayers in the same mosque, and the congregation would assemble in full behind the regular imam. Undoubtedly, praying in congregation with the mosque’s regular imam, even for one rakʿah, is better than praying at home, even in congregation. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who has performed his obligatory prayer, then comes to a congregational mosque and finds the people praying: may he pray with the congregation to make up a missed prayer?

He replied:

If a man has performed the obligatory prayer, then comes to a mosque where that is being offered

---

## Page 11918

, he should offer that prayer with them, whether or not he has a missed prayer to make up, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed when addressing two men who had not prayed with the people. He said:

“Why did you two not pray? Are you not Muslims?”

They replied, “O Messenger of Allah, we prayed at our lodgings.”

He said, “If you pray at your lodgings and then come to a mosque where a congregation is praying, pray with them, for it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.”

Anyone who has a missed prayer to make up must do so immediately, whether he missed it deliberately or through forgetfulness, according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others. Likewise, the stronger view in the Shāfiʿī school is that a prayer missed deliberately must be made up immediately. When he prays with the congregation, he should intend the second prayer as a repetition of the first. According to the correct view, the first counts as the obligatory prayer and the second as voluntary, as indicated by this hadith and others.

It has also been said that whichever of the two prayers is more complete counts as the obligatory prayer; and it has been said that this is left to Allah Most High. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About the hadith of Yazīd ibn al-Aswad, who said:

“I attended the pilgrimage of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and prayed the dawn prayer with him in the Mosque of al-Khayf. When he had finished the prayer and turned around, he noticed two men at the back of the gathering who had not prayed. He said:

---

## Page 11919

‘Bring them to me.’ The two men were trembling. He asked, ‘What prevented you from praying with us?’

They replied, ‘O Messenger of Allah, we had already prayed at our lodgings.’

He said, ‘Do not do that. If you pray at your lodgings and then come to a mosque where a congregation is praying, pray with them, for it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.’”

The second hadith is narrated by Salmān ibn Sālim, who said:

“I saw ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar sitting in the paved courtyard while the people were praying. I asked, ‘O ʿAbd Allāh, why are you not praying?’ He replied, ‘I have already prayed, and I heard the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) say: “A prayer is not to be performed twice.”’”

How, then, can these two hadiths be reconciled?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The hadith of Ibn ʿUmar concerns repeating a prayer without any particular reason. There is no doubt that this is prohibited, and that it is disliked for a person deliberately to repeat a prayer without a reason warranting its repetition. For if a specified number were prescribed for a prayer established by Islamic law, a person could offer the noon prayer several times, the afternoon prayer several times, and so on. There is no doubt that such conduct is disliked.

As for the hadith of Ibn al-Aswad, it concerns repetition occasioned by a particular reason, as expressed in the Prophet’s words:

“If you pray at your lodgings and then come to a mosque where a congregation is praying, pray with them, for it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.”

The reason for repeating the prayer here is being present when the regular congregation prays. It is recommended for someone who has already prayed and then comes upon a regular congregation to pray with them.

---

## Page 11920

But some scholars, such as al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, recommend repeating the prayer without qualification, while others, such as Mālik, recommend it when the second prayer is more complete. If someone repeats it, the first counts as the obligatory prayer according to Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and one of al-Shāfi'ī’s two opinions, because the Prophet said in this hadith:

“For it will count as a voluntary prayer for you.”

Likewise, he said in an authentic hadith:

“There will be rulers who delay the prayer beyond its proper time. Offer the prayer at its proper time, then let your prayer with them be voluntary.”

This, too, entails repeating the prayer for a particular reason and establishes that the second prayer is voluntary. Another view is that whichever of the two is more complete counts as the obligatory prayer; another is that this is for Allah to determine.

Among the reports concerning repetition for a particular reason is the hadith in Sunan Abī Dāwūd in which the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Will someone not give this man charity by praying with him?”

Here, the person giving charity repeated the prayer so that the other worshipper might gain the merit of congregational prayer. Such repetition, which is enjoined, is prescribed even at times when prayer is prohibited according to al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, and Mālik, whereas according to Abū Hanīfah it is not prescribed at those times.

As for the sunset prayer, should it be repeated in its usual form, made even by adding a rak'ah, or not repeated at all? These are three well-known positions among the jurists.

Another established account of repetition for a particular reason is that “the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), during one of the prayers offered in a state of fear, led them in the prayer twice: he led one group in two rak'ahs

---

## Page 11921

then concluded with the salām, then led another group in two rak'ahs and concluded with the salām.” The same applies to the hadith of Mu'ādh ibn Jabal, who used to pray behind the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Here, too, the prayer was repeated and offered twice.

The scholars differ over such cases—the question of someone offering an obligatory prayer behind someone offering a voluntary prayer—and hold three positions. One holds that this is not permissible, as maintained by Abū Hanīfah and by Ahmad according to one report. Another holds that it is permissible, as maintained by al-Shāfi'ī and by Ahmad according to a second report. The third holds that it is permissible when needed, such as in a state of fear or when there is a need to follow someone offering a voluntary prayer, but not otherwise. This is a third position reported from Ahmad.

A similar case is repeating the funeral prayer by someone who has already offered it. The scholars agree that this is not prescribed without a particular reason. Indeed, if he were to pray over the deceased a second time and someone who had not prayed were then to arrive, should he pray over the deceased? The scholars hold two positions on this.

One view is that he should pray over the deceased. This is the position of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. They also permit offering the funeral prayer at the grave, since it is established that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and a number of the Companions offered the funeral prayer over someone for whom others had already prayed.

Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, however, prohibit this, just as they prohibit holding successive congregational prayers in the mosque. They argue that the obligation is discharged by the first prayer, so the second would be voluntary, whereas the funeral prayer is not offered as a voluntary act. This differs from the case of someone offering an obligatory prayer: all Muslims agree that he should offer it, because it is obligatory

---

## Page 11922

upon him. The followers of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad offer two answers:

The first is that the second prayer counts as an obligatory prayer for the person offering it. They say likewise concerning all communal obligations: whoever performs one thereby discharges his own obligation, even if someone else has already performed it. He may choose either to be content with its having been discharged by another or to discharge the obligation through his own action.

The other answer is that it is voluntary. They reject the claim that the funeral prayer cannot be offered voluntarily; rather, it may be offered voluntarily when there is a reason calling for it.

These two lines of reasoning give rise to the following question: if someone who did not offer the funeral prayer initially comes to the funeral, may someone who already offered it pray again alongside him, joining him as is done in the case of an obligatory prayer? There are two views.

One holds that this is not permissible here, because his prayer in this case is indisputably voluntary, and the funeral prayer is not offered voluntarily.

The other holds that he may repeat it, for when the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) offered the funeral prayer at the grave, people who had already offered it prayed behind him. This view is closer to being correct, since this repetition is prompted by a reason calling for it, rather than being sought for its own sake. This is permissible in both the obligatory prayer and the funeral prayer.

And Allah knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 11923

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

If someone arrives when the call to commence prayer has already been made, which is better: to join the obligatory prayer, or to offer the Sunnah prayer first and then join the imam, even if only during the tashahhud? Are the two rak'ahs of dawn a Sunnah prayer associated with the obligatory morning prayer?

He replied:

It is authentically reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“When the call to commence prayer is made, no prayer is to be offered except the obligatory prayer.”

Another narration states:

“No prayer is to be offered except the one for which the call has been made.”

Once the call to commence prayer has been made, one should not occupy oneself with either the prayer of greeting the mosque or the Sunnah prayer of dawn. The scholars agree that one should not occupy oneself with the prayer of greeting the mosque instead of joining the obligatory prayer. They differ, however, concerning the Sunnah prayer of dawn.

The correct view is that, upon hearing the call to commence prayer, one should not offer the Sunnah prayer, whether at home or elsewhere. Rather, one may make it up after the obligatory prayer if one wishes.

The Sunnah is to offer two rak'ahs as a Sunnah prayer after dawn breaks, while the obligatory prayer consists of two rak'ahs. Between the break of dawn and the obligatory prayer, there is no Sunnah prayer other than these two rak'ahs. The obligatory prayer is called both the dawn prayer and the morning prayer. Likewise, the Sunnah prayer is called the Sunnah of dawn, the Sunnah of the morning, the two rak'ahs of dawn, and other similar names. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11924

He was asked about “reciting behind the imam.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The scholars have differed and their views have varied on this matter, despite the widespread need for guidance on it.

There are three principal views: two opposing positions and a middle one. The first is that one does not recite behind the imam under any circumstances. The second is that one recites behind the imam in all circumstances.

The third, which is the view of most of the early Muslims, is that when one hears the imam’s recitation, one listens attentively and does not recite, for listening to the imam’s recitation is better than reciting oneself. When one does not hear his recitation, one recites independently, for reciting is better than remaining silent. Thus, listening to the imam’s recitation is better than reciting, and reciting is better than remaining silent.

This is the view of the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, most of their followers, and a group of the followers of al-Shāfi'ī and Abū Hanīfah. It is also al-Shāfi'ī’s earlier view and the view of Muhammad ibn al-Hasan.

---

## Page 11925

According to this view, is the follower’s recitation of al-Fātihah when the imam recites quietly obligatory or recommended? There are two views within Ahmad’s school. The better-known view is that it is recommended; this is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s earlier view.

When the imam recites aloud, is listening obligatory or recommended? Is reciting while hearing the imam’s recitation forbidden or disliked? And is the prayer invalidated if one recites?

There are two views within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere:

The first is that reciting in these circumstances is forbidden, and that anyone who recites invalidates his prayer. This is one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school reported by Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Hāmid.

The second is that this does not invalidate the prayer. This is the view of the majority and the well-known position within Ahmad’s school. A comparable question concerns someone who recites while bowing or prostrating: does this invalidate the prayer? There are two positions within Ahmad’s school, for “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade reciting the Quran while bowing or prostrating.”

Those who hold that the follower should recite whether the imam recites aloud or quietly instruct him to recite only al-Fātihah when the imam recites aloud. As for anything beyond al-Fātihah, the prescribed practice is to listen rather than recite.

---

## Page 11926

And is the follower’s recitation of al-Fātihah when the imam recites aloud obligatory or recommended? There are two views:

The first is that it is obligatory. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s later view and the view of Ibn Hazm. The second is that it is recommended. This is the view of al-Awzā‘ī and al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, and the position preferred by my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt.

In this matter, there is no precautionary course that avoids all scholarly disagreement, just as there is none concerning the time of ‘Asr, the conversion of Hajj, and similar questions. In such cases, one must examine what the scriptural evidence requires.

Many scholars hold that the time for ‘Asr ends when the shadow of each object reaches twice its length. This is the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī, and one of the two views reported from Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that its time begins at that point. Thus, they have not agreed upon a time at which ‘Asr may validly be performed.

This differs from the other prayers. If someone performs Zuhr after the sun has passed its zenith, after the shadow of each object has reached its own length, excluding the shadow present at the zenith, his prayer is valid. They likewise agree that Maghrib is valid if performed after sunset, and that ‘Ishā’ is valid if performed after the white twilight has disappeared and before the first third of the night has passed, and Fajr

---

## Page 11927

is valid by their unanimous agreement if performed after dawn breaks and up until daylight becomes very bright. As for ‘Asr, one holds that it may be performed until an object’s shadow reaches twice its length, while another holds that it may only be performed after that point. The correct view is that it may be performed from the time each object’s shadow reaches its own length until the sun turns yellow. Its time is thus broader, as both these groups maintain. This is supported by the authentic Medinan hadiths. It is the view of Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad ibn al-Hasan, and the other view reported from Ahmad.

The point here is that there are questions on which it is impossible to follow a position accepted by unanimous agreement. Yet, praise be to Allah, the correct position is supported by scriptural evidence that makes the truth clear.

One such question is converting Hajj into ‘umrah. The forms of Hajj whose permissibility the Muslim community unanimously accepts are to begin with ‘umrah, entering the consecrated state for it at the outset and subsequently performing Hajj, or to combine Hajj and ‘umrah while having brought along the sacrificial animal. But if someone performs Hajj alone, or combines Hajj and ‘umrah without bringing a sacrificial animal, the validity of his Hajj is disputed among earlier and later scholars.

Our subject here, however, is recitation behind the imam. We therefore say: when the imam recites aloud, the follower should listen to his recitation. If he cannot hear because he is too far away, he should recite according to the sounder of the two views. This is the view of Ahmad and others. If, however, he cannot hear because he is deaf, or can hear

---

## Page 11928

the imam’s murmur without understanding what he says, there are two views in Ahmad’s school and among other scholars. The stronger view is that he should recite, for it is preferable to be either listening or reciting. Such a person is not listening and does not attain the intended benefit of listening, so reciting is better for him than remaining silent. We shall therefore present the evidence for both points: that he should listen when the imam recites aloud and recite when the imam recites quietly.

The evidence for the first point comes from the Book, the Sunnah, and reasoned consideration.

As for the first, Allah Most High says:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it and remain silent, so that you may be shown mercy.”

It is widely reported from the early generations that this verse was revealed concerning recitation during prayer. Some said it concerned the sermon. Ahmad ibn Hanbal reported a consensus that it was revealed concerning this, and also reported a consensus that recitation is not obligatory for the follower while the imam recites aloud.

He then says:

The words of Allah Most High,

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it and remain silent, so that you may be shown mercy,”

are general in wording. They must therefore either apply specifically to recitation during prayer, apply specifically to recitation outside prayer, or encompass both. The second possibility is certainly false, for no Muslim has ever maintained that listening is obligatory outside prayer but not during

---

## Page 11929

prayer. Moreover, listening to the recitation of the imam whom one follows, and whom one is obliged to follow, has a stronger claim than listening to someone reciting outside prayer. Recitation during prayer therefore falls within the verse’s scope, whether specifically or as part of its general application. In either case, the verse indicates that the follower is commanded to remain silent during the imam’s recitation. Whether this command denotes an obligation or a recommendation, the point is established: listening is preferable to reciting. The verse clearly indicates this on either interpretation.

The opposing scholar concedes that, for recitation beyond al-Fātihah, listening rather than reciting is commanded. Yet the verse commands silence whenever the Quran is recited. Al-Fātihah is the Mother of the Quran, the surah that must be recited in every prayer, and the greatest surah of the Quran. Nothing like it has been revealed in the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, or the Quran. It is therefore impossible that the verse should mean listening to other portions of the Quran but not to al-Fātihah, given its unrestricted and general wording, especially since al-Fātihah is recited more frequently, is more widely known, and is superior to other surahs.

For His words,

“When the Quran is recited,”

include al-Fātihah just as they include other portions of the Quran. Indeed, its inclusion is all the more evident in both wording and meaning. Someone who turns from listening to al-Fātihah to reciting it does so only because he considers reciting it preferable to listening. This is an error that contradicts the revealed texts and scholarly consensus. The Book and the Sunnah command the follower to listen rather than recite, and the Muslim community agrees that listening to what is recited beyond al-Fātihah is preferable to reciting those passages oneself.

---

## Page 11930

Thus, if reciting what the imam recites were preferable to listening to his recitation, the follower’s recitation would be preferable to his recitation of passages beyond al-Fātihah. Yet no one has maintained this. Those who disputed the matter concerning al-Fātihah did so only because they believed that reciting it was obligatory or recommended for the follower even when the imam recites aloud.

The response is that listening brings him a benefit greater than that which recitation brings him, as demonstrated by his listening to the passages beyond al-Fātihah. Were listening not to bring him something better than recitation, it would be more fitting for him to choose the better of the two, namely recitation. Since the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus indicate that listening is preferable for him to reciting, we know that the listener gains something better than the reciter gains. This applies to al-Fātihah as well as to other passages. Thus, someone listening to the imam’s recitation gains something better than he would gain by reciting himself. It cannot, therefore, be right to command him to do what is inferior and forbid him from doing what is superior. It is established that, in this situation, the imam’s recitation counts as his own, as the great majority of the early and later generations—the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness—have maintained.

Concerning this, the well-known hadith relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever follows an imam, the imam’s recitation counts as his own.”

This hadith has been transmitted with both disconnected and connected chains. Most of the trustworthy hadith authorities, however, transmitted it with a disconnected chain from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shaddād, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Some transmitted it with a connected chain, and Ibn Mājah also narrated it with a connected chain. This disconnected report has been corroborated by

---

## Page 11931

the apparent meaning of the Quran and the Sunnah. The great majority of scholars among the Companions and Successors accepted it, and the narrator who transmitted it directly from the Prophet was one of the foremost Successors. The four imams and others agree that a disconnected report of this kind may be cited as evidence. Al-Shāfiʿī explicitly stated that such a report may be used as evidence.

It is thus clear that listening to the imam’s recitation is conclusively indicated by the Quran. This is one of the evident matters that the entire Muslim community needs to know, so its explanation in the Quran adequately fulfils the purpose of clarification. The Sunnah, moreover, accords with the Quran.

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed us, explaining to us our prescribed practice and teaching us our prayer. He said: ‘Straighten your rows, then let one of you lead you in prayer. When he says “Allāhu akbar,” say “Allāhu akbar,” and when he recites, listen silently.’”

This is part of the well-known, lengthy hadith of Abū Mūsā. However, some narrators included wording that others did not. Some omitted his words:

“And when he recites, listen silently.”

Others included them. This is an addition transmitted by a trustworthy narrator which does not contradict the report to which it is added, but rather accords with its meaning. This is why Muslim included it in his Sahīh.

Listening silently to the reciter is part of fully following him as imam. If someone recites to people who do not listen to his recitation, they are not following him as their imam. This helps explain the wisdom behind waiving the follower’s obligation to recite: following his imam takes precedence over other considerations, even in the physical actions of prayer. Thus, if he joins the imam while he is prostrating, he prostrates with him; and if he joins him in an odd-numbered rakʿah of his prayer,

---

## Page 11932

he recites the tashahhud after that odd-numbered rakʿah. Were he to do this when praying alone, it would not be permissible; he does so only because he is following the imam. This shows that following an imam makes obligatory certain things that are not obligatory upon someone praying alone, and waives certain obligations that apply to someone praying alone.

Abū Hurayrah reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The imam has been appointed only to be followed. When he says ‘Allāhu akbar,’ say ‘Allāhu akbar,’ and when he recites, listen silently.”

This was narrated by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah.

Muslim ibn al-Hajjāj was asked, “Is Abū Hurayrah’s hadith authentic?”—meaning the words, “And when he recites, listen silently.” He replied, “I consider it authentic.”

He was then asked, “Why do you not include it here?”—meaning in his book. He replied, “I have not included here everything I consider authentic. I have included here only what they agreed upon.”

Al-Zuhrī narrated from Ibn Ukaymah al-Laythī, from Abū Hurayrah:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, finished a prayer in which he had recited aloud and asked, ‘Did any of you recite along with me just now?’ A man replied, ‘Yes, O Messenger of Allah.’ He said, ‘I was wondering: why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?’”

He said:

“After hearing this from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the people stopped reciting along with him in those prayers in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited aloud.”

This was narrated by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, al-Nasā’ī and al-Tirmidhī, who said, “It is a good hadith.”

Abū Dāwūd said: “I heard Muhammad ibn Yahyā ibn Fāris say: ‘The words “The people stopped” are al-Zuhrī’s own words.’”

---

## Page 11933

A similar statement is also reported from al-Bukhārī, who said in the section on agnomens in al-Tārīkh:

Abū Sālih said: Al-Layth narrated to me: Yūsuf narrated to me, from Ibn Shihāb, who said: I heard Ibn Ukaymah al-Laythī relate that Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib heard Abū Hurayrah say:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led us in a prayer in which he recited aloud. He then asked, ‘Did any of you recite along with me?’ We replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘I was wondering: why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?’”

He said: “The people then stopped reciting when the imam recited aloud.” Al-Layth said, “Ibn Shihāb narrated it to me,” without including the words, “The people then stopped.” Some said that these were al-Zuhrī’s words, while others said they were Ibn Ukaymah’s words. The correct view is that they were al-Zuhrī’s words.

If these are al-Zuhrī’s words, they constitute some of the strongest evidence that the Companions did not recite along with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he recited aloud. Al-Zuhrī was among the most knowledgeable people of his time—or the most knowledgeable—concerning the Sunnah. Had the Companions’ recitation behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, been prescribed, whether as an obligation or a recommendation, it would have been one of the general rulings known to the Companions at large and those who followed them faithfully. Al-Zuhrī would therefore have been among those most knowledgeable about it. Even had he not mentioned it, that would have been taken as evidence that no such practice was prescribed. How much stronger, then, is the evidence when al-Zuhrī states categorically that the Companions did not recite behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he recited aloud!

[If it is objected: Al-Bayhaqī said, “Ibn Ukaymah is an unknown narrator who narrated only this one hadith, and no one other than al-Zuhrī narrated from him.”

---

## Page 11934

The reply is: This is not so. Rather, Abū Hātim al-Rāzī said of him: “His hadiths are sound; his narrations are accepted.” It is also reported that Abū Hātim al-Bustī said: “Al-Zuhrī, Saʿīd ibn Abī Hilāl, his brother ʿUmar, and Sālim ibn ʿAmmār ibn Ukaymah ibn ʿUmar narrated from him.”] (*).

Mālik narrated in his Muwattaʾ, from Wahb ibn Kaysān, that he heard Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh say:

“Whoever performs a rakʿah without reciting in it has not prayed, unless he is praying behind the imam.”

He also narrated from Nāfiʿ that whenever ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar was asked, “Should one recite behind the imam?” he would say:

“When one of you prays behind the imam, the imam’s recitation suffices for him; when he prays alone, he should recite.”

Nāfiʿ said: “ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar did not recite behind the imam.”

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh, from ʿAtāʾ ibn Yasār, that he asked Zayd ibn Thābit about reciting along with the imam. He replied: “There is to be no recitation along with the imam in any part of the prayer.”

Al-Bayhaqī narrated from Abū Wāʾil that a man asked Ibn Masʿūd about reciting behind the imam. He replied:

“Listen attentively to the Quran, for prayer demands your full attention, and the imam will suffice you in that.”

Ibn Masʿūd and Zayd ibn Thābit were the two Companion jurists of Medina and Kufa. Their statements indicate that attentive listening to the imam’s recitation is what precludes one’s own recitation.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 192), commenting on the two passages, writes:
> 23/275–276:
> “If it is said: Al-Bayhaqī stated, ‘Ibn Ukaymah is an unknown man who narrated only this one hadith, and no one narrated from him except al-Zuhrī.’
> The reply is: That is not so. Rather, Abū Hātim al-Rāzī said of him, ‘His hadith is sound; his reports are accepted.’ It is also related that Abū Hātim al-Bustī said, ‘Al-Zuhrī, Saʿīd ibn Abī Hilāl, his father’s son ʿUmar, and Sālim ibn ʿAmmār ibn Ukaymah ibn ʿUmar narrated from him.’”
> And at 23/318:
> “He also related that Abū Hātim al-Bustī said, ‘Al-Zuhrī, Saʿīd ibn Abī Hilāl, and his grandson ʿAmr ibn Muslim ibn ʿUmārah ibn Ukaymah ibn ʿUmar narrated from him.’”
> I say (the speaker is Shaykh Nāsir al-Fahd):
> The two quotations differ, apparently owing to scribal errors. The original wording, as found in Ibn Hibbān’s al-Thiqāt, 5/169–170, is:
> “Ibn Ukaymah al-Khawlānī: He narrates from Abū Hurayrah. His name is ʿAmr ibn Muslim ibn ʿUmārah ibn Ukaymah. Al-Zuhrī narrated from him, and his brother ʿAmr ibn Muslim ibn ʿUmārah narrates from Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyab, Saʿīd ibn Abī Hilāl, and Muhammad ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAlqamah.”

---

## Page 11935

Likewise, al-Bukhārī cited a report from ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib in his Book of Recitation behind the Imam. He said: “Al-Hārith narrated from ʿAlī that he would glorify Allah in the last two rakʿahs.” He added: “This is not established as authentic, and ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Rāfiʿ narrated a contrary report. ʿUthmān ibn Saʿīd related to us that he heard ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAmr narrate from Ishāq ibn Rāshid, from al-Zuhrī, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Rāfiʿ, the client of Banū Hāshim, who related to him from ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib:

‘When the imam does not recite aloud in prayer, recite the Opening of the Book and another surah in the first two rakʿahs of the noon and afternoon prayers; and recite the Opening of the Book in the last two rakʿahs of the noon and afternoon prayers, in the last rakʿah of the sunset prayer, and in the last two rakʿahs of the night prayer.’”

Furthermore, the Muslims’ consensus that, for recitation beyond al-Fātihah, the worshipper is instructed to listen rather than recite demonstrates that listening to the imam’s recitation is better for him than reciting along with him. Indeed, it demonstrates that he is instructed to listen rather than recite along with the imam.

Moreover, if recitation were obligatory for the worshipper when the imam recites aloud, one of two things would necessarily follow: either he would recite along with the imam, or the imam would be obliged to pause so that he could recite. Yet we know of no disagreement among the scholars that the imam is not obliged to pause for the worshipper to recite al-Fātihah or anything else. Reciting along with the imam, meanwhile, is prohibited by the Quran and the Sunnah.

It is therefore established that the worshipper is not obliged to recite along with the imam while the latter is reciting aloud. Indeed, we say: if it were recommended for the worshipper to recite while the imam is reciting aloud and he is listening, it would be recommended for the imam to pause for the worshipper’s recitation. Yet it is not recommended for the imam

---

## Page 11936

to pause to allow the worshipper to recite, according to the vast majority of scholars. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others. Their evidence is that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not pause to allow those praying behind him to recite, nor did anyone report that he did so. Rather, an authentic hadith establishes that he paused after the opening takbīr to offer the opening supplication, while the Sunan collections report:

“He used to make two pauses: one before beginning the recitation and another after finishing it.”

The latter was a brief pause to separate the recitation from what followed, not long enough to recite al-Fātihah.

It has also been reported that this pause came after al-Fātihah. No one said that he made three or four pauses. Whoever attributes three or four pauses to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, has therefore made a claim that he has not transmitted from any Muslim. The pause following his words,

“nor of those who have gone astray,”

is like the pauses at the ends of verses. Such a pause is not considered a period of silence; hence no scholar has said that the worshipper should recite during it. Some of our fellow scholars whom we knew would recite just after the pauses at the ends of verses. Thus, when the imam said,

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds,”

the worshipper would say,

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

And when the imam said,

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,”

the worshipper would say,

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

No scholar has advocated this.

---

## Page 11937

Scholars have held three views concerning the imam’s pauses:

One view is that there are no pauses at all in the prayer. This is Mālik’s position.

Another is that there is one pause for the opening supplication, as held by Abū Hanīfah.

The third is that there are two pauses. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, based on the hadith of Samurah ibn Jundub:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, used to make two pauses: one when beginning the prayer, and another upon finishing the second surah, before bowing.”

This was mentioned to ʿImrān ibn Husayn, who said, “Samurah’s account is incorrect.” He then wrote about this to Ubayy ibn Kaʿb in Medina, who replied, “Samurah spoke the truth.” This was narrated by Ahmad, whose wording is quoted here, and by Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it as a good hadith.

Abū Dāwūd’s version reads:

“A pause after pronouncing the opening takbīr, and a pause upon finishing ‘not of those who have incurred Your wrath, nor of those who have gone astray.’”

Ahmad preferred the first version and recommended the second pause to separate the recitation from what followed. He did not recommend that the imam pause to allow those praying behind him to recite, though some scholars of his school recommended this.

It is clear that, had the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, made a pause long enough for al-Fātihah to be recited, there would have been ample reason and motivation to transmit this practice. Since no one transmitted it, we know that it did not occur.

ʿImrān ibn Husayn denied the second pause mentioned in Samurah’s hadith because it was a brief pause of a kind that might not be distinctly noticed. It has also been reported that it came after

---

## Page 11938

al-Fātihah. It is known that he made only two pauses. Thus, one of them was long, while the other, in any case, was not long enough to allow al-Fātihah to be recited.

Moreover, if all the Companions had been reciting al-Fātihah behind him during either the first or the second pause, there would have been ample reason and motivation to transmit this practice. How, then, is it that no one has reported from any of the Companions that they used to recite al-Fātihah behind him during the second pause? Had this been prescribed, the Companions would have been foremost in knowing and practising it. Thus, it is clear that this practice is an innovation.

Furthermore, the purpose of reciting aloud is for those praying behind the imam to listen. This is why they say “Āmīn” in response to the imam’s audible recitation, but not his silent recitation. If they are occupied with their own recitation instead of listening to him, this would mean requiring him to recite to people who are not listening to his recitation. It would be like addressing people who do not listen to what one says, or delivering a sermon to people who do not listen to it. Such foolishness is beneath the Sharia.

For this reason, it is reported in the hadith:

“The one who speaks while the imam is delivering the sermon is like a donkey carrying books.”

The same applies to someone who recites while the imam is reciting to him.

---

## Page 11939

Section

Since the person praying behind the imam is commanded to listen attentively and remain silent during the imam’s recitation, he should not occupy himself with anything else—whether recitation, remembrance of Allah, or supplication. Thus, while the imam is reciting aloud, he should neither say the opening supplication nor seek refuge with Allah. There is disagreement on this issue, with three views transmitted from Ahmad.

One view is that, while the imam is reciting aloud, he should say the opening supplication and seek refuge with Allah, but should not recite. This is because listening fulfils the purpose of recitation for him, unlike the opening supplication and the invocation for refuge, neither of which he hears.

Another view is that he should say the opening supplication but not seek refuge with Allah, because the opening supplication is linked to the opening takbīr, whereas seeking refuge is linked to recitation. Whoever does not recite, therefore, does not seek refuge.

A third view is that he should neither say the opening supplication nor seek refuge with Allah while the imam is reciting aloud. This is the soundest view, because doing so distracts him from the attentive listening and silence he has been commanded to observe. He may not allow anything to distract him from what he has been commanded to do.

Ahmad’s followers then differed: some held that this disagreement concerns only the imam’s pauses—whether one should occupy oneself with the opening supplication or seeking refuge, or with one of the two

---

## Page 11940

or occupy himself only with recitation, since its obligation is disputed. During audible recitation, however, he should do nothing but listen attentively in silence. The prevailing understanding among Ahmad’s followers is that this disagreement concerns audible recitation, for the reasons already given. When the imam recites quietly, however, it is better for the person praying behind him to say the opening supplication. According to the apparent position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfa, and others, saying the opening supplication during the imam’s silence is better than reciting, because listening can take the place of recitation, whereas nothing takes the place of the opening supplication.

As for the argument that the obligation of recitation for the person praying behind the imam is disputed, the reply is that the obligation of the opening supplication is likewise disputed: there are two well-known views within Ahmad’s school. Yet Ahmad’s position never varied on the fact that recitation is not obligatory for the person praying behind the imam during audible recitation. Ibn Batta preferred the view that the opening supplication is obligatory, and he mentioned two reports from Ahmad on this matter.

It is thus clear that those among Ahmad’s followers, such as Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī, who claimed that recitation during quiet recitation is preferable to the opening supplication according to his school have misrepresented his position. This would, however, accord with the view of those who recommend reciting al-Fātiha during audible recitation. I know of none of his followers who held this view before my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt. It is neither Ahmad’s position nor that of the general body of his followers.

Moreover, treating disagreement as the rationale for rulings is intrinsically invalid. Disagreement is not one of the attributes upon which the Lawgiver actually makes rulings depend, for it is a circumstance that arose after the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet this approach is adopted by someone who is not acquainted with the scriptural evidence in

---

## Page 11941

the matter itself, and does so as a precaution. Accordingly, when the imam recites quietly, is it recommended for the person praying behind him to seek refuge with Allah as well as say the opening supplication if he does not recite? There are two reports from Ahmad on this.

The correct view is that seeking refuge is prescribed only for someone who recites. If there is enough time for recitation, he should seek refuge and recite; otherwise, he should listen silently.

Section

As for the “second section”—recitation when one does not hear the imam’s recitation, as when the imam recites quietly or remains silent—the command to recite and the encouragement to do so apply even more strongly to someone praying than to anyone else.
