Reciting the Quran during prayer is better than reciting it outside prayer, and the reported merits of reciting the Quran apply even more strongly to someone praying than to anyone else. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever recites the Quran will have ten good deeds credited to him for every letter. I do not mean that Alif-Lām-Mīm is one letter; rather, alif is one letter, lām is one letter, and mīm is one letter.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

Concerning prayer specifically, the following statement is established in the authentic hadith narrated by Muslim from Abū Hurayra, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said: “Whoever

---

## Page 11942

performs a prayer without reciting the Mother of the Quran has offered a deficient prayer.” He repeated this three times, meaning that it is incomplete. Abū Hurayra was asked: “But I pray behind the imam.” He replied:

“Recite it quietly to yourself, for I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, relate that Allah said: ‘I have divided the prayer into two halves between Myself and My servant: half is Mine and half is My servant’s, and My servant shall have what he asks for. When the servant says, “All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds,” Allah says, “My servant has praised Me.” When he says, “The Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,” Allah says, “My servant has extolled Me.” When he says, “Master of the Day of Judgment,” He says, “My servant has glorified Me.” On one occasion he said, “My servant has entrusted his affairs to Me.” When he says, “You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,” He says, “This is between Me and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.” When he says, “Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your anger, nor those who have gone astray,” He says, “This is for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.”’”

Muslim also narrated in his Sahīh from ʿImrān ibn Husayn:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led the noon prayer, and a man behind him began reciting ‘Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High.’ When he finished the prayer, he asked, ‘Which of you recited?’—or, ‘Which of you was reciting?’ A man said, ‘I did.’ He said, ‘I thought that one of you was competing with me in reciting it.’” Narrated by Muslim.

This man recited behind him during the noon prayer, yet he did not forbid either him or anyone else from reciting. Rather, he said, “I thought that one of you was competing with me in reciting it,” meaning that he was contending with him over it. Similarly, he said in the other hadith:

“I say, ‘Why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?’”

---

## Page 11943

In the Musnad, Ibn Mas‘ūd reported:

“They used to recite behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, so he said, ‘You have confused me in my recitation of the Quran.’”

This expresses his disapproval of someone competing with him, contending with him, and disrupting his recitation of the Quran. This cannot happen when someone recites quietly to himself so that no one else hears him; it happens only when he makes himself audible to others. This is disliked because it involves competing with someone else, not because he is reciting behind the imam. As for reciting when the imam recites quietly, no hadith has been reported prohibiting this. Hence his question:

“Which of you was reciting?”

He meant the person whose recitation competed with his, not someone reciting quietly to himself. Such a person does not compete with him, nor is he known to have contended with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Reciting behind the imam is disliked only when it prevents the attentive silence that is commanded, or when it involves competing with someone else. If there is neither a command to listen silently nor any competing recitation, there are no grounds for prohibiting the recitation of the Quran during prayer. Here, the person reciting has not been given listening as a substitute for recitation; thus, if prevented from reciting, he would miss out on both listening and reciting. There is, moreover, a well-known disagreement over whether recitation is obligatory in such circumstances. By contrast, the view that it is obligatory when the imam recites aloud is anomalous; Ahmad even reported a consensus to the contrary.

Abū Hurayra and other Companions understood His words:

“I have divided the prayer into two halves between Myself and My servant. When the servant says, ‘All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds’”

to apply to both the imam and the person praying behind him. Moreover, every remembrance that the imam is prescribed to say quietly is also prescribed for the person praying behind him

---

## Page 11944

to say quietly, such as glorifying Allah while bowing and prostrating, reciting the tashahhud (testimony of faith), and supplicating. It is well known that Quran recitation is superior to remembrance and supplication. Why, then, should recitation not be prescribed for him when the imam recites quietly, since he neither hears that recitation nor says “Āmīn” in response to the imam’s silent recitation?

Furthermore, Allah, glorified be He, says:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it attentively and remain silent, so that you may receive mercy.”

He also says:

“Remember your Lord within yourself, humbly and fearfully, without raising your voice, in the mornings and evenings, and do not be among the heedless.”

This command is addressed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and to his community, for whatever is addressed to him is also addressed to his community unless a text establishes that it applies exclusively to him. Examples include His words:

“Glorify your Lord with praise before sunrise and before sunset.”

And His words:

“Establish prayer at the two ends of the day and in the early hours of the night.”

And His words:

“Establish prayer from the sun’s decline until the darkness of night.”

There are other similar passages. This command to remember Allah within oneself in the mornings and evenings applies to the imam, the person praying behind him, and the person praying alone. It encompasses the dawn, noon, and afternoon prayers. Thus, the person praying behind the imam is commanded to remember his Lord within himself. When he is listening, however, he is commanded to listen; when he is not listening, he is commanded to remember his Lord within himself.

The Quran is the finest remembrance, as Allah Most High says:

“This is a blessed reminder which We have sent down.”

Allah Most High also says:

“We have given you a reminder from Our presence.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

He also says:

“No reminder comes to them anew from their Lord.”

Moreover, remaining silent without recitation, remembrance, or supplication is not an act of worship

---

## Page 11945

nor is it commanded; rather, it opens the door to intrusive thoughts. Occupying oneself with the remembrance of Allah is therefore better than remaining silent, and reciting the Quran is among the finest good deeds. Accordingly, remembering Allah through the Quran is better than other forms of remembrance, as established in an authentic hadith in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best words after the Quran are four phrases, which are themselves from the Quran: Glory be to Allah; praise be to Allah; there is no god but Allah; and Allah is greatest.”

Muslim narrated it in his Sahīh.

‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā reported:

“A man came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘I cannot learn any of the Quran, so teach me something that will suffice in its place.’ He said, ‘Say: Glory be to Allah; praise be to Allah; there is no god but Allah; Allah is greatest; and there is neither might nor power except through Allah.’ The man said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, this is for Allah; what is there for me?’ He said, ‘Say: O Allah, have mercy on me, provide for me, grant me well-being, and guide me.’ When the man stood up, he gestured thus with his hands. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘This man has indeed filled his hands with goodness.’”

Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, and al-Nasā’ī narrated it.

Those who require recitation when the imam recites aloud cite as evidence the hadith reported in the Sunan collections from ‘Ubādah, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you are behind me, do not recite anything except the Opening of the Book, for there is no prayer for one who does not recite it.”

However, the leading hadith scholars consider this hadith defective on numerous grounds. Ahmad and other leading scholars graded it weak. Its weakness has been discussed at length elsewhere, and it has been explained that the authentic hadith is the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “There is no prayer without

---

## Page 11946

the Mother of the Quran.” This is the report included by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections, and narrated by al-Zuhrī from Mahmūd ibn al-Rabī‘, from ‘Ubādah. As for the other hadith, some Syrian narrators erred in transmitting it. Its origin was that ‘Ubādah led the prayer in Jerusalem and made this statement; they confused the report attributed to the Prophet with the statement of ‘Ubādah himself.

Furthermore, scholars, both earlier and later, have discussed this issue at length, along with other questions. At times, they devoted separate treatises to it. Some, such as al-Bukhārī and others, wrote separate works arguing in favour of recitation behind the imam; others, such as Abū Mutī‘ al-Balkhī, Karrām, and others, argued against it.

Anyone who examines the writings of these groups will see the middle position clearly. Most of these separate treatises address cases covered by each of the two divergent positions: that of those who forbid recitation behind the imam even in silent prayers, and that of those who require recitation behind him even while hearing him recite aloud.

Al-Bukhārī was among those who argued forcefully in favour of recitation even when the imam recites aloud. Indeed, he regarded it as obligatory, as did al-Shāfi‘ī in his later position and Ibn Hazm. Nevertheless, his arguments and his treatise only establish the weakness of Abū Hanīfah’s position on this issue and its related questions, such as its being (1).

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 11947

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him—concerning recitation behind the imam, after some preceding discussion:

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“No prayer is valid for one who does not recite the Opening of the Book.”

This was recorded by the compilers of the authentic hadith collections, including al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections. Al-Bukhārī relied upon it in his treatise, under the chapter heading “The Obligation of Recitation in Every Rak‘ah.” He narrated this hadith through several chains, including the transmissions of Ibn ‘Uyaynah, Sālih ibn Kaysān, and Yūsuf ibn Yazīd.

Al-Bukhārī said:

Ma‘mar narrated from al-Zuhrī:

“No prayer is valid for one who does not recite the Opening of the Book or more.”

Most reliable transmitters did not corroborate Ma‘mar’s wording “or more,” although he did affirm the recitation of the Opening of the Book. It is unclear what he meant by “or more”: two letters, or more than that? Unless it is like the Prophet’s statement:

“The hand is not to be cut off except for a quarter of a dinar or more.”

The hand may thus be cut off for a quarter of a dinar, or for more than a dinar.

Al-Bukhārī said:

It is said that ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Ishāq corroborated Ma‘mar. However, ‘Abd al-Rahmān would sometimes narrate from al-Zuhrī and then insert another transmitter between himself and al-Zuhrī. It is not known whether this is among his authentic reports.

I say: The substance of this is conveyed in an authentic hadith, as narrated by the compilers of the Sunan collections, and indeed

---

## Page 11948

al-Bukhārī narrated it in this work:

Musaddad narrated to us: Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd narrated to us: [Ja‘far ibn Maymūn narrated to us] (*) Abū ‘Uthmān al-Nahdī narrated to us, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed him to proclaim:

“No prayer is valid without reciting the Opening of the Book and something more.”

He also said: Muhammad ibn Yūsuf narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj, from ‘Atā’, from Abū Hurayrah, who said:

“Reciting the Opening of the Book suffices; if one recites more, that is better.”

He also mentioned the other hadith from Abū Sa‘īd recorded in the Sunan collections. Al-Bukhārī said: Abū al-Walīd narrated to us: Hammām narrated to us, from Qatādah, from Abū Nadrah, who said:

“Our Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded us to recite the Opening of the Book and whatever else was easy for us.”

I say: This indicates that what is meant here is not the recitation of someone praying behind an imam while hearing the imam recite aloud. No one says that, in this situation, it is better for him to recite more than al-Fātihah rather than listen silently to the imam’s recitation, or that he is commanded to recite anything beyond al-Fātihah while the imam recites aloud. Al-Bukhārī likewise criticized this addition in the hadith of ‘Ubādah, for it indicates that someone praying behind an imam and listening to him is not included in the hadith. Yet even supposing that this addition is absent from the hadith of ‘Ubādah, it is present in the hadith of Abū Hurayrah.

Furthermore, the Book and the Sunnah command those praying behind the imam to listen silently to his recitation. Some scholars even hold that a person’s prayer is invalid if, instead of listening silently, he recites along with the imam.

At this point, it may be said that there is a conflict between the general scope of his statement:

“No prayer is valid without reciting the Mother of the Quran”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) The text in square brackets is from Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Protecting the Collected Fatwas from Omissions and Scribal Errors).

---

## Page 11949

and the general scope of the command to listen silently. The former say: “He should listen silently except while reciting al-Fātihah.” The latter say that anyone commanded to listen silently is exempted from his statement:

“No prayer is valid without reciting the Mother of the Quran.”

This is if they concede that its wording includes him in the first place, for they maintain that the hadith does not establish that recitation is obligatory for someone praying behind an imam. He only said:

“No prayer is valid for one who does not recite the Mother of the Quran.”

The Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus establish that a follower’s silent attention to his imam’s recitation fulfils the purpose of reciting along with him, and more. They all agree that, during the recitation beyond al-Fātihah, listening is preferable for him to reciting himself. If the follower listening to his imam’s recitation were not better than one reciting himself, then reciting would be better for him. Furthermore, the command to listen silently to the recitation of the Quran is established, and one cannot both listen silently and recite. Were silent listening not to fulfil the purpose of recitation and more, Allah would not have commanded abandoning what is better for the sake of what is less meritorious.

Moreover, this is a general statement from whose scope the latecomer has been excepted on the basis of the hadith of Abū Bakrah and other evidence. Prayer led by two successive imams has also been excepted. When the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) led the people in prayer after Abū Bakr had already led part of it, he continued reciting from where Abū Bakr had stopped. He did not begin reciting al-Fātihah anew, because he continued the prayer begun by Abū Bakr. If the obligation to recite al-Fātihah was waived for him in this situation, then it is all the more fitting that it should be waived for someone praying behind an imam.

Cases involving an excuse have also been excepted, and listening to the imam is a circumstance constituting an excuse. Thus, this general statement has been qualified by exceptions. Yet no exception has been made to the command that the follower listen silently to the imam’s recitation, whether by a text

---

## Page 11950

specific to it or by scholarly consensus. When two general statements conflict, one retaining its full general scope and the other qualified by exceptions, the former must take precedence.

Furthermore, the command to listen silently falls within the follower’s duty to follow his imam. It indicates that, through attentive, silent listening, he gains something better for him than reciting himself. Muslims agree on this concerning the sermon and recitation during prayer outside the point under dispute. Thus, the underlying reason requiring silent listening applies to refraining from reciting al-Fātihah as well as anything else.

As for the obligation to recite it in every prayer, listening silently to the imam reciting it is better than reciting it oneself. If someone vowed to pray in al-Aqsā Mosque, praying in the Sacred Mosque or the Prophet’s Mosque (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would fulfil his obligation. Indeed, it would be better for him, as the Sunnah indicates. He had only obliged himself to pray in Jerusalem, but this alternative is better.

If, then, in an obligation a person imposes upon himself, the Lawgiver allows the more meritorious act to take the place of what was vowed, setting aside the particular specification he himself made through his vow, how could the Lawgiver impose an obligation without allowing something better to take its place? The Lawgiver is wise: He never prescribes a particular act when another would be more worthy of being performed. A person, by contrast, may specify something in his vow, charitable endowment, or bequest when something else would be better.

Moreover, in more than one hadith, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded anyone who makes an inadvertent error in prayer to perform the prostrations of forgetfulness.

---

## Page 11951

Yet when someone following an imam makes an inadvertent error, his imam bears responsibility for that lapse on his behalf because he is following him, even though he could perform the prostrations after the closing salutation. Listening silently to the imam’s recitation is an even fuller expression of following him, for the imam recites aloud precisely so that others may listen to his recitation. If one of the worshippers occupies himself with his own recitation, it is like addressing someone who is not listening, or delivering a sermon to people who are all talking. Whoever does this is, as stated in the hadith,

“like a donkey carrying books.”

He has not understood what following means, just like someone who raises his head before the imam: he too is like a donkey. Hence the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Does the one who raises his head before the imam not fear that his head will be turned into a donkey’s head?”

He is following the imam, so how can he race ahead of him? For this reason, ʿUmar struck someone who did this and said, “You have neither prayed alone nor followed your imam.” Someone who raises his head inadvertently is instructed to return to his former posture and delay following the imam by the same interval by which he had preceded him. Ahmad and others explicitly stated this, and he and others cited reports from the Companions concerning it.

As for the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever offers a prayer without reciting the Mother of the Quran in it, that prayer is deficient.”

The report continues: I said, “Abū Hurayrah, I sometimes pray behind the imam.” He replied, “Recite it to yourself, Persian, for I heard the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) say, ‘Allah said: “I have divided the prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves”’”—and so on to the end of the hadith. This is an authentic hadith, recorded by Muslim in his Sahīh.

---

## Page 11952

Al-Bukhārī also cited this hadith as evidence in this work, although he did not include it in his Sahīh, as was his practice in such cases. Its well-known chain of transmission, recorded by Muslim, is through al-ʿAlāʾ, from Ibn al-Sāʾib, from Abū Hurayrah. Some transmitters give it as: “From his father, from Abū Hurayrah.” It was also transmitted through ʿĀʾidh and through ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather.

Al-Bukhārī said:

“Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Raqāshī narrated to us: Yazīd ibn Zurayʿ narrated to us: Muhammad ibn Ishāq narrated to us: Yahyā ibn ʿAbbād narrated to us, from his father, from ʿĀʾishah, who said: I heard the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) say:

‘Every prayer in which the Mother of the Quran is not recited is deficient.’”

Al-Bukhārī said: “Yazīd ibn Hārūn added: ‘with the Opening of the Book.’” He also said:

“Mūsā ibn Ismāʿīl narrated to us: Abān narrated to us: ʿĀmir al-Ahwal narrated to us, from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

‘Every prayer in which the Mother of the Quran is not recited is deficient.’”

He also said:

“Hilāl ibn Bishr narrated to us: Yūsuf ibn Yaʿqūb al-Silaʿī narrated to us: Husayn al-Muʿallim narrated to us, from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, who said: The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

‘Every prayer in which the Opening of the Book is not recited is deficient; it is deficient.’”

---

## Page 11953

Al-Bukhārī also said: Mūsā narrated to us: Dāwūd ibn Abī al-Furāt narrated to us, from Ibrāhīm al-Sāʾigh, from ʿAtāʾ, from Abū Hurayrah:

“Every prayer includes recitation, even if only the Opening of the Book. Whatever the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) recited aloud for us, we recite aloud; whatever he recited silently, we recite silently.”

It was also narrated through two chains from Abū al-Zāhiriyyah: Kathīr ibn Murrah narrated to us that he heard Abū al-Dardāʾ say:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) was asked, ‘Does every prayer include recitation?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ A man from the Ansār then said, ‘This has become obligatory.’”

These hadiths have the same meaning as his statement:

“No prayer is valid for one who does not recite the Opening of the Book.”

For someone who listens attentively and remains silent counts as reciting; indeed, he is superior to someone who recites for himself. This is indicated by the statement:

“No prayer is valid without the Opening of the Book and some additional recitation,”

and the statement:

“We were commanded to recite it and whatever else we could easily recite.”

For someone who listens attentively and remains silent is not commanded to recite the additional passage.

Furthermore, Abū Hurayrah’s statement, “Whatever he made us hear, we make you hear; whatever he recited silently, we recite silently,” indicates that the imam is meant, since a person praying behind an imam does not make anyone hear his recitation.

As for the question, “Does every prayer include recitation?” and the statement, “No prayer is valid without the Mother of the Quran,” the prayer of someone who listens to the imam’s recitation does include recitation. Indeed, most scholars say that the imam bears responsibility for that person’s prayer: his prayer is encompassed within the imam’s prayer, and thus includes recitation. The majority of them also say that someone able to recite may not follow an imam who is unable to recite properly. Thus, if the imam’s recitation did not suffice for

---

## Page 11954

the person praying behind him at all, but each person instead recited for himself, there would be no difference between his inability to recite and his inability to perform any other obligation. Moreover, the imam is commanded to listen to what is recited beyond al-Fātihah, although that recitation is not obligatory. How, then, could one not be commanded to listen to the imam’s recitation of al-Fātihah, which is obligatory? How could one be commanded to listen to voluntary recitation but not obligatory recitation? If listening to the recitation beyond al-Fātihah is obligatory according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus, then listening to the recitation of al-Fātihah is even more binding.

Al-Bukhārī then said:

He was also asked: “You base your argument on Allah’s words, ‘When the Quran is recited, listen to it and remain silent.’ Tell me: if the imam does not recite aloud, should a person recite behind him?” If he says, “No,” his claim is invalidated, because Allah said, “Listen to it and remain silent,” and one can only listen to what is recited aloud. We, too, apply Allah’s words, “Listen to it,” for we say that a person should recite behind the imam during the two pauses.

Samurah said:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would pause twice: once after pronouncing the opening declaration of Allah’s greatness, and once after finishing his recitation.”

Ibn Khuthaym said:

I asked Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, “Should I recite behind the imam?”

He replied: “Yes, even if you hear his recitation. For they have introduced something they did not previously do. When one of the early generations led the people in prayer, he would say, ‘Allah is the Greatest,’ then remain silent until he thought those behind him had recited the Opening of the Book. Then he would recite, and they would remain silent.”

Abū Hurayrah said:

“When the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) intended to recite, he would pause.”

He said:

---

## Page 11955

And Abū Salamah ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān, Maymūn ibn Mihrān, Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, and others held that one should recite during the imam’s silence, thereby following the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“No prayer is valid without the Opening of the Book.”

Thus, one recites during the pause, then listens silently when the imam recites, thereby following Allah’s words:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And His words:

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows a path other than that of the believers, We will leave him to what he has chosen and burn him in Hell. What an evil destination!”

If the imam omits something required for the prayer, those behind him must make up the deficiency. ‘Alqamah said: “If the imam does not complete it, we do.”

Al-Hasan, Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, and Humayd ibn Hilāl each said: “I recite the Opening of the Book on Fridays.”

He said: Others among these people said that reciting in Persian is sufficient, and that reciting a single verse is sufficient. Those who come later contradict those who came before them, without any basis in the Book or the Sunnah.

He was also asked: “Who has permitted you, on the basis of a report or an analogy, to recite praises while the imam is reciting, yet forbidden others to perform the obligation—namely, recitation—when you have neither a report nor scholarly agreement to support you? For a number of the people of Medina did not hold that either the imam or anyone else should recite praises: they pronounce ‘Allah is the Greatest’ and then begin reciting.”

Thus he became bewildered among them, while they wavered in their doubt. Moreover, he did the same with several obligatory acts, treating what is obligatory as less important than what is voluntary.

---

## Page 11956

You claim (1) that if he omits recitation in two rak‘ahs of Zuhr, ‘Asr, or ‘Ishā’, his prayer is valid, whereas if he omits recitation in one of four voluntary rak‘ahs, it is invalid.

I say: If he omits recitation in one rak‘ah of Maghrib, his prayer is valid, whereas if he omits recitation in one rak‘ah of Witr, it is invalid. It is as though he wishes to join together what the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) distinguished, or distinguish what the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) joined together.

I say: As for the Prophet’s pause after pronouncing “Allah is the Greatest,” Abū Hurayrah explained in his hadith, whose authenticity is agreed upon, that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would recite the opening supplication during it. It was not a period of complete silence intended to allow those following him to recite. It is also established in an authentic report that ‘Umar would pronounce “Allah is the Greatest” and recite the opening supplication aloud to teach it to the people.

As for his argument against someone who recites the opening supplication while the imam is reciting aloud, there is a well-known disagreement concerning this: should one recite the opening supplication and seek refuge in Allah while the imam recites aloud; recite the opening supplication but seek refuge only when reciting oneself; or neither recite the opening supplication nor seek refuge while the imam recites aloud? These are three positions, corresponding to three views transmitted from Ahmad.

The stronger position, however, is the one supported by al-Bukhārī’s argument: the command to listen silently requires refraining from anything that prevents one from hearing the recitation, whether praise, recitation, or supplication, just as one listens silently to the sermon. Indeed, listening silently to the recitation is even more emphatically required. But if he pauses

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 11957

—the imam, that is—for the initial pause devoted to praise, then, according to Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others, it is preferable for the follower to recite the opening supplication rather than al-Fātihah during this pause. This is because the purpose of recitation is fulfilled for him by listening to the imam’s recitation, whereas the purpose of the opening supplication is fulfilled only by reciting it himself. Moreover, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would pause to recite the opening supplication, and ‘Umar would recite it aloud to teach it to those praying behind him. This establishes that it is prescribed for the follower. If he were to occupy himself with recitation instead, he would miss the opening supplication.

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not pause so that those praying behind him could recite during his silence. This is the position of the majority of scholars: they do not recommend that the imam pause to allow the follower to recite. It is the position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and others.

Some of Ahmad’s followers, however, recommended that the imam pause to allow the follower to recite. Others recommended that the follower recite during the imam’s silence rather than say the opening supplication. This was the position chosen by Abū Bakr al-Dīnawarī and Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī. Others recommended that he recite al-Fātihah while the imam recites aloud, as my grandfather Abū al-Barakāt preferred. This is also the position of al-Layth, al-Awzā‘ī and others. Among these scholars, some recommend that the follower say the opening supplication during the imam’s silence and also recite, thus combining both. Others recommend that he recite rather than remain silent.

Likewise, among those who disapprove of his reciting while the imam recites aloud, some recommend that he

---

## Page 11958

recite the opening supplication while the imam recites aloud, whereas others disapprove of this. Both views are reported from Ahmad. The position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others is that the follower should recite the opening supplication during the imam’s pause for it. This is the stronger view.

Al-Bukhārī’s report that a number of the scholars of Medina did not consider the opening supplication prescribed—as is Mālik’s position—supports the majority’s view. They argue that the imam makes no pause in this case, so if we recite along with him, we contravene the Quran and Sunnah. However, what al-Bukhārī mentions is evidence against those who recite the opening supplication in this situation, thereby occupying themselves with it instead of listening to the recitation. Their reasoning is that the imam’s recitation suffices for the follower, whereas his opening supplication does not suffice for him. Yet this only establishes that recitation is not obligatory upon the follower. The follower is commanded to listen attentively and remain silent, so he should not occupy himself with praise instead, just as he should not occupy himself with recitation—and recitation is superior to praise. If the imam pauses to offer praise and the follower joins him during that pause, he too offers praise. But if the imam does not pause, or the follower joins him while he is reciting, the follower is commanded to remain silent and listen attentively; he should not turn away from what he has been commanded to do.

If it is argued that there are two views within Ahmad’s school concerning whether praise is obligatory, the reply is that there are also two views within Ahmad’s school concerning whether recitation is obligatory upon the follower. If he is prohibited from reciting so that he may listen to the imam’s recitation, then there is even greater reason to prohibit him from offering praise, in accordance with His words:

---

## Page 11959

“So listen to it and remain silent.” Otherwise, they would contradict themselves, as al-Bukhārī mentioned.

As for Abū Hurayrah’s statement:

“Recite it privately, O Persian, for I heard the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, say: ‘Allah said: I have divided the prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves’”—and so on.

It may be said that Abū Hurayrah only instructed him to recite because of the merit mentioned in the hadith about this division, not because of the Prophet’s words:

“Whoever offers a prayer in which he does not recite the Mother of the Quran, that prayer is deficient.”

For if the follower’s prayer were deficient without his reciting, Abū Hurayrah would have instructed him to recite on the strength of that hadith, rather than explaining his instruction by reference to the hadith about the division.

Unless, of course, it is argued that he cited it for emphasis, or because, in dividing the recitation, Allah divided the prayer, indicating that recitation is indispensable to prayer: if prayer contained no recitation, that division would not exist. On this understanding, the two hadiths would convey the same meaning.

His words, “Recite it privately,” are open to more than one interpretation. If he meant what others meant—reciting when the imam recites quietly or remains silent—this would not conflict with their view. This is supported by the fact that Abū Hurayrah was among those who narrated the Prophet’s words:

“When he recites, remain silent.”

He also narrated his words:

“There is no prayer without the Opening of the Book and something more.”

And he said:

“The Opening of the Book suffices, and if one recites more, that is better.”

It is clear that this does not include the follower who is listening to the imam’s recitation. For such a person, reciting more than the Opening would not be better; rather, listening attentively and remaining silent would be better for him. It cannot, then, be asserted with certainty that he instructed him

---

## Page 11960

to recite while listening to the imam’s recitation, on the basis of wording open to more than one interpretation.

Al-Bukhārī (*) said: Ibn Sālih narrated from al-Asfahānī, from al-Mukhtār, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Laylā, from his father, from ‘Alī:

“Whoever recites behind the imam has departed from the natural disposition.”

He said: This has not been authenticated, for al-Mukhtār is unknown, and it is not known whether he heard from his son, or whether his father heard from ‘Alī. Hadith scholars do not accept such reports as evidence. The report of al-Zuhrī, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Rāfi‘, from ‘Alī, deserves preference and is more authentic.

I say: Al-Zuhrī’s report clearly indicates that he instructed him to recite in prayers where recitation is quiet, not in prayers where it is aloud. Accordingly, if he did indeed make this statement, he made it concerning prayers with audible recitation, when one hears the imam. Thus, there is no contradiction between the two statements. Similar reports from Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn ‘Umar and others have already been mentioned.

Al-Bukhārī said: Dāwūd ibn Qays narrated from Ibn Nijād, a descendant of Sa‘d, from Sa‘d:

“I wish that whoever recites behind the imam had burning coals in his mouth.”

He said: This report has a disconnected chain, and Ibn Nijād is neither known nor identified by name. No one may say, “May there be a burning coal in the mouth of one who recites behind the imam,” for burning coals are a means of Allah’s punishment. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Do not punish with Allah’s punishment.”

No one should suppose that Sa‘d said such a thing, given the report’s disconnected chain and weakness.

He said:

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 192):
> The copyists introduced errors into this quotation. The corrected text, from al-Bukhārī’s al-Qirā’ah khalf al-Imām (Recitation behind the Imam), p. 13—may Allah have mercy on him—reads:
> 
> “Al-Bukhārī said: [‘Alī] ibn Sālih narrated from al-Asbahānī, from al-Mukhtār [ibn] ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Laylā, from his father, from ‘Alī: ‘Whoever recites behind the imam has departed from the fitrah (natural disposition).’
> 
> He said: This report has not been authenticated, because al-Mukhtār is unknown, and it is not known whether he heard from [his father or not], nor whether [his father] heard from ‘Alī. Hadith scholars do not accept such reports as evidence. Al-Zuhrī’s report from [‘Ubayd Allāh] ibn Abī Rāfi‘, from ‘Alī, has precedence and is more authentic.”

---

## Page 11961

And Ibn Hibbān narrated from Salama ibn Kuhayl, from Ibrāhīm, who said: ‘Abd Allāh said:

“I wish that whoever recites behind the imam had his mouth filled with straw.”

He said: This report has a disconnected chain and cannot be used as evidence. Ibn ‘Awn narrated a differing version from Ibrāhīm, from al-Aswad, with the wording “heated stones.” Such words do not befit people of knowledge, for several reasons.

First, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Do not curse one another by invoking Allah’s curse or the Fire, and do not punish with Allah’s punishment.”

Second, no one should wish to fill the mouths of the Prophet’s Companions—‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, Hudhayfa and the others we have mentioned—with heated stones, straw or dust.

Third, once a report from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his Companions is established, the statement of al-Aswad or anyone like him carries no authority against it. Ibn ‘Abbās and Mujāhid said:

“After the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, everyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected.”

Hammād ibn Salama said:

“I wish that whoever recites behind the imam had his mouth filled with sugar.”

Al-Bukhārī said: ‘Umar ibn Muhammad narrated from Mūsā ibn Sa‘d, from Zayd ibn Thābit, who said:

“Whoever recites behind the imam, his prayer is invalid.”

It is not known whether, in this

---

## Page 11962

chain, its narrators heard directly from one another, and a report with such a chain cannot be authenticated.

He said:

Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib, ‘Urwa, al-Sha‘bī, ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Abd Allāh, Nāfi‘ ibn Jubayr, Abū al-Mulayh, al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, Abū Mijlaz, Makhūl, Mālik, Ibn ‘Awn and Sa‘īd ibn Abī ‘Arūba held that one should recite.

(Anas and ‘Abd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Ansārī regarded [recitation] behind the imam as recommended) (*).

I say:

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh that ‘Atā’ ibn Yasār asked Zayd ibn Thābit al-Ansārī about reciting along with the imam. He replied:

“One should not recite along with the imam at all.”

This concerns reciting along with him when he recites aloud, as al-Zuhrī said:

“The people then stopped reciting along with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, in those prayers in which he recited aloud.”

As for prayers in which recitation is quiet, one is not said to recite “along with him,” just as one member of the congregation is not said to recite along with another. Nor is one said to offer the opening supplication along with him, recite the testimony of faith along with him, or glorify Allah along with him while bowing and prostrating.

Likewise, the report cited earlier from Ibn Mas‘ūd states that he used to instruct those praying behind the imam to listen silently to the imam’s recitation, yet he himself would recite behind the imam.

Accordingly, his statement—if he did say it—or the statement of his companions who transmitted from him, such as al-Aswad:

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 193):
> This is a statement by al-Bukhārī, may Allah have mercy on him. The correct reading is yusabbihāni khalfa al-imām (“they both recite behind the imam”), as in al-Qirā’a khalf al-imām (Recitation behind the Imam), p. 15. Here, yusabbihāni means yaqra’āni (“they both recite”). The word yusabbihāni was miscopied as yastahibbāni (“they both consider recommended”), so the compiler, may Allah have mercy on him, thought that some text had been omitted and added [recitation] in square brackets to complete the sense. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11963

“I wish that the mouth of whoever recites behind the imam were filled with red-hot stones, straw, or dust,” applies to someone who recites while hearing the imam recite, thereby neglecting the command to remain silent and listen. This is also the person to whom Sa‘d’s statement—if he did say it—applies:

“I wish there were glowing coals in his mouth.”

This is especially so if he competes with the imam’s recitation, such that the imam, or someone listening to the imam’s recitation, can hear his voice. He would then be among those concerning whom the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?”

And concerning whom he said:

“I knew that some of you were competing with me in its recitation.”

Likewise, if someone recites during a prayer with quiet recitation but raises his voice so that he interferes with and competes with the imam, or with others praying behind him, he would be acting wrongly.

As for the statement of Hammād ibn Salama and others:

“I wish his mouth were filled with sugar,”

this concerns someone who recites where recitation is recommended for him, on account of his reciting behind the imam in a prayer with quiet recitation.

Likewise, the statement reported from Zayd ibn Thābit:

“There is no prayer for one who recites behind the imam,”

applies to someone who neglects what he has been commanded to do and does what he has been forbidden to do, reciting while hearing the imam’s recitation. There are two views within Ahmad’s school concerning whether such a person’s prayer is invalid. Any Companion or Successor among the early generations who said this may have intended a sound meaning, as in the saying of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“For one of you to sit on a glowing coal that reaches his skin and burns his clothes is better for him than sitting on a grave.”

For a person to suffer punishment in this world is less severe for him than committing

---

## Page 11964

what Allah has forbidden. Thus, someone who considers a person’s recitation while listening to his imam to be disobedience to Allah and His Messenger—whereby he neglects what Allah has commanded him and does what Allah has forbidden—may say:

“For something painful to be in his mouth, preventing him from disobedience, is better for him than doing what he has been forbidden to do.”

Similarly, someone who utters forbidden words may be told: “It would have been better for you to be unable to speak.” This does not mean that we ourselves should inflict such suffering on him. Rather, it means that, had Allah afflicted him with this, it would have been better for him than falling into sin.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to the spouses invoking Allah’s curse upon one another:

“The punishment of this world is less severe than the punishment of the Hereafter.”

A member of the early generations might mention the threatened punishment for an act even when someone else commits it on the basis of an interpretation, as in ‘Ā’ishah’s statement:

“Tell Zayd that he has nullified his jihad alongside the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, unless he repents.”

None of this involves invoking Allah’s curse or the Fire upon one another, nor inflicting Allah’s punishment. Rather, it expresses the wish that a person be afflicted with something that prevents him from disobedience, even if it causes him pain.

A scholar may mention the threatened punishment for what he considers a sin while knowing that someone acting on an interpretation is forgiven and will not incur that punishment. He mentions it, however, to explain that, in his view, this act warrants that punishment. How much more so, then, when he has mentioned only something that would prevent the person from doing what he considers a sin.

---

## Page 11965

Likewise, the statement, “I wish his mouth were filled with sugar,” extends to someone whose recitation fulfils what Allah has commanded. Nevertheless, anyone who recites when recitation is forbidden, believing it to be commanded, or omits what is commanded, believing it to be forbidden, is rewarded for his scholarly judgment and forgiven his error—even though a scholar may speak about an act he considers obligatory or forbidden in terms appropriate to its obligation or prohibition.

This does not involve wishing that the mouths of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, or of any believers, should be filled with heated stones or straw. For most reports of those Companions reciting behind the imam concern silent prayers, whereas the critics’ censure concerns those who recite during audible prayers. Thus, the censure and the practice did not concern the same circumstances. Even supposing that they did coincide among the early generations, this would be no different from their coinciding among later generations. In that case, each speaks on the basis of his own scholarly judgment.

This is no more serious than one leading Companion saying to another in the Prophet’s presence, peace and blessings be upon him: “You are a hypocrite, defending the hypocrites”; or someone saying: “Let me behead this hypocrite.” Nor is it more serious than their fighting one another during the civil strife on the basis of interpretation, or their invoking curses and the like during qunūt (the supplication in prayer), despite the Prophet’s authentically reported words, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Do not revert to disbelief after me, striking one another’s necks.”

And his words:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire.”

Thus, if this threatened punishment is averted from them on account of their interpretation

---

## Page 11966

in matters of bloodshed, then all the more should it be averted on account of interpretation in lesser matters. It is established that Alī burned apostates with fire, and it is likewise reported that Abū Bakr al-Siddīq did so. If this was permissible on the basis of interpretation—despite the disagreement over it and an established text to the contrary—it is not inconceivable that some of them might err by using such language about what they regard as sinful and disobedient.

It is well known that the prohibition against reciting behind the imam during audible recitation has been transmitted through numerous channels from the Companions, the Successors and those after them. Likewise, reciting behind the imam during silent recitation has been transmitted through numerous channels from the Companions, the Successors and those after them. Indeed, the view that recitation is not obligatory upon someone following an imam under any circumstances is also well known among them.

Al-Bukhārī narrated in this book:

Abd Allāh ibn Munīr narrated to us, having heard Yazīd ibn Hārūn say: Ziyād—namely al-Jassās—narrated to us; al-Hasan narrated to us, saying: Imrān ibn Husayn told me:

“Do not deem a Muslim’s prayer valid without purification, bowing and prostration behind the imam; and if he prays alone, without al-Fātihah and two or three verses.”

Thus, he did not require him to recite al-Fātihah when serving as imam, as he required purification, bowing and prostration. Rather, he required it when praying alone.

---

## Page 11967

Then al-Bukhārī narrated the saying:

“Do not recite behind me except al-Fātihah.”

He mentioned its chains of transmission and the variations in its transmission, saying: Shujā ibn al-Walīd narrated to us; al-Nadr narrated to us; Ikrimah narrated to us; Amr ibn Sa‘d narrated to us, from Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from his father, from his grandfather, who said (1).

> The passage in square brackets is missing from the original, as the compiler, may Allah have mercy on him, noted. It appears in al-Bukhārī’s treatise al-Qirāʾa (Recitation), p. 18. See also al-Bayhaqī’s al-Qirāʾa khalf al-Imām (Recitation behind the Imam), p. 79, hadith 167.

---

## Page 11968

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section

Scholars differ over whether reciting behind the imam is obligatory or recommended.

Some hold that it is disliked in all circumstances, as is the view of Abū Hanīfah and others.

Others hold that reciting al-Fātihah is obligatory in all circumstances, as is al-Shāfiʿī’s later position and the view of others. This is also Ibn Hazm’s view, with the further stipulation that reciting anything else is not prescribed under any circumstances.

Still others hold that reciting al-Fātihah is obligatory only in prayers with silent recitation, as in al-Shāfiʿī’s earlier position. Imam Ahmad reported a consensus that it is not obligatory in prayers with audible recitation.

The majority hold that it is not obligatory, nor is it disliked in all circumstances. Rather, reciting al-Fātihah and other passages is recommended in prayers with silent recitation and during the imam’s pauses. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, and others.

If, however, the imam leaves no pauses in which one can recite, is recitation disliked, or is reciting al-Fātihah recommended? There are two views on this. The position of Ahmad and the majority of his followers is that reciting either al-Fātihah or anything else is disliked, while a group held that it is recommended

---

## Page 11969

in that case to recite al-Fātihah. This is the view preferred by my grandfather, and it is also the position of al-Layth and al-Awzāʿī.

This view rests on two arguments:

First, reciting it avoids the disagreement over whether it is obligatory. If a person does not recite it, the validity of his prayer is disputed; if he does recite it, all he misses is listening to the imam during his own recitation of it.

Second, the hadith of ʿUbādah recorded in the Sunan:

“When you are praying behind me or behind the imam, do not recite anything except the Mother of the Book, for there is no prayer for one who does not recite it.”

This is the evidence cited by those who hold its recitation to be obligatory.

These scholars say that the prohibition applies only while listening to the imam’s recitation; at other times, recitation is prescribed. It is thus understood that al-Fātihah is excepted when reciting anything else is prohibited. This establishes that it is to be recited while listening to the imam’s audible recitation.

There are then three positions on this:

One holds that reciting it is obligatory and that nothing else is to be recited under any circumstances, as Ibn Hazm maintained.

Another holds that reciting it is obligatory, but that the prohibition on reciting anything else applies while the imam is reciting aloud, and therefore does not imply an unrestricted prohibition.

A third holds that it merely establishes an exception for reciting al-Fātihah from the prohibition, and an exception to a prohibition

---

## Page 11970

does not establish an obligation.

His words, “For there is no prayer for one who does not recite it,” explain this exception by reference to the obligation to recite it in prayer. Its status as a pillar of prayer warrants exempting its recitation from the prohibition in this situation for someone praying behind an imam, even if it is not obligatory upon him. This resembles collective obligations: when one group fulfils them, the obligation is discharged, yet others may subsequently perform them. They are still described as collective obligations, even though those others may regard the obligation as discharged through someone else’s performance.

For this reason, it is said that the funeral prayer may be performed at times when prayer is prohibited because it is obligatory—even when performed a second time, according to the sounder of the two views. This is because those performing it do so as an obligatory prayer, although they may regard the obligation as discharged through someone else’s performance. Likewise, reciting al-Fātihah is a pillar of prayer, but someone praying behind an imam may content himself with his imam’s recitation, or he may discharge the obligation himself.

This resembles the charity due at the end of Ramadan, which a person assumes on someone else’s behalf, such as that due from his wife. Does this obligation fall upon the husband from the outset, or does he assume it on her behalf? There are two views. The sounder is that he assumes it on her behalf. Thus, if the wife paid it herself, that would be valid. She therefore has the choice of paying it herself or requiring her husband to pay it. If the husband paid it and she subsequently paid it herself without counting his payment, that too would be valid.

The imam, however, must recite, and he assumes the recitation on behalf of the person praying behind him. A single recitation therefore suffices for both the imam and the person following him. If the latter recites for himself, that is good, as with other collective obligations. For imams, however, this is an individual obligation.

---

## Page 11971

As for those who disapproved of reciting while listening to the imam’s recitation without exception—and they are the majority—their evidence is the saying of Allah Most High:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it and remain silent, so that you may receive mercy.”

He commanded silence without qualification, and someone who recites while listening has not remained silent.

Those who respond that the verse does not apply when one is reciting al-Fātihah may be answered in several ways:

First is what Imam Ahmad mentioned concerning the consensus that this verse was revealed concerning prayer and the sermon. Likewise, the Prophet said:

“When he recites, remain silent.”

Furthermore, someone listening to al-Fātihah is like someone reciting it. This is why he says Āmīn to its supplication. The Prophet also said:

“When the reciter says Āmīn, say Āmīn, for whoever says Āmīn in unison with the angels will have his previous sins forgiven.”

The silence commanded during the imam’s recitation is a form of following the imam. The listener thus fulfils the commanded act of following, thereby fulfilling the purpose of recitation. If he recites al-Fātihah, however, he abandons the commanded following of the imam through silence, as well as the silence commanded in the Quran. In exchange for these two things, he gains only the recitation of al-Fātihah, whose purpose has already been achieved by listening to the imam’s recitation and saying Āmīn to it. He has thus abandoned the commanded silence without anything to take its place. He has missed this obligation, gaining in exchange only something whose purpose is achieved without it. It is well known that when the choice lies

---

## Page 11972

between forgoing one of two things while securing the other, it is preferable to secure what would otherwise be lost without a substitute rather than what can be adequately replaced.

Furthermore, if the listener were not like the reciter, it would be recommended for the follower to recite while the imam recites aloud passages other than al-Fātihah. Since Muslims agree that the prescribed practice for the follower, when hearing this recommended recitation, is to listen rather than recite, it follows that listening achieves the purpose of recitation for him. Otherwise, reciting would be the prescribed practice for him. Indeed, they have made silence obligatory during the recommended recitation, so remaining silent during the obligatory recitation is all the more appropriate. As for the hadith, Imam Ahmad and others have criticized it. The wording found in the two Sahīh collections contains only an unqualified statement.

Moreover, if the hadith is authentic, it is to be understood as referring to an imam who pauses in his recitation. This is supported by the fact that its wording does not have universal scope, for it is reported that he said:

“When you are behind me, do not recite anything except the Mother of the Book.”

This is an exception to the prohibition against their reciting behind him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to make two pauses, as reported by Samurah and Ubayy ibn Kaʿb. His pause between the opening takbīr and the recitation is likewise established by the hadith of Abū Hurayrah recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections. The time taken by the supplication that Abū Hurayrah reported for this pause is sufficient to recite al-Fātihah. How much more so if one recites part of it during one pause and the rest during another!

Accordingly, his statement:

“When you are behind me, do not recite anything except the Mother of the Quran.”

does not constitute evidence that one should recite it while the imam is reciting aloud.

---

## Page 11973

For this is an exception to a prohibition and therefore conveys only unrestricted permission, meaning that they are not forbidden to recite it; it cannot be recited during his pauses. This is supported by the fact that most of those who disagree concede that, in prayers recited silently, the follower recites al-Fātihah and other passages. They also concede that, if he can recite additional passages during the imam’s pauses, he should do so, and that someone too far away to hear recites al-Fātihah and additional passages. Thus, this prohibition applies specifically to those praying behind him in prayers recited aloud. The exception permitting the recitation of al-Fātihah is because it can be recited during his pauses.

This is clarified by the wording of the hadith in the two Sahīh collections, narrated by al-Zuhrī from Mahmūd ibn al-Rabīʿ, from ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No prayer is valid for one who does not recite the Mother of the Quran.”

Another version says, “the Opening of the Book.”

As for the additional wording, it was narrated (1) from ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit, who said:

“We were behind the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the dawn prayer. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited, but the recitation became difficult for him. When he finished, he said, ‘Perhaps you are reciting behind your imam?’ We replied, ‘Yes, O Messenger of Allah.’ He said, ‘Do not do so, except with the Opening of the Book, for no prayer is valid for one who does not recite it.’”

It was reported by Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, who graded it a good hadith, and al-Dāraqutnī, who graded its chain of transmission good.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 194):
> Perhaps the first blank [meaning the one on this page] should read: [Ibn Ishāq, from Makhūl, from Mahmūd ibn al-Rabīʿ]. It was transmitted with this wording and chain of transmission by Ahmad (5:311), Abū Dāwūd (823), al-Tirmidhī (311), Ibn Khuzayma (1581), and others.

---

## Page 11974

It was also narrated (1) from ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led us in one of the prayers in which the recitation is aloud, and his recitation became confused. When he finished, he turned to face us and said, ‘Do you recite when I recite aloud?’ Some of us replied, ‘We do indeed.’ He said, ‘Then do not. I was wondering, “Why are others competing with me as I recite the Quran?” So do not recite any of the Quran when I recite aloud, except the Mother of the Quran.’”

It was reported by Abū Dāwūd, whose wording this is, al-Nasāʾī, and al-Dāraqutnī. Al-Dāraqutnī also reports:

“No prayer is valid in which a man does not recite the Opening of the Book.”

He said, “Its chain of transmission is good, and all its narrators are trustworthy.”

This hadith makes clear that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not know whether they were reciting anything behind him. It is evident that, had recitation been obligatory for those praying behind the imam, he would have instructed them to do so, since clarification may not be delayed beyond the time when it is needed. Had he explained this to them, most of them would have done it, rather than just one or two, and he would not have needed to ask about it.

This therefore shows that he had not required them to recite behind him while he was reciting aloud. Then, when he learned that they were reciting, he forbade them to recite anything other than the Mother of the Book. The confusion in his recitation mentioned here results from others reciting along with him while he recites aloud, whether they recite al-Fātihah or anything else. Thus, the underlying reason applies to both, for anything that makes the imam’s recitation difficult or confuses it is forbidden.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 194):
> As for the second blank [meaning the one on this page], perhaps it should read: [Zayd ibn Wāqid, from Makhūl, from Nāfiʿ ibn Mahmūd ibn al-Rabīʿ]. It was transmitted with this wording and chain of transmission by Abū Dāwūd (824), al-Dāraqutnī (1:319), and others.

---

## Page 11975

This is what many people praying behind an imam do when they consider reciting al-Fātihah while he recites aloud to be obligatory or recommended. They make his recitation difficult and confuse him, while also preventing those nearby from listening attentively as they have been commanded. They thus defeat both the purpose of the imam’s reciting aloud and the purpose of those behind him listening. Clearly, such conduct is disliked.

Moreover, if all those praying behind him were reciting, his recitation aloud would have no listener and would therefore serve no purpose. Nor would there be any benefit in his instruction, “When he says Āmīn, say Āmīn.” They would be saying Āmīn to a recitation of the Quran that they had not listened to—that none of them had listened to—unless it were argued that the imam must pause long enough for them to recite. Yet they do not regard a pause long enough for this recitation as obligatory; they merely recommend it. It is therefore clear that recommending the pause accords with recommending recitation during it. If recitation were obligatory for those praying behind the imam, he would have to pause long enough for it, either engaging in remembrance of Allah during that pause or remaining completely silent. I know of no one who has made such a pause obligatory for the sake of those reciting behind the imam.

This is further confirmed by the fact that he made listening silently obligatory while the imam recites, as related in Sahīh Muslim from Abū Mūsā, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed us, explained to us our prescribed practice, and taught us how to pray. He said, ‘Straighten your rows, then let one of you lead you in prayer. When he pronounces the takbīr, pronounce the takbīr, and when he recites, listen silently.’”

He also narrated it from Abū Hurayrah, who reported that the Messenger of Allah said:

---

## Page 11976

may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The imam has only been appointed to be followed. When he pronounces the takbīr, pronounce the takbīr, and when he recites, listen silently.”

Narrated by Imam Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, and al-Nasā’ī.

Muslim ibn al-Hajjāj was asked, “Is Abū Hurayrah’s hadith authentic?”—meaning the wording, “When he recites, listen silently.” He replied, “I consider it authentic.” He was asked, “Why do you not include it here?”—meaning in his book. He replied, “I have not included here everything I consider authentic. I have included here only what they agreed upon.” This means that they did not agree upon the version transmitted through Abū Hurayrah, whereas they agreed upon the version narrated by Abū Mūsā. Muslim narrated it through Abū Mūsā, but did not narrate it through Abū Hurayrah.

Ibn Ukaymah al-Laythī narrated from Abū Hurayrah:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, finished a prayer in which he had recited aloud and asked, ‘Did anyone’—meaning one of us—‘recite just now?’ A man replied, ‘Yes, O Messenger of Allah.’ He said, ‘I find myself asking: Why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?’”

After hearing this from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the people stopped reciting along with him in those prayers in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited aloud. Narrated by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah, al-Nasā’ī, and al-Tirmidhī, who said, “It is a good hadith.”

Abū Dāwūd said: “I heard Muhammad ibn Yahyā ibn Fāris say that the words, ‘The people stopped reciting,’ through to the end, are the words of al-Zuhrī.” Al-Bukhārī also related something to this effect.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Ibn Ukaymah is an unknown narrator who narrated no hadith other than this one

---

## Page 11977

alone, and no one other than al-Zuhrī narrated from him.” This may be answered in several ways:

First: Abū Hātim al-Rāzī said of him, “He transmits sound hadith; his narrations are acceptable.” Abū Hātim’s endorsement carries the greatest weight.

[It is related that Abū Hātim al-Bustī said: “Al-Zuhrī, Sa‘īd ibn Abī Hilāl, and his grandson Amr ibn Muslim ibn Umārah ibn Ukaymah ibn Umar narrated from him.”] (*)

Second: Ibn Ukaymah’s hadith contains only what is also found in the hadith of Ubādah upon which al-Bayhaqī and others relied: that they recited behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and that he said:

“Why is someone competing with me in reciting the Quran?”

Third: Ibn Ukaymah’s hadith was narrated by the four compilers of the Sunan. If the soundness of this hadith’s text is conceded, and the hadith he cited as evidence and that cited by his opponents agree on this account, then what they agree upon is to be acted upon by agreement. The additional wording in his hadith, however, was transmitted uniquely through that particular chain. Only some of the compilers of the Sunan narrated it, and the leading scholars criticized it. Within the core report whose transmission is agreed upon, the disputed addition was more deserving of criticism.

As for the statement, “The people stopped,” if this is al-Zuhrī’s wording, it would be supplementary, for al-Zuhrī was the most knowledgeable of the Successors of his time concerning the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah

> **Footnotes:**
> See vol. 23, p. 275 of this collection.

---

## Page 11978

may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is a matter concerning which there was ample motivation and concern to transmit what was done behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; its transmission would not have been confined to one or two people. Al-Zuhrī’s categorical statement is therefore among the strongest pieces of evidence that they stopped reciting behind him when he recited aloud, after previously doing so. This supports what has already been mentioned and accords with his saying:

“When he recites, listen attentively.”

He made no exception for al-Fātihah or anything else. It is thus established that the addition in question either rests on a weak basis or was not accurately preserved in wording by its narrator, its meaning being consistent with the other narrations. Otherwise, the general principles established in the Book and Sunnah cannot be altered on the basis of this matter, which admits of different interpretations. And Allah knows best.

The discussion is further clarified by what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from Imrān ibn Husayn:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led the noon prayer, and a man began reciting behind him, ‘Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High.’ When he finished, he asked, ‘Which of you recited?’ or ‘Which of you was reciting?’ A man said, ‘I did.’ He said, ‘I thought that one of you was competing with me in reciting it.’”

This hadith shows that someone recited behind him in a silent prayer, reciting more than al-Fātihah. Despite this, he did not forbid them to do so. This constitutes his tacit approval of their reciting more than al-Fātihah behind him in a silent prayer, contrary to those who say that one must never recite behind him, or must not recite anything beyond al-Fātihah.

His statement, “I thought that one of you was competing with me in reciting it,” does not prohibit the act of

---

## Page 11979

reciting as such. Rather, it indicates that a follower should not raise his voice so as to compete with the imam, as some followers do, and as the imam himself may sometimes do. As Abū Qatādah said:

“He would sometimes let us hear a verse.”

It also provides evidence that he did not command them to recite behind him in silent prayers, whether al-Fātihah or anything else. Had he commanded them to do so, he would not have questioned their reciting behind him. Nor did he question their recitation of a particular surah; rather, he said:

“Which of you recited?” or “Which of you was reciting?”

Moreover, it is clear from customary practice that the person reciting behind him would only have recited “Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High” after al-Fātihah. This therefore indicates that a follower is not obliged to recite in silent prayers, whether al-Fātihah or anything else.

This is also indicated by the hadith of Abū Bakr: when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed him to lead the prayer while he went to reconcile Banū Amr ibn Awf, he then returned and continued reciting from where Abū Bakr had left off. Likewise, in the hadith of Abū Bakrah narrated by al-Bukhārī in his Sahīh, he bowed before reaching the row and then joined the prayer. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“May Allah increase your eagerness, but do not do that again.”

Had reciting al-Fātihah been obligatory upon the follower in every circumstance, it would not have been waived because of late arrival or ignorance. Thus, he said to the Bedouin who prayed incorrectly:

“Go back and pray, for you have not prayed.”

And he commanded the man who prayed alone behind the row to repeat the prayer.

---

## Page 11980

Furthermore, the imam’s undertaking the recitation on behalf of the follower does not preclude the follower from reciting himself, thereby attaining greater completeness in this respect. That is better than remaining silent without listening. It is known with certainty from the Sharia that someone who recites the Quran is better than someone who remains silent without listening to another’s recitation. The former is included in his words:

“Whoever recites the Quran receives ten good deeds for every letter. I do not mean that Alif Lām Mīm is one letter; rather, alif is a letter, lām is a letter, and mīm is a letter.”

There is therefore no basis whatsoever for regarding this righteous deed, which Allah and His Messenger love, as disliked. This differs from the case of someone who is listening, for his listening takes the place of his own recitation. The evidence for this is their agreement that, during recommended recitation, he is instructed to listen silently—either as an obligation or as a recommendation—and that it is disliked for followers to recite while listening. Were listening not equivalent to reciting, indeed better than it, he would not be instructed to listen silently and forbidden to recite, for Allah does not command what is inferior and forbid what is superior.

This is further supported by his words in the hadith of Ubādah:

“Do not recite any of the Quran when I recite aloud, except the Mother of the Quran.”

Thus, he only forbade them to recite when he recited aloud. Likewise, al-Zuhrī said:

“The people then ceased reciting alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in those prayers in which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited aloud, after they heard that from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

---

## Page 11981

This explicit wording qualifies the unrestricted wording in the other version. He said:

“Do you recite behind your imam?” We replied, “Yes.” He said, “Then do not do so except with the Opening of the Book.”

This means when the imam recites aloud. It also explains what Ahmad narrated in al-Musnad from Abd Allah ibn Masʿūd, who said:

“They used to recite behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, so he said, ‘You have confused my recitation of the Quran.’”

This occurs either in a prayer recited aloud or in a silent prayer in which a follower raises his voice enough for the imam to hear him. Otherwise, a follower who recites quietly to himself neither confuses the imam nor is confused by him. This differs from a follower who recites while the imam is reciting, for the imam’s recitation certainly confuses him. Indeed, some followers repeat al-Fātihah several times because the imam’s voice inevitably distracts them.

Indeed, if the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described the follower as confusing the imam, muddling his recitation, and competing with him, what, then, of the effect on the follower when the imam recites aloud? One follower can also confuse another when reciting aloud, for when someone recites aloud on his own, the slightest sound confuses him and makes recitation difficult. Unless all sounds are completely hushed, his recitation becomes difficult and he becomes confused. This is something readily experienced.

Thus, you find that those who attend gatherings for listening to poetry—listening accompanied by whistling and clapping—are disturbed by the slightest sound and object to anyone who disrupts them. Likewise, someone reciting the Quran outside prayer is disturbed by the slightest sound; how much more so someone reciting during prayer? If someone outside prayer were to recite to a group who did not listen silently to him, but instead

---

## Page 11982

recited to themselves, his recitation would be disrupted. Thus, the balanced position on this issue has become clear through textual evidence and analogy. And Allah knows best.

The reports transmitted from the Companions on this subject clarify the correct view. ʿAtāʾ ibn Yasār asked Zayd ibn Thābit about reciting along with the imam. He replied:

“There is to be no recitation alongside the imam at all.”

Narrated by Muslim. It is well known that Zayd ibn Thābit was among the Companions most knowledgeable about the Sunnah, and he was the leading scholar of the people of Medina. Had reciting al-Fātihah or anything else while the imam recites aloud been prescribed, he would not have said, “There is to be no recitation alongside the imam at all.”

His phrase “alongside the imam” applies only to someone reciting along with him while he recites aloud. When recitation is quiet, neither is reciting along with the other. This statement of Zayd rules out both obligation and recommendation, and expresses a prohibition and disapproval.

Wahb ibn Kaysān reported that he heard Jābir ibn Abd Allah say:

“Whoever prays a rakʿah without reciting the Mother of the Quran in it has not prayed—unless he is praying behind the imam.”

Narrated by Mālik in al-Muwattaʾ.

Jābir was the last of the Companions to die in Medina and was one of the eminent figures of that generation. Mālik also narrated from Nāfiʿ that whenever Abd Allah ibn Umar was asked, “Should one recite behind the imam?” he would say:

“When any of you prays

---

## Page 11983

behind the imam, the imam’s recitation is sufficient for him. When he prays alone, let him recite.”

He added:

“Abd Allah ibn Umar did not recite behind the imam.”

Ibn Umar was among those most knowledgeable about the Sunnah and most faithful in following it. Had recitation been obligatory for those praying behind an imam, this would have been a matter of common knowledge that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had explained publicly to all. Had he explained it to them, they would have acted upon it generally, and it would have been widely known among the Companions. Such an obligation could not have escaped Ibn Umar, leading him to neglect it, when it applied generally to all worshippers and had been publicly explained to all. This is unlike a recommended practice, which might remain unknown to someone.

Al-Bayhaqī narrated from Abū Wā’il that a man asked Ibn Mas‘ūd about reciting behind the imam. He replied:

“Listen attentively to the Quran, for prayer demands your attention. The imam will suffice you in that.”

This statement of Ibn Mas‘ūd makes clear that he only forbade him to recite behind the imam so that he would listen attentively and occupy himself with that. He did not forbid recitation when one was not listening, as in prayers with quiet recitation or during pauses. At such times, the person praying behind the imam is neither listening attentively nor occupied with anything. This is evidence against those scholars of Kufa who differed with Ibn Mas‘ūd, and it clarifies what he narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as mentioned earlier.

---

## Page 11984

The previously mentioned hadith of Jābir has been transmitted as a report traced back to the Prophet, with a connected chain, and with a Successor reporting directly from the Prophet. The version attributed to Jābir himself is established without dispute. Likewise, the version in which a Successor reports directly from the Prophet is established without dispute, transmitted by the leading hadith scholars from Abd Allah ibn Shaddād, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Whoever has an imam, the imam’s recitation counts as his recitation.”
