The version with a connected chain, however, has been criticized. Ibn Mājah transmitted it through Jābir al-Ju‘fī, from Jābir ibn Abd Allah. Ayyūb and Zā’idah declared Jābir al-Ju‘fī a liar, whereas al-Thawrī and Sa‘īd considered him trustworthy. Ibn Ma‘īn said: “His hadith is not to be recorded, nor does he deserve such consideration. He is of no worth as a transmitter.” Al-Nasā’ī said: “He is an abandoned transmitter.”

Abū Dāwūd related that Ahmad said: “Jābir was not criticized for his hadith transmission; he was criticized only for his views.” Abū Dāwūd said: “I do not consider him strong in his hadith transmission.”

The statement, “The imam’s recitation counts as his recitation,” does not establish, as some scholars of Kufa argued on its basis, that recitation is not recommended for one praying behind an imam. Rather, the statement, “The imam’s recitation counts as his recitation,” indicates that he may suffice himself with it and that his obligation is thereby discharged. It does not indicate that he may not recite himself. This is like many other cases in which one may discharge an obligation through another person’s action, yet may also perform it oneself. The same applies to recommended acts. The most that can be said is that he is as though he had recited himself.

Moreover, once a worshipper has recited the remembrances of prayer, whether obligatory or recommended,

---

## Page 11985

it is not disliked for him to repeat them in their proper place for a valid purpose. Indeed, it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say:

“Allah is supremely great. Allah is supremely great. Allah is supremely great.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would also repeat a single verse, as he repeated His words:

“If You punish them, they are Your servants.”

Here ends what was found. All praise is due to Allah alone. May Allah bless Muhammad the Prophet and his family, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 11986

He also said:

Section

As for reciting behind the imam, people hold two extreme positions and a middle one.

Some regard reciting behind the imam as disliked, with some going so far as to deem it forbidden, whether in prayers recited silently or aloud. This is the predominant view among the scholars of Kūfah and their followers, such as the followers of Abū Hanīfah.

Others strongly emphasize reciting behind the imam, even requiring the recitation of al-Fātihah when one can hear the imam reciting. This is the later of al-Shāfi'ī’s two positions, also held by a group of other scholars.

Others instruct worshippers to recite in prayers recited silently and during the imam’s pauses in prayers recited aloud, and likewise instruct those too far away to hear the imam to recite. As for those close enough to hear the imam’s recitation, they instruct them to listen attentively to it, treating listening as taking the place of reciting.

This is the view of the majority, including Mālik, Ahmad, and others

---

## Page 11987

among the jurists of the major centres and those versed in transmitted reports. This is supported by the practice of most of the Companions and accords with most hadiths.

This disagreement resembles their disagreement concerning the prayer of someone following an imam: does it depend on the imam’s prayer, or does each pray independently? This has already been noted. Abū Hanīfah’s underlying principle is that the follower’s prayer is incorporated into the imam’s and depends on it without qualification. He therefore requires the follower to repeat the prayer whenever the imam is required to repeat it.

Al-Shāfi‘ī’s underlying principle is that each person prays independently: the imam does not act on his behalf in either obligatory or recommended acts. Accordingly, he instructs the follower to say, “Allah hears whoever praises Him,” requires him to recite, and does not consider his prayer invalidated by a deficiency in the imam’s prayer. There are, however, certain exceptions: the imam assumes responsibility on the follower’s behalf for the prostrations for forgetfulness, and for recitation when the follower joins the prayer late; the prayer of a proficient reciter behind someone unable to recite properly is invalid; and there are other similar cases.

As for Mālik and Ahmad, they hold that the follower’s prayer depends on the imam’s in some respects but not others. This is illustrated by what we have mentioned: listening to the recitation when the imam recites aloud, and joining in the recitation when he recites silently. According to them, the follower does not say, “Allah hears whoever praises Him,” but responds to the imam’s saying it by praising Allah, as indicated by the authentic texts. The follower’s prayer depends on the imam’s in matters for which both are excused, but not in those for which they are not, as discussed earlier in connection with leading the prayer.

---

## Page 11988

He was asked:

Is it permissible for someone following an imam to recite behind him? If he does recite behind the imam, does he incur any sin?

He replied:

Reciting behind the imam does not invalidate the prayer according to the imams—may Allah be pleased with them. However, the scholars disagreed over which course is better for the follower.

The position of Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad is that it is better for him to recite when the imam is silent, as in the Zuhr and 'Asr prayers and the last two rak'ahs of Maghrib and 'Ishā'. Likewise, he should recite during prayers in which the imam recites aloud if he cannot hear his recitation.

The position of Abū Hanīfah is that it is better not to recite behind him under any circumstances. Among the early generations of Companions and Successors—may Allah be pleased with them—some would recite behind the imam and others would not.

When the follower can hear the imam’s recitation, however, the majority of scholars hold that he should listen and not recite at all. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad and others. Al-Shāfi'ī’s position is that he should recite al-Fātihah alone while the imam recites aloud. The position of

---

## Page 11989

a group, including al-Awzā'ī and other Syrian scholars, is that reciting it is recommended. This was also the view preferred by our grandfather. The majority of scholars distinguish between audible and silent recitation: the follower recites when the imam recites silently, but not when he recites aloud. This is the most balanced view, for Allah Most High says:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it and be silent, so that you may receive mercy.”

Thus, when the imam recites, the follower should listen; when the imam is silent, he should recite. Recitation is better than remaining silent without listening to anything. Whoever recites the Quran receives ten good deeds for every letter, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said. One should not forgo this reward to no purpose; rather, one should either be listening or reciting. And Allah, Glorified and Most High, knows best.

He was asked:

How much of the Friday prayer and congregational prayer must one catch to be considered to have joined them?

He replied:

The jurists differed on how much of the Friday prayer and congregational prayer one must catch, holding three views:

(The first is that neither is caught unless one catches a rak'ah. This is the position of Mālik

---

## Page 11990

and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, which a number of his followers preferred. It is also an opinion within the Shāfi'ī school, preferred by some of its scholars, such as Abū al-Mahāsin al-Riyānī and others.

The second view is that both are caught by a single takbīr (saying “Allah is greatest”). This is the position of Abū Hanīfah.

(The third view is that the Friday prayer is caught only by catching a rak'ah, whereas congregational prayer is caught by a single takbīr. This is the well-known position in the schools of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. The correct view, however, is the first, for several reasons:

First, the Lawgiver has not made any ruling depend on the time needed for a single takbīr, whether concerning the prescribed prayer time, Friday prayer, congregational prayer, or anything else. It is therefore a criterion to which the Lawgiver has given no weight, and it is not permissible to treat it as legally significant.

Second, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made the rulings depend on catching a rak'ah. Making them depend instead on a takbīr disregards what he treated as significant and gives significance to what he disregarded. All of this is unsound in matters where he made the rak'ah the determining criterion and made catching the prayer within its prescribed time depend upon it.

Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If any of you catches a rak'ah of the 'Asr prayer before the setting of

---

## Page 11991

the sun, let him complete his prayer. If he catches a rak'ah of the Fajr prayer before the sun rises, let him complete his prayer.”

As for the wording found in some chains of transmission, “If any of you catches a prostration,” what is meant is a complete rak'ah, as in the other wording. A complete prayer unit may be named after bowing and called a rak'ah, or after prostration and called a sajdah. This usage occurs frequently in the wording of hadiths, including this hadith and others.

Third, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made catching the prayer with the imam depend on catching a rak'ah. This explicitly addresses the issue. In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever catches a rak'ah of the prayer with the imam has caught the prayer.”

This is an explicit text that settles the dispute.

Fourth, the Friday prayer is caught only by catching a rak'ah, as stated in the rulings of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, including Ibn 'Umar, Ibn Mas'ūd, Anas and others. No Companion is known to have disagreed with them. Indeed, several scholars have reported that this was the consensus of the Companions. Drawing a distinction between Friday prayer and congregational prayer is unsound. For this reason, Abū Hanīfah consistently applied his principle and treated both alike. However, the established hadiths and the reports from the Companions invalidate his position.

Fifth, anything less than a rak'ah does not count towards the prayer, for he begins it afresh

---

## Page 11992

in its entirety on his own. Thus, he has not caught anything with the imam that counts towards his prayer. He and the imam have therefore not shared any part of the prayer that counts for him, so his entire prayer is that of someone praying alone.

This is clarified by the fact that he catches a rak'ah only if he catches the imam bowing. If he joins him after bowing, what he performs with him does not count towards that rak'ah, even though he has joined him in standing after bowing, prostrating, and sitting between the two prostrations. Since he has missed the greater part of the rak'ah—standing and bowing—he has missed the rak'ah. How, then, can it be said that he has caught the congregational prayer when he has not caught anything with the congregation that counts towards his prayer?

Catching the prayer by catching a rak'ah is analogous to catching a rak'ah by catching the bowing, for in both cases he has caught something that counts towards his prayer. If he has not caught a rak'ah of the prayer, he is like someone who has missed bowing with the imam and has therefore missed the rak'ah: in both cases, he has not caught anything that counts towards his prayer. This is among the soundest forms of analogy.

(Sixth:

A consequence of this is that if a traveller follows a resident imam and catches one rak'ah or more with him, he must perform the prayer in full. If he catches less than a rak'ah with him, he offers it in its shortened form. Imam Ahmad explicitly stated this in one of the two reports transmitted from him. This is because, by catching a rak'ah, he has followed a resident imam in part of his prayer and must therefore complete it in full. If he has not caught a rak'ah with him, his prayer is that of someone praying alone, so he offers it in its shortened form.

---

## Page 11993

It also follows that if a woman’s menstruation ends with enough time remaining before sunset for one rak'ah, she must offer the ‘Asr prayer. If it ends with enough time remaining before dawn for one rak'ah, she must offer the ‘Ishā’ prayer. If less than the time needed for one rak'ah remains, neither prayer is obligatory upon her.

As for the Zuhr and Maghrib prayers, must she also offer them in these circumstances? There is a well-known disagreement on this point. One view holds that she need not do so; this is the position of Abū Hanīfah. Another holds that she must; this is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. Imam Ahmad also related this view from Ibn ‘Abbās and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf.

Those who hold the latter view then differed over how much time must remain for the earlier prayer to become obligatory. They held two opinions:

The first is that it becomes obligatory when enough time remains to make the second prayer obligatory. Whether this means the time needed for one rak'ah or for saying “Allah is greatest” once is itself a matter of two opinions.

The second is that the earlier prayer becomes obligatory only if she has enough time remaining to perform it. This is the more correct view.

A similar disagreement concerns a woman who is free of menstruation when the prayer time begins, but whose menstruation then starts: must she make up that prayer or not? There are two opinions. The first is that she need not make it up, as Mālik and Abū Hanīfah maintain. The second is that she must, as al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad maintain.

---

## Page 11994

Those who hold that she must make up the prayer then differed over how much time must elapse for that obligation to become binding. They held two views:

The first is the time needed to say “Allah is greatest” once. This is the well-known position within Ahmad’s school.

The second is that enough time must have elapsed for her to purify herself and perform the prayer. This is the second view within the schools of Ahmad and al-Shāfi‘ī.

They then differed over whether she must also perform the second of the two prayers that may be combined, along with the first. There are two views, both reported from Imam Ahmad.

The position better supported by the evidence is that of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik: she is not required to make up any prayer. This is because making up a prayer requires a separate command, and there is no command here requiring her to do so. Moreover, she delayed the prayer permissibly and was therefore not negligent.

As for someone who sleeps through a prayer or forgets it, although he too is not negligent, his subsequent prayer is not a make-up prayer. Rather, when he wakes or remembers, that is the prayer’s appointed time for him. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sleeps through a prayer or forgets it, let him offer it when he remembers it, for that is its appointed time.”

There is not a single hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanding that a prayer be made up after its appointed time. Rather, the Sunnah prescribes repeating the prayer within its appointed time for someone who has omitted one of its obligatory elements. Examples include his command to the man who prayed improperly to repeat his prayer because he had omitted the required stillness; his command to someone who prayed alone behind the row to repeat his prayer because he had neglected the obligation to stand alongside others in the row; and his command

---

## Page 11995

to someone who had left a patch of his foot untouched by water to repeat his prayer, because he had failed to perform ablution as commanded. He instructed those who had slept through a prayer or forgotten it to pray when they remembered, for that is its appointed time for them. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling on the prayer of someone who rises and goes down before the imam, and persists despite being told not to do so? What must he do?

He answered:

Preceding the imam is forbidden by agreement of the imams. No one may bow before his imam, rise before him, or prostrate before him.

Numerous hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibit this, including his statement in an authentic hadith:

“Do not precede me in bowing or prostrating. Whatever head start I have over you when I bow, you will make up when I rise. I have grown heavy.”

He also said:

“The imam has only been appointed to be followed. When he says ‘Allah is greatest,’ say ‘Allah is greatest’; and when he bows, bow, for the imam bows before you and rises before you.”

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “The one offsets the other. When he says, ‘Allah hears whoever praises Him,’ say, ‘Our Lord, to You belongs all praise,’ and Allah will hear you. When he says ‘Allah is greatest’ and prostrates, say ‘Allah is greatest’ and prostrate, for the imam prostrates before you and rises before you. The one offsets the other.”

---

## Page 11996

And likewise his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Does the one who raises his head before the imam not fear that Allah may turn his head into a donkey’s head?”

This is because the follower follows the imam and takes him as his example. Someone who follows another and takes him as his example does not go ahead of him. If he does, he is like a donkey that does not understand the purpose of its work, as stated in another hadith:

“The one who speaks while the preacher is delivering the sermon is like a donkey carrying books.”

Whoever does this deserves punishment and disciplinary action that will deter him and others like him. It is related that ʿUmar saw a man moving ahead of the imam, so he struck him and said: “You have neither prayed on your own nor followed your imam.”

If someone inadvertently precedes the imam, his prayer is not invalidated, but he must lag behind him by as much as he had preceded him, as the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed. This is because the follower’s prayer is governed by the imam’s prayer. What he inadvertently does before the imam does not invalidate his prayer, because he has inadvertently added an act of the same kind as those prescribed in prayer. It is as though he had inadvertently added an extra bow or prostration, which does not invalidate the prayer according to the Sunnah and scholarly consensus.

However, according to the correct view, whatever he does before the imam does not count, because he has done it out of its proper place: the time before the imam acts is not the time for the follower to act. He is therefore like someone who prays before the appointed time, or someone who pronounces the opening declaration, “Allah is greatest,” before the imam does. This does not discharge the obligation Allah has imposed upon him. Rather, he must begin his prayer when its time arrives, not before it, and the follower must begin his prayer when the imam begins, not before him. Likewise, the follower

---

## Page 11997

must bow and prostrate when the imam bows and prostrates, not before him. Whatever he inadvertently does ahead of the imam is pardoned, but does not count. For this reason, the Companions and the leading scholars instructed him to lag behind by the same amount, so that his actions correspond to those of the imam.

As for someone who deliberately precedes the imam, there are two well-known views within the school of Ahmad and other schools concerning whether his prayer is invalidated. Those who hold it invalid argue that he has deliberately added something to the prayer, thereby invalidating it—just as his prayer would undoubtedly be invalid if he deliberately performed a bow or prostration before the imam, or deliberately added an extra bow or prostration to the prayer.

The Companions said to someone who preceded the imam: “You have neither prayed on your own nor followed your imam.” Whoever has prayed neither on his own nor as a follower has no valid prayer.

Accordingly, the worshipper must repent of preceding the imam, of rushing through the prayer, and of failing to come to rest in its postures. If he does not desist, everyone must enjoin upon him the right that Allah has commanded him to do and forbid him the wrong that Allah has forbidden him. If some of them undertake this, that suffices; otherwise, they are all sinful. Whoever is able to impose a discretionary punishment upon him and discipline him in the manner prescribed by Islamic law should do so. Whoever can do no more than shun him, and whose doing so would have an effect on him, should shun him until he repents. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11998

He was also asked about shaking hands immediately after prayer: “Is it a sunnah or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Shaking hands immediately after prayer is not a sunnah; rather, it is an innovation. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 11999

Chapter on Leading the Prayer

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked whether it is better to lead the prayer or refrain from doing so.

He replied:

Rather, he should lead them in prayer, and he will be rewarded for doing so, as stated in the hadith:

“Three will be upon mounds of musk on the Day of Resurrection: a man who leads people in prayer while they are pleased with him.”

The hadith continues. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked about two men: one has memorized the Quran and is a preacher who attends gatherings where the frame drum and flute are played; the other is a scholar who is scrupulous in religious observance. Which of them has the greater right to lead the prayer?

He replied:

It is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Abū Masʿūd al-Badrī, that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are

---

## Page 12000

equal in recitation, then the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then the one who migrated earliest. If they migrated at the same time, then the eldest.”

If both men are religiously upright, whichever is more knowledgeable about the Quran and Sunnah must be given precedence over the other. But if one of them is immoral—for example, known for lying, dishonesty, or similar conduct constituting sinfulness—while the other is a believer who fears Allah, then the latter has the greater right to lead the prayer, provided he is qualified to do so, even if the former is more proficient in recitation and more knowledgeable. For praying behind a sinful person is prohibited according to some scholars and discouraged, though not forbidden, according to others.

It has been related in a hadith:

“No immoral man is to lead a believer in prayer unless he overpowers him with a whip or stick.”

It is not permissible to appoint a sinful person to lead the prayer when a righteous person can be appointed. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12001

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section

As for praying behind people of deviant inclinations and religious innovations, or behind people of immoral conduct, there is well-known disagreement and further detail which this is not the place to discuss at length.

However, the most balanced view concerning such people is that it is not permissible to appoint one of them to lead the prayer when someone else can be appointed. Anyone who openly displays immoral conduct or religious innovations must be rebuked and forbidden to persist in them. The least degree of such rebuke is to shun him so that he gives up his immorality and innovation.

For this reason, the majority of leading scholars distinguished between someone who actively promotes his deviation and someone who does not. The former has made the wrongdoing public and thus deserves to be rebuked, unlike someone who remains silent: he is like a person who conceals his sin and is therefore not publicly rebuked. When a sin remains concealed, it harms only the person committing it; but when it is made public and goes unchallenged, it harms the community at large.

This is why the hypocrites’ outward professions were accepted and their inner secrets left to Allah Most High, unlike those who openly displayed disbelief.

---

## Page 12002

Thus, someone who actively promotes his deviation is barred from holding office, leading the prayer, giving testimony, and transmitting hadith, because this constitutes forbidding wrongdoing—not because the prayer is invalid or because his testimony and narrations are suspect. If a person can avoid appointing someone who openly displays wrongdoing to lead the prayer, he must do so.

However, if someone else has appointed him and one cannot remove him from leading the prayer, or can remove him only at the cost of an evil more harmful than the wrongdoing he openly displays, then it is not permissible to remedy a lesser evil with a greater one, or to avert the lesser of two harms by bringing about the greater. For the Sharia came to secure and enhance benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms as far as possible. Its aim is to give precedence to the greater of two goods when both cannot be attained, and to avert the worse of two evils when both cannot be averted.

If preventing someone who openly displays religious innovation and immorality from leading the prayer would cause greater harm than his leadership, doing so is not permissible. Rather, one should pray behind him those prayers that one cannot perform except behind him, such as Friday prayers, Eid prayers, and congregational prayers, when no other imam is available.

This is why the Companions would perform Friday and congregational prayers behind al-Hajjāj, al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd al-Thaqafī, and others. Missing Friday and congregational prayers is a greater evil than following an immoral imam in them, especially when staying away does not put an end to his immorality. In that case, a benefit prescribed by the Sharia is forfeited without that evil being averted. For this reason, those who categorically abandon Friday and congregational prayers behind unjust imams were regarded by

---

## Page 12003

the early generations and the imams as religious innovators. However, when Friday and congregational prayers can be performed behind a righteous imam, that is preferable to performing them behind an immoral one. If someone nevertheless prays behind an immoral imam without an excuse, the ruling is a matter of scholarly judgment.

Some scholars hold that he must repeat the prayer, because he has done something not sanctioned by the Sharia: by praying behind such a person, he has neglected his duty to condemn wrongdoing. His prayer behind that imam was therefore forbidden, so he must repeat it.

Others hold that he need not repeat it. They explain that the prayer itself is valid, while the failure to condemn wrongdoing is a matter separate from the prayer. This resembles conducting a sale after the call to Friday prayer.

However, if he can only pray behind that imam, as in the case of Friday prayer, then the prayer is not to be repeated. Repeating it is a practice of religious innovators. Some jurists have assumed that, if prayer behind an openly sinful imam is held to be invalid, Friday prayer performed behind him must be repeated; otherwise, it need not be repeated. But this is not so. Rather, the disagreement over repeating the prayer concerns cases in which a person is forbidden to pray behind that imam. When, however, he is commanded to pray behind him, the correct view is that he need not repeat the prayer, for, as explained earlier, the servant has not been commanded to perform the prayer twice.

---

## Page 12004

As for praying behind an adherent of deviant doctrines whose innovation renders him an unbeliever, scholars have disagreed even over Friday prayer behind him. Anyone who holds that he is an unbeliever is instructed to repeat the prayer, since it was offered behind an unbeliever. This question, however, concerns declaring adherents of deviant doctrines unbelievers—a matter over which there is considerable confusion. Two positions have been reported from Mālik, two from al-Shāfiʿī, and two from Imam Ahmad. The same applies to the speculative theologians: two positions have been attributed to al-Ashʿarī. Most of the imams’ positions on this matter involve distinctions.

The reality of the matter is this: a statement may constitute unbelief, so that a general ruling is pronounced declaring its proponent an unbeliever, and it is said, “Whoever says such-and-such is an unbeliever.” Yet a particular individual who makes that statement is not judged to be an unbeliever until the proof whose rejection constitutes unbelief has been established against him.

This is comparable to the scriptural texts threatening punishment. Allah, glorified and exalted, says:

“Indeed, those who unjustly devour the property of orphans are only consuming fire into their bellies, and they will burn in a blazing Fire.”

This and similar scriptural threats are true. Yet it cannot be affirmed that a particular individual will suffer the threatened punishment. Thus, no particular person among those who face the qiblah is declared destined for the Fire, since the threatened punishment may not apply to him because a condition is absent or an impediment is present. The prohibition may not have reached him; he may repent of the prohibited act; he may have great good deeds that erase the punishment for that act; he may be afflicted with calamities that expiate his sins; or an intercessor whose intercession is accepted may intercede for him.

---

## Page 12005

The same applies to statements whose utterance constitutes unbelief. A person may not have received the scriptural texts that would make the truth known to him; or he may have received them without their authenticity having been established to his satisfaction, or without being able to understand them. He may also have encountered doubts for which Allah excuses him. Any believer who strives diligently to seek the truth but errs will have his error forgiven by Allah, whatever it may be, whether in theoretical or practical matters. This is the position of the Prophet’s Companions—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the great majority of the imams of Islam. They did not divide questions into fundamental matters whose denial constitutes unbelief and subsidiary matters whose denial does not.

As for distinguishing between one category called fundamental matters and another called subsidiary matters, this distinction has no basis in the teachings of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, or the imams of Islam. Rather, it derives from the Muʿtazilah and similar adherents of religious innovation. It was from them that the jurists who mentioned it in their books acquired it. Moreover, this distinction is inconsistent, for anyone who distinguishes between the two categories is asked:

What defines the fundamental matters in which a person’s error constitutes unbelief? What distinguishes them from subsidiary matters?

If he says that fundamental matters concern belief, whereas subsidiary matters concern practice, he is told in response:

People have disagreed over whether Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—saw his Lord; whether ʿUthmān was superior to ʿAlī or ʿAlī was superior; and over many meanings of the Quran and the authenticity of certain hadiths. These are matters of belief and knowledge, yet there is agreement that error in them does not constitute unbelief.

---

## Page 12006

And the obligation to pray, pay zakat, fast, and perform Hajj, and the prohibition of shameful deeds and intoxicants, are practical matters; yet there is agreement that denying them constitutes unbelief.

If he says, “Fundamental matters are those established with certainty,” he is told in reply:

“No. Many practical matters are established with certainty, while many matters of knowledge are not. Whether a matter is certain or probable is relative. A matter may be certain to one person because the conclusive evidence is clear to him—as in the case of someone who heard the text directly from the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and knew with certainty what he meant by it. To another person, it may not even be probable, let alone certain, because the text has not reached him, its authenticity has not been established to his satisfaction, or he has been unable to ascertain its meaning.”

The authentic collections record the Prophet’s account—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—of the man who said to his family:

“‘When I die, burn me, then crush me, then scatter me in the sea. By Allah, if Allah has power over me, He will punish me as He has never punished anyone in all creation.’

“Then Allah commanded the land to return what it had taken of him, and the sea to return what it had taken of him. He said, ‘What made you do what you did?’ The man replied, ‘Fear of You, my Lord.’ So Allah forgave him.”

This man doubted Allah’s power and the resurrection. Indeed, he thought that he would not be brought back and that Allah would have no power over him if he did that; yet Allah forgave him. These matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 12007

But the point here is that the positions of the imams rest on this distinction between the general category and the particular individual. Some people have therefore reported disagreement among them on this matter without grasping the full import of their statements. Some report two positions from Ahmad, without qualification, on declaring adherents of religious innovation unbelievers. They even extend this disagreement to declaring the Murji’ah and the Shia who accord precedence to Alī unbelievers, and sometimes favor declaring them unbelievers condemned to remain in Hell forever. This is not the position of Ahmad or any other imam of Islam.

Rather, Ahmad’s position is consistent: he did not declare unbelievers those Murji’ah who say, “Faith consists of speech without deeds,” nor those who give Alī precedence over Uthmān. Indeed, his explicit statements clearly refrain from declaring the Khawārij, the Qadariyyah, and others unbelievers. He did, however, declare the Jahmiyyah who deny Allah’s names and attributes unbelievers, because their statements plainly and clearly contradict what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought, and because their doctrine amounts in reality to negating the Creator. He had suffered at their hands and thus came to understand the true nature of their doctrine and that it revolves around negation. That the early generations and the imams declared the Jahmiyyah unbelievers is well known.

Yet he did not declare their individual members unbelievers. One who propagates a doctrine is more culpable than one who merely holds it; one who punishes those who disagree with him is more culpable than one who merely propagates it; and one who declares his opponents unbelievers is more culpable than one who punishes them. Nevertheless, those in authority held the Jahmiyyah’s doctrines—that the Quran is created, that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter, and other such beliefs—and called people to accept them

---

## Page 12008

and subjected them to tests, punished them if they refused to comply, and declared those who refused to comply unbelievers. They even went so far as to withhold a captive’s release until he professed the Jahmiyyah’s doctrine that the Quran is created, and their other beliefs. They would neither appoint anyone to office nor grant anyone a stipend from the public treasury unless he professed these beliefs. Despite this, Imam Ahmad—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—prayed for mercy and forgiveness for them, knowing that it had not been made clear to them that they were denying the Messenger or rejecting what he had brought. Rather, they had interpreted it incorrectly and followed those who taught them these doctrines.

Likewise, when Hafs al-Fard said, “The Quran is created,” al-Shāfi‘ī told him, “You have disbelieved in Allah the Almighty.” He explained to him that this statement constituted unbelief, but did not judge Hafs an apostate merely on that account, because the proof on which such a judgment would rest had not become clear to Hafs. Had he considered him an apostate, he would have sought his execution. Moreover, he explicitly stated in his books that the testimony of adherents of religious innovation is admissible and that prayer behind them is valid.

Similarly, Mālik—may Allah have mercy on him—al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad said concerning an adherent of the Qadariyyah: “If he denies Allah’s knowledge, he is an unbeliever.” Some of them expressed it as follows: “Debate the Qadariyyah on the basis of Allah’s knowledge: if they acknowledge it, they are defeated in argument; if they deny it, they are unbelievers.”

Ahmad was asked whether an adherent of the Qadariyyah becomes an unbeliever. He replied, “If he denies Allah’s knowledge, he becomes an unbeliever.” In that case, one who denies Allah’s knowledge belongs to the same category as the Jahmiyyah. As for executing one who calls to

---

## Page 12009

religious innovation, he may be executed to protect people from his harm, just as one who engages in violent aggression may be executed, even when no actual unbelief is involved. Not everyone whose execution is ordered is executed for apostasy. Accordingly, the execution of Ghaylān al-Qadarī and others may have been on this basis. These questions are discussed at length elsewhere; here we have merely drawn attention to them.

Section

As for someone who cannot recite al-Fātihah correctly, only someone similarly unable may pray behind him. Thus, one must not pray behind someone whose speech impediment causes him to substitute one letter for another, except in the case of the letter dād when he pronounces it from the front of the mouth, as many people habitually do. There are two views on this:

Some hold that one may not pray behind him and that even his own prayer is invalid, because he has substituted one letter for another: dād is articulated at the side of the mouth, whereas zā’ is articulated at the tips of the teeth. Thus, if he says “wa lā al-zāllīn,” the meaning would derive from zalla: “he continued doing such-and-such.”

The second view is that the prayer is valid. This is the more persuasive view, because the two letters sound the same to the ear, and the auditory impression of one is akin to that of the other, owing to the similarity of their points of articulation. The reciter intends only going astray, the opposite of guidance, and that is what the listener understands. The meaning derived from zalla does not occur to anyone. This is unlike the case of two letters

---

## Page 12010

that differ in sound, point of articulation, and auditory impression, as when rā’ is replaced with ghayn, for such a substitution does not fulfil the purpose of recitation.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about praying behind al-Marāziqah and about their religious innovation.

He replied:

A person may perform the five daily prayers, Friday prayer, and other prayers behind someone whom he does not know to be guilty of religious innovation or sinfulness. This is agreed upon by the four imams and other leading Muslim scholars.

It is not a condition of following an imam in prayer that one know his beliefs or examine him by asking, “What do you believe?” Rather, one may pray behind someone whose religious standing is unknown to him. If someone prays behind a man whom he knows to be sinful or an innovator, there are two well-known views concerning the validity of his prayer within the schools of Ahmad and Mālik. The position of al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfah is that it is valid.

As for someone who says, “I entrust my wealth only to someone I know,” meaning, “I will not pray behind someone I do not know, just as I entrust my wealth only to someone I know,” these are the words of an ignorant person; none of the imams of Islam has said this. For if someone deposits his wealth with a man he does not know, that man may betray him

---

## Page 12011

in that trust or may let it go to waste.

As for the imam, if he makes a mistake or forgets, those praying behind him are not held accountable for it. As recorded in al-Bukhārī and elsewhere, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Your imams pray for you and for themselves. If they do what is right, you and they are rewarded; if they err, you are rewarded, while they bear responsibility.”

Thus, responsibility for the imam’s mistake rests with him, not with those praying behind him. ʿUmar and other Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—led the prayer while in a state of major ritual impurity, having forgotten their condition. They repeated their prayers but did not instruct those who had prayed behind them to repeat theirs. This is the position of the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, according to the well-known position reported from him.

The same applies if the imam does something he considers permissible but which the follower considers to invalidate the prayer—for example, praying without performing ablution in accordance with his own conviction, touching his private part, or omitting the basmalah (the opening invocation of Allah’s name). The imam believes that his prayer remains valid despite this, whereas the follower believes that it does not. The majority of scholars hold that the follower’s prayer is valid. This is the position of Mālik and of Ahmad according to the stronger of the two reports from him—indeed, the more explicit of them. It is also one of the two positions within the school of al-Shāfiʿī, adopted by al-Qaffāl and others.

Even if the imam deliberately prayed without ablution, and the follower remained unaware of this until his death, Allah would not hold the follower accountable for it, nor would he bear any sin, by agreement of the Muslims. This differs from the case in which he knows that the imam is praying without ablution: he may not pray behind him, for such a person is not praying but merely playing. If, however, he learns after the prayer that the imam prayed without ablution, there is disagreement over whether he must repeat it.

If the follower knows that the imam

---

## Page 12012

is an innovator who calls others to his innovation, or an openly sinful man, and is the appointed imam behind whom alone the prayer can be offered—such as the imam of the Friday and Eid prayers, or the imam leading the prayer at ʿArafah during Hajj—then the follower should pray behind him, according to the generality of earlier and later scholars. This is the position of Ahmad, al-Shāfiʿī, Abū Hanīfah, and others.

For this reason, they state in their creeds that one should offer the Friday and Eid prayers behind every imam, whether righteous or wicked. Likewise, if a village has only one imam, the congregational prayers should be offered behind him, for praying in congregation is better than praying alone, even if the imam is sinful.

This is the position of the vast majority of scholars, including Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Shāfiʿī, and others. Indeed, according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school, congregational prayer is an individual obligation. Whoever abandons the Friday and congregational prayers because the imam is wicked is himself an innovator according to Imam Ahmad and other imams of the Sunnah, as he stated in the epistles of ʿAbdūs, Ibn Mālik, and al-ʿAttār.

The correct view is that he should offer these prayers without repeating them. The Companions used to offer the Friday and congregational prayers behind wicked imams without repeating them. Ibn ʿUmar prayed behind al-Hajjāj, while Ibn Masʿūd and others prayed behind al-Walīd ibn ʿUqbah, who used to drink wine. On one occasion, he even led them in four units of the dawn prayer, then asked, “Shall I give you more?”

Ibn Masʿūd replied, “You have been giving us extra all day!” For this reason, they reported him to ʿUthmān. In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, it is related that ʿUthmān—may Allah be pleased with him—

---

## Page 12013

was under siege when a man led the people in prayer. Someone asked ʿUthmān, “You are the imam of the community, whereas this man leading the people in prayer is a leader of sedition.”

He replied, “My nephew, prayer is among the best deeds people perform. When they do good, join them in doing good; when they do wrong, avoid their wrongdoing.” There are many similar reports.

The prayer of a sinful person or an innovator is valid in itself, so someone’s prayer behind him is not invalidated. Those who disapproved of praying behind him did so only because enjoining good and forbidding evil is obligatory. This includes not appointing someone who openly displays innovation or wickedness as an imam for the Muslims, for he deserves disciplinary punishment until he repents. If he can be shunned until he repents, that is commendable.

If some people’s refraining from praying behind him and praying behind someone else instead would have an effect—leading him to repent or be removed, or deterring others from committing the same sin—then their refraining from praying behind him serves a beneficial purpose, without causing them to miss either the Friday prayer or congregational prayer. But if refraining from praying behind him would cause a person to miss the Friday and congregational prayers, then only an innovator who opposes the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—would refrain from praying behind such imams.

Likewise, if the imam has been appointed by the authorities and refraining from praying behind him serves no beneficial purpose, a person is not required to refrain from praying behind him. Rather, praying behind the better imam is preferable.

All of this concerns someone whose sinfulness is evident, or whose innovation is manifestly contrary to the Quran and Sunnah, such as the innovations of the Rāfidah and the Jahmiyyah

---

## Page 12014

and others like them. Whoever rejects the doctrine of the Rāfidah yet does not attend Friday or congregational prayers, and even declares Muslims unbelievers, has fallen into a position like theirs. Among the gravest matters for which Ahl al-Sunnah censured them are their abandonment of Friday and congregational prayers and their declaring the majority of Muslims unbelievers.

Section

As for praying behind an innovator, this issue is subject to disagreement and requires distinctions. If no other imam is available—as when Friday prayer is held in only one location, or in the case of the two Eid prayers or the prayers during Hajj behind the imam of the pilgrimage—these prayers are to be performed behind any imam, righteous or sinful, by agreement of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah. Only adherents of innovation, such as the Rāfidah and others who do not recognize Friday and congregational prayers, abandon such prayers behind the imams.

If there is only one mosque in the village, praying in congregation behind a sinful imam is better than praying alone at home, lest this lead to abandoning congregational prayer altogether. If, however, one can pray behind someone who is not an innovator, that is undoubtedly better and preferable. But if one does pray behind an innovator, the scholars disagree over the validity of the prayer. According to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfah, the prayer is valid. As for Mālik and Ahmad, there is disagreement within their respective schools, with further distinctions.

---

## Page 12015

This applies only to innovations known to contradict the Quran and Sunnah, such as those of the Rāfidah, the Jahmiyyah, and others like them. As for religious questions over which many people in these lands dispute, such as “the question of letters and sound” and similar matters, both disputants may be innovators, each ignorant and relying on an interpretation. One is therefore no more justified in refusing to pray behind the other than the reverse. But when the Sunnah has become clear and known, yet someone contravenes it, that is the case concerning which there is disagreement. Allah knows best. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

He was asked:

A man who leads the prayer is widely reported to consume hashish. Someone said, “It is not permissible to pray behind him.” Another man objected, saying, “It is permissible,” and cited the words of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“It is permissible to pray behind both the righteous and the sinful.”

Is the man who objected correct or mistaken? Is someone who consumes hashish permitted to lead people in prayer? If the man who objected is correct, what must the person who challenged him do? May the administrator of the place dismiss the imam or not?

He answered:

It is not permissible to appoint someone who consumes hashish or commits prohibited, reprehensible acts to lead people in prayer when someone better than him can be appointed.

---

## Page 12016

How could it be otherwise, when a hadith states:

“Whoever appoints a man to an office over a group, while he can find among them someone more pleasing to Allah, has betrayed Allah, His Messenger, and the believers.”

Another hadith states:

“Appoint the best among you as your imams, for they are your representatives before Allah.”

Another hadith states:

“When a man leads people in prayer while someone among them is better than him, they will remain in decline.”

It is established in the Sahīh collection that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who migrated earliest. If they migrated at the same time, then by the oldest.”

Thus, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded that precedence be given to the one who excels in knowledge of the Book, then of the Sunnah, then to precedence in righteous deeds acquired through one’s own actions, and then to precedence conferred by Allah Most High.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere, it is reported:

“A man from among the Ansār (Helpers) used to lead a group in prayer. He spat in the direction of the qiblah, so the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) instructed them to remove him from his position as imam and not to pray behind him. He came to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and asked whether he had ordered them to remove him. He replied, ‘Yes. You have offended Allah and His Messenger.’”

If a man is removed for his misconduct during prayer and for spitting towards the qiblah, then what of someone who persists in consuming hashish—especially if he regards its intoxicating forms as lawful, as some people do? Such a person should be called upon to repent: if he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death. For intoxication from it is forbidden by consensus, and deeming that lawful is disbelief, without dispute.

---

## Page 12017

As for the opponent’s reliance on the statement:

“It is permissible to pray behind anyone, whether righteous or wicked,”

he is mistaken for several reasons.

First: This hadith has not been established as a report from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Rather, Sunan Ibn Mājah reports from him:

“No wicked man should lead a believer in prayer unless he compels him with a whip or a stick.”

The chain of transmission of this other report is also subject to criticism.

Second: A worshipper may pray behind someone who has been appointed, even though appointing that person was impermissible. People may not appoint openly sinful men over themselves, even though such a person’s judgments may be legally effective or prayer behind him may be valid.

Third: The imams agree that praying behind an openly sinful person is disliked, but they differ over its validity. Some hold that it is invalid, as reported from Mālik and Ahmad in one of the two reports from each of them. Others hold that it is valid, as is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, and the other report from Mālik and Ahmad. They did not dispute that such a person should not be appointed.

Fourth: There is no disagreement among Muslims that these openly sinful people who become intoxicated by hashish must be rebuked. Indeed, the majority of imams hold that both small and large amounts of it are forbidden. Moreover, the correct view is that whoever consumes it is subject to the prescribed punishment and that it is ritually impure. Thus, if someone who consumes it does not rinse it from his mouth, his prayer is invalid. Even if he rinses his mouth

---

## Page 12018

of it, it remains an intoxicant. The hadith states:

“Whoever drinks wine will have no prayer accepted from him for forty days. If he repents, Allah will accept his repentance. If he returns to drinking it, no prayer will be accepted from him for forty days. If he repents, Allah will accept his repentance. If he returns to drinking it a third or fourth time, it will be incumbent upon Allah to give him Tīnat al-khabāl to drink.” He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, what is Tīnat al-khabāl?” He replied, “The bodily discharges of the inhabitants of the Fire.”

Since his prayer is invalid in some circumstances and unaccepted in others, rebuking him is obligatory by the agreement of the Muslims. Whoever fails to rebuke him is disobeying Allah and His Messenger. Whoever prevents him from being rebuked has opposed Allah and His Messenger. In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is reported to have said:

“Whoever intercedes to prevent the enforcement of one of Allah’s prescribed punishments has opposed Allah’s command. Whoever says something untrue about a believer will be confined in the mire of corruption until he frees himself from the consequences of what he has said. Whoever knowingly argues in support of falsehood will remain under Allah’s wrath until he desists.”

Thus, those who argue in his defence are arguing in support of falsehood and are under Allah’s wrath. Those who obstruct such rebuke of him are opposing Allah’s command. Everyone who knows his condition yet fails to rebuke him to the best of his ability is disobeying Allah and His Messenger. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12019

He was asked about a preacher who came for Friday prayer, but the people refused to pray behind him because he adhered to a religious innovation: “What kind of innovation precludes praying behind him?”

He replied:

They have no right to prevent anyone from performing the Eid and Friday prayers, even if the imam is sinful. Nor may they abandon Friday prayer and similar prayers because of the imam’s sinfulness. Rather, they must perform these prayers behind the imam, even if he is sinful. If they suspend them because of the imam’s sinfulness, they are among the people of innovation. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others.

The scholars differed only concerning an imam who is sinful or adheres to religious innovation when it is possible to pray behind an upright imam.

One view is that prayer behind him is valid even if he is sinful. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, and of Abū Hanīfah.

Another view is that prayer behind a sinful imam is invalid when it is possible to pray behind an upright imam. This is one of the two positions reported from Mālik and Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12020

He was asked:

“An imam says from the pulpit during his Friday sermon: ‘Allah spoke with eternal, beginningless speech consisting of neither letters nor sound.’ Does Friday prayer behind him discharge the obligation or not? And what is required of him?”

He replied:

Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah agree that the Quran is the speech of Allah, revealed and uncreated, and that this Quran which people recite is the speech of Allah, which they recite with their own voices. The speech is the Creator’s, while the voice is the reciter’s. The Quran in its entirety—its letters and its meanings—is the speech of Allah. If the imam adheres to religious innovation, Friday prayer is nevertheless to be performed behind him, and doing so discharges the obligation. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is prayer behind an imam who killed his paternal cousin valid or not?”

He replied:

If this man has deliberately and unlawfully killed a Muslim,

---

## Page 12021

then he should be removed from his position as imam, and prayer should not be performed behind him except out of necessity, such as when no other imam is available. But if he repents and mends his ways, Allah accepts His servants’ repentance and pardons their misdeeds. If he repents in accordance with Islamic law, he may be retained as imam. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

“May prayer be performed behind a mosque imam who has killed someone?”

He replied:

If the killer committed the initial killing, and the victim’s relatives then turned against the killer’s relatives and killed them, these people are aggressors and among the most unjust of people. Concerning them, Allah Most High revealed:

“But whoever transgresses after that will have a painful punishment.”

For this reason, a group of the early Muslim scholars held that the ruler must execute such killers as a prescribed punishment and that they may not be pardoned. The majority of scholars, however, leave their fate to the victim’s next of kin.

Any preacher who becomes involved in bloodshed of this kind is among those guilty of aggression and transgression, who must be removed from office. He is unfit to be an imam for the Muslims; rather, he is an imam for unjust aggressors. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12022

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

“An imam who leads Muslims in prayer turned a woman against her husband until she separated from him, and he began meeting with her in seclusion. May prayer be performed behind him? What is the ruling concerning him?”

He replied:

It is reported in al-Musnad that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“He is not one of us who turns a woman against her husband or a slave against his masters.”

A man’s efforts to separate a woman from her husband are among the grave sins. This is the work of sorcerers and among the most heinous deeds of devils, especially if he turns her against her husband so that he himself may marry her, while persisting in meeting with her in seclusion—and especially if the circumstances indicate something beyond that.

Such a man should not be appointed to lead Muslims in prayer unless he repents. If he repents, Allah will accept his repentance. If it is possible to pray behind an upright man of sound conduct, one should pray behind him. Prayer should not be performed behind someone whose wickedness is manifest unless there is a need. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12023

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

“Is prayer valid behind an imam who recites the Quran at funerals?”

He replied:

If one can pray behind someone who performs the prayer fully and is scrupulously pious, praying behind him is preferable to praying behind someone who recites at funerals. This is disliked on two counts:

For one, reciting at funerals is disliked in the four schools of law. Taking payment for it is even more strongly disliked, for none of the scholars has permitted hiring someone to recite the Quran. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

“Is it permissible to pray behind an imam who spits in the prayer niche?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. He should be told to stop doing that.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is related concerning the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“He dismissed an imam for spitting in the direction of prayer and said to the people of the mosque, ‘Do not pray behind him.’ The man then came to the Prophet

---

## Page 12024

peace and blessings be upon him, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, did you forbid them to pray behind me?’ He replied, ‘Yes. You have offended Allah and His Messenger.’”

If he is removed from leading the prayer for this reason, or the congregation refrains from praying behind him for this reason, that is permissible. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about a learned jurist who had memorized the Quran but had a disability: his left arm lay behind him from the shoulder, and he had fleshy fingers. People had said that praying behind him was not permissible.

He replied:

If both his hands reach the ground when he prostrates, praying behind him is permissible without dispute. The disagreement concerns only someone whose hands and feet have been amputated, or similar cases. But if he can prostrate on the seven bodily parts mentioned by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, when he said:

“I have been commanded to prostrate on seven bones: the forehead, the two hands, the two knees, and the two feet,”

then his prostration is complete, and the prayer of those behind him is complete. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12025

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is prayer behind a eunuch valid?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Prayer behind him is valid, just as it is behind an uncastrated man, by agreement of the leading Muslim scholars. He is more entitled to lead the prayer than someone less qualified than he is. Thus, if he surpasses another in knowledge and religious commitment, he takes precedence over him in leading the prayer, even if the less qualified man is uncastrated. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man does not have enough to support himself and leads prayers for payment. Is that permissible or not?

He replied:

Hiring someone to lead the prayer is not permissible according to the best-known position in the schools of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and Ahmad.

It has also been held that it is permissible. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, one view reported from Ahmad, and an opinion within Mālik’s school. The disagreement also extends to giving the call to prayer.

---

## Page 12026

However, the best-known position in Mālik’s school is that hiring someone to give the call to prayer is permissible, as is hiring someone to lead the prayer, whether together with giving the call or separately. A third opinion in Ahmad’s school and elsewhere holds that hiring someone for these and similar duties, such as teaching, is permissible when there is a need, but not otherwise. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man who serves as a muʿarrif for boats has built a mosque and allocated a monthly payment to its imam from his own funds. Is this lawful or unlawful? Is it permissible to pray in the mosque or not?

He replied:

If he pays this money from the rental income of boats he owns, it is permissible to accept it. But if he pays it from money he takes from people without right, it is not. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man is the imam of a town but is not upright in character. Another man in the town dislikes praying behind him. Is his prayer behind him valid or not? And if he does not pray

---

## Page 12027

behind him and refrains from praying with the congregation, does he thereby incur sin? The man who dislikes praying behind him believes that he does not recite al-Fātihah correctly, and there are others in the town who are more proficient in Quranic recitation and more knowledgeable in jurisprudence.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

Praise be to Allah. As for his supposedly being unable to recite al-Fātihah correctly, that is highly unlikely. Most people, both ordinary people and those of learning, recite al-Fātihah adequately for their prayer to be valid. Subtle errors in recitation and errors that do not alter the meaning do not invalidate the prayer. Moreover, al-Fātihah has many variant readings that have been recited.

For example, one might recite “ʿalayhim,” “ʿalayhum,” or “ʿalayhumu” (“upon them”); or “al-sirāta” with sād, “al-sirāta” with sīn, or “al-zirāta” with zāy (“the path”). These are well-known readings.

Likewise, if one recited “al-hamdu lillāhi” or “al-hamdu lullāhi” (“praise be to Allah”), or recited “rabbu al-ʿālamīn” or “rabbi al-ʿālamīn” (“Lord of the worlds”), or used an i-vowel or something similar, these would be readings that have been recited, and prayer behind someone who uses them is valid.

Even if one recited “rabbi al-ʿālamīn” (“Lord of the worlds”) with a u-vowel, or “māliki yawm al-dīn” (“Master of the Day of Judgment”) with an a-vowel, this would be an error that neither alters the meaning nor invalidates the prayer.

If he is the regularly appointed imam, one should pray behind him even if someone in the town is more proficient in Quranic recitation. For the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No one should lead a man in prayer within his sphere of authority.”

Even if he openly engages in sin, and no one else there conducts the congregational prayer, one should pray

---

## Page 12028

behind him in that case as well, and not abandon the congregation. If he abandons it, he is sinning and acting contrary to the Book, the Sunnah, and the practice of the early generations.

He was asked:

A man led the prayer without ablution, unaware of this, or had an impurity on him of which he was unaware. Is his prayer valid or not? If his prayer is valid, is the prayer of those following him also valid? Please give us a ruling, and may you be rewarded.

He replied:

If someone praying behind an imam does not learn of the imam’s state of ritual impurity, or of the impurity on him, until the prayer has ended, he does not have to repeat the prayer according to al-Shāfiʿī. The same applies according to Mālik and Ahmad if the imam himself was unaware. The imam alone must repeat the prayer if he was in a state of ritual impurity.

This was the established practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs: they led the people in prayer, then discovered afterward that they had been in a state of major ritual impurity. They repeated their prayers but did not instruct the people to repeat theirs. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12029

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section: On the dependence of a follower’s prayer upon the imam’s prayer

There are three views on this matter. The first is that there is no connection between the two prayers: each person prays for himself, and the benefit of following an imam lies in the increased reward of congregational prayer. This is generally what al-Shāfiʿī’s underlying principle entails. However, an objection has been raised based on his prohibition of someone who can recite following someone who cannot, or a man following a woman, and his ruling that a follower’s prayer is invalid if he follows someone whose prayer has no validity, such as an unbeliever or someone in a state of ritual impurity. These questions require discussion that does not belong here.

Among the evidence supporting this view is the Prophet’s statement, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning imams:

“If they do well, you and they receive the reward; if they do wrong, you receive the reward, while they bear the blame.”

The second view is that the follower’s prayer depends upon the imam’s prayer and is subordinate to it without qualification. Thus, every defect in the imam’s prayer carries over into the follower’s prayer, in accordance with the Prophet’s statement, peace and blessings be upon him:

“The imam is a guarantor.”

According to this view, someone who follows an imam in a state of ritual impurity

---

## Page 12030

who has forgotten his state of impurity must repeat the prayer, just as his imam must. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one view reported from Ahmad, adopted by Abū al-Khattāb. Some who hold this view, such as Muhammad ibn al-Hasan, even maintain that someone who has performed ablution should not follow someone who has performed dry ablution, because the latter’s purification is less complete than his own.

The third view is that the follower’s prayer is linked to the imam’s prayer, but a deficiency carries over into the follower’s prayer only when neither has an excuse. Where there is an excuse, the deficiency does not carry over. Thus, if the imam believes himself to be ritually pure, he is excused for leading the prayer, and the follower is excused for following him. This is the view of Mālik, Ahmad and others. The reports transmitted from the Companions on this issue are to be understood accordingly. It is the most balanced of the views, as we have explained concerning deficiencies in the imam himself: the ruling concerning him when there is a need differs from the ruling when there is no need. The ruling concerning his prayer thus follows the ruling concerning him.

This principle also governs following an imam who has omitted something the follower considers obligatory for prayer, when the imam is acting on an admissible interpretation—for example, when he does not renew his ablution after the discharge of impurities or after touching his penis, and similar cases. The imam’s belief here that his prayer is valid is comparable to his belief that it is valid when he is unaware of his state of ritual impurity; indeed, it is even more justified, since in the latter case he is required to repeat the prayer. This too is a useful principle.

The soundness of this view is supported by the report al-Bukhārī included in his Sahīh, from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“They pray

---

## Page 12031

for you. If they do so correctly, both you and they receive the reward; if they err, you receive the reward, while they bear the consequences.” This explicitly establishes that when the imam errs, the consequences of his error fall upon him, not upon those praying behind him.

Thus, if someone prays believing himself ritually pure when he is actually in a state of minor or major ritual impurity, or has physical impurity on him—and we hold that physical impurity requires him to repeat the prayer just as ritual impurity does—then this imam is mistaken in his belief. He therefore bears the consequences of his error and must repeat his prayer. As for those praying behind him, their prayer counts for them, and they bear none of the consequences of his error, as the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, explicitly stated.

This explicitly establishes that their prayer fulfils their obligation. The same applies if the imam omits an obligatory element of prayer on the basis of an interpretation that the follower considers mistaken. For example, he may touch his penis and then pray, undergo cupping and then pray, omit reciting “In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,” or pray with physical impurity on him that the follower does not consider excusable, and so forth. At worst, this imam is mistaken, if he is not in fact correct. The follower’s prayer therefore counts for him, and he bears none of the consequences of his imam’s error.

Likewise, Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd narrated from ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir, may Allah be pleased with him, who said: I heard the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, say:

“Whoever leads the people in prayer, observes its proper time and performs it in full, both he and they receive the reward. Whoever falls short in any of this bears the consequences himself, not they.”

However, Abū Dāwūd did not include the words “and performs it in full.” The first hadith explains that this shortcoming means an error. The implication of his words,

“If he errs, he bears the consequences, not they,”

is that the same does not apply if he acts deliberately. Moreover, Muslims agree that one should not pray behind someone who omits the unanimously agreed-upon pillars of prayer.

---

## Page 12032

He was also asked:

What about a man who leads people in prayer when most of them dislike him?

He replied:

If they dislike this imam because of some fault in his religious conduct—such as lying, injustice, ignorance, religious innovation or the like—and prefer another because he is more upright in his religion, being more truthful, more knowledgeable and more devout, then the imam they prefer must be appointed to lead them. The imam they dislike has no right to lead them.

As related in the hadith, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“There are three whose prayers do not rise beyond their ears: a man who leads people in prayer when they dislike him; a man who does not come to prayer until its time has passed; and a man who enslaves someone who has been freed.”

And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

Regarding the adherents of the four schools of jurisprudence: is their prayer behind one another valid or not? Did any of the early generations say that they should not pray behind one another? Is someone who says this an innovator or not? And if the imam does something which he believes

---

## Page 12033

that his prayer remains valid despite it, while the follower believes otherwise—for example, if the imam has vomited, had a nosebleed, undergone cupping, touched his penis, touched women with or without desire, laughed aloud during prayer, or eaten camel meat, and then prayed without renewing his ablution, whereas the follower believes that these things require ablution; or if the imam does not recite the basmalah (“In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful”), omits the final tashahhud (testimony of faith), or does not conclude the prayer with the salutation of peace, whereas the follower believes these to be obligatory—is the follower’s prayer valid in these circumstances? And if a mosque’s imam is required to follow a particular school of jurisprudence, but someone else who is more knowledgeable about the Quran and Sunnah is appointed, is that permissible? Is prayer behind him valid or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, they may pray behind one another, just as the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the four imams who came after them prayed behind one another despite their disagreements over these and other issues. None of the early generations said that they should not pray behind one another. Whoever rejects this is a misguided innovator who opposes the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations of this Muslim community and its imams.

Among the Companions, the Followers and those who came after them, some recited the basmalah and others did not; some recited it aloud and others did not. Some offered the qunūt supplication in the dawn prayer and others did not; and some of them

---

## Page 12034

renewed their ablution after cupping, nosebleeds, or vomiting, while others did not. Some renewed their ablution after touching the penis or touching women with desire, while others did not. Some renewed their ablution after laughing aloud during prayer, while others did not.

Some renewed their ablution after eating camel meat, while others did not. Despite this, they prayed behind one another.

For example, Abū Hanīfah and his companions, al-Shāfiʿī, and others prayed behind the Mālikī imams of Medina, even though those imams did not recite the basmalah, either silently or aloud. Abū Yūsuf prayed behind al-Rashīd, who had undergone cupping and whom Mālik had advised that he need not renew his ablution. Abū Yūsuf prayed behind him and did not repeat his prayer.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal held that ablution must be renewed after cupping or a nosebleed. He was asked:

“If the imam has bled and has not renewed his ablution, would you pray behind him?”

He replied:

“How could I not pray behind Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib and Mālik?”

In short, these issues involve two situations:

The first is when the follower does not know that his imam has done anything that invalidates the prayer. In this case, the follower may pray behind him, by agreement of the early generations, the four imams, and others.

There was no disagreement on this among the earlier scholars. Only some fanatical later scholars dissented, claiming

---

## Page 12035

that prayer behind a Hanafī is invalid even if he performs all the obligatory acts, because he performs them without believing them to be obligatory. Anyone who holds this view is more deserving of being called to repent—as adherents of religious innovation are—than of having his dissent accorded weight. For Muslims have always prayed behind one another, from the time of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his caliphs. Most prayer leaders do not distinguish between obligatory and recommended acts; rather, they perform the prayer as prescribed. Had knowledge of this distinction been obligatory, the prayers of most Muslims would have been invalid, and taking precautions would not have been possible. Many of these matters are disputed, and the evidence concerning them is not readily apparent. The most a devout person can do is take precautions in light of the disagreement, without being certain which of the two views is correct.

If certainty about either view were obligatory, most people would be unable to attain it. The proponent of this view himself relies on nothing more than following the authority of certain jurists. Were he asked to produce scriptural evidence establishing that his imam’s position, rather than another’s, is correct, he would be unable to do so. This is why the dissent of someone like him carries no weight: he is not qualified to exercise independent juristic reasoning.

The second situation:

The follower is certain that the imam has done something that, in the follower’s view, prevents him from praying validly: for example, the imam touches his penis, touches women with desire, undergoes cupping or bloodletting, or vomits, and then prays without renewing his ablution. There is a well-known disagreement concerning this situation. One of the two views is that the follower’s prayer is invalid because he believes his imam’s prayer to be invalid. This is the view held by some followers of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad.

---

## Page 12036

The second view:

The follower’s prayer is valid. This is the view of the majority of the early Muslim scholars and the position of Mālik. It is also the other view within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad—and indeed, Abū Hanīfah as well. Most of Ahmad’s explicit statements support this view. This is the correct position, because it is established in the Sahīh and elsewhere that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“They lead you in prayer. If they do so correctly, the reward is yours and theirs; if they err, the reward is yours, while the responsibility rests upon them.”
