The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) thus made clear that the imam’s error does not extend to the follower. Moreover, the follower believes that the imam is justified in what he has done and incurs no sin thereby, since he either exercises independent juristic reasoning or follows someone qualified to do so. The follower knows that Allah has forgiven such a person his error, and therefore considers the imam’s prayer valid and believes that he incurs no sin by not repeating it. Indeed, if a judicial ruling were issued on such a basis, he would not be permitted to overturn it; rather, he would enforce it.

Since the imam has acted according to his independent juristic reasoning—and Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity—and the follower has done what is required of him, the prayer of each is valid. Each has fulfilled his obligation, and the follower has conformed to the imam’s outward actions.

The assertion that the follower believes the imam’s prayer to be invalid is mistaken. The follower believes that the imam has done what is required of him, that Allah has forgiven him his error, and that his prayer is not invalidated on that account. If both the imam and the follower were mistaken, such that the imam mistakenly pronounced the salutation ending the prayer and the follower believed it permissible to

---

## Page 12037

follow him and therefore pronounced the salutation—as the Muslims did behind the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) when he inadvertently ended the prayer after two units, although they knew that he had prayed only two—or if the imam inadvertently prayed five units and they prayed five behind him, as the Companions did behind the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) when he led them in five units, following him despite knowing that he had prayed five because they believed this permissible, then the follower’s prayer would be valid in such a case. How much more so, then, when the imam alone is mistaken!

They have all agreed that if the imam mistakenly pronounces the salutation, the follower’s prayer is not invalidated provided he does not follow him in doing so. Likewise, if the imam prays five units, the follower’s prayer is not invalidated provided he does not follow him in that. This shows that an error committed by the imam does not necessarily invalidate the follower’s prayer. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is a follower’s prayer valid behind someone whose legal school differs from his own?

He replied:

A man’s prayer behind someone whose legal school differs from his own is valid by the consensus of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the four imams. There is, however, disagreement concerning two cases:

The first, in which the dissenting view is anomalous, is when the imam performs the obligatory acts as understood by

---

## Page 12038

the follower, even though the imam does not consider them obligatory—for example, when the imam recites the final tashahhud (testimony of faith) without considering it obligatory, whereas the follower considers it obligatory. The dissenting view in this case is anomalous. The correct position, held by the early generations and the majority of later scholars, is that the prayer is valid.

The second case is the subject of well-known disagreement: the imam omits something the follower considers obligatory. For example, he does not recite the basmalah, either silently or aloud, while the follower considers its recitation obligatory; or he does not perform ablution after touching the penis, touching women, eating camel meat, laughing aloud, the discharge of impurities, or an uncommon impurity, while the follower considers ablution obligatory on account of that. There are two opinions concerning this. The sounder is that the follower’s prayer is valid. This is Mālik’s position and the more explicit of the two reports from Ahmad concerning such questions. It is also one of the two positions within al-Shāfiʿī’s school; indeed, it is expressly reported from him, for he used to pray behind adherents of Mālik’s school who did not recite the basmalah, although his own position was that its recitation was obligatory.

The evidence for this is the report narrated by al-Bukhārī and others from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), who said:

“They lead you in prayer. If they do so correctly, you and they will receive the reward; if they err, you will receive the reward, while they will bear responsibility.”

Thus, he placed responsibility for the imam’s error upon the imam, not the follower.

If the imam’s position on these questions is correct, there is no dispute. If he is mistaken, responsibility for his error rests with him alone. The opposing view maintains: “The follower believes that his imam’s prayer is invalid.” That is not so; rather, he believes that the imam is praying

---

## Page 12039

on the basis of independent legal judgment or by following a scholarly authority. If he is correct, he receives two rewards; if he errs, he receives one. The follower also gives effect to a judge’s ruling in matters open to independent legal judgment, which is a weightier matter than following him in prayer. If the jurist’s ruling were invalid, it would not be permissible to give effect to what is invalid.

If the imam forgetfully omits ritual purification, the follower need not repeat the prayer according to the majority of scholars, as is established in reports from the Rightly Guided Caliphs. Yet the one who forgot must repeat his prayer, whereas one acting on an interpretive judgment need not. If prayer behind someone who must repeat his prayer is valid, then it is all the more valid behind someone who need not repeat it. The imam repeats the prayer when he remembers, but the follower does not. Neither the imam nor the follower has been negligent, for the imam would not abandon his conviction on the follower’s word.

This differs from a case in which the follower sees an impurity on the imam but does not alert him to it. Here the follower is negligent; if he prays, he must repeat the prayer because of his negligence. The imam, however, need not repeat it in this case, according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī in his earlier view, and Ahmad according to the sounder of the two reports from him.

When the follower knows the imam’s circumstances in a case involving interpretive judgment, this means that he knows the imam is exercising independent legal judgment and that his error is forgiven. His prayer is therefore not invalid. This position is unquestionably correct. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12040

He was asked:

May a Shāfiʿī follow a Hanafī, and vice versa, regarding the witr prayer and combining prayers because of rain, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

Yes, a Hanafī or anyone else may follow a scholar who permits combining prayers because of rain, especially since this is the position of the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar used to combine prayers with the rulers in Medina when they combined them because of rain.

No one is obliged to follow any particular person in everything he commands, prohibits, or recommends except the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Muslims have always sought rulings from Muslim scholars, following one scholar at times and another at other times. If someone follows a scholarly authority on an issue because he considers that better for his religious practice, or considers that position stronger, or for a similar reason, this is permissible by agreement of the great majority of Muslim scholars. Neither Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, nor Ahmad prohibited this.

Likewise, in witr and other prayers, the follower should follow his imam. If

---

## Page 12041

he recites the qunūt supplication, the follower should recite it with him; if he does not, neither should the follower. If he prays three rakʿahs continuously, the follower should do likewise; if he separates them, the follower should also separate them. Some prefer that the follower pray them continuously when his imam separates them, but the first view is more correct. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

If someone joins the imam for part of the prayer, then stands to complete what he missed, may others follow him in prayer?

He answered:

If someone joins the imam for part of the prayer, then stands to complete what he missed and others follow him, this is permissible according to the more correct of the two scholarly views.

He was asked:

About an imam who leads the people in the obligatory prayer, then leads another prayer afterward, saying, “This is for a prayer you missed.” Is this permissible?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The regular imam may not make a habit of leading the people in the obligatory prayer twice, for this is an innovation contrary to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 12042

and the Sunnah of his rightly guided caliphs. None of the four imams of the Muslims, nor any other imam, recommended this—not Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, or Ahmad ibn Hanbal. Rather, they agree that an imam’s regular practice of leading the same congregation in the prayer twice is a reprehensible innovation, and whoever does so as an act of devotion is misguided.

Their disagreement concerns only an imam who leads the prayer a second time with a different congregation. Some permit this, such as al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad ibn Hanbal according to one of the two reports from him. Others prohibit it, such as Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad according to the other report from him.

Anyone who owes missed prayers should make them up as best he can. But for the imam regularly to repeat the prayer alongside the current obligatory prayer, with the congregation praying behind him, is not prescribed, even if he says, “I do this because of the missed prayers they owe.”

At the very least, this opens the way for other imams to imitate him, so that it becomes an established practice on which children are raised, and through which the law of Islam is altered in desert regions and places where ignorance prevails. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12043

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

If someone has already performed his obligatory prayer—as an imam, a follower, or alone—may he then lead that same prayer for others who are performing their obligatory prayer, as when an imam leads the prayer twice? There is a well-known disagreement on this issue, and three positions are reported from Ahmad:

The first: It is not permissible. This is the view preferred by many of his followers and is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

The second: It is permissible without restriction. This is the view preferred by some of his followers, such as Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī.

The second: It is permissible when there is a need, as in the prayer of fear.

The Shaykh said:

This is the view preferred by our grandfather Abū al-Barakāt, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on some occasions led his Companions in the prayer of fear twice: he led one group and concluded with the salutation of peace, then led another group and concluded with the salutation of peace.

---

## Page 12044

Those who permit this without restriction cite the well-known hadith of Mu‘ādh:

“He used to pray behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then go and lead his people in prayer.”

One version states:

“The first prayer was obligatory for him, and the second was voluntary.”

Those who prohibit this have no sound proof. They cite wording that does not address the point in dispute, such as his saying:

“The imam has only been appointed to be followed, so do not differ from him.”

They also cite the statement that “the imam is a guarantor,” arguing that his prayer cannot be of a lesser status than that of the follower. Neither statement refutes the evidence cited above. The difference intended here concerns actions, as the explanatory wording makes clear. Otherwise, a follower may repeat the prayer, thereby offering a voluntary prayer behind someone offering an obligatory prayer, as is the position of the overwhelming majority of scholars.

This is supported by his statement in the authentic hadith:

“After me there will be rulers who delay the prayer beyond its prescribed time. Pray at its prescribed time, then let your prayer with them be voluntary.”

Furthermore:

“He prayed at al-Khayf Mosque and saw two men who had not prayed. He asked, ‘What prevented you from praying with us?’ They replied, ‘We have already prayed at our lodgings.’ He said, ‘If you have prayed at your lodgings and then come to a mosque where a congregation is praying, pray with them, for it will be a voluntary prayer for you.’”

The Sunan collections also relate:

“He saw a man praying alone and said, ‘Will someone not give this man charity by praying with him?’”

Thus, several hadiths establish that someone offering a voluntary prayer may pray behind someone offering an obligatory prayer. The reverse has also been established. It is therefore clear that matching the imam’s intention regarding whether the prayer is obligatory or

---

## Page 12045

voluntary is not required. The imam remains a guarantor even when offering a voluntary prayer.

This also includes offering the ‘Ishā’ prayer behind someone leading the Ramadan night prayers: one prays two rak‘ahs behind him, then stands to complete another two. The strongest view is that all of this is permissible. However, one should not lead another group in prayer a second time unless there is a need or benefit—for example, if no one else present is suitable to lead, or if he has the strongest claim to lead because he is the most knowledgeable about the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger. If they are equal in knowledge, he may have been the earliest to abandon what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, or he may be the oldest. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who emigrated earliest. If they are equal in emigration, then by the oldest.”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave precedence to superior knowledge of the Book and the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge, precedence is given to the one who was earliest in performing righteous deeds. The one whose precedence resulted from his own choice—the emigrant—is given priority over the one whose precedence resulted from Allah creating him earlier—the older person.

It is also established in the two authentic collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand Muslims are safe, and the emigrant is the one who abandons what Allah has forbidden.”

Whoever was earliest to abandon sins by repenting of them is therefore the earliest of them in emigration and is given precedence in leading the prayer. Thus, if someone with the strongest claim to lead is present and has already offered

---

## Page 12046

his obligatory prayer, he may lead them, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led two groups of his Companions in succession, praying once with each. Likewise, Mu‘ādh would pray and then lead his own people, the people of Qubā’, because he had the strongest claim to lead them.

Some have claimed that the hadith of Mu‘ādh was abrogated, but they have produced no sound evidence for this. Rulings established by the Quran and the Sunnah cannot be declared abrogated on grounds equally compatible with abrogation and its absence. This is a broad subject in which many people have fallen into such errors, as explained in detail elsewhere.

The same applies to the funeral prayer. If a man leads it and another group is then brought forward, he may lead the second group if he has the strongest claim to lead them. If someone else leads the funeral prayer a second time, he may join them in repeating it, just as he may repeat an obligatory prayer by joining a congregation. For example, he may pray at home and then come to a mosque with a regular imam and pray with them. This is prescribed in the school of Imam Ahmad, without disagreement within the school.

Likewise, according to his school, someone who has not offered the funeral prayer may offer it after others have done so, and may pray over the grave if he missed the funeral prayer. This is the position of all the jurists of the hadith tradition, such as al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Ishāq and others. Mālik, however, does not permit repeating it, while Abū Hanīfah permits it only for the deceased’s guardian.

As for someone who has already offered the funeral prayer, after which others offer it: may he repeat it with the second group? There are two views within Ahmad’s school.

It has been said:

---

## Page 12047

he may not repeat it. Those who hold this view argue that the second prayer is voluntary, and the funeral prayer is not offered as a voluntary prayer.

The other view is that he may repeat it, and this is the correct view. When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed over an isolated grave, those who had already offered the funeral prayer joined him. Repeating the funeral prayer is akin to repeating an obligatory prayer: it is prescribed wherever Allah and His Messenger have prescribed it.

On this basis, may he lead the funeral prayer twice? There are two reported positions. The correct view is that he may do so. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man prayed with the imam, then came upon another congregation and led them in prayer. Is this permissible or not?

He answered:

This is the question of “someone offering an obligatory prayer following an imam offering a voluntary prayer.” Since the imam has already performed his obligatory prayer, his subsequently leading others is permissible according to the school of al-Shāfi‘ī and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

There is also a third view within Ahmad’s school: it is permissible when there is a need, but not otherwise. Thus, if that imam is the one proficient in Quran recitation and has the rightful claim to lead them rather than the others, doing so in such circumstances is good. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12048

He was asked:

Is it permissible to pray behind an imam who leads the prayers at two mosques?

He answered:

If a regular imam can be appointed for each mosque, it is not proper to appoint one imam for two mosques. If he leads the prayer in two places, scholars differ over the validity of the second prayer for those praying behind him to fulfil their obligation. The position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, is that the congregation of the second mosque has not discharged its obligation. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

May someone offering an obligatory prayer pray behind someone offering a voluntary prayer?

He answered:

This is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly views. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him.

---

## Page 12049

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If a man performs a prayer as a precaution while doubting whether it is obligatory for him, may someone offering an obligatory prayer follow him as imam?

He answered:

By analogy with the school’s established rulings, this is valid, because the person in doubt performs it with the intention of fulfilling an obligation. This accords with what we have said concerning intention in cases of unconsciousness, even though we do not hold fasting to be obligatory. Likewise, we have said that someone who doubts whether his ablution has been invalidated should perform ablution.

The same applies to cases of doubt about whether purification, fasting, zakat, prayer, a ritual observance, expiation, or anything else is obligatory. This differs from someone believing an act to be obligatory and subsequently discovering that it was not. Differing rulings have been inferred concerning that case, since the act is actually voluntary, although he believed it to be obligatory. In the doubtful case, however, he intends the act as obligatory while remaining uncertain whether it is.

---

## Page 12050

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man found a congregation performing the noon prayer and wished to join them to make up the dawn prayer. When the imam rose for the third rak‘ah, he ceased following him and concluded his own prayer with the salutation of peace. Is this prayer valid? According to which school is it valid?

He answered:

This prayer is not valid according to the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, or according to one of the two reports from Ahmad. It is valid according to the school of al-Shāfi‘ī and the other report from Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man found the congregational prayer underway and intended to follow the imam, thinking that he was Zayd, but it turned out that he was Amr. Does this affect the validity of his prayer? Likewise, what if the imam made a similar mistake about someone praying behind him?

He answered:

If his intention was to pray behind the imam of that congregation, whoever

---

## Page 12051

he was, and he thought that he was Zayd but it turned out that he was Amr, his prayer is valid. This is like believing that the imam was white when he turned out to be black, or that he was wearing a wrap when it turned out to be a cloak, and similar mistaken assumptions that do not undermine the validity of following him in prayer.

But if his intention was to pray behind Zayd, and he would not have prayed behind him had he known that he was Amr, then, if the imam was Amr, he has not taken him as his imam. Actions are judged only by intentions. Is he then regarded as someone who prayed without following an imam, or is his prayer invalid? There is disagreement concerning this, just as there is when the imam’s prayer is invalid and the person following him is unaware of it.

Thus, not knowing the imam’s identity does not harm the person following him, provided that his intention is to pray behind the imam leading that congregation. Likewise, the imam is not harmed by not knowing the identities of those following him; rather, if he intends to lead those behind him in prayer, that is valid.

It has also been said that if he specifies a particular person but is mistaken, his prayer is invalid in all cases.

The correct view is to distinguish between specifying someone as the object of his intention—meaning that he intends to pray behind no one else—and identifying someone merely by assumption—meaning that he intends to pray behind the imam, whoever he may be, but assumes that he is Zayd. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12052

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Is the prayer of someone who prays alone behind the row valid or not? Are the hadiths reported concerning this authentic? Regarding the imams other than the four imams who hold this view—such as Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān, Ibn al-Mubārak, Sufyān al-Thawrī and al-Awzā'ī—a man has said of them, meaning these particular imams: “These men need not be heeded.” What ruling applies to someone who says this? Is someone who is permitted to follow scholarly authority allowed to follow these imams, just as he may follow the four imams, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. One scholarly view is that the prayer of someone who prays alone behind the row is invalid. This is because there are two hadiths concerning this from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: he ordered someone who prayed behind the row to repeat his prayer, and he said:

“There is no valid prayer for someone who prays alone behind the row.”

More than one leading hadith scholar has authenticated these two hadiths, and their chains of transmission are sufficient to establish them as authoritative evidence. Indeed, those who rule contrary to them rely, in many questions, on reports with weaker chains of transmission. Neither hadith contradicts established principles; rather, their content accords with what the well-known scriptural texts and established principles entail. For congregational prayer is so named

---

## Page 12053

because the worshippers come together in performing the prayer, in both place and time. If they fail to maintain this unity of place or time—for example, if all or some of them go ahead of the imam, or lag far behind him without an excuse—this is prohibited by agreement of the imams. Likewise, if they are scattered rather than arranged in rows, with one standing behind another, and another behind him, this would be among the most objectionable practices. Rather, they have been commanded to form rows. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded them to straighten and align their rows, stand closely together, close the gaps, and fill each row in turn, beginning with the first. All this emphatically ensures that they come together in the best possible manner. If forming rows were not obligatory, it would be permissible for one person to stand behind another, and so on.

Yet everyone knows, as a matter of common knowledge, that this is not how Muslims pray. Had it been permissible, Muslims would have done it at least once. The same applies if they make the row uneven, with one person standing ahead of another and another standing farther back. This is something the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is known to have forbidden, and a prohibition entails that an act is unlawful. Indeed, praying in front of the imam would be better than this. If the majority of scholars regard prayer in front of the imam as invalid—either in all circumstances or when there is no excuse—how can prayer be valid without forming rows?

Thus, reasoning by analogy from established principles entails that forming rows is obligatory and that the prayer of someone standing alone is invalid, just as these two hadiths state. As for any scholar who disagrees, there is no doubt that this Sunnah did not reach him

---

## Page 12054

through a channel he trusts. Indeed, he may never have heard it, or he may have thought the hadith weak, as some have stated. Those who opposed it argued that a woman’s prayer is valid when she stands alone, as established in the authentic narration:

“Anas and the orphan formed a row behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the elderly woman stood behind them.”

The scholars agree that her standing alone is valid when there is no other woman in the congregation, as established by the Sunnah. They also cited the imam’s standing alone. They further cited the hadith of Abū Bakrah, who bowed before reaching the row and then joined it. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“May Allah increase your eagerness, but do not do that again.”

This argument is weak and cannot counter the evidence prohibiting this practice, for several reasons:

First, a woman’s standing behind the men’s row is a prescribed Sunnah, whereas standing in the men’s row would be disliked. Does this invalidate the prayer of someone standing beside her? Scholars within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold two views. The first is that it does invalidate his prayer, as Abū Hanīfah held. This was also the view preferred by Abū Bakr and Abū Hafs, scholars of Ahmad’s school. The second is that it does not invalidate his prayer, as Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī held. This is also the view of Ibn

---

## Page 12055

Hāmid, al-Qādī, and others, although they disagree over whether a man standing beside her counts as standing alone. Ahmad’s explicitly reported position is that the prayer of someone standing immediately beside her is invalid. As for a man standing alone behind the row, they agree that this is disliked and contrary to the Sunnah. How, then, can what is prohibited be deemed analogous to what is commanded? Likewise, the imam’s standing ahead of the row is the Sunnah. How can what is commanded be deemed analogous to what is prohibited? Valid analogy compares a case on which the texts are silent with one they explicitly address. Comparing one explicitly addressed case with another for which the texts establish a contrary ruling is invalid by scholarly agreement—as with comparing usury to trade, when Allah has permitted trade and forbidden usury.

(Second, the woman stood behind the row because she had no one with whom to form a row, and she could not stand in a row with the men. Thus, had another woman been praying with her, she ought to have stood alongside her, and the same ruling would have applied to her as to a man standing apart from the men’s row. The analogous case is that of a man who can find nowhere to stand except behind the row. Those who consider the prayer of someone standing alone invalid disagree over this case. Otherwise, it is evident that his prayer in this situation is valid, because all obligatory acts of prayer are waived when one is unable to perform them. Applying this principle consistently also entails the validity of praying ahead of the imam when there is a need, as a group of scholars holds. This is also one view within Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 12056

Since standing, recitation, bowing and prostrating fully, purification with water, and other such duties are waived when one is unable to perform them, so too are standing in a row and refraining from standing ahead of the imam. The same principle extends to the remaining questions concerning prayer rows, such as praying without seeing either the imam or those behind him while still hearing the declarations of “Allah is the Greatest,” and other such cases. The imam is placed ahead so that those praying behind him can see and follow him; this consideration does not apply to a follower.

As for the hadith of Abū Bakrah, it does not state that he prayed alone behind the row before the imam raised his head from bowing. He thus fulfilled enough of the prescribed requirement to join the row for the unit of prayer to count. His case is like that of someone who stands alone and is then joined by another person standing beside him during the standing portion of the prayer. This is permissible by agreement of the imams. The hadith of Abū Bakrah contains a prohibition in the Prophet’s words:

“Do not do that again.”

It does not state that he ordered him to repeat that unit of prayer, unlike the hadith concerning the man praying alone, whom he ordered to repeat the prayer. The latter report is clear and explicit, whereas the former leaves the matter unspecified. Even supposing that the hadith of Abū Bakrah expressly stated that he joined the row after the imam had stood upright—as is permitted according to one of the two views in Ahmad’s school and by others—this would be permissible in such a case, but not in the case in which he ordered the prayer to be repeated. Each of these views has a basis.

As for distinguishing between someone who knows the prohibition and someone who does not, as one view within Ahmad’s school does, this is untenable. The man who prayed alone did not know of the prohibition, yet the Prophet ordered him to repeat the prayer, just as he ordered the Bedouin who prayed incorrectly to repeat his prayer.

---

## Page 12057

As for the imams mentioned, they are among the foremost imams of Islam. Al-Thawrī was the imam of the people of Iraq, and most of them regarded him as more eminent than his contemporaries, such as Ibn Abī Laylā, al-Hasan ibn Sālih ibn Hayy, Abū Hanīfah, and others (*). His school survives to this day in Khurāsān. Al-Awzāʿī was the imam of the people of Syria, who continued to follow his school until the fourth century. Indeed, the people of the Maghrib followed his school before Mālik’s school reached them.

Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān was Abū Hanīfah’s teacher. Moreover, this view is also held by Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and others, and his school survives to this day. It is also the position of Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī and his followers, whose school likewise survives to this day. Thus, people today have not reached a consensus against this view; rather, many uphold it in both the East and the West.

The Quran and Sunnah draw no distinction between individual imams qualified to exercise independent legal reasoning. Mālik, al-Layth ibn Saʿd, al-Awzāʿī, and al-Thawrī were all imams in their time, and following the legal authority of any one of them is like following that of another. No Muslim says that it is permissible to follow one but not another. However, when someone prohibits following any of these imams in our time, such a prohibition rests on only one of two grounds:

The first is his belief that no one remains who knows their schools. There is a well-known disagreement over following a deceased scholar. Those who prohibit it say, “These scholars are dead,” while those who permit it say, “There must be someone living who knows the deceased scholar’s position.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 195):
> This passage was separated from the beginning of the answer, which appears in vol. 20, pp. 583–585. This has already been noted in the discussion of volume twenty.

---

## Page 12058

The second is to say, “A consensus has now been established against this view.” This rests on a well-known question in the principles of jurisprudence: if the Companions, for example, or the scholars of another generation differed over an issue, holding two positions, and then the Successors or the scholars of the following generation agreed upon one of them, does this constitute a consensus that supersedes the earlier disagreement?

There is a well-known disagreement on this question within the school of Ahmad and among other scholars. Anyone who holds that the consensus of the second generation makes it impermissible to adopt the other position, and also believes that the scholars of the age have reached such a consensus, bases his prohibition on these two beliefs. Whoever recognizes that the earlier disagreement retains its force—since opinions do not die with those who voiced them—considers it permissible for a scholar qualified to exercise independent legal reasoning to adopt the other position if his reasoning leads him to it. As for following a scholar’s legal authority, this depends on the question of following a deceased scholar, concerning which there are likewise two well-known positions within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others.

If, however, the position held by these imams or others is also held by some scholars whose schools remain in existence, then there is no doubt that their agreement supports and strengthens that position. One may cite their authority against those of their contemporaries who disagreed with them. Thus, one may cite al-Thawrī and al-Awzā‘ī against Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, for the Muslim community agrees that when Mālik

---

## Page 12059

and al-Awzā‘ī, al-Thawrī, and Abū Hanīfah differ, it is impermissible to declare one person’s view correct rather than another’s without evidence. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Was the practice of relaying the imam’s words from behind him observed during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, or at any time during the rule of the Rightly Guided Caliphs? If not, then, assuming there is no fear of disrupting the following of the imam, the prescribed composure in prayer, the continuity of the rows, or the listening of those behind the imam to him, is the person who does this to be described as committing an innovation if any of these things is disrupted? Have any Muslim scholars held that his prayer is thereby invalidated? What is the ruling on someone who, after the matter has been explained to him, believes this to be an act that brings him closer to Allah, whether or not he performs it?

He replied:

The practice of relaying the imam’s words, repeating the declarations of Allah’s greatness, and raising one’s voice with words of praise and the concluding salutation was not observed during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, nor during the rule of his caliphs, nor for a long time thereafter, except on two occasions:

On one occasion, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, was thrown from a horse he was riding, so he prayed seated in his house, and Abū Bakr relayed his declarations of Allah’s greatness. This is how Muslim narrated it in his Sahīh. On another occasion, during his final illness, Abū Bakr relayed his words. This is well known.

---

## Page 12060

Yet the apparent position of Imam Ahmad’s school is that, in this prayer, Abū Bakr was following the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, while leading the people. Thus, Abū Bakr relayed the Prophet’s words in his capacity as the people’s imam, even though he himself was following the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. As ʿĀ’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said:

“The people followed Abū Bakr, and Abū Bakr followed the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.”

No scholar has mentioned any instance of relaying the imam’s words during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, apart from these two occasions, owing to his illness. When scholars writing on the subject needed evidence that relaying the imam’s words is permissible when necessary, this was the only precedent they had from the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. Anyone familiar with his Sunnah knows this with certainty. Scholars agree that relaying the imam’s words without a need is not recommended; indeed, many have explicitly stated that it is disliked.

Some have said that the prayer of someone who does so is invalid. This view is found in the schools of Mālik, Ahmad, and others. As for cases of need, because a worshipper is far from the imam, the imam is weak, or for some other reason, scholars have differed over this practice in such circumstances. The recognized position among Ahmad’s followers is that it is permissible in this situation, and this is the sounder of the two views held by Mālik’s followers. I have been informed that Ahmad withheld judgment on the matter. Where it is permissible and does not invalidate the prayer, it is subject to the condition that it must not compromise any of the obligatory elements of prayer.

---

## Page 12061

If the person relaying the imam’s words does not come to rest in the postures of prayer, his prayer is invalid according to scholars generally, as the Sunnah indicates. If he also moves ahead of the imam, his prayer is invalid according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. This is what the Sunnah and the statements of the Companions indicate. If he neglects the remembrance recited in bowing and prostration, the glorification of Allah, or the like, scholars differ over whether his prayer is invalid. The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that it is invalid.

There is no doubt that relaying the imam’s words without a need is an innovation. Whoever believes it to be a means of drawing near to Allah in all circumstances is undoubtedly either ignorant or wilfully defiant. After all, scholars of all schools have stated this in their books, even in their abridged manuals. They say:

“No one should recite any takbīr (saying ‘Allah is the Greatest’) aloud unless he is the imam.”

Anyone who persists in believing this practice to be a means of drawing near to Allah is to be disciplined for contravening scholarly consensus. This is the least that applies to him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Is it permissible to recite the takbīr aloud behind the imam?”

He answered:

Reciting the takbīr aloud behind the imam—that is, relaying his words—is not prescribed without a need, by agreement of the imams. Neither Bilāl nor anyone else used to relay the Prophet’s words from behind him, peace and blessings be upon him, nor was this done behind the Rightly Guided Caliphs. However, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, fell ill, he once led the people in prayer while his voice was weak. Abū Bakr prayed beside him, making the takbīr audible to the people. The scholars therefore cited this as evidence

---

## Page 12062

that reciting the takbīr aloud is prescribed when needed, such as when the imam’s voice is weak. Without such a need, however, they agreed that it is disliked and not prescribed. They held two opinions as to whether the prayer of someone who does so is invalid. This disagreement over its validity is well known within the schools of Mālik, Ahmad, and others. Nevertheless, it is disliked by agreement of all the schools. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is relaying the imam’s words from behind him recommended, or is it an innovation?”

He answered:

Relaying the imam’s words from behind him without a need is an innovation, not a recommended practice, by agreement of the imams. Only the imam should recite the takbīr aloud, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his caliphs used to do. No one used to relay the Prophet’s words from behind him, peace and blessings be upon him. However, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, fell ill, his voice grew weak, so Abū Bakr—may Allah be pleased with him—would make the takbīr audible to the people.

The scholars differed as to whether the prayer of the person relaying the imam’s words is invalid. There are two opinions within the schools of Mālik, Ahmad, and others.

---

## Page 12063

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is prayer valid when one stands ahead of the imam, or behind him in the mosque with a barrier between them?”

He answered:

Regarding a follower praying ahead of the imam, the scholars hold three opinions:

The first is that the prayer is valid in all cases, even if it is said to be disliked. This is the well-known position in Mālik’s school and al-Shāfiʿī’s earlier opinion.

The second is that it is invalid in all cases. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, and the well-known position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

The third is that it is valid when there is a legitimate excuse, but not otherwise. For example, if overcrowding prevents someone from performing the Friday prayer or funeral prayer except by standing ahead of the imam, then praying ahead of him is better than foregoing the prayer. This is the opinion of a group of scholars and is one position within the school of Ahmad and others. It is the most balanced and strongest of the opinions.

---

## Page 12064

This is because refraining from standing ahead of the imam is, at most, one of the obligations of congregational prayer. All obligations are waived when there is a valid excuse, even those required in prayer itself; an obligation specific to congregational prayer is therefore all the more deserving of being waived. Thus, a worshipper is excused from whatever he is unable to perform, whether standing, reciting, covering himself, purifying himself, or anything else.

In congregational prayer, a follower sits during his odd-numbered units of prayer in order to follow the imam, whereas deliberately doing so when praying alone would invalidate his prayer. If he joins the imam while the latter is prostrating or sitting, he pronounces the opening takbīr and prostrates or sits with him in order to follow him, even though this does not count as a unit of prayer for him. He also performs the prostrations for the imam’s forgetfulness, even if he himself has not forgotten anything.

Likewise, in the prayer performed in circumstances of fear, a worshipper may pray without facing the qiblah, make extensive movements, separate from the imam before the closing salutation, and make up his first unit of prayer before the imam’s closing salutation. These and other actions are performed for the sake of congregational prayer, although doing them without an excuse would invalidate his prayer.

An even stronger example is the position of most scholars of Basra and most hadith scholars: if the regularly appointed imam prays sitting, the followers also pray sitting in order to follow him. They thus forgo the obligatory standing posture in order to follow him, as has been widely transmitted

---

## Page 12065

in the reports from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“And when he prays sitting, all of you should pray sitting.”

There are three views on this issue:

One view is that a seated person may not lead a standing person in prayer, and that such leadership was a privilege exclusive to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). This is the view of Mālik and Muhammad ibn al-Hasan.

Another view is that he may lead them while they stand, and that the command to sit has been abrogated. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī.

A third view is that the command remains in force. More than one Companion acted upon it after the death of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), including Usayd ibn Hudayr and others. This is the position of Hammād ibn Zayd, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others. According to this position, if the followers pray standing, there are two views regarding the validity of their prayer.

The point here is that congregational prayer is performed according to what is possible. If a follower can only follow his imam by standing ahead of him, the most that can be said is that he has forgone the prescribed position for the sake of congregational prayer. This is a lesser concession than the others mentioned. A similar case is that of someone who is forbidden to pray alone behind the row: if he finds no one to stand beside him and does not draw anyone over to pray with him, he should pray alone behind the row rather than forgo congregational prayer, just as

---

## Page 12066

a woman who cannot find another woman to stand beside her stands alone behind the row, by agreement of the imams. A person is commanded to stand alongside others only when able to do so, not when unable.

Section

As for a follower praying behind the imam outside the mosque, or inside the mosque with a barrier between them, this is permissible by agreement of the imams if the rows are continuous. If a road or a river navigated by boats separates them, there are two well-known views, both reported from Ahmad:

The first is that it is not permissible, as Abū Hanīfah held.

The second is that it is permissible, as al-Shāfi'ī held.

If a barrier between them prevents both sight and passage, there are several views within Ahmad’s school and those of others. One view permits it; another does not. Another permits it within the mosque but not elsewhere. Yet another permits it when there is a need, but not otherwise.

There is no doubt that it is permissible whenever there is a need, without qualification, such as when the mosque doors are closed, or when the enclosure containing the imam is

---

## Page 12067

closed, or the like. In such cases, even if seeing the imam were obligatory, that requirement would be waived because of the need. As explained above, the obligations of prayer and congregational worship are waived when there is a valid excuse, and praying in congregation is better than praying alone in all circumstances.

He was asked:

Is the prayer of someone who prays with the imam valid if a barrier between them prevents him from seeing either the imam or anyone who can see him?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, his prayer is valid according to most scholars. This is also Ahmad’s explicitly stated position, for he expressly stated that the pulpit does not prevent one from following the imam in prayer.

The Sunnah regarding the rows is to complete the first row, then each successive row, and to stand close together within the row. If someone prays at the rear of the mosque while the space nearest the imam remains empty, his prayer is disliked. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12068

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

An imam leads a congregation, with some people behind him and others ahead of him. Is the prayer of those ahead of the imam valid or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The prayer of those behind the imam is undoubtedly valid. As for those ahead of him, the scholars hold three views: that their prayer is valid; that it is invalid; and that it is valid if they cannot pray with him except with difficulty. This last view is the strongest. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Regarding market traders’ shops adjoining the congregational mosque: if the rows extend to them ن, is it permissible for them to perform the Friday prayer in their shops?

He answered:

For the Friday prayer and other prayers, people must complete the first row, then each successive row, as reported in the two Sahīh collections from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said:

“

---

## Page 12069

Will you not form rows as the angels form rows before their Lord?”

They asked, “How do the angels form rows before their Lord?”

He replied, “They complete the first row, then each successive row, and stand close together in the row.”

No one may therefore fill the rear rows while the front rows remain vacant, nor may people form rows in the streets and shops while there is still space in the mosque. Anyone who does so deserves disciplinary action. Those arriving after him may step over him and enter to complete the front rows, for he has no right to protection from being stepped over in this situation.

Likewise, no one may send ahead something to be spread out for him in the mosque while he himself stays behind. Whatever has been spread out for him has no protected status; rather, according to the correct view, it may be removed and someone else may pray in its place.

Instead, when the mosque is filled with rows, people should form rows outside it. If the rows then extend continuously into the streets and marketplaces, their prayer is valid. But if they form rows with a road used by pedestrians separating them from the next row, their prayer is invalid according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views.

Likewise, if a wall separates them from the rows, so that they cannot see the rows but can hear the takbīr (the proclamation “Allah is Most Great”), and they pray in this way without any need to do so, their prayer is invalid according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views.

Similarly, if someone prays in his shop while the road is empty, his prayer is invalid. Nor is he permitted

---

## Page 12070

to sit in his shop and wait for the rows to reach him. Rather, he must go to the mosque and fill the first row, then each successive row. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is a person’s Friday prayer valid if he chooses to pray in marketplaces, shops, or streets?”

He replied:

If the rows are continuous, there is no harm in praying there for someone who arrives late and has no other option. But those who deliberately remain there instead of entering the mosque, such as those who sit in the shops, are acting wrongly and contrary to the Sunnah.

For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Will you not form rows as the angels form rows before their Lord?”

They asked, “How do the angels form rows before their Lord?”

He replied, “They complete the first row, then each successive row, and stand close together in the row.”

He also said:

“The best rows for men are the first, and the worst are the last.”

If, however, the rows are not continuous, but a road separates them, the scholars hold two views concerning the validity of the prayer, both transmitted from Ahmad.

---

## Page 12071

The first view is that the prayer is not valid, as Abū Hanīfah holds. The second is that it is valid, as al-Shāfi‘ī holds. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Regarding a congregational mosque beside the marketplace, from which the proclamation ‘Allah is Most Great’ can be heard: is it permissible to perform Friday prayer in the marketplace, on its roof, or in the shops, or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the congregational mosque is full, it is permissible to pray in the streets. When these are full, people may pray in the shops and other premises between them. But if the rows are not continuous, it is not permissible. The same applies to praying on rooftops. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12072

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“About a man who gathers people for voluntary prayer and leads them from the beginning of Rajab to the end of Ramadan, praying twenty rak‘ahs with ten concluding salutations between Maghrib and ‘Ishā’. In each rak‘ah, he recites al-Fātihah and, three times, the surah beginning ‘Say: He is Allah, One.’ He makes this a regular observance and argues that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, led Ibn ‘Abbās and the Ansārī who said to him, ‘Floodwaters come between me and you.’ Does this accord with the Sharia or not? And, under these circumstances, will he be rewarded for it or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

Voluntary prayer performed in congregation is of two kinds:

The first consists of prayers for which regular congregational observance is recommended, such as the eclipse prayer, the prayer for rain, and the Ramadan night prayers. These are consistently performed in congregation, as established by the Sunnah.

The second consists of prayers for which regular congregational observance is not recommended, such as the night prayer, the regular Sunnah prayers, the forenoon prayer, the prayer of greeting the mosque, and similar prayers.

---

## Page 12073

It is permissible to perform these prayers in congregation occasionally. Regular congregational observance of them, however, is not prescribed; rather, it is a blameworthy innovation. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, the Companions, and the Successors did not habitually gather for regular voluntary prayers, even on a lesser scale than this. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, only performed such voluntary prayers with a small congregation on occasion. He ordinarily prayed at night alone, but when Ibn ‘Abbās stayed overnight with him, he prayed with him. On another night, Hudhayfah prayed with him, and on yet another, Ibn Mas‘ūd prayed with him. Likewise, he prayed at the home of ‘Itbān ibn Mālik al-Ansārī, in a place that ‘Itbān used for prayer, and ‘Itbān prayed with him. He also led Anas, his mother, and the orphan in prayer.

He performed most of his voluntary prayers alone. What we have explained concerns voluntary prayers established by the Sunnah. As for instituting a prayer with a fixed number of rak‘ahs and a specified recitation, at a designated time, to be performed regularly in congregation—such as the prayers asked about here: the “Raghā’ib prayer” on the first Friday of Rajab, and the “Alfiyyah prayer” at the beginning of Rajab, in the middle of Sha‘bān, and on the twenty-seventh night of Rajab, and similar practices—this is not prescribed, by agreement of the leading scholars of Islam, as recognized scholars have explicitly stated. Only an ignorant innovator would institute such a practice. Opening this door leads to altering the laws of Islam and sharing in the condition of those who have instituted religious practices for which Allah has given no permission. And Allah knows best.

> End of Volume Twenty-Three.
